<meta>This page serves as the introductory disclaimer and mission statement for a digitized work, detailing the provenance of the physical book and the terms of its digital use.</meta>

<image-desc>The multi-colored Google logo with a small superscript TM trademark symbol.</image-desc>

This is a digital copy of a book that was preserved for generations on library shelves before it was carefully scanned by Google as part of a project to make the world’s books discoverable online.

It has survived long enough for the copyright to expire and the book to enter the <note>The 'public domain' consists of all the creative work to which no exclusive intellectual property rights apply. Those rights may have expired, been forfeited, expressly waived, or may be inapplicable.</note> public domain. A public domain book is one that was never subject to copyright or whose legal copyright term has expired. Whether a book is in the public domain may vary country to country. Public domain books are our gateways to the past, representing a wealth of history, culture and knowledge that’s often difficult to discover.

Marks, notations and other marginalia present in the original volume will appear in this file—a reminder of this book’s long journey from the publisher to a library and finally to you.

## Usage guidelines

Google is proud to partner with libraries to digitize public domain materials and make them widely accessible. Public domain books belong to the public and we are merely their custodians. Nevertheless, this work is expensive, so in order to keep providing this resource, we have taken steps to prevent abuse by commercial parties, including placing technical restrictions on <term>automated querying|The practice of using software scripts or 'bots' to automatically search and download data from a system</term>.

We also ask that you:

+ *Make non-commercial use of the files* We designed Google Book Search for use by individuals, and we request that you use these files for personal, non-commercial purposes.
+ *Refrain from automated querying* Do not send automated queries of any sort to Google’s system: If you are conducting research on machine translation, <term>optical character recognition|The electronic conversion of images of typed, handwritten, or printed text into machine-encoded text</term> or other areas where access to a large amount of text is helpful, please contact us. We encourage the use of public domain materials for these purposes and may be able to help.
+ *Maintain attribution* The Google “watermark” you see on each file is essential for informing people about this project and helping them find additional materials through Google Book Search. Please do not remove it.
+ *Keep it legal* Whatever your use, remember that you are responsible for ensuring that what you are doing is legal. Do not assume that just because we believe a book is in the public domain for users in the United States, that the work is also in the public domain for users in other countries. Whether a book is still in copyright varies from country to country, and we can’t offer guidance on whether any specific use of any specific book is allowed. Please do not assume that a book’s appearance in Google Book Search means it can be used in any manner anywhere in the world. Copyright infringement liability can be quite severe.

## About Google Book Search

Google’s mission is to organize the world’s information and to make it universally accessible and useful. Google Book Search helps readers discover the world’s books while helping authors and publishers reach new audiences. ### DELLA PORTA OF NAPLES,
### AUTHOR. <note>Giambattista della Porta (1535–1615) was an Italian polymath. This work, "Magia Naturalis," was his most famous, exploring everything from optics and botany to alchemy and cryptography. To Porta, "natural magic" was the peak of natural philosophy—the study of the hidden forces of nature through observation and experiment, rather than supernatural sorcery.</note>

# TO THE MOST SERENE
# AND CATHOLIC KING PHILIP,
## GIOVANNI BATTISTA DELLA PORTA
### OF NAPLES.
### SENDS GREETING. <note>original: "S. D." stands for "Salutem Dicit," a standard Latin salutation meaning "sends greeting" or "wishes health."</note>

---

<image-desc>A large historiated drop cap 'E' ornate with vine and leaf patterns within a square border.</image-desc>
I HAVE ALWAYS BEEN of such a mind, Most Invincible King, that if there were anything anywhere either great or wonderful, in which the powers of the mind might be tested and humbled, I would delight in it, and wish to devote myself entirely to it. For a long time, from my earliest childhood, I have considered with a certain tireless zeal what <term>science</term> <note>In the 16th century, "scientia" referred to a systematic body of knowledge or certain understanding, rather than the modern laboratory-based method.</note> might be neither unworthy of this mind of mine, nor lacking in nobility and Royal character. At last I discovered that there is no greater work for a wise man than to produce wondrous works, by which indeed not only is the mind nourished through understanding, but all the senses of the body are also enticed and refreshed by these experiences. This sublime science is occupied with the knowledge of things and their causes; and while it pursues and investigates the <term>secrets of nature</term>, <note>original: "naturæ arcana." This refers to the hidden properties of plants, minerals, and animals that "natural magic" sought to uncover and utilize.</note> it produces not only common works, as is seen in many things, but—having cast aside all superstition—it brings forth certain marvels and miracles of Nature. Thus it so excels all others (excepting, of course, divine Philosophy) that the other arts and sciences seem to serve it as if it were a queen. Therefore, I rightly judge it to be as lofty and royal above the rest as it is by far the most difficult. For <term>Cicero</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero (106–43 BCE), the Roman statesman and orator whose writings were the gold standard for Latin style and philosophy during the Renaissance.</note> says: No one among...

<summary>In this formal dedication, Giambattista della Porta presents his study of "Natural Magic" to King Philip II of Spain, arguing that the investigation of nature's secrets is the highest and most regal of all intellectual pursuits. He distinguishes his work from mere superstition, framing it instead as a sublime science that reveals the true wonders of the natural world.</summary>
<keywords>Philip II of Spain, Giambattista della Porta, Natural Magic, philosophy of science, secrets of nature, Renaissance science, royal dedication</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to King Philip II of Spain. Della Porta is concluding his justification for the study of "Natural Magic" by aligning it with the great philosophers and kings of antiquity, transitioning from the general history of the craft to his personal sacrifices in its pursuit.</meta>

It was the custom of the Persians that no one would gain the kingdom who had not first mastered <term>Magic</term>. <note>original: "Magiam"; in this context, the term refers to "Natural Magic"—the study of the hidden secrets of nature and experimental science—rather than modern stage magic or sorcery.</note> Plato indeed says this in his *Alcibiades*: the sons of the Persian Kings are so steeped in it that they might learn to administer and organize their own Commonwealth according to the pattern of the world's Commonwealth. Why should I mention Pythagoras, Democritus, Empedocles, and Plato, the most famous men in nearly the whole world? They burned with such a desire to learn this science that they traveled the world in their voyages, undertaking what were more like exiles than voyages; then, having returned, they preached it and kept it among their secrets. Why should I speak of the <term>Magi Kings</term> <note>The "Wise Men" from the biblical Nativity story, often cited by Renaissance authors to prove that magic was a holy and royal pursuit.</note> coming to adore Christ, and many others famous in this art, with whose names the monuments of the ancients are filled?

In these matters, you will surely consider with how much labor and how many sleepless nights I have been occupied—not merely by searching through books, but by testing through experiment, and not without the <note>The following phrase is underlined in the original:</note> loss of my own family's wealth <note>original: "familiaris rei iactura"; Della Porta spent a significant portion of his personal fortune on scientific instruments, rare materials, and experiments.</note>—so that I might one day be able to honor you with some worthy gift. I have drawn much from the fountains of the ancients, and I myself have added some things from the small streams of my own wit. Therefore, I dedicate these books on **Natural Magic** (although they may not be a sufficient return to you, for I am not of such great worth, yet it will be enough to have wished it) to you alone, **King PHILIP**, as they were begun under your auspices. I do this both so that this science of ours, cultivated by ancient Kings, may receive splendor and orna-
->ment from your name and your favor,<-
->and so that from those who, being of<-
->a humbler wit and slow mind, will<-
->be more hostile to my studies, they<-
->may through you obtain the strong-<-
->est protection and pa-<-
->tronage.<-

### FAREWELL.

<summary>Della Porta concludes his dedication by linking Natural Magic to the wisdom of ancient kings and philosophers, and he requests King Philip II's protection against critics of his scientific work.</summary>
<keywords>Philip II, Natural Magic, Persian Kings, Plato, Pythagoras, experimental science, patronage, Magi</keywords>

Following the dedication to King Philip II, Della Porta begins his preface by justifying the state of the current publication, addressing the challenges of plagiarism, intellectual theft, and the heavy hand of ecclesiastical censorship.</meta>

<image-desc>A rectangular decorative woodcut headpiece featuring scrolling foliage, floral motifs, and two dogs' heads facing outward in the center.</image-desc>

# THE AUTHOR'S PREFACE

## TO THE READERS.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'E' featuring scrolling vine motifs on a rectangular background.</image-desc>

*Behold this premature work, honest Readers; if I had added to it that embellishment which I had truly intended in my mind, I might perhaps have better satisfied—with a fuller service—those who are greedy for the hidden secrets of nature and literature. For while these things began to be tested, some individuals, seeking glory from the labors of others, since they claimed such a task for themselves, were forging various writings from every source. The malice of the envious was so great that while these secrets were being searched for too greedily, they came forth into the light of men mutilated and disorganized sooner than we had expected. Indeed, every diligent student will be able to notice that many things regarding that famous <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "pharmaco"; here referring to the "Philosopher's Stone" or the "Universal Medicine" sought by alchemists to cure all diseases and transmute metals.</note> of the philosophers—matters neither unpleasant nor useless—are omitted, as well as things of greater weight which, because they required a long time, were still in progress. Furthermore, many things, examined too severely by the sharp <term>rod of the censors</term> <note>original: "censorum virga"; a reference to the Roman Inquisition and the censors of the Press. Della Porta’s work was often under scrutiny for bordering on forbidden magic.</note>, are deleted, torn apart, and removed, not without my grief. These were things worthy of admiration and a clever philosopher, rather than being a profane work; hence, since I cannot do what I wish, I must necessarily wish for what I can do.*

*Since from my early youth an innate desire for hearing and knowing these things was born in me, it grew so much as I came of age that with more accurate and persistent study, day and night, I turned over whatever monuments of our ancestors survived; and if they uttered even a word on the subject, I noted it down. Nor did I apply less diligent ears to any who were experts in these matters, or if such things could be known in any way; and I explored what was read and heard through long-term experience, so that nothing remained untried. For I often remembered the sentiment of <term>Cicero</term> <note>Marcus Tullius Cicero, the Roman statesman and orator whose works were the foundation of Renaissance Latin education.</note>, who said thus:*

> *It is only fair that those who desire to pass down to memory things most useful, well-weighed, and explored for the human race, should try all things, and nev-* <note>The sentence continues onto the next page.</note>

<vocab>preface, Cicero, philosopher, nature of things, medicine/remedy</vocab>

<summary>In this preface, Della Porta apologizes for the "premature" and "mutilated" state of his book, blaming plagiarists who forced him to publish early and the harsh deletions made by religious censors. He emphasizes his lifelong dedication to researching the secrets of nature through both ancient texts and practical experimentation.</summary>
<keywords>Giambattista della Porta, Natural Magic, censorship, Inquisition, alchemy, plagiarism, experimental science, Cicero</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, moving from a defense against censors and plagiarists to a critique of classical and medieval authorities who relied on hearsay rather than direct observation.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<margin><note>original: "Psudepurgeias"; likely a reference to false or forged works, from the Greek for "false labors."</note></margin>

I was noting down how these things stood up to the test of experience, with a mind more intent on finding truths than falsehoods. For I saw clearly that those authors had not committed such things to writing for the sake of empty profit or the pursuit of vain glory—things that were meant to endure forever—but rather to unearth the secrets of nature and reveal them, not without the greatest labor in writing and discovery. And when we found things that rang true, it was not nearly as precious to me as the discovery that the same care had occupied and troubled their minds. But after a long period of <term>testing natural things</term> <note>original: "naturalium rerum periculum"; in this context, "periculum" refers to an experiment or trial.</note>, it was perceived through frequent observation, and we clearly <gloss>saw</gloss> knew that they had been more eager for writing than for investigating. They write many things far removed from the truth, passing information from one to another as if the actual operation were too difficult.

---

<margin>Cato.</margin>
Cato relates that a vessel made of ivy wood naturally separates wine, even if it has water mixed into it; when you pour into it that wine which you think contains water, he says that if the wine has been diluted, the wine flows out and the water remains, because ivy wood cannot hold wine. In ancient times, vessels were commonly fashioned to test diluted wine, devised to detect the frauds of the grape-harvesters. 

Pliny, and almost everyone else, copies this from him; yet there is no one in such a long line of succession who actually tests it, for the opposite is apparent, <margin>Galen.</margin> and we do not know by what reasoning or experience they were led. Galen mocks the claim everyone put forward: that crushed basil [ocymum] very quickly generates scorpions. He himself, testing this with empty pots and heating them daily in the sun, detected the falsehood with great skill; and yet, if it is lightly bruised on the sides in a damp place and exposed to the sun, it produces those little ones <note>i.e., small scorpions.</note> which then grow day by day—and it is not that others were summoned there, attracted by the scent. 

<margin>Pliny. Albertus.</margin>
Who would believe that those authors so famous in our <unclear>literature</unclear> <note>original: "ligna"; possibly a transcription error for "lingua" (language) or "literis" (letters/literature).</note>, Pliny and Albert [Albertus Magnus], are very often mistaken? One of whom is called noble, the other a lying rustic; for while the former copies by relying on the testimony of others, the latter lies on his own authority. He is not consistent in his speech, nor does he know what he says about these things, and he often fills pages with the kind of chatter old women babble. 

Why should I mention other men most famous for their authority? If they produced anything, they did not know the facts and the simple materials entering into the work through direct inspection alone; instead, they taught through the traditions of others, driven by a certain innate and untimely zeal for adding to the story. <margin>Aristotle & reason</margin> Thus, errors are successively propagated and eventually grow to such an immense size that not a trace of the original truth appears, and they are hardly recognizable from the start. They are written such that they are not only difficult to experiment upon, but cannot even be read without frequent laughter. Furthermore, we omit many others whom we did not think necessary to review at present, who [wrote] about salt, beans...

<summary>Della Porta criticizes classical authorities like Cato, Pliny, and Albertus Magnus for propagating scientific myths—such as ivy wood separating wine from water—without performing experiments. He argues that errors grow over time as authors prioritize writing over direct observation.</summary>
<keywords>empiricism, scientific method, Cato the Elder, Pliny the Elder, Galen, Albertus Magnus, basil, scorpions, ivy wood, forgery</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of alchemical and philosophical writers who intentionally obscure their knowledge. While the previous page discussed the lack of experimental testing in classical authors like Pliny and Cato, this section addresses the "darkness" created by those who use riddles and metaphors to hide their ignorance or protect their secrets.</meta>

# PREFACE.

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: and per-</meta>

And with certain coverings of riddles, parables, and linguistic inventions, they have deliberately spread the deepest darkness while passing down marvelous things to be learned by posterity; they *promise mountains of gold* <note>An ancient Roman idiom, "aureos montes polliceri," meaning to make extravagant or impossible promises.</note>. For who can tell if they truly knew such things, or if they were writing something entirely different from what they believed? *Thus, those minds which are most noble and eager for knowledge are held back for the longest time. Eventually, realizing the ignorance of these authors, and despairing of ever reaching their goals, they understand that they have "wasted their effort and their oil" <note>original: "operam, & oleum contriuisse"; a Latin proverb for wasting time and resources on a fruitless task, referring to the oil in a student's lamp.</note>. Driven by this desperation, they are consumed by a late and bitter regret.* Others, becoming wise through the peril of those who went before, learn to hate these studies before they even get to know them. Many also, while they tell of wonders, do not bring forth even one word from which any light might occur, or from which the ingenious might investigate; but just as they have (I believe) received it from others, so they presented it—cautious lest they betray their own ignorance by a single uncovered word. 

If you thought I would follow such a path, I surely could have filled many volumes, and their number would increase without end. Far be it from me; instead, I have given you what I have gathered from our natural sciences, however small they may be—not only as useful results but as a means <note>original: "ansam," literally a "handle" or opportunity.</note> for conceiving and accomplishing even greater things. *For the number of uncomprehended things is infinite; it stretches out immensely and is greater than can be considered by everyone.*

<margin>—</margin>Having cast aside all ambition and ambiguity, and without any pretense or deception, I am revealing those things that have long remained silent, whether due to the envy of the one who knew them or the ignorance of the one trying to learn. I have torn away the veil that covered them, so that what has until now lay hidden in the womb of wondrous nature and the secret cabinets <note>original: "promptuariis," storage places or repositories.</note> of the most renowned men may come to light. I will show how each thing may be explored. Here you will hear no boasting and no convoluted wordplay. I have not relied on the authority of others, nor did it seem honorable to me to "err with the best" <note>A reference to the Latin sentiment "errare cum Platone," suggesting it is better to be wrong with a great thinker than right with a commoner; the author rejects this elitism in favor of truth.</note> by following famous guides. Instead, I have spoken as simply and clearly as possible. I have named simple things without the circumlocutions and complex distinctions that usually pour out so much darkness. 

Let everyone read these words, though I know well that I will not escape slander but will instead offend the ears of the more learned. For as Plato said to Dionysius: *“They seem to make philosophy look ridiculous who attempt to pass these things on to the unrefined and the common people.”* <note>Referencing Plato's Second Letter, which warns against the vulgarization of deep philosophical truths.</note> But let such ambition be rejected and this envy be driven out; these are not the marks of noble minds. Let the desire to help future generations prevail, for I know these things will be of use to them and will bring no small fruit to their studies. Cicero, following Plato, tells us that we are not born for ourselves alone; our country, our parents, and our friends all claim a part of our origin. I do not deny, however, that I have kept some things silent, <margin>Motiaderas <note>This is likely a cryptic anagram, common in the works of Michael Sendivogius.</note></margin> transposed others, and hidden them within twists of phrasing. Yet, I have not done so in such a way that a clever person would be unable to uncover them.

<vocab>Preface, Plato, Cicero, Dionysius, Philosophy, Nature, Sendivogius</vocab>

<summary>The author critiques writers who use obscure language to hide their lack of knowledge or to mislead students, promising to instead provide clear "handles" for scientific discovery. He acknowledges using some intentional concealment but asserts that his work is intended to help future generations rather than for personal glory.</summary>
<keywords>alchemical riddles, natural science, Sendivogius, intellectual honesty, Plato, Cicero, experimental method, obscurity in writing</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the author’s justification for his writing style. On the previous page, he explained that he avoids the intentional obscurity and "riddles" used by other authors who often hide their own ignorance behind metaphors. He now explains that while he aims for clarity for the worthy, he still maintains a level of protection to prevent dangerous or profound knowledge from being misused by the uninitiated.</meta>

# PREFACE.

*...and perceive: nor should you think it out of place that I have done this, lest the profane crowd, not yet initiated into the sacred mysteries of philosophy, might be able to attain these things, or lest, having fallen into their hands, they become cheapened—especially regarding those things which are harmful or of greater weight.* <note>The author refers to the "profane crowd" (original: "prophana turba"), a common term in Renaissance esotericism for those who lack the moral or intellectual preparation to handle powerful natural secrets safely.</note> *You, however, who hold this treasure in your hands: add to it, take away from it, and as it is easy for you, extract the true sense; and if experiments occur that are either common or well-known, let them be passed over without disgust; for we have not written for you, but for others, so that each may draw for himself the food he has sought.*

*Receive therefore, eager readers, these long labors, produced not without zeal, sleepless nights, expense, and many inconveniences, with the same spirit in which they are bestowed; and cast away all ambiguity of the intellect and soul, as well as envy, which are accustomed to dazzle the sharpness of the mind and hinder the truth. Weigh these matters with a right judgment while you test what we have written, for by discovering the truth, you will perhaps be more favorable to my studies. Although it does not escape me that many ignorant people will be present—those idle from every serious matter—who will shudder at these things and envy them; they will rashly affirm that these things are not only false, but cannot even be done. While they strive to extract the truth through arguments and infinite disputes, they "act so as to understand nothing by their understanding,"* <note>original: "faciunt ne intelligendo, vt nihil intelligant"; a quote from the Roman playwright Terence, suggesting that critics often over-analyze a subject until they lose the basic truth of it.</note> *and their ignorance is betrayed. Such people, like the profane, ought to be kept away from the thresholds of our Magic.* <term>Magic: in this context, "Magiæ" refers to Natural Magic—the study of the hidden powers of plants, minerals, and animals—rather than sorcery.</term> *For those who do not give credit to the miracles of nature are, in a certain way, attempting to abolish philosophy itself.*

->*But if we have omitted anything, and*<-
->*spoken unsuitably, I know nothing is so*<-
->*well-adorned that it cannot be polished, nor*<-
->*so full that it cannot*<-
->*receive*<-
->*increase.*<-

---

<image-desc>A decorative typographical woodcut ornament featuring a symmetrical arrangement of scrolling leaves and floral flourishes around a central spiral motif.</image-desc>

<summary>In this concluding section of the preface, the author defends his work against critics and explains that his "Magic" is the study of nature's wonders, meant for the serious student rather than the idle or the "profane." He humbly acknowledges that his work is not perfect and invites further refinement by the reader.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, philosophy, initiation, experimental science, criticism, nature's miracles, intellectual humility</keywords>

# THE WORKS OF JOHN BAPTIST
## PORTA OF NAPLES
### ON NATURAL MAGIC
### BOOK THE FIRST.

#### What Natural Magic is.
#### CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'M' depicting a seated figure, likely a scholar or saint, at a desk within an ornate frame of scrolling foliage and architectural elements.</image-desc>

*Porphyry and Apuleius, notable figures among the Platonists, assert that Magic derived its name and origin from Persia; although Suidas thinks it was drawn from the Maguseans. They call those "Magi" whom the* Latins call Sages, *the* Greeks call Philosophers *after the name of* Pythagoras, *the* Indians call Gymnosophists <note>literally "naked philosophers," a group of ancient Indian ascetics known for their wisdom and simple living</note> in Greek, *the* Egyptians call Priests, *the* Cabalists call Prophets, *the* Babylonians *as well as the* Assyrians call Chaldeans, *and the* Celts of Gaul call Druids and Bards, *who were also called* Semnothei <note>original: "Semnothei," a term used by ancient writers to describe the venerable status of Celtic or Ethiopian philosophers</note>: *and in the end, Magic overflows with diverse names among diverse nations. We find that many men most skilled in the nature of things have shone in this art, such as* Zoroaster the son of Oromasius *among the* Persians, Numa Pompilius *among the* Romans, Thespion *among the* Gymnosophists, Hermes *among the* Egyptians, Buddha *among the* Babylonians, <margin>sorcerers <note>original: "γόητας" (goētas)</note></margin> Zamolxis *among the* Thracians, <margin>among the Assyrians</margin> *and* Abaris *among the* Hyperboreans. *They divide Magic into two parts: one is infamous and composed of the incantations of unclean spirits, fashioned from a wicked curiosity, which the Greeks of more hidden literature call* sorcery <note>original: "γοητείαν" (goēteian)</note> *or* Theurgy <note>the practice of rituals intended to invoke the presence of gods or spirits</note>, *to which all are opposed, as it offers up illusions and phantoms of which no trace soon remains. For—*

<summary>This opening page introduces the history and definitions of "Magic," distinguishing the honorable "Natural Magic" of ancient sages from the forbidden "Theurgy" or sorcery. It lists various titles for wise men across different global cultures who studied the secrets of nature.</summary>
<keywords>Natural Magic, Giambattista della Porta, Zoroaster, Hermes Trismegistus, Theurgy, Gymnosophists, Porphyry, Apuleius, ancient philosophy, history of science</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the introduction to Natural Magic. This page picks up with the description of Natural Magic as the pinnacle of philosophy and moves into Chapter II regarding the education of the practitioner.</meta>

->OF NATURAL MAGIC<-

<margin>magic <note>original: "μαγεία"</note></margin>
...the other [natural] kind, which everyone venerates and honors as something unsurpassed; nothing is more worthy of applause for students of good literature. Nor do they consider it to be anything other than the fulfillment of natural philosophy and the highest form of knowledge. Possessing great power, it overflows with hidden mysteries and provides the contemplation of things lying concealed, along with their qualities, properties, and the knowledge of all nature, standing as the very peak and summit of all philosophy. It teaches how to perform works through the assistance of things and their mutual and timely application—works which the common people regard as miracles, exceeding all wonder and human understanding. For this reason, it flourished most especially in 
<margin>India, Ethiopia</margin>
India and Ethiopia, where there was an abundant supply of animals, herbs, stones, and other materials pertaining to it. Hence, you who approach to see these things should believe the works of Natural Magic to be nothing other than the works of nature; the art is merely its servant and diligently follows nature’s lead. Whenever it recognizes something lacking in a natural kinship, it restores it at the appropriate times through vapors, numbers, and qualities. Just as in agriculture Nature herself brings forth herbs, seedlings, and crops, while art prepares them. Thus, Plotinus <note>Plotinus (c. 204–270 AD) was a major Hellenistic philosopher; he viewed the world as a single living organism where all parts are interconnected</note> rightly calls the Magician the servant of nature, not its creator. What sort of duty this servant of learning performs, we intend to demonstrate in the following section.

## On the Education of a Magician, and what kind of person 
## a professor of natural magic ought to be.

### CHAPTER II.

Now it is right to make known those things which a Magician must remember and know throughout this entire work, so that, being fully prepared in every way, he may begin to attain the hidden and wonderful effects of Nature. Already [he must understand] the active and absolute portion of natural philosophy...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the end of the previous page, which concluded with a reference to the philosopher Plotinus. It transitions from defining Natural Magic as the summit of philosophy to detailing the specific fields of study a practitioner must master.</meta>

### BOOK ONE.

*...as we have described. Indeed, I would first desire that one who ought to possess such great majesty be precise and perfected in **Philosophy**, and most eloquent in the matters of **Nature**. For such a person explores the causes, principles, and elements of things; they bring forth their marvelous gifts and the mutual connection and combination of the elements—from which the origin and destruction of all mixed things arise. They must understand meteorology <note>original: "meteoriam"; the study of atmospheric phenomena like clouds, wind, and lightning</note>, the tides of the sea, and the hidden tremors that shake the earth. They must know the natures, locations, and names of living creatures—namely quadrupeds, those that wander the air, those of the water, and all things enjoying life—as well as the nature of metals. In these matters, it is necessary to be very well-practiced, as will become clear to the reader. For we have labored long and intensely over certain works due to the diversity, similarity, and ambiguity of names. Indeed, it would be no more appropriate for a craftsman to be ignorant of the tools with which he works than for a magician to be ignorant of these things.*

*He should also be not ignorant of **Medicine**, for this is very much akin and most similar to this art. They say that medicine crept in under the guise of magic and thus enticed the minds of men. It also provides many defenses: for it teaches mixtures, temperaments <note>original: "temperies"; the balance of the four humors—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which was thought to determine health</note>, and how to compose and apply them in a similar way to achieve desired results. From this flows the knowledge of plants, so that foreign and native herbs may be diligently inspected—a very necessary thing, as everything depends upon this. It is also necessary to know the **Mathematical** disciplines. For through stellar heat and the wandering paths and conditions of the heavens, which **Astrology** <note>In the Renaissance, "Astrologia" often encompassed both astronomy (the study of stars) and astrology (their influence on Earth)</note> teaches, there are many things that extend the power of acting and being acted upon, and from which the hidden properties and virtues of things flow. Magic also contains the faculty of **Optics**, pertaining to the eyes: how it repeatedly deceives the sight, creating visions in water, at a distance, and in mirrors—whether they be gathered into a sphere, concave, extended, or formed in various other ways...*

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the prerequisite knowledge for a practitioner of Natural Magic, specifically building upon the previous page's mention of Medicine and moving into the relationship between the Magician's natural talent and the importance of understanding causes.</meta>

->OF NATURAL MAGIC<-

...from which a great part of Natural Magic depends. Having weighed everything, Magic takes these as its attendants and helpful arts; whoever is ignorant of these must be utterly excluded from here. Nor should anyone be thought a Magician <term>Magus: a wise practitioner of natural science and philosophy, distinct from a common sorcerer</term> unless he is distinguished by these disciplines. Let the Magician be a craftsman by nature’s gift and highly knowledgeable; for the knowledgeable man without craftsmanship, or the ignorant craftsman—should he lack certain natural qualities—are so joined that he will waste his effort in vain and not achieve his desired goal.

There are some, however, so naturally skilled and knowledgeable in these matters by the gifts of Nature that they seem to have been formed by God Himself. I do not say this as if art cannot refine anything, or that what is good cannot be made better, or that what is not at its best cannot somehow be sharpened and corrected. Weighing with lynx-like eyes <note>original: "Lynceis oculis"; a reference to Lynceus, the Argonaut famous for his superhumanly sharp sight</note> what reveals itself to him, so that having inspected the matter he may work diligently—I wanted to say this so that if an unlearned man is deceived, he does not turn the blame on us, but blames his own ignorance. This is a weakness not of the teacher, but of the practitioner.

For if these things are handled by someone less ingenious, the credibility of the science is diminished; it leads to things that are most true and occur through necessary causes appearing to be mere accidents. Thus, if you apply the proper active agents to passive subjects <note>The concept that "active" forces (like heat or spirit) acting upon "passive" matter (like water or earth) produces all natural effects</note>, you will produce wonders. And if you seek even more wonderful things—those which can scarcely be obtained—remove the knowledge of their underlying cause. For he who knows the causes diminishes the authority of the wonder too much. Indeed, that is a wonder 

<margin>Arrusio Curnus: <note>Likely a reference to an ancient author or a specific cited case</note></margin> 

whose cause is hidden from the spectator; it retains its rarity and strangeness only so long as the causes remain concealed. Someone once extinguished a lamp and then, by bringing it near a wall or a stone, lit it again as if it were a marvelous thing. But then (says Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon, the famous Greek physician and philosopher</note>) that wonder ceased to seem so when they discovered it had been in contact with sulfur <note>original: "sulphure"; sulfur was known to ignite easily, explaining the "miracle"</note>. And the Ephesian <note>Likely referring to a philosopher from Ephesus, such as Heraclitus</note> says: "A miracle is dissolved by the very thing from which it seems to be a miracle."

# BOOK ONE.

->OF NATURAL MAGIC<-

...the proper active agents to passive subjects. Let the practitioner also be wealthy: for we work with difficulty if resources are not available; it is necessary to be enriched so that we may philosophize, not to philosophize so that we may be enriched. Let him not spare expenses, but be lavish in his searching; and while he searches more attentively and thoroughly, let him not be reluctant to repeat the process with patience, nor spare labor: for the secrets of Nature are not opened to the idle and lazy. Whence **Epicharmus** <note>A Greek philosopher and dramatist known for his pithy observations on human nature</note> used to say: <margin>The gods sell us all good things for the price of labor. <note>original Greek: τῶν πόνων πωλοῦσιν ἁμιν πάντα τἀγαθὰ θεοί</note></margin> **The gods sell everything to mortals for the price of labor.** And if the result does not respond to the description, know that something was lacking; for we have written briefly not for the unrefined and beginners, but for the ingenious and the skilled.

## The opinions of the ancients regarding the 
## causes of wonderful operations. CHAPTER III.

Those effects of Nature which we often witness so inflamed the minds of the ancient philosophers in the discernment of causes that they labored over it not a little, and even wandered in error; different men expressed different opinions, which we have decided to report before we proceed further. At the beginning, to trace the opinions from the most remote times, all the **Egyptians** <margin>Egyptians</margin>, who it is clear were the first to dare to scrutinize and measure the effects of the heavens—after they lived in the level expanses of open fields with the benefit of perpetual clear weather, and since nothing rose from the earth that could obstruct the contemplation of the sky—discovering the stars to be eternally bright and clear, they placed all their care in the knowledge of the stars’ influences. And since the hunt <margin>astrologers</margin> for causes greatly terrified the idle, they ascribed everything to the sky and the stars, so that they believed each **fate** <term>fatum: here meaning the predetermined course of a person's life</term> and the influence of the heaven was induced at the hour of birth and death, by the moving back and forth of the stars...

<sig>A 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: ...nibus (referring to "reciprocationibus," meaning the cycles or movements of the stars)</meta>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the opinions of ancient philosophers concerning the causes of natural phenomena, specifically moving from the astronomical observations of the Egyptians to the elemental theories of the Greeks and the substantial forms of later philosophers.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

->OF NATURAL MAGIC<-

...by the motions <note>This likely completes the word "operationibus" (operations) or "motionibus" (motions) from the previous page</note> of the stars, they produced wonderful effects. Whence they began to prepare and collect all things at certain hours, fixed times, and according to the aspects of the heavens; and having progressed no further, they defend their <margin>Sextus <note>Likely referring to Sextus Empiricus, an ancient philosopher known for chronicling various schools of thought</note></margin> opinion. Then other philosophers established that all things proceed from the elements, and set these as the principles and causes: As **Hippasus of Metapontum** and **Heraclides of Pontus** chose fire; **Diogenes Apolloniates** and **Anaximenes** chose air; **Thales of Miletus** chose water; **Hesiod** chose earth; while **Hippon** and **Critias** chose the vapors of these. Others ascribed the causes to <margin>Qualities <note>original Greek: ποιότητες</note></margin> qualities: as **Parmenides** did to the cold and the hot; and a great part of the physicians did so through the cold, the moist, the hot, and the dry: when these are brought together, they established the roots of these wonders from the victory of one quality over the others; and whatever experiments they brought into the public view, they believe can be composed by these and their causes found. **Empedocles of Agrigento** added to the elements—as if they were not sufficient—Concord and Strife <note>original: concordia & litem</note>, so that things are born from the one and perish from the <margin>Strife. Love. Matter. God. <note>original Greek: ἔρις, φιλία, ὕλη, θεός</note></margin> other. **Zeno of Citium** <note>The founder of Stoicism</note> added God to matter; of which he made one the principle of acting and the other the principle of suffering <note>In the sense of "being acted upon" (passive)</note>. But the age of more recent philosophers, having inspected the matter, judged that this could not be maintained, for things often operate contrary to their qualities; and they conjectured that there is something else beyond the elements and qualities. For **Plato** and **Aristotle**, who reached the summit of philosophy and set its limits, searched more deeply and found many things outside the qualities of the elements, such as **Virtues** <term>Virtues: here meaning the inherent powers or "souls" of objects</term> born together with their **substantial forms**: one being through itself, another by accident, and many things which are revealed in the following pages.

### Whence the manifest and hidden powers of things arise.
### CHAPTER IV.

<summary>This page catalogs the various theories of ancient Greek and Roman philosophers regarding the fundamental causes of natural wonders, ranging from elemental theories to the more complex concepts of substantial forms introduced by Plato and Aristotle.</summary>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's critique of ancient philosophical schools, specifically shifting from the specific elemental theories of individuals like Thales or Empedocles to the systematic refutation of these ideas by Aristotle. It serves as a transition into the author's own framework of matter, form, and qualities.</meta>

# BOOK ONE.

Already each of the ancients has clashed over the manifest and hidden **Virtues** <term>Virtues: the inherent natural powers or properties of a substance</term> of things: yet I have judged that there is no need to refute them, since they have been sufficiently disproved by the common teacher of all and the supreme Leader of the **Peripatetics** <term>Peripatetics: the followers of Aristotle, who famously taught while walking about the Lyceum in Athens</term>. Now, however, so that all things may be revealed more clearly, it is right to remember from what sources we obtain these **Powers**, which will be of no small use in discovering new things and composing them, so that students may learn to separate and distinguish them, lest they disturb the entire order of Truth. Since many and very diverse effects may flow from the same mixture, it is admitted by all that they arise from a single principle, many examples of which may be read in the following discourse. And now, as we are about to speak openly of where they originate, we must begin a little deeper.

For the composition of any natural **substance** (for I call "substance" that which is compacted of both), **matter** and **form** result as the principles; nor do we cast out the functions of the **qualities** <note>original: qualitatum functiones</note> which lay hidden within the elements from the beginning, and these together fulfill a threefold number. When the elements themselves enter into the shaping of something, the thing formed retains certain prevailing qualities; although all these qualities concur in the production of effects, it is believed that the whole proceeds from the superior ones, since 
<margin>
it is <unclear>argued</unclear>
</margin>
they ascribe the powers of the rest to themselves: for if they all fought equally, their **Virtue** would be invisible. Nor is matter in some way widowed of its powers: I do not speak of that primary and simple matter, but that which is born from the power and substances of the elements, namely from the two most passive ones, **earth** and **water**. And these Aristotle is sometimes accustomed to call secondary qualities and corporeal effects: we call them the functions of matter,

<meta>catchword: whether (siue)</meta>

<summary>This page critiques ancient philosophical disputes and establishes the Aristotelian framework of matter, form, and elemental qualities as the basis for natural properties. It emphasizes that while many qualities interact within a substance, the dominant ones determine its observable "virtues" or powers.</summary>

--- Page 29 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the principles of natural substances. The previous page ended mid-sentence, discussing how all qualities concur in the production of effects; this page begins by defining these as "Powers" or "Virtues" and distinguishing between the roles of matter, temperament (mixture), and Form.</meta>

# NATURAL MAGIC

...whether we call them **Powers**, or by some other name they enjoy; such as rare, dense, rough, smooth, hard, or easy to split—though these are situated deep in the heart of the material, they all nevertheless come from the elements. Therefore, I have judged it more correct that they not be confused: that the effects of **qualities** flow from the **temperament** <term>Temperament: the specific balance or "mixture" of the four primary qualities (hot, cold, moist, and dry) within a body</term>, while those of **matter** flow from its consistency. 

But such great power resides in **Form** <term>Form: in Aristotelian philosophy, the "essential nature" or organizing principle that makes a thing what it is and gives it its specific properties</term> that everyone knows all the effects we observe are first produced by it. It has a divine origin, as it is superior and most excellent in itself; without the help of anything else, it uses these other things as instruments to carry out its action more quickly and conveniently. 

Anyone whose mind is not at all devoted or accustomed to these contemplations might think everything can be achieved by temperament and matter alone, though things are actually done through them as if through instruments. <margin>3 efficient causes</margin> For if a craftsman uses a stylus or a chisel in the construction of some image, he does not use it as the primary agent, but by supplying it so that he may work more skillfully. 

Therefore, since there are three **efficient causes** <term>Efficient Causes: the agents or forces that bring a thing into being or produce an effect</term> in every single thing, you should not think they are idle or at rest. Instead, they are all "bringing forth fruit"—one more mildly, the other more strongly. But most of all, Form itself achieves this, strengthening the rest; and if Form were to fail, it would render the others void and they would be frustrated, since they are not sufficient on their own to receive celestial gifts. 

And although Form alone cannot express these gifts without the others bringing forth their own powers, nevertheless they are not confused, nor do they become different things; instead, they are so bound together that they require each other's work. He who knows how to perceive this correctly through the investigation of reason will find no obscurity and will not confuse the knowledge of truth. 

Hence that power, which is called the **property** <term>Property: a specific power or "virtue" unique to a particular substance, often considered "occult" or hidden because it cannot be explained by the simple mixture of elements</term> of a thing, does not come from the temperament, but from the Form itself. And as it is the most excellent of all, it happens to be compared to its place of origin: from the supreme vertex <note>The "supreme vertex" refers to the highest point of the heavens or the divine realm</note>, most closely,

-><insert>
**Like** <note>original Greek: ὅμοιος (homoios)</note>
**Good** <note>original Greek: ἀγαθὸς (agathos)</note>
**God** <note>original Greek: θεός (theos)</note>
</insert><-

<summary>This page argues that the unique "properties" of objects stem from their "Form" rather than just their physical mixture. The author uses the analogy of a craftsman and his tools to explain how Form acts as the primary divine agent using matter and temperament as instruments.</summary>

<keywords>Natural Magic, Form vs Matter, Temperament, Efficient Causes, Occult Properties, Aristotelian Philosophy, Divine Origin</keywords>

<page-num>5</page-num>
->### BOOK ONE.<-
<meta>catchword: What</meta>

...thing, it flows to this from those **Intelligences** <term>Intelligences: in medieval cosmology, these are non-material, angelic beings that guide the movement of the celestial spheres</term>, and finally from God himself; and thus the origin of **Form** and its properties is the same. For God—as **Plato** <note>Plato (c. 427–348 BC), the Greek philosopher whose "Timaeus" describes the creation of the world by a divine craftsman.</note> believes—when he had first established the heavens, the stars, and the very first beginnings of things (which wither away in the constant cycle of birth and death) by the omnipotence of his divinity and with fitting harmony, then he assigned the kinds of living creatures, plants, and inanimate objects through various grades. He did this by summoning the forces of the heavens and the elements, so that these earthly things would not share the same eternal condition as the heavens. He decreed by a law of fate that lower things should serve those above, and he infused into each thing its own **Form**, heaped with power from the celestial paths. Moreover, so that the continuous procreation of things might not cease, he commanded each to bring forth seed and to pass on its Form to those things prepared for it.

<margin>The Soul is the giver to the world; Nature is the giver of forms. <note>original Greek: ψυχὴ κόσµω διαδότης Φύσις µορφωδότης</note></margin>

Thus, you will necessarily declare that the Forms descending from the heavens themselves are divine and celestial. In these heavens resides the archetype and most noble cause of all forms—which the same Plato, the Prince of Philosophers, calls the **Soul of the World** <term>Soul of the World (Anima Mundi): the internal spirit that animates the entire universe as if it were a single living organism</term>; the supreme **Aristotle** likewise calls it **Universal Nature**; and **Avicenna** calls it the **Giver of Forms** <note>Avicenna (Ibn Sina, c. 980–1037) was a Persian polymath; his "Dator Formarum" refers to the Active Intellect that bestows forms upon matter.</note>. This Giver, acting not upon a perishable thing but drawing from himself and instilling, first imparts these Forms to the intelligences and the stars. Then, he shapes the elements—using them like instruments that prepare the matter—by means of their celestial aspects. 

Who, then, is so insane, or so poorly shaped by Nature herself, that if these powers come from the elements, the heavens, the intelligences, and finally from God himself, he would deny they are "celestial"? As if they did not taste of that higher nature, or did not somehow smell of divine majesty? And when there is such an affinity with the Divine, who could deny that they produce works so marvelous that nothing greater could be imagined or thought of? We omit many arguments that might weary the reader, since we are going to explain these powers more fully and individually as we proceed.

<summary>This page argues that the "Form" of all things originates from God and descends through celestial intelligences and the stars. It synthesizes the views of Plato, Aristotle, and Avicenna to explain how divine power is infused into the natural world.</summary>
<keywords>Form, Plato, Aristotle, Avicenna, Soul of the World, Giver of Forms, Intelligences, celestial influence, cosmology, divine majesty</keywords>

## What the rings of Plato and the chain of Homer signify.

### CHAPTER 5.

<margin>
God
Mind
Soul
<unclear>living creature</unclear>
plant
<note>original Greek: Θεὸς, Νοῦς, Ψυχη, ζῶον, φυτὸν</note>
</margin>
Therefore, this combination of things, this series and order serving divine providence, ensures that all these lower things being governed proceed in order from God himself at the very start and receive their power of operation. For God—as **Macrobius** <note>Macrobius (c. 385–430 AD) was a Roman provincial and Neoplatonist whose commentary on the "Dream of Scipio" was a primary source for medieval cosmology.</note> says—who is the first cause, the prince and origin of all things, created the **Mind** <note>original Latin: *mens*</note> out of the fruitfulness of his majesty. Through this Mind, he created the **Soul** <note>original Latin: *anima*</note>, which imparts reason—partly to divine things like the heavens and the eternal fires (for this reason they are said to be animated by divine minds) and partly bestows the power of sensing and growing upon perishable things. **Maro** <note>Maro refers to the poet Virgil (Publius Vergilius Maro), specifically his description of the "World Soul" in Book 6 of the Aeneid.</note>, sensing this soul of the world, calls this the "Mind":

> The spirit nourishes from within, and Mind, infused through the limbs,
> moves the entire mass and mingles with the great body.

Man, therefore, since he stands in the middle of both—receding from the heavens in nobility yet sharing in reason (by which he deserves to stand above other animals) while still retaining the power of sensing—serves as a bridge. The rest of the animals, as if degenerating from him, retain only two remaining powers: sensing and growing. Trees, however, because they lack sense and reason, and since they only require the use of growing, are said to possess this alone: to grow and to live in this single capacity. The Poet expresses this again shortly after:

> From this comes the race of men and of cattle, the life of winged things,
> and the monsters that the sea bears beneath its marble surface, etc.

Since, therefore, the Mind proceeds from God himself, and the Soul from the Mind, and the Soul itself animates all things that follow, it creates a connection: the brute animal matches the plant in its growth, and the man matches the brute in sense, while man stands above the rest through intellect. Thus, from the pri-

<summary>This chapter explains the Neoplatonic "Chain of Being," illustrating how life and intellect flow from God through the Mind and Soul down to animals and plants. It uses Virgil's poetry and Macrobius's philosophy to describe the interconnectedness of all levels of existence.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the philosophical argument regarding the "Great Chain of Being" and the interconnectedness of the cosmos before transitioning into the specific study of the four elements. <page-num>6</page-num>

<header>BOOK ONE.</header>

[the first] cause, like a small cord <note>original: *funiculus*</note> stretching from heaven all the way to the lowest things, through a certain mutual binding and continuity; so that the higher **Power** <note>original: *Virtus*; in this context, it refers to a divine or celestial influence rather than moral virtue.</note>, by scattering its rays, reaches even to these things. Thus, if one end is touched, the rest trembles and moves. We might rightly call this bond "rings" or a "chain," which seems to agree with the **rings of Plato** and the **golden chain of Homer**. Homer, who is the source and origin of all divine inventions, allowed this to be understood by the wise under the cloud of a poetic fiction:

> Come now, gods, and make the trial, that you all may know:
> Hang a golden chain from heaven,
> and all you gods and all goddesses, lay hold of it.
> Yet you could not drag Zeus, the highest counselor,
> from heaven to the ground.
> <note>original Greek: Εἰδ' ἄγε πειρήσασθε θεοὶ... This is a famous passage from the *Iliad* (Book 8, lines 18-22) used by Neoplatonists to symbolize the unbreakable link between the divine and the material worlds.</note>

Just as God, the craftsman of all things, first ordained by his providence that all these lower things are governed by the higher through a certain necessary law of nature, so the **Magus** <note>The "Magus" here is a "Natural Magician"—a precursor to the scientist who studies the hidden laws of nature to perform wonders.</note>, knowing these things, acts like a farmer. Just as the farmer marries elms to vines, the Magus marries the earth to heaven—or to speak more clearly, he marries these lower things to the gifts and miraculous powers of the higher things. As a servant of nature, he brings into the public view those secrets lying deeply hidden in the lap of nature. Through constant exploration, he discovers the truth of these things, so that everyone, burning with love for the craftsman of all things, may strive to praise and worship his omnipotence.

### Concerning the elements and their powers.

#### CHAPTER 6.

So far we have spoken of the origin of <term>substantial form</term> <note>Substantial form: a philosophical term meaning the essential nature or "blueprint" that makes a thing what it is.</note> and the series of things. Now, however, we must attempt to teach those things which happen through **hidden properties**, those which disagree in strife, and those which are bound by the bond of friendship, and how these are to be tested through similarity and other means. But lest we disturb the order of things, we shall begin with the elements of things, which [at first]...

<page-num>1</page-num>

<header>NATURAL MAGIC</header>

[pri]mordial seeds of things Nature established, we shall gradually come to the others, which will seem necessary to be recognized and known in our work. The seeds of all these things are the **elements** <note>original: *elementa*; the basic building blocks of the universe in Aristotelian physics: fire, air, water, and earth.</note>, simple bodies (though now illegitimate, false, and adulterated; for being mixed with others, they are transformed more or less). They are the material principle of the natural body, agitated by a perpetual succession of changes and turnings, and are so massed together within the vast arches of the heaven that they fill this entire sublunary world.

For **Fire**, being the lightest and purest, so that it escapes our sight, has raised itself on high and claimed for itself the superior place, which they call the **ether** <note>original: *æthera*; the "fifth element" or the substance that fills the space of the universe above the terrestrial sphere.</note>. The element closest to this is the spirit, which they call **Air**, a little heavier than fire, poured around in an immense breadth; being passable through all things, it renders us to its own quality, now thickening into clouds, now compressed into mists, and then dissolved. **Water** follows these; then the last, scraped and gathered from the refined elements, which is called **Earth**, and it is spread beneath all things—vast, impenetrable, and most solid, so that nothing solid can be touched that is devoid of earth, and no vacuum exists without fire.

The Earth itself, balanced in the space of the middle, is walled in by all things, residing alone and motionless; for the others are carried around in a rotating whirl by a circular motion. Yet each neighboring part embraces the other as if with certain arms, even though they disagree in contrary qualities. But wise Nature has designed this **machine** <note>original: *machinam*; a common Renaissance metaphor for the cosmos as a structured, functional device designed by God.</note> with a fixed measure and wonderful fitness. Since there were two qualities in each element, in some there is a joinable fellowship, but in others a discrepancy; she gave to each one of the two qualities such a nature that the one to which it adheres is kindred and similar to it in qua-

<page-num>7</page-num>
<header>BOOK ONE</header>

...lity <note>This page begins mid-word, concluding the word *qualitatem* (quality) from the previous page.</note>: as with air and fire, for the one is hot and dry, while the other is hot and moist. Indeed, dry and moist are opposites, yet they are joined through the reconciliation of their shared heat. So too, earth is cold and dry, while water is cold and moist; and even if they are adversaries by way of the dry and the moist, they are nonetheless connected by the fellowship of coldness; otherwise, their harmony would be achieved with greater difficulty. 

Thus, fire is gradually turned into air through heat; air into water through moisture; water into earth through cold; and earth is joined to fire through dryness. In this way, they proceed most providently. From there, they are transmuted once more in a reverse order and are made from one another. These transitions are easy when they have obtained a common quality, such as fire and air through heat; however, those which are opposed in both qualities—such as fire and water <note>Fire is hot and dry, while water is cold and moist; having no shared qualities, they do not transform into each other as easily as fire and air (which share heat).</note>—are transformed more slowly and with greater difficulty. Therefore, let these be established as the foundations of all mixtures, from which many operations proceed.

## On the qualities of the elements, and their operations.
### CHAPTER VII.

<margin>primary qualities</margin>
In the four bodies already mentioned, there are four elementary qualities that flow between one another, from which all things that experience birth and decay are born and destroyed: namely, heat, cold, moisture, and dryness. They are called "primary" <note>original: *principes*; literally "first" or "chief," referring to the fundamental building blocks of Aristotelian physics.</note> because they emerge first from the elements, and from them secondary effects emanate. Two of these are active—heat and cold—which are born more for acting than for being acted upon. The others, moisture and dryness, are passive—not because they arise entirely [from nothing], but because they are preserved and induced...

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of elemental qualities, moving from the primary qualities (heat, cold, moisture, dryness) discussed on the previous page to "secondary" and "tertiary" qualities used in medicine and alchemy. It then begins Chapter VIII, shifting the focus from elemental physics to the "Form" or soul of a thing, which governs its hidden or "occult" powers.</meta>

<header>OF NATURAL MAGIC</header>

<margin>
Secondary
Heat
Cold
Dryness
Moisture
3rd qualities
</margin>
...be derived. These are called secondary, as if serving the primary ones; they are said to operate in a second degree, such as to soften, to ripen, to dissolve, or to make thinner. For instance, when heat acts upon a mixture, it draws out the impure parts; while it attempts to make the substance suitable for its action—so that it may become simpler—the substance ends up becoming thin. In this same way, cold preserves, constricts, and congeals. Dryness thickens and creates roughness; for while it consumes the moisture on a surface that it cannot fully exhaust, it hardens it. From this, a coarseness of the surface is induced, for as the empty spaces are pressed down and the hardness is raised up, a roughness of the parts occurs and protrusions emerge. Likewise, moisture increases, corrupts, and frequently makes one thing by its own nature, but another by accident: as through ripening, constriction, or expulsion, it calls forth milk, urine, the menses, or sweat. These are called **third qualities** by Physicians, serving the secondary qualities just as those serve the primary ones. Sometimes they operate in specific limbs, such as to strengthen the head or to assist the kidneys; these, some also call **fourth qualities**. From these principles many experiments arise, as is seen throughout this work; however, to complete our history of them, it was not out of place to provide this reminder, lest anything be found wanting, so that the trained practitioner may rightly know these forces and the path of operation.

### Various hidden properties of things, which come from the Form itself. CHAPTER VIII.

There are many <term>idiosyncrasies <note>original: ἰδιοσυγκρισίαι (idiosynkrisiai) — the unique, specific temperament or internal constitution of a substance that gives it its character.</note></term> of things, which is to say, hidden properties and powers. These do not arise from the qualities of the elements, but from the **Form** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "Form" is the essential nature or "soul" of an object that makes it what it is, as opposed to the "Matter" it is made of.</note> itself, as we have said. Since these powers come from the Form, a tiny amount of matter demonstrates a massive effect; this is the opposite of how matter usually behaves, for matter requires a larger volume to act more readily. These properties are "occult" or hidden because they cannot be known through certain demonstrations or logical proofs. Therefore...

# BOOK THE FIRST

Those ancient wise men decreed that a certain boundary must be set, beyond which reason could not progress, since there are many things in the inner chambers of Nature, hidden and full of energy, whose causes human guesswork can neither investigate nor comprehend. Indeed, they lie hidden by the obscurity and secluded majesty of Nature, who wished for these things to be admired rather than known. Whence **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle, known for his works on botany and metaphysics.</note> says: *He who seeks a reason for all things, destroys knowledge along with reason.* And **Alexander** <note>Likely Alexander of Aphrodisias, a famous commentator on Aristotle who discussed the limits of physical causes.</note> says there are many things for which no reason can be given, utterly exceeding the capacity of the human mind, known only to the immortal God, who is the parent and author of all things. Since these things surpass the nature and powers of the elements, they can by no means be constrained by logical demonstrations; thus, the ancient Philosophers chose to leave the things they discovered to be admired, rather than attempting to offer a reason for them. Nor did they only marvel that God created every animal and made them differ in various shapes and sizes, but also that He attributed to each one, according to the variety of its species, a certain property and peculiar trait by which they differ from others in their habits and operations.

We shall set forth many examples of these, which will perhaps be delightful and not unpleasant to the readers. For instance, when a fierce **Bull** is tied to a **fig tree**, it becomes mild and is tamed; and if its nostrils are smeared with oil of roses, it falls down in a spinning dizziness. As **Zoroaster** <note>The text refers to the *Geoponica*, a Byzantine collection of agricultural lore often attributed to ancient figures like Zoroaster.</note> says, who wrote on the *Geoponica* <term>Agricultural Matters</term> from the selected teachings of the ancients: a **Cock** also becomes tender if it is hung from that same tree. **Aristotle** records that **Vultures** and **Scarab beetles** are killed by the scent of roses. If one takes the beard of a **Goat**, which they call the <term>aruncum</term> <note>The tuft of hair under a goat's chin.</note>, grabbing and pulling it from the herd with his hands, the entire herd stands still; they abandon their pasture and all stand in a daze, nor do they cease to wonder until one who is skilled in the matter...

...as **Aristotle** <note>The famous Greek philosopher whose works on natural history were foundational for Renaissance science.</note> says, he left the matter undecided. Many, being deceived, have made claims regarding the **sea-holly** herb <note>original: *eringio*</note>; for the Greeks call the goat's-beard *kryngon* <note>original: *κρύγχον*</note>, and the similarity of the name was the cause of the error, since practical experience does not match the claim. 

The **Hyena**, if it catches sight of a man or a dog sleeping, stretches its own body out next to them; if its body proves longer than the sleeper, it drives the person mad, and to prevent any resistance, it gnaws at their hands. If it is found to be shorter in length, it takes to swift flight, as **Nestor** <note>Likely referring to a lost medical or pharmacological text often cited in antiquity.</note> records in his *Panacea*. Furthermore, if you encounter a raging hyena, beware of approaching it from the right side, for it induces a profound stupor that leaves you no ability to resist or help yourself. If you attack it from the left, however, the beast is seized by stupor and you may kill it. Its shadow makes dogs "tongueless" and strikes them mute; sensing this, the hyena runs when pursued so that it is positioned against the light of the stars <note>Likely referring to the sun or moon as "stars" (sideris) to cast a shadow toward the pursuer.</note>, and the shadow of the beast strikes the mouths of the pursuing dogs. 

A **Lion** suffering from fever is cured by devouring a monkey. **Goats**—both male and female—are poison to agriculture; they ruin certain fruit-bearing olives and vines, causing them to become sterile. Thus, it was not without justice that a he-goat was sacrificed to **Father Liber** <note>Another name for Bacchus/Dionysus, the Roman god of wine and agriculture.</note>, the discoverer of the vine, and a she-goat to **Minerva** <note>The Roman goddess of wisdom and olives.</note>, so that they might pay the penalty with their lives. 

An **Olive tree** plucked and planted by a virgin’s hand provides more abundant fruit, but it becomes barren if handled by a prostitute. A **Serpent** or **Viper** becomes numb if struck with a reed; however, if you strike it a second time, it recovers its strength and flees. **Apuleius** <note>A 2nd-century Roman writer and philosopher interested in magic and natural wonders.</note> says this: a serpent entering its burrow can be pulled out with little effort if you use your left hand, but it cannot be extracted with the right. A **Viper** is dazed if a beech twig is thrown upon it. 

**Ants**, to prevent stored piles of grain from sprouting, pull out the "marrow" <note>The germ or embryo of the seed, which prevents it from growing even in moist conditions.</note>. The **Ostrich** <note>original: *struthiocamelus*</note> digests iron by a more hidden power and converts it into nourishment. **Roosters** do not crow if a sprig of ferment <note>original: *fermentis*; likely referring to a specific plant or a bit of leavened dough.</note> is placed on their necks. Similarly, the **Starfish** [sea-star] is so...

### BOOK ONE

-> B <-

*...is endowed with the power of digestion, so that it devours and breaks down whole snails and starfish. The **echeneis** <note>original: ἐχενηΐς. A suckerfish, commonly known as the remora.</note>, called by the Romans the **Remora** and **Remiligo**, is a very small fish that, by clinging to the rudder, slows down sailing ships—even those driven by a favorable wind—as if with a powerful curb. Even if the winds rush and the storms rage, it suppresses all their strength and renders the ships immobile, just as if they were tied with anchors and firm chains. The **Torpedo** <note>The electric ray, famous in antiquity for its ability to deliver a numbing shock through solid objects.</note> has such great power of numbing that even when caught from a distance—by touching it with a spear, a hook, a line, or a reed—the limbs of the fisherman grow numb. It feeds on various fish it desires, even swift ones, by striking them with stupor; however, when applied to the head, it eases and suppresses pains. This frequent use is confirmed by the testimony of **Plato**, **Aristotle**, **Galen**, and **Aelian**. The **Sea-hare** <note>original: *Marinus lepus*. A type of sea slug (Aplysia) which was considered highly toxic and even magical in ancient times.</note> makes all who look upon it feel nauseous, so that they easily vomit, and it brings harm to pregnant women by causing miscarriage. Nothing in the sea is more execrable than the sting of the **Stingray** <note>original: *Pastinacæ radio*. Referring to the serrated tail spine of the ray.</note>; if you were to drive it into a green and vigorous tree, it would kill it immediately. It can knock out teeth, yet it also eases their pain. The **Laurel** and the **fig tree** are never touched by lightning from heaven, nor is the hide of the **Seal** <note>original: *Vituli marini*, literally "sea-calf."</note>, nor the skin of the **hyena**, nor the **white Vine**. Therefore, sailors protect their sails with these materials so that they do not burn and fall from a lightning strike. **Octavius Augustus** <note>The first Roman Emperor, who was famously terrified of lightning and reportedly wore seal skin for protection.</note> used to protect himself with this; **Tiberius Caesar** used the laurel, which he wore as a crown, so that he would not be blasted by thunder. For these things not only escape the force of the lightning, but they possess a contrary nature that turns back the strike of the lightning. **Tarchon** <note>A legendary Etruscan hero and seer credited with founding the art of divination and the protection of cities.</note> had enclosed his house with white vines. A body that is struck by lightning remains incorruptible; hence the ancients did not care to burn those very bodies "struck by the stars" <note>original: *siderata*. Ancient medicine and natural philosophy often linked lightning strikes to celestial or "star" influence.</note>, nor did they bury them in the earth, since they do not feel corruption, but remain immune to rot. Thus we consider the prophets worthy of rebuke: who...*
