# NATURAL MAGIC

...they sang that **Phaëton**, the driver of the celestial chariot, after being struck from the sky by a thunderbolt, rotted away in the valleys. The "royal disease" <note>Jaundice, historically called *morbus regius* because it was thought to require expensive, "royal" remedies.</note> is cured by the mere sight of the **stone-curlew** <note>original: *rupicis auiculae*. Ancient lore held that if a person with jaundice looked at this bird—likely a plover or curlew—the bird would draw the disease into itself and the person would be healed.</note>. So great is the power of **loosestrife** <note>original: *lysimachia*.</note> that when it is placed upon the yoke of quarreling draft animals, it restrains their wildness and makes them peaceful. The herb **bugloss** <note>original: *buglosson*.</note>, when thrown into wine, increases the soul's delight to such an extent that they call it **Euphrosian** <note>original: *Euphrosianum*. Derived from the Greek *euphrosyne*, meaning "gladness" or "mirth"; the plant was believed to drive away sadness.</note>.

**Basil** <note>original: *ocymum*.</note>, as **Theophrastus** <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the "father of botany."</note> recorded, grows more abundantly and more quickly if it is provoked with curses and insults while being sown. From this same source, I believe, arose the proverb commonly used among us: "Sow the basil," and perhaps **Persius** <note>A Roman satirist.</note> alluded to this in his short verse:

->*When the loosely-dressed slave has sung his basil.*<- 
<note>original: *Cum bene discincto cantauerit ocyma verna*. This line from Persius's Satires refers to a slave shouting insults, mirroring the belief that basil thrives on verbal abuse.</note>

It is likewise certain that **rue** <note>original: *ruta*.</note> benefits if one pursues it with injuries; indeed, the ancients believed it grows even better if it is stolen. The same happens to **parsley** <note>original: *apio*.</note>: it thrives the more it is trampled underfoot. The **Indian diamond** <note>original: *adamas Indicus*.</note> resists all hard materials, yet when it is soaked in goat's blood, it softens and can be broken. **Rhubarb** <note>original: *rhabarbarum*.</note> purges only bile from the bodily humors, **dodder of thyme** <note>original: *epithymum* (transcribed as *etiphymum*).</note> purges black bile, and **agaric** <note>original: *agaricum*. A type of medicinal fungus.</note> purges phlegm.

No less wonder is found in the remedies discovered by physicians through observing the self-healing practices of animals: just as the dog purges its bowels with certain herbs to induce vomiting, and the **Egyptian ibis** cures itself <note>The ibis was famously believed to perform self-enemas using its long beak and seawater.</note>. In Crete, when goats are struck by poisoned arrows and wounded, they seek the herb they call **dittany** <note>original: *dictamnum*.</note>; upon tasting it, they expel the arrows from their bodies. Sea birds heal their ulcerated beaks with **savory** <note>original: *cunila*.</note>. The tortoise, weakened after eating a snake, removes the venom by eating **oregano** <note>original: *origanum*.</note>; it also fortifies itself with this herb before going to fight a serpent. When bears have tasted the fruit of the **mandrake** <note>original: *mandragora*.</note>, they go to seek a remedy lest the poison turn fatal and they die; they do this by eating ants. And the stag, when it notices poisoned fodder...

<header>BOOK ONE.</header>
<sig>B 2</sig>
<meta>catchword: so (original: ita)</meta>

The tortoise <note>The Latin text begins with a word fragment, "bula," which is the end of *testudo* (tortoise). Ancient naturalists like Pliny the Elder wrote that tortoises eat snakes and then use artichokes as an antidote.</note> heals itself with the **artichoke** <note>original: *cinara*.</note> herb. The **elephant**, having grazed upon a **chameleon**—which matches the color of the leaves it sits upon—counters its venom by taking a **wild olive** branch <note>original: *oleastrum*.</note>. When **panthers** have devoured poisoned meat scattered by hunters, they find **human dung** to heal themselves so they are not destroyed. **Wood pigeons**, **jackdaws**, and **blackbirds** use the **laurel leaf**. **Doves** and **chickens** fed on **pellitory** <note>original: *helxine*. A common plant often found growing on stone walls, historically used for various ailments.</note> rid themselves of their yearly loss of appetite. **Swallows** showed that **celandine** <note>original: *chelidonia*. The name of the plant is derived from the Greek word for swallow, *chelidōn*. It was a common folk belief that swallows used the sap of this plant to heal the eyes of their young.</note> is most beneficial for the sight, healing the eyes of their chicks with it.

In the same way, as the earth decays, certain animals fail into a foreign nature <note>The author is describing the concept of "spontaneous generation" or metamorphosis, where one creature was thought to transform into another due to environmental factors.</note>. The **caterpillar**, having taken wings, turns into a **butterfly**. Caterpillars born on a **fig tree** are changed into **Spanish flies** <note>original: *cantharides*. These are actually iridescent green beetles, famously used in historical medicine, though they are quite toxic.</note>. The **water snake** <note>original: *hydrus*.</note>, once the lakes or stagnant waters are dried up, degenerates into a [land] **serpent**. Other changes happen according to the seasons: just as the **hawk**, the **hoopoe**, the **robin**, and the **redstart** are often changed in the summer. The **fig-eater** and the **blackcap**—which the Greeks call ***Melancoryphos***—exchange identities, so that what was a fig-eater in autumn, at the end of the vintage, is turned into a blackcap <note>This refers to an ancient belief that these were the same bird in different seasonal plumage, as they appeared at different times of the year.</note>. In this way **wheat** turns into **darnel** <note>original: *lolium*. A common weed in grain fields; it was widely believed that bad weather or soil could cause wheat to "degenerate" into this lesser plant.</note>, and from darnel back into wheat, and from seed it is changed into **oats**. **Martial** <note>A famous Roman poet.</note> affirms that if **basil** <note>original: *ocymum*.</note> seed is sown frequently, it changes now into **wild thyme**, now into **water-mint**. By the testimony of the father of **Galen** <note>The most influential physician of antiquity. His father, Nicon, was an architect and mathematician who conducted agricultural experiments.</note>—who, to know for certain, sowed carefully selected wheat and barley—he found darnel in the wheat and very little in the barley. He relates many such things, but let it suffice to have recounted these.

## On Sympathy and Antipathy, and how through them the virtues of things can be tested and discovered.

### CHAPTER IX.

# OF NATURAL MAGIC

...so to speak, a certain **compassion**, which the Greeks call *sympatheia* <note>original: συμπάθειαν; sympathy or "feeling together"</note> and *antipatheia* <note>original: αντιπάθειαν; antipathy or "feeling against"</note>, and which we more commonly call **consensus** and **dissensus** <note>Agreement and disagreement</note>. For some things are associated with one another by a mutual marriage and are bound together as if by a treaty; yet others are hostile to themselves and disagree with a grudge, laboring under blind discords. Or they possess something horrible and destructive, which cannot be sought out or constrained by any probable reasoning. It is not the part of a prudent man to prove these kinds of causes by hunting for them, except to say that Nature herself was delighted by such a spectacle; she was pleased that nothing should exist without a peer, and that nothing in the secrets of Nature should lack a certain **hidden property** <term>proprietate</term> <note>In Renaissance science, a "property" was an inherent, often occult power unique to a specific substance that science could observe but not always explain through the four elements.</note> and a peculiar power.

Led by admiration for these things, **Empedocles** <note>A Greek pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed that the forces of Love and Strife act upon the elements to create and destroy the universe.</note> affirmed that all things are created and destroyed through **strife** and **concord**, and that these are the "seeds" of all things found in the elements. This happens through the qualities that disagree or agree with one another, as we have mentioned. Finally, this is seen in the heavens themselves: just as **Jupiter** and **Venus** love all the planets except for **Mars** and **Saturn**, yet **Venus** is a friend to **Mars**, whom the other planets oppose. There is also another friendship and enmity found through the oppositions and exaltations of the "houses" <note>Refers to the twelve divisions of the celestial sphere in astrology.</note>. For the zodiac signs burn with hatred or are bound by the bond of friendship, as **Manilius** <note>Marcus Manilius, a Roman poet who wrote the *Astronomica*, a poem on astrology, in the 1st century AD.</note> sings:

> Indeed, the stars also agree among themselves by their own laws,
> and carry out a fixed commerce of things;
> in turn they offer sights and cling to ears,
> or they harbor hatred or a treaty; and some,
> turned back on themselves, are led by their own madness, etc.

These matters are laid out more widely in the books of the **Astrologers**, but they are even more clearly seen in living creatures. For example, **men** and **serpents** disagree with such an irreconcilable grudge that a man is terrified the moment a serpent is seen, and a pregnant woman... <note>The text breaks off here as the author prepares to describe the physical effects of this antipathy on human health.</note>

# BOOK ONE

...meeting [a pregnant woman], the creature's presence causes a miscarriage. <note>The text begins mid-sentence, likely referring to the effect of a specific animal—perhaps a wolf or hyena mentioned on the preceding page—upon a pregnancy.</note> No less does the **saliva of a fasting human** kill scorpions. The **Nile Crocodile** and the savage **panther** are enemies to man; the panther devours the man, but is itself most terrified by the **hyena**.

The **Ichneumon** <note>Often identified as the Egyptian Mongoose, famously described in antiquity as the mortal enemy of the crocodile.</note> is fatal to the crocodile, for it is an enemy given to it by Nature. It lies in wait for the crocodile while it suns itself; for while the crocodile holds its immense mouth wide open, the Ichneumon, gaping for its destruction, enters the opening of the mouth. It glides through the wide throat into the belly, and after eating away the internal organs, it emerges from the belly of the dead beast. However, the Ichneumon also disagrees with the **venomous spider** <note>original: "phalange"; likely a reference to the Phalangium or a similar stinging spider.</note> and, while waging war with the **asp**, often perishes.

The sight of a **wolf** is so harmful to a man that if the wolf looks at him first, it takes away his voice. <note>This refers to a common ancient superstition: if a wolf sees you before you see it, you are struck mute.</note> Anticipated by the gaze of the harmful creature, even if the man desires to cry out, he lacks the use of his voice. Yet, if the wolf perceives it has been seen first, it falls silent, and as its ferocity grows numb, it suffers a heavy loss of strength. From this arose the proverb: **The wolf is in the story** <note>original: "Lupus est in fabula"; an idiom similar to "speak of the devil," used when someone being discussed suddenly appears.</note>, as passed down by **Plato** in his *Republic* <note>original: "Politijs"</note>. 

Furthermore, if a wolf wounds a **horse** with its bite, the horse will excel in extraordinary speed; yet if the horse happens to trample on the tracks of wolves by chance, it is stunned, and its legs grow dull with numbness, as **Pamphilus** <note>Likely referring to Pamphilus of Alexandria, an ancient writer on agriculture and nature.</note> says. There is also a deadly war between the wolf and the **sheep**. The sheep fears the wolf so much that if garments are made from the skins and fleeces of a sheep killed by a wolf, they are far more prone to breeding lice than any others. Conversely, meats that have felt the bite of a wolf become tender and more pleasant to the taste. If the tail or head of a wolf is hung in a manger, it will strike the sheep with such sorrow that they will abandon their desire to graze and cry out for protection.

The **dog** is an enemy to the wolf but a friend to man—as is the **horse**, which is opposed by **griffins** and **bears**. The **spider** disagrees with the **toad** and with **serpents**; upon seeing the enemy, it balances itself on its thread, strikes its stinger into the enemy's forehead, and [brings] death...

...melts away. The **Lion**, the most noble of wild beasts and a terror to all other animals, is struck with dread and terrified by the crowing of a **rooster**—especially a white one—and by its crest. The **monkey** shudders at the **tortoise**; as soon as it is seen, the monkey flees and screams. The **elephant**, the largest of land animals and of a stupendous size, is most horrified by the grunting of a <term>suckling pig</term><note>original: "subrumi suis"; a pig still at the breast.</note>, as <term>Zoroaster</term><note>Refers to the legendary Persian sage to whom many occult and agricultural works were attributed in the Renaissance.</note> says in the *Geoponica* <note>A 10th-century collection of ancient agricultural lore.</note>. There is also a continuous struggle between the elephant and the **dragon** <note>In ancient natural history, "draco" often referred to giant serpents or pythons said to haunt India and Africa.</note>. The **rooster** does not fear the dragon for the sake of its chicks, and treats its immense mass as a joke; yet the rooster fears the shadow of a **kite** and is driven away by it. The elephant also fears the **ram** no less than the pig. For while it is driven wild with ferocity, it becomes tame at the sight of a ram, and its violent impulse withers away. By these devices, the Romans turned the elephants of **Pyrrhus**, the King of the Epirotes, to flight, and gained a great victory.

The **goldfinch** <note>original: "Salo"; likely a variant or error for "Acanthis" or "Salius," a bird traditionally described in bestiaries as the enemy of the donkey.</note> hates the **donkey**, and there is a battle between them: for as the donkey rubs itself against thickets or briars to scratch its skin, it destroys the bird's nests so that it might devour the still-tender leaves. This causes the eggs to be cast down and the chicks to fall from the nest in fear; but the bird, digging and tearing at the donkey's sores with its beak, pecks at the soft parts of its nostrils. The **hawk** is a dangerous enemy to the **dove** kind, but they are protected by the **kestrel**, whose sight and voice the hawk dreads. This fact is not hidden from the doves, for wherever the kestrel stays, they do not leave the place, trusting in their protector.

The **crow** and the **owl** wage a perpetual war, mutually lying in wait for each other's young; they snatch and eat each other's eggs, the owl by night and the crow by day, when it is more powerful in the light. All small birds fly around and strike at the owl. The **weasel** is an enemy to the crow. The **kite** is an enemy to the **raven**, and being superior in flight and stronger in its talons, it snatches away the raven's prey. The kite is also an enemy to the **fox**. Likewise, the **duck** hates the **gull**, the **harpa** <note>A type of predatory bird, possibly a harrier or falcon.</note> is an enemy to the **spider** and the **newt**, and the **woodpecker** hates the ar—

# BOOK ONE

...[the young] perish. The **heron** <note>original: "Ardeola"; the text continues from the previous page's description of birds that hate one another.</note>, the **merlin** <note>original: "asalo"; a small species of hawk.</note>, and the **raven** are enemies to the **vulture**; the **stonechat** <note>original: "florus"; likely a translation of the Greek "anthos," a bird said to mimic the neighing of horses while hating them.</note> is an enemy to the **horse**; and the **gnat** <note>original: "coluta"; likely a corruption of "culix," the common gnat.</note> to the **donkey**: for while the donkey is sleeping in the manger, the gnat enters its nostril and prevents it from eating. The **heron** also wars with the **eagle**, the **lark** with the **fox**, and the **eagle** with the **serpent**. The eagle also fights with the **wren** <note>original: "trochilo"</note> and the **goldcrest** <note>original: "oleribus"; this appears to be an OCR error for "orchilo" (the goldcrest), as "oleribus" would mean "vegetables," which does not fit the context of warring birds.</note>, and the **night-hawk** <note>original: "cybidus nocturnus accipiter"; likely a species of owl or night-jar.</note> battles with them as well; they stab at each other in turn so that they die together. Even **aquatic creatures** burn with hatred, such as the **mullet** and the **sea-bass** <note>original: "mugil, & lupus"; the sea-bass was known to the Romans as the "wolf of the sea" for its voracity.</note>, so much so that the sea-bass often bites off the mullet's tail; likewise, the **conger eel** and the **moray eel** mutually gnaw at each other's tails. **Lobsters** <note>original: "Locusta"; in ancient texts, this referred to the "locust of the sea" or spiny lobster.</note> shudder at **octopuses**, which bind them with their arms until they die. There is also a little worm in the sea called the **sea-gadfly** <note>original: "æstrum"</note>, which resembles a scorpion and is the size of a spider; it attaches itself with a sting under the fins of **tuna** and **swordfish**, causing such immense pain that they sometimes leap into passing ships.

This same enmity even creeps among **plants**: such as between the **oak** and the **olive**. They differ in hatred so stubbornly that if an oak is born in an olive grove, it flees, turns inward, and withers away; or if an olive is in an oak grove, it leaves behind most harmful roots to kill the oaks. If you plant the olive near a **walnut tree**, one of them must necessarily either die or remain forever weak. Indeed, the **shadow of the walnut** is hostile to almost all plants, and it immediately attacks with poison whatever it touches; it also causes harm by the manner of its **dripping** <note>original: "stillicidy"; referring to the toxic rainwater or dew that falls from its leaves.</note> when moisture is shed from its foliage. The hatreds between **cabbages** <note>original: "brassicæ"</note> and the **grapevine** are quite deadly, and their struggle is a sight to behold: for although the vine is accustomed to embrace all things with its twisting tendrils, it flees from the cabbage alone. Sensing it nearby, the vine turns itself in the opposite direction, as if someone had warned it that an enemy was close. Even while cabbage is being cooked, if you drop in even the tiniest amount of **wine**, it will neither cook nor retain its color. Furthermore, that very cabbage which puts the vine to flight will itself wither away when faced with **cyclamen** and **oregano**, so that while one flourishes, the other [fades].

There is also a wondrous discord and stubbornness between the **reed** <note>original: "arundinis"</note> and the **fern** <note>original: "filicis"</note>, such that one kills the other: for crushed fern root can expel reed splinters that have become stuck in the body; and if anyone wishes for reeds to stop growing entirely, let the place be plowed with a fern placed upon the plowshare, for he will find that ferns cut by a reed will not grow back. **Cucumbers** <note>original: "Cucumeres"</note> strangely hate **oil**, so much so that they flee from it, and if they are hanging, they will bend like a hook: this can be observed in a single night. Nor is this pleasant for all, for the stalk that has been smeared with oil will die; hence, oily trees reject the grafting and companionship of others, weeping a thick resin and pitch, or mourning with some oily substance. Yet, it is said that the **oak** can be made to bear **pears**, the **plane tree** to bear **apples**, and the **myrtle** to bear **pomegranates**. ### BOOK ONE

...aid. It is well established that **Hellebore** <note>original: "Veratrum"</note> and **hemlock** <note>original: "cicuta"</note> are deadly to humans, yet quails feed on the former and starlings on the latter; a fact which Lucretius expressed elegantly:

> For indeed one may see that bearded cattle often grow fat on **hemlock**, <note>elsewhere:</note> 
> though for men it is a sharp poison. And 
> furthermore, **hellebore** is a sharp poison for us, 
> yet it increases the fat on goats and quails.

**Giant fennel** <note>original: "Ferulæ"</note> is a most pleasing fodder for donkeys, but an instant poison for other beasts of burden; for this reason, that animal is sacrificed to Father Liber <note>The Roman name for Bacchus/Dionysus, the god of wine and fertility, who carried a fennel-tipped staff called a thyrsus.</note>, to whom the fennel is also sacred. Pure hemlock is a poison, yet when poured upon the roots of a **plane tree**, it brings it joy. If a **scorpion** crawls over **aconite** <note>Also known as monkshood or wolfsbane.</note>, it becomes dazed and sluggish. There is also a herb named **Cerastis**, whose seed, if handled with the hands or fingers, allows one to crush a scorpion without harm. Farmstead **cats** do not attack hens and birds if sprigs of **wild rue** <note>original: "rutæ"</note> are attached under their wings. A **weasel** about to fight a snake first fortifies and "armors" itself with this food. A **lion** is struck with stupor if it treads upon or passes over the leaves of the **holm oak**. A **wolf**, if by chance it touches the bulbs of the **sea squill** <note>original: "scyllæ"; a coastal flowering plant</note>, becomes weakened and faint; therefore, foxes place squills at the entrances of their dens to keep them away. **Plane tree** leaves drive away **bats**, so **storks** bring them to their nests to ward them off. **Swallows** protect themselves against moths and beetles by sprinkling **wild celery** <note>original: "Apium"</note> in their nests; and to repel other harmful animals, **wood pigeons** use **laurel**, **hawks** use **wild lettuce**—which for this reason they call *hieraciam* <note>From the Greek 'hierax' (hawk); known today as Hawkweed.</note>—**harpy-birds** use **ivy**, **crows** use **cuckoo-pint** <note>original: "aron"</note>, **hoopoes** use **maidenhair fern** <note>original: "adiantum"</note>, **ravens** use **prostrate vervain**, the **thrush** uses **myrtle**, the **partridge** uses **reed**, the **heron** uses **wild cumin** <note>original: "careon"</note>, the **eagle** uses **water-starwort** <note>original: "callitrichum"</note>, and the **crested lark** <note>original: "galerita"</note> uses **grass**. Whence the Greek adage:

-> In the twisted bed of the lark, the couch-grass is hidden, <-
-> *In the haunt of twisted grass the Crested Lark reclines.* <-

<meta>This page transitions from discussing natural antipathies (hostility between species) to "sympathies"—natural bonds of friendship, cooperation, and even love between different animals and plants.</meta>

# NATURAL MAGIC
<note>original: MAGIÆ NATVRALIS</note>

Swans about to give birth also bring the **chaste-tree** <note>original: "viticem"; also known as Vitex agnus-castus, believed in antiquity to have various protective or medicinal properties.</note> into their nests. If we have recounted those things that are hostile to one another through mutual contagion, how much more marvelous is it to witness so many things bound together by a friendly partnership and a natural feeling of benevolence? By a wondrous secret of Nature, they coalesce through the bond of a pact with others. If the serpent is an enemy to man, the **lizard** is a friend to man; it rejoices in his sight and watches closely so that it may lick his saliva. What is friendlier to man than the **dog**? And among aquatic creatures, the **dolphin**? Indeed, it is called a **Lover of Humanity** <note>original Greek: "Φιλάνθρωπος" (Philanthropos)</note>. It is well established that they are also erotic and prone to falling in love; as **Apion** <note>An ancient grammarian who famously recorded a story of a dolphin's friendship with a boy at Dicaearchia.</note> recorded in his writings, and as **Theophrastus** <note>A Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle.</note> reports happened several centuries earlier at **Naupactus** <note>A coastal city in Greece.</note>. There, dolphins were most ardent lovers; when they chanced to see boys of noble beauty in little boats or in the coastal shallows, they were inflamed with passion in wondrous and human-like ways. The **fox** lives in friendship with the **serpent**. **Peacocks** love **doves**; **blackbirds**, **thrushes**, **turtle-doves**, and **parrots** all flock together.

->Whence Ovid says:<-

> And the black turtle-dove is loved by the green bird.

**Crows** love **herons**, and they help one another in turn against the race of **foxes** due to their shared enmities. The same is true for the **golden oriole** <note>original: "galgulus"</note> and the **reed-warbler** <note>original: "ledus"</note>, and for the **rush-sparrow** and the **lark**. So too do the **harpy-hawk** <note>original: "harpa"</note> and the **kite** join against the **buzzard** <note>original: "triorchem"</note>, their common foe. This is no less true among fish that live in schools. There exists such love between the **whale** <note>original: "balænam"</note> and a certain small fish, similar in size and shape to a **gudgeon** <note>original: "gobij"; Porta is likely referring to the pilot fish, which was believed to lead whales to safety.</note>, that the little fish willingly swims ahead to guide the way, while the whale follows. In this guide, the whale finds its hope of safety and life; when the small fish rests, she rests; when it moves forward, she moves forward. It is not apparent why the fish wishes to serve her so. In the same way among plants, **vines** love the **elm** and the **poplar**, because they flourish almost too luxuriantly upon them. They are, as it were, wedded and married to them, climbing through their branches and clinging so tightly with their tendrils and follow-

# BOOK ONE
<note>original: LIBER PRIMVS</note>

...they embrace with bindings like thongs, so that they cannot be torn away or pulled apart, and they bear much fruit most extensively, which does not happen in others in the same way. **Palm trees** pursue each other with a more vehement love, so that one desires the other; they pine away with the wasting of desire and are tickled by the itch of lust to such an extent that they lean their fronds down together, or interweave them if they stand close by. But even if they are tied together by the binding of a rope, they embrace through that mutual contact as if performing the **duty of Venus** <note>original: "Veneris munere"; a common euphemism for sexual intercourse or reproduction.</note>, so that they joyfully raise the canopies of their heads. Farmers bring a remedy to this madness: the "insane love" is dispelled, and they make the trees fruitful.

**Leontius** <note>Likely Leontius Scholasticus, an author whose work on agriculture was preserved in the *Geoponica*.</note> also narrates an even more ardent longing of love, perhaps relying on the words of the ancients, saying that the tree desires to be so united—such is the spirit of Venus—that she does not rest from the desire-stimulated passion until the beloved male consoles her. Indeed, anyone may see her leaning down, reclining on her own fronds, burdened by her impatient trunk, and living as if a fruitless widow; and unless help is brought, she dies. 

This fact is not hidden from the farmer—that she is afflicted by love. Nor is he lacking a remedy; to recognize exactly which male she burns with the **desire to marry** <note>original: "maritandi cupidine"</note> (since many are clustered nearby), he touches the nearby males and then brings his hand back to the afflicted lover. When he feels her rub against his hand as if with a kiss, it signals that her desire is satisfied; stroking her with softer fronds, she nods. From this, he takes the flowers torn from the male trunk and places them around the head of his **mistress** <note>original: "amasiae"</note>. Thus, now burdened by the gift of her lover, she gives offspring, and cheered by this pledge of love, she shall be made fertile. Nor can the fruit on a female palm endure unless the farmer has shaken the flower of the male with its **dust** <note>An early, poetic observation of pollen.</note> over her. 

There is also a great love between the **olive** and the **myrtle**; and as **Androcius** <note>An ancient writer on botanical matters, often cited in agricultural texts.</note> reports, the branches and shoots of the myrtle...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of natural sympathies in the botanical world, specifically focusing on how certain plants thrive in the company of others or "marry" through grafting, concluding the author's survey of these hidden biological friendships.</meta>

# NATURAL MAGIC
<note>original: MAGIÆ NATVRALIS</note>

[The roots] creep through the **olive tree**, mixing together and mutually entwining their roots; no other plant is sown near it except its own kind. The same is true for the **fig**, which is hostile to all others. No less is the **myrtle** delighted beyond measure by its mutual habit with the **pomegranate**; for if they enjoy this partnership, they turn out more fertile. And although their roots may be separated by some distance, they are nevertheless gladdened by an embrace; but they rejoice more in **marriage** <note>original: "coniugio"; used here to describe the union of two plants through grafting or close proximity.</note>, so that a pomegranate grafted onto a myrtle yields larger fruit, according to **Didymus** <note>Didymus of Alexandria, an ancient Greek scholar whose agricultural observations were preserved in the *Geoponica*.</note>.

There is also a great association between the **asparagus** and the **reed**, which is why wild asparagus <note>original: "corruda"</note> is best sown in reed-beds, where it will spring up more broadly. The **almond tree** alone is less fertile on its own, but when joined with a companion, it bears fruit more swiftly. Many trees also become sterile if there is no pole or "male" tree nearby to form a certain "treaty" so that they may give birth. A cutting of the **wild olive** <note>original: "oleastri"</note> removes the sterility of the cultivated olive. Hence the poem:

> The sterile wild olive fertilizes the fat olives,
> And teaches that which knew not how, to bear its gifts.

Among other things, there is a secret commerce between **roses** and **lilies**, such that when growing close together, they please one another, and the flowers of the lily and the rose smell sweeter. All things grow broader if a **squill** <note>original: "scylla"; the sea onion (*Drimia maritima*), which ancient gardeners believed acted as a growth stimulant for nearby plants.</note> is planted nearby, and all garden vegetables in general are helped if **arugula** <note>original: "eruca"</note> is brought near them, according to the sayings of **Fronto** <note>Likely referring to Fronto of Emesa, an ancient writer on agriculture.</note>. **Cucumbers** love water as much as they hate oil, so that they will creep toward water even if it is hanging over them or at a slight distance. **Rue** will spring up broader nowhere than under the shade of a **fig tree**, or if it is grafted into its bark. The **cat** is exceedingly delighted by the scent of the root of <term>phu</term> <note>The ancient name for Valerian (*Valeriana officinalis*), which has a catnip-like effect on felines.</note>, because it strengthens the cat's eyes. From this, the name **catnip** <note>original: "gattaria"</note> was bestowed, because they dig it up intensely and roll their heads over it frequently. The same is true for **catmint** <note>original: "nepta"</note>. And let these things suffice, for we have held your ears with too long a speech.

<meta>catchword: Because (Quod)</meta>

It also notes the famous attraction cats have to valerian and catmint, ending the discourse on natural plant and animal affinities.</summary>

BOOK ONE.

# CHAPTER 10. That extraordinary gifts from heaven reside most especially within the particular individual.

Individuals are by no means lacking in brilliant and wondrous gifts; rather, they retain great power in their actions—even a greater power than they might possess from their species alone—due to the celestial positions of the stars and hidden properties. Albert <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a medieval philosopher and scientist whose works on the "secrets of nature" were highly influential in the Renaissance.</note> says: "Every individual born under a fixed horoscope and celestial influence draws a fitting property and a marvelous energy for acting or being acted upon <note>original: "energiam ... operandi, patiendiue"</note>, which is not merely a trait of the species, but one that is personal and unique." From this, various effects and inclinations are attributed to individuals based on the diverse influx and celestial arrangement. The Magician <note>term: "Magum" (Magus), here referring to a natural philosopher or practitioner who studies the hidden forces of nature to achieve practical effects.</note> must be knowledgeable of all these things so that, when many ways of operating are revealed, he may choose the most convenient one and adapt it to his use, should he ever find himself lacking in resources. For we have fulfilled our task when we have handed down the method of investigating and composing, so that nothing may be found wanting in our histories.

But let us return to the point from which the discourse flowed. Albert also reports that there were twins, one of whom had a side whose touch caused all bolts and gates to open, while the other, in a reverse exchange, closed whatever had been opened. Others are so terrified by the sight of cats, mice, and other animals that they cannot help but be distressed and fall into a faint. Likewise, a power is impressed from heaven upon some for healing scrofula <note>term: "strumas" (scrofula), a swelling of the lymph glands once thought to be curable by the "Royal Touch" or specific gifted individuals.</note> and curing various ulcers. And that which has greatly exhausted the surgeon, which could not be cured by medicine nor benefited by any remedy, is healed by the mere touch of saliva. Nor should those things be less considered which do not belong to the whole race, but which belong to and reside in individuals alone: such as boldness in harlots, insolence in gluttons, fearfulness in thieves, and of this sort— <meta>The word "huiusmo-" is a catchword for "huiusmodi," meaning "of this sort" or "likewise," which will continue on the following page.</meta>

<summary>This page argues that individuals possess unique "occult" powers and temperaments derived from their specific astrological birth (horoscope), rather than just their species. It provides examples of miraculous individual abilities, such as twins who can open or close locks by touch and healers who cure with saliva.</summary>

### CHAPTER 11. On the powers of things which are present in animals only during life.

It is fitting to weigh and observe certain remarkable functions—indeed, the greater part of those which operate only during life, but after death are dulled, vanish, and rarely serve any purpose. The eyes of wolves take away the voice; the **catoblepas** <note>A legendary Ethiopian creature, often described as a bull-like animal with a heavy head; its gaze or breath was said to be lethal.</note> and the **basilisk** <note>A mythical serpent king whose glance or breath could kill.</note> take away life instantly; the **remora** <note>original: "echeneis," a small fish believed in antiquity to be able to stop large ships by clinging to them.</note> delays the onset of ships; the **ostrich** <note>original: "struthiocamelus."</note> digests iron. These do not perform such tasks once they are dead, because they then clearly do nothing at all; for that **power** <note>term: "virtus" (virtue), meaning a specific inherent power or efficacy.</note> dies and perishes as life itself vanishes. Indeed, if parts are to be chosen from them, they must be sought from the living. Therefore, in the precepts of **Natural Magic**, I believe it is not established without reason that if anything is to be obtained from animals, it should be taken while they are still alive; and it would be even better, if possible, if they were to survive the procedure, for as the animal expires, that power is weakened and withers away.

For the soul (says **Albert** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), the medieval philosopher previously cited.</note>) contributes much to those things which are produced in animals; for death or decay perverts them, especially if it is a natural death, when they die with corrupted humors. Living things are accustomed to operate far differently and have more brilliant powers. This is observed much by the agreement of physicians and others who devote themselves to this kind of study. If, henceforth, tongues are extracted from frogs, stings <note>original: "radij," referring to the barbed tail spine of a stingray (pastinaca).</note> from stingrays, stones from the heads of animals, or eyes, so that they may work effectively, they should not be taken from the dead, but from those still enjoying life; and the animals should be thrown back into the water alive so they may live, ensuring that their innate power does not perish and wither, but that the power...

<summary>Della Porta argues that the occult powers of many animals—such as the basilisk's lethal gaze or the remora's ship-stopping ability—are tied to their life force and vanish at death, meaning magical ingredients must be harvested from living specimens.</summary>

### CHAPTER 12. That certain powers remain in bodies even after death.

*It is no less possible to perceive this in things that lack life: for certain **properties** <note>term: "proprietates" (properties), referring to the specific occult qualities or "signatures" of a substance.</note> remain so joined to them that they do not cease to work; indeed, they work much more powerfully. Wolves are so ruinous to sheep that they preserve their hatred even after death; for if you fit a drum with a wolf's skin and play it among other drums made of sheepskin, you will hear the wolf skin while all the sheep skins fall silent—it will actually make them go mute. A drum made from the hide of a bear or wolf, when beaten, drives horses away and puts them to flight even from a distance. Furthermore, if strings made from their intestines are stretched upon a lyre, they will jar with one another and never produce a proper tuning. The hyena is at odds with the panther; therefore, one who wears armor made from the hide of a dead hyena makes everything flee, as they cannot even bear to encounter him. Also, if you hang their skins directly opposite each other, the hair falls off the panther's skin. A lion's skin consumes and erodes the skins of other animals, as do wolf skins to those of lambs; likewise, the feathers of other birds mixed with **eagle feathers** <note>original: "aquilinis pennæ."</note> are reduced to decay and fall away on their own. The **florus** <note>original: "florus"; likely a small bird like the greenfinch.</note> and the **agithus** <note>original: "agithus"; often identified as a hedge-sparrow or titmouse.</note> are so opposed to each other, and their hatred is so persistent, that it is said the blood of the dead birds cannot be mixed together. Doves love the **kestrel** <note>original: "tinnunculus."</note> so much that, as **Columella** <note>A 1st-century Roman writer who specialized in agriculture and animal husbandry.</note> records, if one buries kestrel chicks in earthenware pots, puts on tight lids, and hangs the plaster-sealed vessels in the four corners of a dovecote, it fosters such a love of the place in the birds that, seized by this desire, they never change their home; thus after*

## On the mutual communication of things, and that some work by their whole substance and others by their parts.

### CHAPTER 13.

Furthermore, there are certain communications within natural things that act and work upon each other; observe these examples in other things and make use of them. In a most shameless prostitute, there resides not only boldness, but even a power <note>original: "virtus" (virtue/power). In this context, it refers to an occult quality or influence rather than moral excellence.</note>, such that the things she has touched or kept with her possess the strength to induce boldness and make one shameless. Hence, whoever gazes too often into her mirror or wears her cast-off clothes will be made like her, both in shamelessness and in lust. Not only is iron drawn by contact with a magnet, but it then attracts other iron objects; as we shall say, a single ring caught by a magnet attracts many others, so that they appear to hang like a chain, and the magnet's power is transferred from one to the next. In the same way, mournful clothes, or those used at funerals, make a person both mournful and deathly. One may observe the same in other things.

I also think it worthy of notice that the powers of things reside in some by their whole substance, but in others by their parts. As we have said, the <term>remora</term> <note>original: "echeneis." A legendary small fish believed to be capable of stopping large ships by attaching itself to them.</note> holds back a ship, not particularly by its parts, but by its entire substance; many such examples are read everywhere. Many things work according to specific parts: for instance, the <term>basilisk</term> <note>original: "regulus" (little king). A mythical serpent said to kill with its gaze.</note>, the <term>catoblepas</term> <note>original: "catoblepa." A legendary creature from Ethiopia, often described as looking down at the ground, whose gaze or breath was lethal.</note>, and the <term>wolf</term> <note>referring to the ancient belief that a wolf's gaze could strike a person dumb if it saw them first.</note> work through their eyes. Ants flee from the wings of a bat, but not from its heart or head; and they flee...

## *On the similarity of things, and which powers ought to be*
## *worked and investigated through it.*

### CHAPTER 14.

*Those things which we have already said arise from the property of the whole substance* <note>original: "totius substantiæ". In Renaissance natural magic, this refers to "occult" powers that belong to the specific nature of an object rather than its elemental makeup (hot, cold, wet, dry).</note>*, if they are compared, we have seen them to be either bound by a natural affinity or to battle in disagreement. Now, however, we shall speak of those things which work by a certain similarity. There is no principle more profitable in the things to be said, nor a root from which the operations of secrets and wonders sprout more vigorously. Therefore, you should apply the greatest diligence, such as we find the ancients most accurately applied in their writings. From this, the greater part of those secrets is established. From this source, as if in a composition, you may learn both how to recognize and how to assimilate them. We see that all species and qualities of things are able to incline, draw, and allure anything toward themselves according to their whole power, and to convert it to their own likeness; and if they are excellent in their action, this happens more easily: just as fire moves toward fire, and water toward water. And <term>Avicenna</term>* <note>original: "Auicenna". A famous 11th-century Persian physician and philosopher whose works were central to European medicine and alchemy.</note> *asserts that if anything stays in salt for a long time, the whole will begin to taste of saltiness; and if in something foul-smelling, it becomes foul; and if one stays with the bold and the fearful, one becomes bold and fearful. If any animal is accustomed to living with humans, it turns out human-like and tame. Many such things the dogmas of the Physicians affirm: how certain parts of bodies delight in their likes: as brain is drawn to brain, teeth to teeth, lung to lung, and liver to liver. The brain of a human or a chicken contributes to memory and <term>intelligence</term>* <note>original: "ingenium". Refers to natural talent, wit, or the inventive power of the mind.</note>*.*

# NATURAL MAGIC

...and a fresh skull of the same <note>Referring back to a person or animal mentioned on the preceding page.</note> mixed into a feast helps with epilepsy. The right eye of a weasel placed in a ring frees one from fascination <note>original: "fascinatione"; the "evil eye" or a malevolent gaze believed to cause harm.</note>, which occurs through the eyes, as we shall discuss. And he who carries the eye of a wolf or a man with him will hardly be seen. If [one carries] their tongues, the tongues or words of envious people will cause less harm. If you eat a chicken's gizzard <note>original: "ventriculum"</note> before dinner, even if you digest it with great difficulty, it nonetheless strengthens your own stomach. A monkey's heart prevents palpitations of the heart and increases audacity, which resides in the heart. A wolf's penis <note>original: "Lupi virga"</note>, roasted and sliced, if eaten, excites sexual desire <note>original: "venerem"</note> when one's strength is flagging. A hare's womb is effective for fertility. If you place the skin of a vulture's right heel upon the right foot of a man with gout <note>original: "podagrici"</note>, or the left on the left, it eases the pains of the joints. And finally, any limb benefits a similar limb. You will learn many such things from the schools of the Physicians; for it is not our intention to record all the things which they themselves have already recorded.

Furthermore, one must gather and pay great attention to those things in which a quality exists, or an excess of some property—and not an ordinary one—or an affection, or disturbances of this kind; and whether that excess is implanted by chance, nature, or art: such as heat-producing, cold-producing, love, audacity, sterility, fertility, sadness, loquacity, or whatever else we wish to achieve. These should be operated with using these things, and I believe with great effect <note>original: "quam minimè impunè"; literally "not at all without result."</note>; for instance, if you intend to make a woman sterile, consider a sterile animal, and one which surpasses all others in that remarkable passion <note>original: "passione"; here meaning a dominant physical state or inherent quality.</note>, so that you may accomplish it with easier effort. Such is the mule: whose sweat, urine, heart, womb, vulva, and the testicles of the male—if placed upon the belly, or drunk, or consumed with some seasoning, or taken as a fumigation <note>original: "suffitu"</note> through a funnel with a gaping mouth—prevent a woman from conceiving.

[Perform the work] at the hour when they are chiefly occupied with these things, whether by skill or by chance. And it will be worthwhile if you take the parts where these qualities most flourish: such as the brain, heart, testicles, vulva, womb, sperm, menses <note>original: *menstruum*</note>, or the afterbirth <note>original: *secundinam*</note>. If you prepare these snares for women, use the testicles or sperm; if for a man, use the menses, vulva, or womb. If you desire someone to be talkative, the tongues of water frogs, wild ducks, and geese provide the means to obtain what is desired; and indeed any animals upon which the mark of talkativeness is fixed—noisy creatures not unknown for their chatter—whose tongues, if you place them upon the breast or head of a sleeping woman (because they are more talkative at night), she will reveal the secrets of her heart. There are many more things, which would be for extravagant reading rather than for profit. How they should be properly administered, we shall teach below when we treat them more extensively. Now, however, let us put forward something regarding the operations of the heavens.

<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding the "Doctrine of Signatures" and the sympathy between earthly things and the heavens. # NATURAL MAGIC

...change, and in a continuous series are corrupted and begotten.
For this reason, I believe it was not without reason that the Egyptians attributed all things to the influences <note>original: *influxibus*</note> of the heavens, since they saw that all these earthly things were subject to them. Ptolemy confirms this, having dared to map out the celestial influences according to a standard <note>original: *ad normam*</note> and to predict many things from them; he says that this point does not require a long-winded proof. It is no less true that by the impact <note>original: *verbere*; literally "the lash" or "beating," referring to the energetic influence of starlight</note> of all the stars, all living things and seeds grow and decrease: from some stars this happens more frequently and clearly, while from others it is more uncertain and occurs at rarer intervals.
Aristotle, when he contemplated the "superior motion" <note>original: *superiorem lationem*; referring to the movement of the celestial spheres</note> as the cause and principle of all things—which, if it were to cease, these earthly things would immediately perish—said: "By necessity, this world was contiguous to the superior motions, so that its entire power <note>original: *virtus*</note> might be governed from there." He knew that such great force was spread by the Sun that he expressed it elegantly: "In the oblique circle, the winding path of the sun is the cause of the birth and death of all transient things, and the intervals of the seasons are caused by its approach and retreat." Plato says that certain celestial circuits are the cause of fertility and sterility. The Sun itself is the ruler of the seasons and the guide of life.
Whence Iamblichus, relying on the doctrine of the Egyptians, says: "Whatever good is present, it is certain to be present by solar power, and if we have anything from others, it is nevertheless perfected by the Sun." Heraclitus calls the Sun the "fountain of celestial light"; Orpheus calls it the "light of life"; Plato calls it the "celestial fire," an "eternal living being," the "animate star, greatest and of the day"; the Natural Philosophers <note>original: *Physici*</note> call it the "heart of the heaven."
Plotinus also reports that the Sun was worshipped by the ancients in the place of God. Nor does the Moon work with any less effect by her own power and that of the Sun, since she is more akin and specific to us. Albumasar said that through the Sun and Moon, power is infused and instilled into all things. The most learned Hermes says that after God, the Sun and Moon [possess] the power...

It cites a "who's who" of ancient philosophy—from Aristotle and Plato to Hermes Trismegistus—to validate the study of natural magic through astrology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 18, completing a thought regarding the celestial influences on life. # BOOK ONE.

<sig>C 3</sig>

*...certainly is the life of all living things. She, being nearest to the earth, surpassing all others in proximity, is the mistress and conciliator of moist things; and all things have such a great affinity with her that both animate and inanimate things feel her increases and her losses <note>original: "incrementa, & damna"; referring to the waxing and waning of the moon</note>. The seas, rivers, and the flows of waters grow and fail: now they flow rapidly, now they are carried with a slow course. The tide of the sea is agitated in perpetual change by its ebbing and flowing, and all men have attributed this to nothing but the motion of the Moon, so that now she swallows the water with a greedy draft, and now, swelling, it flows back as it recedes; nor does it appear from where else this could happen. She incites animals more generously, as if they were enslaved to her; for as she fills her orb <note>original: "orbem replens"; reaching the full moon</note> (as Lucillius <note>likely Gaius Lucilius, the Roman satirist</note> says), she nourishes oysters, sea urchins, mussels, shellfish, crabs, and the rest. Because at night she softens things with her warm glow, but when she is humped <note>original: "gibbosa"; the gibbous phase</note> or curved into horns on both sides <note>the crescent moon</note>, she empties them and leaves them exhausted. The same star <note>the Moon was considered a "wandering star" or planet in the geocentric model</note> that we just mentioned is also felt by cucumbers, gourds, melons, and those things which abound most in watery moisture, so that they swell as she grows and decrease as she ages. As Athenaeus <note>a Greek rhetorician and grammarian</note> reports, their greatest increases and decreases can be perceived when they are turned away from or facing the Sun. Nor do the buds of plants reject the condition of the heavens; farmers know this, having frequently tested it in grafting: for the growing wood does not thicken the fruit, but the "silent" <note>original: "silens"; the "silent moon" refers to the new moon or the period when the moon is not visible in the sky</note> moon thickens the fruit, while the wood becomes weak and thin. Therefore, those most skilled in agriculture have judged both the circuit of the year and the monthly course of the Moon to be so necessary for plants that this part of farming is considered especially essential. Also, while the Moon travels through the Earth signs <note>Taurus, Virgo, Capricorn</note> of the Zodiac, planted trees strike deep roots and subterranean parts; if, migrating through the Air signs...*

->OF NATURAL MAGIC<-

...will linger; the tree will spread its branches, luxuriant with leaves, and growing more upwards than downwards. Indeed, what more certain sign could be found than what is seen in the pomegranate tree: that for as many days as the Moon has moved away from the new moon <note>original: "interlunio"; the period when the moon is invisible</note>, so many years it will produce offspring? And it is said of garlic, that if it is sown when the Moon is positioned beneath the earth <note>below the horizon</note>, and likewise harvested when she is hidden beneath the earth, it will lack its foul odor. All timber for felling, such as beams and wood, while they obtain the new light of the Moon, teem with moisture, and as if softened through the intake of moisture, they become worm-eaten and decay. For this reason Democritus <note>a Greek philosopher who studied natural science</note> commands—nor does it displease Vitruvius <note>a famous Roman architect and engineer</note>—that by a fixed rule, timber is more harmlessly cut during the Moon's waning. Thus, material felled at the proper time, not failing from rot, may gain durability. 

Furthermore, by varying her ages <note>her phases</note>, she demonstrates various effects; for from her meeting with the Sun until she is horned <note>crescent</note> and halved <note>the first quarter</note>, she moistens and warms, but moistens more—the proof being that all moist things grow and receive a moistening power from her. While she becomes quite swollen and curved into a full circle <note>the full moon</note>, she possesses heat and moisture in equal measure: trees and minerals feel this. Waning, indeed, until cut in half again, she is warm and moist, yet with more heat, since she has more light. From this it happens that fish travel through the surface of the waters; yet in her there is a hidden warmth, because she spreads moisture, and with the moisture increased, rot follows, wherefore she dissolves things into decay. When, however, she once again makes a conjunction <note>original: "synodus"; a meeting of two celestial bodies</note> with the Sun, and being deprived of light ceases to appear, the Chaldean <note>ancient Babylonian</note> sages assert that she is warm and in a more excellent state of the heavens. Thus they relate that there is also a lunar herb, with round, circular, and sky-blue leaves, which knows the days of the Moon: for as she grows, one...

### BOOK ONE.

*...it produces a leaf by day, and as it wanes, it sheds it. We may observe this more broadly and frequently among us in domestic animals and plants, and we experience it continuously with our own eyes. Even the ant, that smallest of animals, feels the cycles of the stars, so that during the new moon <note>original: "interlunio"; the period when the moon is invisible</note> it ceases its work and rests, but during the full moon <note>original: "plenilunio"</note> it labors through the nights. The lobes of a mouse's liver correspond to the Lunar number, so that they grow large when her orb is swollen <note>full</note>, but decrease when it is hollow <note>new</note>. Hair that is cut and nails that are trimmed after the new moon grow back more quickly, but if done before, they grow back more slowly. The pupils of cats <note>original: "aelulorum"; from the Greek "ailouros"</note> know these same shifts of the Moon, appearing now wider and now narrower. Anyone who wishes to put this to the test must ensure the light remains the same; for a greater light exceptionally narrows the pupil, while a lesser light makes it more relaxed and wide. The scarab beetle also manifests the ages and seasons of the stars: it rolls a ball of dung into a circle and buries it in a pit dug in the ground for twenty-eight days, keeping it hidden for as long as it takes the Moon to circle the Zodiac <note>original: "signiferum"; literally the "sign-bearer," referring to the belt of constellations</note> and return to the new moon; then, opening the ball, it brings forth new offspring. The onion (which is much more wondrous) is the only one among all vegetables that knows the opposite cycles of the stars and possesses contrary phases of increasing and decreasing; for it revives and sprouts when the Moon is waning, but on the contrary, it shrinks when the Moon is growing. For this reason, the Egyptian priests do not eat it, as I have read in Plutarch’s <note>a Greek historian and philosopher</note> fourth commentary on Hesiod <note>an early Greek poet</note>. There is one kind of spurge <note>original: "Tithymali"</note> which they call the Sun-watcher <note>original: "Helioscopium"</note>, which turns its foliage according to the rule of the sun, so that it wakes up and lies down to sleep at twilight, stirred by a certain desire to watch the sun’s rising in the morning, while at night it closes its flower. There are also many solar herbs, such as the heliotrope <note>original: "heliotropion"; literally "sun-turner"</note>; for once the sunlight has appeared, it always watches with a bowed head during the day, so that it is never seen...*

...is seen to have twisted its husk, by a certain bond of love, and it inclines itself in the same direction toward which the star <note>In this context, "sidus" or "star" refers specifically to the Sun.</note> is carried; thus do the flowers of the mallow and the chicory. The lupine also gazes upon the setting Sun, so that it never twists its stem; daily it shows the hour to farmers even when it is obscured by clouds, acting as an index of the hours, and to them, it is a sign of the star's completed course. And in the region of the Euphrates, says Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC) was a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle; he is often called the "father of botany" for his extensive writings on plants.</note>, the flower of the lotus <note>original: "Loti florem"</note> not only opens and closes, but also sometimes hides its stem and sometimes reveals it from the setting of the Sun until midnight. 

In the same way, the olive, the willow, the linden, the elm, and the white poplar demonstrate the solstice; they turn their leaves over, showing the back of the leaf, which grows white with a pale down. Hedge-mustard <term>Irium: likely *Sisymbrium officinale*, a plant used in ancient medicine for the throat</term> and the herb of dried pennyroyal <term>pulegium: *Mentha pulegium*, a medicinal and aromatic mint</term>, even though they lack a root, will flower when hung and fixed to wood, and they reveal the equinox <note>original: "æquidium"; the time when day and night are of equal length</note>.

The Selenite <term>Selenites: literally "moon-stone" (from the Greek *Selene*); likely a translucent variety of gypsum or mica that reflects light in a way that suggests the moon's glow</term>—as if you were to say "the radiance of the Moon"—is a stone that others call *aphroselinum* <note>original: "aphroselinum"; from the Greek meaning "moon-froth"</note>. It contains an image of the Moon and reflects it, waxing and waning day by day. And there is another stone containing a little cloud which, like the Sun, revolves by emerging and submerging. 

The baboon <term>cynocephalus: literally "dog-headed" (from Greek *kion*, dog, and *kephale*, head). This refers to the Hamadryas baboon, which was sacred to the Egyptians and associated with the moon-god Thoth</term> greets the Moon at her rising, for standing on its feet, it raises its hands to heaven and wears a royal emblem upon its head; and it has such a harmony with the Moon that during her conjunction <note>original: "congressu"; the "meeting" of the sun and moon, or the New Moon, when the moon is invisible</note>—at which time it is usually affected—when during the monthly cycles she is not shining through the night or illuminating all things with a charming color, but is dark and silent, the male baboon will not look at anything, nor will it eat; instead, with its face cast down to the earth, it mourns, grieving indignantly at the Moon's seizure <note>original: "raptu"; referring to the moon being "taken away" or hidden during the new moon phase</note>. Indeed, the female, dissatisfied by the moonless night and never turning her eyes away, suffers the same as the male, and releases blood from her genitals. For this reason, even to this day, baboons are nourished in sacred rites, so that from them...

# BOOK ONE

...the time of the conjunction of the Sun and Moon can be known. From Horus <note>Referring to Horapollo, an author of a 5th-century treatise on Egyptian hieroglyphics.</note> in his hieroglyphics. The rising of <term>Arcturus</term> <note>The brightest star in the constellation Boötes, often associated with the transition of seasons.</note> brings about showers and rain. Dogs recognize the rising of <term>Sirius</term> <note>Also known as the Dog Star, the brightest star in the night sky, located in the constellation Canis Major.</note>, for they are driven into madness. Vipers and serpents become frenzied, ponds are stirred, wines in the cellars fluctuate, and the greatest effects are felt upon the earth. Basil <note>original: "Ocymum"</note> turns pale at its rising, and coriander dries up, as recorded by <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle, widely considered the father of botany.</note>. And they used to observe the rising of the Dog Star <note>original: "caniculae ortum"</note> diligently every year, and from it they would make a conjecture—as <term>Heraclides Ponticus</term> writes—as to whether the coming year would be healthy or pestilential. For if it appeared dimmer and, as it were, misty, it meant the sky was thick and dense, so that its quality would be heavy and pestilential. If the star appeared bright and clear, it signified the sky was thin and pure, and therefore healthy. The ancients feared it so much that they established the sacrifice of a dog to it. <term>Columella</term> <note>A Roman writer who produced an extensive work on agriculture in the 1st century AD.</note> wrote:

> ->Hence, so that evil blight does not scorch the green herbs,<-
> ->It is appeased with the blood and entrails of a suckling pup.<-
> ->& <term>Ovid</term>:<-
> ->In place of the star-dog, this dog is placed upon the altar.<-

The wild beast which Egypt calls the <term>Oryx</term> <note>original: "Oringem"; an antelope species believed by the ancients to have a mystical connection to the stars.</note> senses its arrival; for then, gazing at the rays of the Sun, it adores the Dog Star. <term>Hippocrates</term> <note>The famous Greek physician of the Age of Pericles, considered the father of Western medicine.</note> says that purgations are troublesome both before and after its rising, and that veins should not be cut <note>A reference to the practice of bloodletting, which was governed by astrological timing.</note>. <term>Galen</term> <note>A Greek physician whose views dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note> demonstrates that many operations must be observed on the "critical days" <note>original: "diebus decretorys"; specific days in the course of a disease believed to determine whether a patient would live or die.</note>, which are quite necessary to track; no less so in the sowing of crops, the casting of seeds, and the propagation of plants. Nor should the configurations of the great stars be hidden from you: their departures from the zodiac signs, such that watery and fiery impressions may be seen in the air. For if you look upon these things with an open mind, who among all would not conclude that the stars themselves are the causes of all inferior things?

...by which, if now unknown, the greatest knowledge of secret operations is found to perish.

### All simple medicines should be gathered at fixed times,
### and equally they must be prepared. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; CHAPTER XVI.

For this reason, we have judged that all things ought to be prepared at fixed times, and that this should not be overlooked: for as the heavens produce the various conditions of the year, so too do they vary the plants. Both the temperature of the sky (as <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>A Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle, widely considered the father of botany.</note> said) and the overall condition of the year contribute greatly to growth and nourishment. Hence it is not without reason that they say in the proverb: "The year produces the fruit, not the field." So that our <term>simples</term> <note>original: "simplicia"; medicinal plants or single-ingredient drugs used in their natural state, rather than compounds.</note> might retain more powerful operations, know that some are preserved for a long time, while others lose their power in a short span of time, as everyone can see and as is warned in the rules of physicians: which herbs are kept for many years and which should be considered useless. 

Thus, experiments passed down by the ancients are often thought to be invalid and vain, when perhaps some ingredients have fallen into one's hands that are decayed and consumed by age—especially those found in gems and stones. Furthermore, more potent virtues reside in the roots, leaves, and flowers of herbs if they are plucked at the fixed time. For all roots are to be dug up in autumn, when they abound most with moisture and <term>virtue</term> <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to the inherent medicinal power or "soul" of the plant.</note>; for otherwise, they vanish by drying out. At that time the leaves are falling, and the power is hidden within the roots. Flowers should be gathered in the spring, since they are born at that time and retain great power; leaves in the summer. Thus we command that these observations be made for the rest, which is similar to the opinion of <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>A Greek physician and botanist whose work was the primary pharmacological text for over 1,500 years.</note>. But (he says), above all, care must be taken so that each thing is both harvested and stored away at its proper time: for certainly, according to the reckoning of these things, either the power... <note>original: "viri-", likely "virtus". The sentence continues on the next page.</note>

# That regions and locations must be carefully considered where <term>simples</term> <note>original: "simplicia"; medicinal plants or single-ingredient drugs used in their natural state.</note> grow.

## CHAPTER XVII.

It is not uncommon for errors to occur regarding plants, <term>minerals</term> <note>original: "metallicis"; substances derived from mines or the earth.</note>, and similar things for those who, disregarding the location, offer up whatever comes to hand for use. If someone truly wishes to achieve this perfectly, it helps significantly if the state of the sky and the proper locations are observed. Just as a location possesses diverse temperatures, so too can it produce diversity in plants, and sometimes cause such a great change in the medicinal powers of plants that not only those initiated in the basics of this discipline are frequently deceived, but also Physicians and those who devote no small effort to the study of Philosophy when investigating their virtues. <term>Plato</term> <note>The classical Greek philosopher; the author likely refers to ideas found in the *Timaeus* regarding the soul of the world and the properties of matter.</note> mentions this matter: "Nature fortified the natures of places on earth with diverse virtues, so that diverse virtues might be sealed within them: as in plants and other things, such as they deserve according to their kind." Similar to this is the opinion of <term>Porphyry</term> <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) who wrote on the powers of nature and the soul.</note>: "Location," he says, "is the principle of generation, just like a father." It is also clear by the authority of <term>Dioscorides</term> <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a Greek physician and botanist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the standard pharmacological text for 1,500 years.</note>: it matters greatly if the locations in which they emerge are hilly, exposed to the winds, breezy, cold, and lacking water; for in these places, their powers are understood to be far stronger. On the contrary, those that spring up in damp, shaded plains and other places sheltered from the wind generally degenerate and have less strength. And Achaia <note>A region in ancient Greece.</note> near Cabyria...

Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle, often called the "father of botany" for his extensive writings on plants.</note> reports this most particularly: a certain kind of vine is held to exist, the wine of which causes miscarriage; and where dogs <margin>1</margin> have eaten the grapes, it is necessary that they also risk miscarriage. In its taste, the grape produces no different sensation than other grapes, nor is the wine perceived to differ from other wines. Nor does the region itself only change the natures of plants, but also the characters and forms of men. Who does not see the Asians and Libyans <note>In this context, Libyans refers generally to the peoples of North Africa known to the classical world.</note> as un-warlike and timid, while on the contrary, Europeans are entirely varied in mind and body—bold, warlike, great-souled, and skillful. The Scythians <note>Ancient nomadic people of the Eurasian steppes; classical authors often attributed their physical conditions to the cold climate.</note> are effeminate, like eunuchs, and impotent for intercourse; some have a thick and fleshy form, while others are slender. Not only is the soil varied in these regions, but also in their specific parts, as <term>Hippocrates</term> <note>The famous Greek physician (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) whose work *On Airs, Waters, and Places* is a foundational text on environmental health.</note> reports in his book *On Airs, Waters, and Places*, and I see that this was also the opinion of <term>Plato</term> and <term>Galen</term> <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire whose theories dominated Western medical science for over a millennium.</note>. Hence, if plants in other regions seem to differ greatly from their original forms, or do not retain equal strengths, they should be brought from those places where they are seen to obtain that power most strongly. For those that turn toward the North or the South, or those that face the rising or setting sun, do not act with equal effect. Pines, firs, and turpentine trees inhabit the mountains; the plane tree, poplar, and willow inhabit the river; holm oaks, ashes, maples, and hazels inhabit the woods; as do those that inhabit stagnant waters, flowing streams, marshy places, shadowy caves, the spray of springs and walls, and dripping rocks. I do not deny that they may spring up in other places, yet not with equal virtue; for they work more intensely in one place and more weakly in another, according to the disposition of Nature, which loves to be born here or elsewhere. The juice of <term>Hemlock</term> <note>original: "Cicuta"; a highly poisonous plant used in ancient Greece for executions.</note> is a poison in Athens, hated as the public punishment of the Athenians; when <term>Socrates</term> <note>The iconic Greek philosopher sentenced to death by drinking hemlock in 399 BC.</note> had taken this <image-desc>leaf fleuron</image-desc>, he was taken from our midst by an instantaneous death. This, however...

### Certain properties of places and springs,
### which can contribute to our work.

## CHAPTER XVIII.

No less does the diversity of places operate in various effects of things: for the location of lands and waters also retains many miracles, which it behooves a <term>Magus</term> <note>A practitioner of "natural magic," which in the Renaissance was considered a branch of science and philosophy focused on understanding the hidden powers within nature.</note> to rightly know and understand. For we frequently see some things operate solely by reason of their site, and bring about wonders because of the inclination of the sky, and the force of the Sun as it approaches and recedes further or nearer. For if one land did not differ from another, not only would there be scents in the reeds, rushes, and herbs in Syria and Arabia, and frankincense-bearing trees, nor would they give pepper berries or nuggets of myrrh, but all things of the same kind would be produced in every place on earth. From springs also certain properties are drawn, which could not happen otherwise, unless the moisture of the earth—infused with its own properties of flavors into the roots—nourishes the matter, through which it rises to the top to pour out the flavor of the fruit proper to that place and its own kind. There are also many types of waters, and they possess many properties. ->### BOOK ONE.<-

...[if you] immerse it, or even wood, it gradually turns to stone, yet the part that remains above the water does not change. In the city of Hierapolis, across the river Maeander, there is water that turns into tufa <note>original: *tophum*; a porous rock formed by mineral precipitation</note>, such that those who divert it into aqueducts build entire solid walls out of it. There are rivers in Boeotia, the Cephysus and the Melas, and when cattle drink from them continuously during the season for conception, even if the cattle are white, they produce offspring that are ash-colored <note>original: *leucophæa*</note> in some places, dark brown <note>original: *pulla*</note> in others, and in others still, a raven-black <note>original: *corecino*</note> color. Likewise, sheep turn black by drinking from the river Peneus in Thessaly and the Astaces in Pontus. There are also types of deadly water which, through the harmful dryness of the earth, absorb a poisonous power into themselves: such as the spring at Terracina called the Neptunian spring. Those who drank from it were deprived of life, which is why it is said the ancients blocked it up. 

And at Cychros in Thrace, there is a lake from which not only those who drink, but even those who bathe in it, die. In the region of Arcadia called Nonacris, there are extremely cold droplets trickling from the rock; this water is called the "Water of the Styx" <note>original: *Stygos Hydo*; the Styx was the legendary river of the Underworld, but this refers to a real waterfall in Greece believed to have toxic properties</note>. Neither silver nor bronze vessels can contain it, for they burst and shatter; nothing can hold it except a mule’s hoof. It is recorded that this water was brought by Antipater into the province where Alexander the Great was, delivered by his son Iollas, and that the king was killed by that very water. In the territory of Falerii <note>original: *Phalisco*</note>, on the Campanian Way in the field of Cornetum, there is a lake where a spring rises. In it, the bones of snakes, lizards, and other reptiles appear to be lying; yet if you try to pull them out, you find nothing <note>This likely refers to an optical illusion caused by mineral deposits or the clarity of the water</note>. 

Likewise, there are several veins of acidic springs: such as at Lyncestis, and in Italy at Teanum in the Campanian territory, and in many other places. These have the power <note>original: *virtutem*</note> to dissolve stones in the bladder when drunk. There is a certain spring in Paphlagonia from which people become intoxicated even without wine...
