<header>BOOK ONE</header>

*Timaeus, Posidonius, Hegesias, Herodotus, Aristides, and Metrodorus, who with vigilance and infinite study sought out the particular properties of places and declared them in their writings. From there, these things were narrated by Pliny, Solinus, and many histories.*

->How simple substances ought to be mixed and composed, when they enter into our mixtures.<-

### C H A P T E R  X I X .

*We have now judged it necessary to hand down the compositions of <term>simples</term> <note>individual, unmixed natural ingredients, usually plants or minerals</note>, or mixtures, so that after the studious have learned to investigate the latent effects of Nature and how to select all things, they may bring those wonders into the light and learn the method of composing; a method which I see is also observed by physicians in the highest degree.* Because we often do not require a single effect, but a double or triple one, we must use a mixture of simples so that they may bring forth the sought-after effects. And for this reason, I judge that the art of mixing was invented. Indeed, it happens quite often that substances act too feebly at times; we, however, in order that they may act more promptly and finish their action more quickly, are accustomed to strengthen them with multiple aids. And on the contrary, if they bring forth their effect too intensely, we dull and extinguish their powers. Most often, however, it happens that when we wish to strike a specific limb—for instance, the head, the heart, or the bladder—we associate the primary substance with others, so that they might target that part directly <note>literally "wound" that part; the author uses the metaphor of an arrow hitting a target to describe medicine reaching an organ</note> and provide for all needs; from this, it happens that even contrary things are mixed together. 

Let us therefore follow through with the discourse we have begun. When you undertake any work, consider first that which we chiefly intend, and toward which we direct that simple or mixture. Let us establish a "base" or foundation for the composition, from which the composite mixture may take its name; and let it be of such a quantity as the actions of the material form ought to be. For in order that they may act, they must have a proper [quantity]...

<sig>D</sig>
<meta>catchword: proper (original: tam)</meta>

<summary>This page explains the theory of "compositions," arguing that mixing individual ingredients (simples) allows a practitioner to strengthen weak effects, temper overly strong ones, or direct a remedy to a specific organ.</summary>
<keywords>pharmacology, simples, medicinal mixtures, Timaeus, Pliny, medical method, targeting organs, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the composition of substances from page 24. The previous page ended with "provid-," which completes here as "provide a path for the medicine."</meta>

<header>OF NATURAL MAGIC</header>

...e a path [for the medicine]. For certain things <note>original: "quibusdam," completing the word from the previous page</note> demand a specific and determined quantity, while others are added as aids and seasonings to the primary ingredient; without these, they would not work as easily, nor would they act more slowly or more quickly as desired. Thus, the fragrant is mixed with the foul-smelling, and the bitter with the sweet, for the sake of taste or scent. For if a bitter and foul mixture were to be presented, it would be rejected by those for whom it is intended; the <term>animal spirits</term> <note>in early modern physiology, these were subtle fluids that carried sensation and motion through the nerves</note> flee and abhor it, so that even if it is swallowed, a harm to its efficacy follows. In the same way, thick and hard parts are mixed with thin and soft ones for the sake of pleasantness. 

It sometimes happens that a portion of an ingredient is so small that before it can warm the body, it is consumed by the body's own internal heat; in such cases, we add some bulk. While this does not hinder the operation, it provides suitable fuel for the heat, so that the material is not consumed faster than is necessary, and remains fit for its intended purpose. 

If, for example, we attempt to capture birds by putting them to sleep, the <term>methel-nut</term> <note>original: "nux methella"; likely *Datura metel*, a plant known for its sedative and hallucinogenic properties</note> provides us this convenience, being possessed of such powers that it induces sleep, renders them senseless, and by a heaviness of the brain, they are given over to slumber. We lay this down as the base of the mixture for the rest of the ingredients. To make it work more vividly, we add <term>opium</term> and the dregs of pure wine. If the ingredients are hard and we want them to flow—so that legumes or other bait might be more effectively soaked in them, as we shall explain in the sections on preparations—we will also entice the birds with these: let the seeds be dissolved in the juice of <term>mandragora</term> <note>mandrake</note>, or <term>hemlock</term>, or ox-gall. 

And so that these do not seem foul or bitter to the birds, we mix in honey, cheese, or flour to make the bait more edible. Let the legumes be soaked in this and offered to them to be eaten; for when they have tasted the food, they fall to the ground lulled to sleep. They do not dare to fly and can easily be caught by hand. I advise that this method be observed in other matters as well.

## How the weight of a mixture ought to be investigated.

### CHAPTER 20.

It is also to be noted most carefully that the correct dosage of a mixture be understood, and the proportion of weights be found: because the excellence of operations in all things consists only in the best proportion and right harmony. Nor will mixtures provide the effects they promise, unless they are perfect in every part <note>original: "omnibus numeris absolutae"; a Latin idiom meaning complete or perfect in all respects</note>. Hence we find that the ancients were always accustomed to use nothing but careful selection and weight, both in their mixtures and in individual substances; and thus, things proven by experience have been recorded for us. 

Therefore, you who devote the greatest labor to these things, should first find the weight of the simple medicine, as much as the matter requires, according to the estimation <note>original: "imaginationem"; here meaning a calculated mental image or conception of the formula</note> of the sought-after proportion. Conceive for yourself that medicine as being made of any components you like, considering what proportion it holds to the whole. For that which is found in the parts ought also to be in the whole; and if you find that more than its dosage has been added, once the components are held, let it be subtracted from the whole. 

Thus, let that be established as the foundation with a known weight, and let it support only other weights, and let there be removed from it as much as will allow it to equal its full dosage when other things are mixed with it, since many things must enter into the medicine, and these are perceived by the operator's judgment. Thus, regarding a composite mixture, you should never give that which exceeds the dose, even if it were simple in its power. But once all the <term>degrees</term> <note>in early medicine, the intensity of a quality—such as heat or moisture—was measured in four "degrees"</note> are calculated, it should not be greater in quantity or power so as to have a different quality and power: for we do not add an ingredient to increase the dose, but so that it might carry out the work more easily. This is also especially worthy of notice in mixtures and medicines—

<keywords>dosage, proportions, harmony, medicine, weights, degrees, pharmacology, ancients</keywords>

*the proportion of weights ought to vary for all things, just as regions and climates become diverse: for in these different places, ingredients acquire a different power. In one place they operate more vividly, but in another more vigorously, as we have already advised. You should weigh these matters with a balanced mind, and according to the effect of the simples <note>A "simple" is a term used in historical medicine and alchemy to refer to a single medicinal herb or substance, as opposed to a "compound" mixture.</note>, the ratio of weights should be changed—by taking away, adding, and adapting with the skill of your own wit—so that the ingredients perform exactly what we seek. In our work, we have followed the best reasoning in the description of experiments by describing weights in "parts" rather than fixed units, and not rashly, so that they might be more easily understood. This is because the diverse names of weights used by others might hinder the craftsman in his work. So that each person may use the specific quantity they desire, we have seen that Cornelius Celsus <note>A Roman encyclopedist known for his medical work *De Medicina*, which emphasized practical treatment and anatomy.</note> followed this same method; for in this way, the needs of everyone are more suitably satisfied.*

### On the preparations of simples.
-> CHAPTER 21. <-

We have now taught how to compose mixtures and how to investigate their weight. It remains to describe certain preparations of "simples" which seem most suited and necessary to the work, and which require far greater skill than the rest. Indeed, the success of these operations does not depend so much on the simples themselves as on their preparations, without which they will accomplish little or nothing. Simples are usually prepared by many artisanal methods so that they may be more suitable and convenient for use. Those which we use most frequently are these: Ripening, Putrefaction <note>The process of allowing organic matter to decay or ferment to release its active "virtues."</note>, Maceration, Boiling, Reduction to Lime <note>original: "Calcem redactio"; also known as calcination, the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to reduce it to a powder.</note>, Incineration <note>Reducing a substance to ash.</note>, Distillation, Drying, and others like them. For we macerate something when we submerge it in some liquid—

<summary>The author explains that medicinal weights must be adjusted based on local climate and introduces the foundational chemical processes—such as distillation and calcination—required to prepare individual ingredients for use.</summary>
<keywords>weights and measures, climate, simples, pharmaceutical preparation, Cornelius Celsus, calcination, distillation, maceration</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page concludes Book One of the treatise. It completes the discussion on the preparation of medicinal substances, specifically detailing the physical methods—boiling, calcining, roasting, and distilling—used to extract the "virtues" or active powers of raw ingredients before the end of the first volume.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>BOOK THE FIRST.</header>

...[we act,] so that the substance may be moistened and macerated <note>Maceration involves soaking a substance in a liquid to soften it and extract its soluble parts.</note> both internally and externally. As we have said, the more refined part is drawn out through pressing, while the earthy residue is left behind, allowing the core to receive the desired moisture. We utilize boiling <note>original: *elixamus*. In early modern pharmacy, this meant boiling a substance in liquid to extract its essence.</note> when the juice cannot be drawn out from a substance; for by boiling, we extract from the center toward the outer surface. In cases where the essence is not found through maceration or infusion, boiling releases delicate vapors.

We also employ scorching, roasting, and reduction into ash <note>original: *calcem*. Calcination is the process of heating a substance to high temperatures to remove volatile components or reduce it to a fine powder.</note> so that we may utterly deprive the parts of all moisture. This occurs when we reduce a substance to ash, so that it may be more easily dissolved, or turned into a liquid, and mixed with other substances. We roast substances when they cannot be ground, so that they may be turned into powder. In this, one must take care that the substance is not burned away; if it is burned, it loses its desired powers <note>original: *vires*. In this context, "virtues" or medicinal potencies.</note>. It should be roasted only until it reaches a state of fineness.

Substances or "simples" <note>Individual medicinal ingredients, usually herbs or minerals, not yet mixed into a compound.</note> are distilled so that a "water" of more powerful virtue may be drawn out, allowing the substance to work more easily and effectively. We do this because we require the more refined parts of the medicine, rejecting the thicker parts which would hinder our purpose. The same logic should be understood for other operations.

I have judged these matters to be opportune and necessary for our work. If anyone desires a more extensive explanation, let him consult the books of the physicians. But now, let us move our pen forward from these things.

# OF GIAMBATTISTA
## DELLA PORTA, NEAPOLITAN,
### ON NATURAL MAGIC
#### BOOK II.

##### P R E F A C E .

<image-desc>A large decorative woodcut initial 'H' featuring elaborate foliate designs, vines, and a small winged figure (putto or cherub) at the base.</image-desc>

So far, we have wandered more widely than was perhaps fitting regarding the causes and their actions—topics which we seem to have taught in the first volume. Now, however, it does not seem untimely to undertake those operations which we have frequently promised. But first, let us teach the wondrous <note>original: *monstrificas*. While it can mean "monstrous," in this context Porta refers to marvelous or extraordinary changes that provoke wonder.</note> transformations of plants; for among other experiments of this kind, agriculture retains many such things that are not unpleasant to behold. But before I discuss how to perform them, I find it necessary to say a few things about the whole matter. Nature demonstrates many paths by which we may easily achieve our desire, since we know for certain that plants easily pass into a foreign nature and are subject to various changes. 

Starting from this auspicious principle: some plants thrive through cultivation and demand to be tended, while others ignore and reject it. If you withhold care from the former or force it upon the latter, they become worse and fall into decay. The fir tree <note>original: *abies*</note> becomes worse through cultivation and grows wild; the wild pine and the bittersweet <note>original: *celastrus*. A name used in antiquity for various hardy or evergreen shrubs.</note> degenerate; and cultivated plants grow wild when neglected, while wild ones become mild through cultivation. For instance, a neglected vine degenerates into a wild vine <note>original: *labrusca*</note>, and water-mint <note>original: *symbrium*</note>, unless constrained by cultivation, shifts its form into catmint <note>original: *nepeta*</note>. Likewise, if you fertilize it...

<header>PREFACE</header>

...those which require pruning <note>original: *amputationem*</note>, and if the plant is one that rejects dung, you should lop off the tops of those that seek dung. Furthermore, it will be worth the effort if you learn their origins, as you may learn from **Theophrastus** <note>Theophrastus (c. 371–287 BCE) was a Greek philosopher and the successor to Aristotle; he is known as the "Father of Botany" for his systematic study of plants.</note> and others. This plant is suited to rise from seed; that one spontaneously; others from a root, by tearing away, from a branch, a shoot, a trunk, or from wood cut into small pieces. For the crushed bark of the white poplar, worked into manured furrows, yields edible mushrooms throughout the year; **Virgil Maro** <note>The Roman poet Virgil, whose *Georgics* is a famous instructional poem on agriculture and rural life.</note> also teaches their origin in his *Georgics*. 

If you, however, plant those which are known to be born from a root or spontaneously; or if you set down by roots those grown from seed, or those from a cutting <term>talea: a cutting or small branch used for grafting or planting</term>; or if you sow by a shoot: it will bestow unusual fruits, and you will see them come forth against the rite of Nature. We already know that the sowing of the fig happens in such a way: if you sow the seed in a fig-bed, know that it produces some diversity, since that is not its natural way of sowing. Hence, seeds sown from a black fig will yield white figs, and white ones black; or no kind is produced at all, except the <term>caprificus: the wild fig tree, which produces fruit that does not ripen but is essential for pollinating domestic figs</term>, or the forest fig. 

And they say a white grape comes forth from a black one, which they call the hooded grape <note>original: *capuciam*</note>. And the white poplar passes into the black, or a tree of good fruit will never be perfectly restored. The sweet almond, if grown from the fruit <note>meaning the seed or nut</note>, becomes bitter, hard, and degenerates in flavor. And from the seed of a sweet pomegranate <note>original: *punici*</note> sour ones come forth, whereas when it comes from a branch it puts forth a bud; and from its bluish-gray seeds, worthless things come out. There is also in Crete a certain kind of onion <note>original: *Cæpæ*</note>, as Theophrastus says, which when sown from seed grows thick in the root, but when planted as a bulb it grows a stalk, and in...

<header>NATURAL MAGIC</header>

...the whole seed is shed without a head, and it is sweet in flavor: for it has this quality, as it were, contrary to the others. For all plants that are set down <note>original: *depositae*; referring to planting by cuttings or transplanting rather than sowing seeds</note> are perfected better and more quickly. If turnips <note>original: *rapae*</note> are planted far apart, they become "effeminate" <note>likely meaning thin or weak</note>; but if they are planted densely, they say they all become "masculine" <note>meaning thick or robust</note>. It is also well known that from the red fruit of the laurel and the myrtle, black fruit is produced. Nor would it be out of place to consider the mercy of the heavens and the changes of the seasons, so that plants might be changed according to their own times, and be brought forth more quickly or more slowly.

For in certain cases, old seed has the power to change their nature. Seeds of the gourd and the cucumber, if they are fresher, are born more maturely <note>meaning they sprout or ripen sooner</note>. However, celery <note>original: *apium*; can also refer to parsley</note> and watercress <note>original: *nasturtium*</note> come forth more quickly from old seed. And they say that from the seed of cabbage <note>original: *brassicae*</note>, when it grows old, the turnip is born; and conversely, from the seed of turnips, cabbage is born <note>This reflects an ancient belief, documented by Pliny and others, that certain vegetable seeds could transform into others if stored too long or grown in poor soil</note>. They say that the seed of spelt <note>original: *tiphae*; a type of ancient grain</note> is accustomed to change into wheat if it is sown crushed, though this does not happen immediately, but in the third year.

If, therefore, you water those things which usually disregard watering <note>plants that prefer dry soil</note>, they produce a diversity of form. Pomegranates <note>original: *mala punica*</note> are believed to become sour if you water them constantly; for dryness in these provides sweetness and abundance. Thus, when all nutrients are changed, **Theophrastus** <note>The Greek "Father of Botany" whose works were a primary source for Porta</note> says that the kinds of trees are also transformed, and likewise, an altered seed committed to the earth can transform both the plant and the fruit. The reason is this: whatever the beginning <note>original: *principium*</note> is like, that which comes forth from that very beginning must necessarily follow its nature. Thus, we shall teach in order about those that are early-ripening, late-ripening, larger, and "monstrous" <note>original: *monstruosos*; in this context, meaning "prodigious" or of unusual size and shape</note>. Let it be enough for me now to have demonstrated the method.

<summary>This page discusses the transformation of plants through environmental factors like planting density, water, and the age of seeds. Porta cites Theophrastus to argue that changing a plant's "beginning" or environment can fundamentally alter its fruit and nature.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 28, where the author discussed the peculiar habits of seeds and how their age or planting density can transform one species into another, such as cabbage into turnips. He now transitions into the "magical" art of manipulating the seasons themselves.</meta>

<header>BOOK TWO</header>

## How we may produce early or late flowers and fruits.

### CHAPTER I.

Art, the rival and imitator of nature, achieves more sublime things while following her. Thus the Magician <note>In this context, a "Magician" is a natural philosopher or scientist who understands the hidden laws of nature to perform seemingly miraculous feats.</note>, acting as a "second Nature," dressed in her own garments, examines through the evidence of his eyes and the conjecture of his mind what nature effects in reality through her hidden majesty. Nature often covers her tracks with manifold windings and obscure veils; however, because the Magician knows that many things can come about through human preparation and that nature may favor these efforts, he can produce unusual births and unconventional growth that nature would otherwise entirely refuse to grant. Thus, by his efforts, he hinders nature's work, makes it retreat, or compels things that would have ripened late to produce early. He makes nature obey his command through the very power with which he is endowed from heaven. He also understands that flowers and fruits vary due to the diversity of the seasons and the restless circuit of celestial heat <note>This refers to the Sun's path and its influence on the climate.</note>. Therefore, if he desires to delay plants or bring them forth more quickly—making them more highly valued because they are out of season—he executes this by creating false intervals of time, effectively turning winter and summer into spring. Furthermore, the practice of grafting <note>original: *insitio*; the technique of joining a part of one plant onto another so they grow together</note> brings no small advantage and supports us greatly in these matters. Hence, if it pleases you

->To bring forth flowers and fruits ahead of time:<-

Choose whichever flower you prefer, for what works for one works for all; let it be the rose. At an early time, around the end of October, sow it in earthenware vessels in earth that has been passed through a sieve and made richer <note>original: *liquidiorem*; literally "more liquid" or "looser," referring to the consistency of soil mixed with fertilizer</note> by mixing in manure. You shall nourish it twice a day with warm water; and if the air is stirred by wind or there is excessive rain, you shall bring it into a sheltered house,

...[at the] most, nor leave it in the open air at night. But when the frosts and winter rains have ceased, and the mildness of the air is drawn out, expose it to the sun, if the day allows for sunshine. When the time demands this, and the first of spring shines forth, and the bud begins to germinate from the calyx, let it be watered with warmer water: for the plant delights in a continuous and slow flow of water. In this way, that which was the last among the spring flowers will be the first to burst forth. It must also be considered that untimely germination happens during a calm winter, or when a south wind follows, rather than in a rough and snowy one, as is gathered from the writings of Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus (c. 371 – c. 287 BC) was a Greek philosopher and the "father of botany."</note>, since the generative force and fertile moisture gather together—sometimes of their own accord, and sometimes from the remaining part of the moisture that moved forward from which the previous fruits had been produced. In a similar manner, we shall have

->**Very early cucumbers and gourds:**<-

Before spring has even begun, you shall plant the seed, as is clear from what was mentioned before and from the teachings of the Quintilii <note>Sextus Quintilius Condianus and Sextus Quintilius Valerius Maximus, two brothers who authored a famous Roman work on agriculture.</note>. After the plant has begun to grow strong and the colds cease, in a tilled place improved by frequent watering, break the vessel in the dug pits and bury it up to the rim until it is level with the surface of the earth. If you remove the overgrown shoots while they are still young, they will bear fruit far more quickly. For you cannot safely leave them in gardens or open places because of the harshness of the season; it is better to plant them in "bedroom carts" <note>original: *cubicularibus rhedis*; portable wheeled planters that could be moved indoors to protect plants from the cold</note> so that when the cold sets in, they may stay in hidden places protected by windows <term>specularia</term> <note>original: *specularia*; ancient Roman window-panes made of thin, transparent stone like mica, which functioned like a primitive greenhouse</note>, and thus you will ward off the rigors of winter. In almost the same way, cucumbers were provided every day to Tiberius Caesar <note>Roman Emperor (14–37 AD) who famously insisted on having cucumbers on his table every day of the year.</note>, for which he had a marvelous craving. Nor should we think there is any other reason why the soil of Inarime <note>The ancient name for the volcanic island of Ischia, near Naples.</note> and Puteoli <note>The modern city of Pozzuoli, located in the Phlegraean Fields, a highly volcanic area.</note> produces early-ripening fruits faster than the neighboring lands. For the earth warms the trees with subterranean heat and fires, with which that entire soil abounds...

...so that they may digest [their nutrients] more quickly. I command this to be done in all other cases as well, lest we wander too far afield. But for a younger plant, and by a different craft as well, 

->**To produce grapes in the spring**<-

We are able to do this. If in the springtime (as is evident) we see the cherry tree bearing its own fruit <note>The Latin *mala* literally means "apples," but in this context refers generally to the fruit of the tree.</note>, and we desire grapes at that time, an abundance will not be lacking, as is drawn from the sayings of Tarentinus and Pamphilus <note>Tarentinus and Pamphilus were ancient authorities on agriculture whose works contributed to the *Geoponica*, a famous Byzantine collection of farming lore.</note>. When the gum has ceased to flow from the trees, and to prevent it from rotting or becoming worm-eaten, I command you to scrape away the downy moss that surrounds the tree, for it will greatly hinder the grafts. Let the grafting be done, which they call **emphyllismon** <term>emphyllismon: a Greek term for "budding" or "in-leafing," a specific method of grafting where a bud or shoot is inserted under the bark.</term>; for in this way it joins together more easily. Then, let the bark of the tree be loosened, and a thin, strong wedge be driven between the wood and the inner bark <note>original: *librum*, referring to the phloem or inner layer of bark.</note>, but do so gradually with a light balancing of the hand, so that the band of bark is not damaged. Once the wedge is withdrawn, let a sharpened shoot of a black and fertile vine be immersed into the gap and tied. In this way, it will yield grapes at the usual time for cherries, since the vine is forced to snatch its nourishment from the underlying trunk. You shall do the same for the pear and the apple, if you desire them at different times. In this manner, we shall make autumn figs into spring figs, and twice-bearing, and we may often eat grapes in the autumn. Thus we may have fruit perpetually, as Didymus <note>Didymus was a 4th-century agricultural writer.</note> taught, if an apple is grafted into a citron tree, since that tree possesses a perpetual fertility throughout the whole year: always having fruit while others are just beginning to grow, you will have them both earlier and later than usual. Note also that these things happen only to those trees which are moist and very fertile, but they never occur in dry or less fertile ones. There are also many remedies which provide for these things. For example,

->**To have all fruits and flowers ripen earlier**<-

# NATURAL MAGIC

...twice, you shall bear [fruit], you shall think, and every month, without any interval of time, the roses will come out in bloom, as **Didymus** <note>Didymus of Alexandria was an ancient authority on agriculture whose work is preserved in the *Geoponica*.</note> taught. **LILIES**, so that they may bear flowers in succession, let the bulbs be sown, some at twelve, some at ten, eight, or four fingers' depth. **ARTICHOKES** <note>original: *cinarae*</note> also should be planted often, and they will frequently bring forth fruit. We shall have **FIGS** early and more mature if you apply and smear them with pigeon dung, oil, and pepper. And caprification <term>caprification: the ancient practice of hanging "wild" figs in cultivated trees to allow fig-wasps to transfer pollen and accelerate ripening</term> contributes to this: for as the seeds rot, certain fig-wasps are generated, and being deprived of food within those [wild figs], they fly to their kin; by their frequent biting and greedily opening the mouths [of the figs], they bring the sun in with them, and having opened these windows, they let in the ripening breezes, and by consuming the milky moisture, they prepare the fruit for maturity. Or the trunk of the fig tree should be scarred where it swells with milk, for when one moisture flows out, the rest is quickly ripened. If ram horns are also buried in the fig orchards around the roots of the trees, and if they are planted near squill <note>squill: a bulbous Mediterranean plant (*Drimia maritima*) used in ancient times to protect or stimulate the growth of other plants</note>, they will yield fruit more swiftly. Lime applied to the roots makes cherries ripen early. But the human mind has dared to scrutinize natural things so exactly: it does not refuse to open the inner chambers of Nature with a more skillful search of experience, so that it dares not only [to produce them] more swiftly, but—

Here, be a diligent operator: for committing even a small error, you will be cheated of your desired result. # BOOK TWO

...roll the earth together and mix in the ash of burnt beanstalks. ### The same regarding cucumbers

It will happen [the same way] if you immerse their fresh seeds, or those of the large pumpkins which they call **melons** <note>original: *peponum, quos melones vocant*</note>, during the summer time in human blood—not from a weak man, but from one who is healthy, adult, and of a "tawny" or vigorous complexion. For such blood retains within itself a warmer and more effective power. Change the blood frequently so that it does not spoil, but it must remain fresh. Let the seeds stay for a week; then, having been dried in the sun, let them be planted in small trenches in fertile and powdered earth. Take care that they are not turned upside down. Nor will it hurt if **quicklime** is spread beneath them. Then, the stalk, irrigated with warm water or "burning water," will spring forth. Cover it with cloths so that the heat, once raised, does not fly away; you will watch it crawl, provided you do not allow anything nearby to which the falling vine might cling, and it will grow monstrously to an immense size—though in a short process of time, it will breathe out its **artificial life** <note>original: *vitam ascititiam*; refers to life sustained by external "magic" rather than natural vigor</note>. For those things which bring forth such precocious fruit are weaker than others, so that they cannot endure the rush of moisture created there. We have already said how we may produce early and very early fruits; now it remains for us to teach regarding **late-ripening ones** <note>original: *serotinos*</note>, which you may learn to produce by the opposite methods: just as you previously heated things, now cool them down. ->How a fruit composed of diverse parts may be made.<-

### CHAPTER II.

In the marvelous compositions and transmutations of Nature, no better path is taken than through grafting <term>grafting: *insitio*, the horticultural technique of joining a shoot or bud (the scion) from one plant to the stem or root of another (the rootstock)</term>. We have praised this technique sufficiently, and shall praise it far more hereafter, since by the mutual embrace of diverse things it creates one indissoluble bond and offers a reason for wonder. And if anyone should consider these graftings laborious or impossible (for I know many will not be lacking who mock and trample upon these things), let the result be a relief for the labor; you shall perfect even nearly impossible graftings with skillful care. Do not let the words of a crude farmer or an ignorant craftsman dissuade you from that which is demonstrated by experience. Turn over in your mind those things handed down to memory by the ancients: such as the fig being grafted onto the plane tree and the mulberry. The mulberry onto the chestnut, the terebinth <term>terebinth: *terebinthus*, the turpentine tree, often used in antiquity as a hardy rootstock</term>, and the white poplar—from which white mulberries are born. Chestnuts onto the walnut tree and the oak. The pomegranate delights in various "adulteries" <note>original: *adulterijs*, a classical metaphorical term for the mixing of species through grafting</note> and rejoices to be mixed with all others. The cherry onto the peach and the terebinth. Onto the cherry, the terebinth and the peach—

...with Quince on Hawthorn. A vine on an olive tree produces fruit and is called "oil-grapes" <term>oil-grapes: *elæostaphylos* in Greek, *oleuuam* in Latin; a mythical or rare hybrid described by ancient authors</term>, which we call *oleuuam* in Latin. Florentinus <note>Florentinus was a 3rd-century author whose agricultural writings were later collected in the *Geoponica*</note> claims in the eleventh book of his *Georgics* that he saw this at the home of Marius Maximus; he tasted and evaluated the fruit, noting that it truly tasted like a grape berry <note>original: *acinum*, though the OCR reads *asinum* (donkey), the botanical context requires *acinum* (berry/grape)</note> and an olive berry together. They say that Myrtle grafted onto Willow also bears Pomegranates; and as for the others (lest we tire the readers with a longer narrative) which it is right to observe, they are nonetheless far more difficult than our own methods. Finally, Columella <note>Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella, a Roman authority on agriculture</note> affirms and teaches that every kind of shoot can be grafted onto every kind of tree. From this comes every composition of fruits; from this their adoption; from this, unusual fruits and foliage are bestowed. Hence the Poet in the *Georgics* says:

> "And it marveled at the new leaves and fruits not its own." <note>A famous line from Virgil’s *Georgics* (2.82) describing the wonder of a grafted tree.</note>

And it is so effective that it has discovered a new kind of "adultery" <term>adultery: *adulterij*, used here in the classical sense of cross-breeding or botanical hybridization</term>. For example, from the mixture of a nectarine <term>nectarine: *nucipersicum*, literally a 'nut-peach'</term> and a peach, an offspring unknown to our ancestors, unheard of, and not even fully imagined...

### To compose a fruit from a peach and a nectarine:

You will achieve this through grafting, which farmers call "budding" <term>budding: *emplastrationem*, a technique of inserting a single bud into the bark of another tree</term>. From a peach and a nectarine, you shall take young, fertile branches that show great promise of growing and budding. Having marked off a space of two fingers, so that the buds are placed in the center, gently peel the bark from the wood with a sharp knife so they are not damaged. Split these down the middle so that when joined together they may grow together properly, and the place of the scar may not appear, but rather a single bud is seen. Insert this into that part of the tree which appears most bright and especially vigorous <note>original: *hilaris*, literally "cheerful," used here to describe a healthy, sap-filled branch</note>. Prune away the remaining branches so they do not steal the nourishment, so that all the sap serves the graft. Open the bark—ensuring the iron blade does not wound the wood—and cut around a space of the same size as the bud, and so [place] the bud...

...fit [the bud] <note>The Latin *mam* is the conclusion of *mamillam*, meaning a bud or nipple-shaped shoot</note> so that the cut part is equal to its counterpart. In this way, it will sprout and produce offspring that retains a nature midway between both parents, a kind which trees have never brought forth on their own: for it will be a peach, representing a nectarine <term>nectarine: *nuci persicum*, literally "nut-peach"</term> with a matching appearance. **S**o pomegranates <note>original: *mala punica*</note> appear sweet on one side and sour on the other. **D**iophanes <note>Diophanes of Nicaea, a Greek writer on agriculture from the 1st century BCE</note> also orders that apples be joined with fragrant pears, and that these be named pear-apples <term>pear-apples: *myrapidia*, or "ointment-pears" from the Greek *myron* (perfume) and *apion* (pear)</term>. **A**pples are also grafted onto quinces, and the most beautiful apples result, called honey-apples <term>honey-apples: *melimela*</term> among the Athenians, as Diophanes also left in writing. **A**nd likewise, different kinds are mixed together: citron-apples with lemons, and citrons <term>citrons: *medica mala*, literally "Median apples"</term> with sour varieties; thus they are semi-acid and semi-sweet. **I**t is well established that a peach can result from a blood-red and a white variety, and similarly honey-apples and the rest have arisen from a manifold production. **N**or should this cause us any wonder, since every living thing is sociable with another living thing: especially those that agree in kind, since they coalesce into a single nature, and the nourishment serves both without the mixture of the other—just as a diverse fruit is born and two meet in one, like two rivers flowing together, each having the spring from which they emanate. **T**here also arises from the diligence of grafting a fruit <note>The Latin *malum* (apple) is used here as a general term for orchard fruit</note> which outwardly will emulate a peach, but inside mimics a sweet almond, which we can rightly name:

### Almond-Peach:
<note>original: *Amygdalopersicum*</note>

**Y**ou should pluck a small branch from a peach tree; if you graft it onto a sweet almond, and then regraft the sprout that grows from it onto another almond, and do this three or four times, it will yield a fruit containing a sweet kernel inside. Most things can be [achieved] by ingenuity...

**And another way, so that the Vine may bear white clusters and black berries.**

Although we could produce grapes like those we proposed using the previous example, nevertheless, to satisfy those greedy for fuller literary instruction, we shall add other methods. So that the same branch might bear both a white and a black grape simultaneously, and in the same cluster some berries are black and others white, appearing equally divided: take three, four, or more cuttings <term>cuttings: *malleolos*, literally "little hammers," referring to the shape of vine shoots cut with a piece of old wood at the base</term> of different kinds and colors, and which are easily grown. Arrange these evenly and bind them very tightly together, then insert them into a small pipe or a ram's horn so that they stick out from both ends. Below, loosen the shoots and bury them in a trench with manured earth; water them until they produce buds. After two or three years have passed, when a single structure has been joined <note>original: *coagmentata*</note> from them, break the pipe (or the horn, if it has already rotted away). At the point where they have adhered most strongly, cut them with a saw, and heap pulverized earth over the top so that it covers the trunk by three finger-breadths. When the shoots have budded, leave only one and cut away the rest, lest the shoots be unable to supply enough sap if all are left. Then, from the union <term>union: *coïtu*</term> of the combined twigs, a single tree rises up, providing multi-colored grapes.

**ANOTHER WAY** from the teaching of Didymus <note>Didymus of Alexandria, an agricultural writer whose work is largely preserved in the Byzantine *Geoponica*</note>, and we thought we should satisfy the reader more easily thus: let there be shoots, one black and the other white. When they are pruned, they must be split through the middle, taking care that none of the pith <note>original: *medulla*, the soft central tissue of the vine</note> falls out. You will then join the divided parts together so that the eyes <term>eyes: *oculos*, the vegetative buds of the vine</term> of both coincide exactly, so that the two almost become one. Then you will bind them tightly and take care to smear them with sticky earth, and for three days frequent watering should follow...

-># BOOK TWO #<-

...growth, until from both a single sprout breaks forth, and produces grapes in which there are berries of different kinds and colors. But if the cuttings <term>cuttings: *malleoli*, vine shoots cut with a piece of the previous year's wood</term> taken from the tree do not grow together so easily, or if it is another type of plant that cannot be hosted on a foreign trunk, you shall perform the grafting more cautiously in this manner, which has been recently observed in neighboring trees. From the tree from which we seek the shoot, we lead a small branch across to the plant we wish to graft. Its branch is cut and split, and the shoot is entwined within it. The part of the small branch that makes contact on either side where it enters the split is scraped with a pruning hook and thinned out. On the side where it faces the sky, let it have its bark leveled precisely with the bark of the host. The tip of that small branch which he inserts should be directed so that it runs toward the sky. After a year has passed, when it has taken hold, cut it away from the original plant from which it was propagated. In this way, the branch, stripped of its own bark, is clothed in the foreign bark; it is bound so that the edges match. In this way, pomegranates and quinces of different colors are produced, and many other things which we have deemed unnecessary to recount. However, because the rods are hard, they pre-soften them with a hammer; for once bruised in this way, they grow together more easily. Now for another preparation.

### How a fig tree may become equally white and black.

Let us pass down a different method from another source, which Leontius <note>Leontius was a 4th-century writer on agriculture often cited in the *Geoponica*</note> recorded. Choose the more convenient of these, which the ancients learned from the jackdaw bird: when the bird hid seeds in the gaping cracks of trees, the plants that sprouted from them showed not only one tree growing within another, but multi-colored berries. This observation provided the inspiration for the technique. Therefore, take grains <note>original: *grana*, referring to the tiny seeds inside the fruit</note> of white and black figs, bind them tightly in linen or paper, and write upon it <note>original: *scribito*; likely meaning to label the seeds for identification</note>. When the season requires, transplant them, and two-colored figs will grow, so that the fruit turns black on one side and white on the other...

...shines white. What we have described once, we have judged to be laborious; for as time passes, we can propagate offspring with many cuttings <term>cuttings: *sarmentis*, young twigs or vine-shoots</term>, grafting others so that we create a variety that will rarely fail. Now, anyone can compose many things for themselves: for the kinds of compositions are innumerable, and if they could be grasped and written down, it would nevertheless be superfluous to recount them.

### How fruit may come forth without bone or pits.
### CHAPTER III.

There is an ancient saying of the philosophers—especially those who handed down selected precepts of agriculture—that if you remove the pith <term>pith: *medulla*, the soft, spongy tissue in the center of a plant stem</term> from the shoots to be grafted or from living roots with an ear-pick <note>original: *auriscalpio*; Porta suggests using this small, delicate tool for precise surgical removal of the plant's core</note> or a bone knife, the fruit will come forth "boneless" <note>original: *exos*, meaning without a hard inner stone or pit</note> and without the woody shells of the pits, since the pith itself is the nourisher and parent of the bony substance. However, the Arcadians <note>Arcadians: referring here to ancient Greek pastoral authorities or inhabitants of Arcadia known for their rural lore</note> oppose this opinion: for every tree, they say, in which the pith has been partially removed, survives; but if the pith is completely withdrawn, not only will pitless fruit fail to bulge forth, but the tree must necessarily be stripped of life and dry up entirely. They are supported by this reasoning: that part is the most moist and vital, for the nourishment provided by the soil runs through it until it reaches all parts. By a natural spirit, all nourishment of a living thing—acting like a kind of soul—is drawn through the pith of the trunk as if through a siphon. This is demonstrated by the fact that wood devoid of pith bends back and is pulled into a circle until it is completely dried out; and the ancients persistently dreaded this. For us, however, whom the teachings of Theophrastus <note>Theophrastus: a student of Aristotle and the "father of botany," whose works provided the scientific foundation for Porta's experiments</note> remind of truth and experience, this will be of great use and brings no small advantage.

<meta>catchword: As if</meta>

### So that, if you wish, it may be as Democritus teaches:
## Grapes without seeds:
<note>original: *Vua vinaceis vacua*; literally, "grapes empty of grape-stones"</note>

A shoot <term>shoot: *sarmentum*, a young twig or vine-cutting</term>, or a branch to be buried, should be split evenly from the horse-side <note>original: *ex equo*, likely meaning "equally" or "levelly" in this context</note>, and you shall wedge it open from the top with a small stone. From there, scrape out all the pith <term>pith: *medulla*, the soft inner core of the stem</term> on both sides with a bone stylus <note>original: *osseo stylo*; bone was preferred over metal to avoid "burning" or poisoning the delicate plant tissue</note>, either in that part which the earth will cover, or as deeply as it can possibly be hollowed out. Immediately afterward, having bound it carefully with a tight wicker band or papyrus, bury it in one trench in moist and very rich soil. Lay it down and let it be tied to a fixed reed so that it cannot be twisted; thus the joint is joined together as one, as it was before. It will be even more excellent if you sink a bulb of sea-onion <note>original: *scyllæ bulbum*; the squill or sea-onion was believed to act as a medicinal "glue" and nutrient source for grafts</note> inside as far as the shoot has been hollowed out. For it joins the parts together as if with a kind of glue, moistens them with moisture, and fosters them with heat, like a sort of grafting. 

The same result will also occur if the entire pith is withdrawn while grafting a scion. In this way you will also succeed if you wish a cherry tree to produce fruits without stones: cut a still-tender trunk and remove the pith, bind the separated parts together, and smear them with mud and dung until they grow together, and you shall have it. And if it tires you for this to happen over the long cycles of time, when the year-old sprout has appeared, you may graft that sprout onto other young shoots, or graft others onto it. We can also produce

## A peach without a stone
<note>original: *Persicum sine osse*</note>

by a new method of grafting: We plant a peach sapling near a willow <term>willow: *salix*</term> in a well-watered, moist, and overflowing place; if it is not naturally so, assist it with waterings so that the wood swells and offers its overflowing sap both to itself and to the foreign shoots. Let the willow be the thickness of an arm, and let it be bored through the middle; leaving the head of the peach tree intact, we will cut off all its branches and lead them through the hole of the willow trunk:

After a year has passed, once they have joined so that one tree is made from two, we cut back however much sticks out above the drilling and the joint, so that nourishment is not carried there, and the sap is diverted from the union of the graft, lest the other tree, weighed down by the attached trunk, should bend under the weight of the adopted fruits. Or, with the top of a willow <term>willow: *salix*</term> submerged in the earth, let it be bent into the shape of an arch; once they have grown together, the peach tree <term>peach: *persicus*</term> is cut, moved, and covered with earth. Thus the peach tree, wedded and married to the willow, will bring forth fruit of wonderful sweetness without pits <note>original: *sine ossibus*; literally "without bones"</note>. In a slightly different way, the same is said regarding:

### Gourds without seeds

This will happen, as is derived from the sayings of the Quintilii <note>The Quintilii brothers, Condianus and Maximus, were famous Roman landowners and agricultural writers in the 2nd century AD</note>: if the shoot of gourds, melons, or cucumbers—once it has grown and stretched out—is layered like a vine and buried in dug-up earth so that only the head stands upright above it. When it has matured, do this again, a second and third time. Sprinkle water over it, and when you know that by this enticement it has struck and established all its roots, cut through the middle of the curved shoots projecting above the ground. The final stem will indeed give fruit without internal seeds, wrapped only in fleshy pulp <note>original: *cartilaginibus*; referring to the firm, seedless flesh of the fruit</note>. **In this way** they will also grow without seeds if you soak them for three days, or a few more, in sesame oil before you sow them. From these sources it is also taught how there can be produced:

### A tender nut, without a shell,

As Africanus says: When the nut is already fully [broken] on both sides...

### BOOK TWO

...the shells <note>original: *carinæ*; literally "hulls" or "keels," used here for the nut's hard outer casing</note> are broken, so that the whole kernel, separated into four parts, is pulled apart by an intervening wooden membrane, with the flesh unharmed. Thus wrap it in wool, papyrus, or vine leaves, lest being bare it be gnawed away by ants, and it will yield tender fruits. **OR**, in the place where you have intended to plant it in an acorn-sized hole, dig a pit and place in powdered earth, and plant the seed with a reed <term>reed: *ferula*</term>. When it has sprouted and grown, open it and hide the kernel of a bare nut in its pith; and thus for a long time you will have the fruits you most desired.

**THUS** we can also make the Tarentine nut—others call it the *Mollusca* <note>original: *Molluscam*; from *mollis*, meaning "soft," referring to a soft-shelled variety of nut</note>—for they call the Tarentine that which has a soft and fragile shell, so that it is easily broken when handled by the hands. Often water this with lye <term>lye: *lixiuium*, an alkaline solution made from wood ashes</term> throughout the year, and throw ash onto the roots, as Damegeron <note>An ancient, likely Hellenistic, author known for writing on agriculture and the virtues of stones</note> has handed down. **AND** by also boring deep into the tree, you will make hard and "masculine" nuts friable <term>friable: easily crumbled or shattered</term>.

**THUS** concerning the filbert nut <note>original: *auellana*</note> and the almond <note>original: *amygdala*</note>: before it has produced a flower, with the roots laid bare <term>laid bare: *ablaqueatis*, the Roman agricultural practice of digging around the base of a tree to expose the roots to air or water</term>, let warm water be poured in for several days, and they will yield tender nuts, as Africanus <note>Sextus Julius Africanus, a 3rd-century scholar</note> says. It happens in the former way so that it brings forth a naked kernel and a friable shell, so that it is covered not by a shell but more truly by a skin, so tender that it is chewed at the same time; and finally, this applies to all fruits that are covered by a shell. 

We can turn the same method to the Myrtle, so that it produces berries without small pits <note>The 'p' in 'producat' has a horizontal stroke through the descender, a standard Latin abbreviation for 'pro'.</note>. Dig in a circle two palms away from it, and frequently water it with a rain of tepid water. Theophrastus <note>An ancient Greek philosopher and the "Father of Botany"</note> relates that this was discovered by chance: for when a neglected myrtle grew next to a bathhouse, it gave fruit without pits, and people sought seeds from it; thus this kind began to exist in Athens. He also reported that the same happens with the spring apple. **NOR** should the saying of Africanus be passed over...

*I think, to make a pomegranate without seeds <note>original: "sine granis punicum"; the author is describing a method to produce seedless fruit</note>: and this occurs if you remove a visible part of the pith, as is done in the Grapevine, and plant the split wood; and after a time, if you cut the part of the plant standing above, it will yield the desired fruit.*

### How fruits may be made sweeter, more fragrant, and larger. CHAPTER IV.

There are some trees which perish from the splitting of the trunk and various mutilations, due to the sudden intrusion of air and external heat; for as the corruption descends to the lowest parts, they waste away and dry up immediately. There are, moreover, many trees which sustain not only a wound, but even the splitting and boring of the trunk, and from being poor-bearers, they are rendered highly productive: such as the pomegranate, the almond, and the apple, which we use most frequently. For when wounded, they bring forth better and sweeter fruit: they take in as much nourishment as is sufficient, and from there they sweat out the harmful humor <note>The author is applying "humoral theory"—the ancient medical belief that health depends on the balance of fluids—to plant life.</note> (as one may often observe in animals), and they digest the rest more easily, making the fruits sweeter and bringing forth more beautiful specimens. Since they live with a delicate structure, they easily compress and join together the split and scattered parts. From this, many experiments have arisen. Thus, so that

->*Almonds and citrons may become sweeter,*<-

*Although the bitter Almond is believed to be more healthful, it is nevertheless rejected and trodden underfoot. But if you wish to make that same bitter tree sweeter, according to Africanus <note>Sextus Julius Africanus, a 3rd-century author whose work "Kestoi" contained many agricultural "charms" and techniques.</note>: after digging around the root four fingers' breadth on all sides, bore a hole through the lowest part of the tree. In this way, the phlegm <term>phlegm: *pituita*, a term for watery or sticky plant sap thought to cause bitterness if it accumulates</term> or undigested humor with which it swells will continually flow out; this makes it sweeter and brings mature fruit more quickly. Thus we shall have an edible citron, even if it cannot be eaten due to internal acidity: the Root*

*Or, you may cut a thicker branch and hollow it out to the depth of a palm; drip honey into it, and cover it with straw mats, tiles, and other such coverings to keep out the sun and rain. When the plant has drunk up all the honey, pour more in again, and pour urine <note>original: "lotium"; ancient agriculturalists frequently used human and animal urine as a nitrogen-rich fertilizer</note> over the roots. Rub off and scrape away any budding or swelling gems <note>term: "gemmas" (buds); the author refers to the growth points of the plant</note> except for those where you provided the honey; spare the rest, and in this way we shall have sweet citron-groves <note>original: "citreta"</note>.*

*And even these can, as Paxamus <note>A writer on husbandry often cited in the "Geoponica," an ancient collection of agricultural lore</note> says, and*
->Pomegranates become sweet,<-
*if they were sour, by correcting them thus: Make a trench in a circle around the tree, then water the exposed roots with pig dung, human dung, and old urine. Or, before it has produced flowers, uncover the roots and apply boiling water; they will then please the mouth with a slightly acidic but pleasant flavor. According to Anatolius <note>Vindanius Anatolius, a 4th-century author of agricultural texts</note>, apples produce a sweeter fruit if you continually water the roots of the plant with urine, goat dung, and the dregs of old wine. You can make a pear sweet and more fertile in the same way, following the care of Diophanes <note>Diophanes of Nicaea, who famously condensed earlier agricultural works</note>, if you drill into the trunk right at ground level and drive in a wedge made of oak or beech, and then bury it.*

## To make fruits and flowers sweeter and more fragrant

*We can make them so—such as melons, cucumbers, artichokes, medick <note>A type of clover or alfalfa, often grown for fodder</note>, citrons, and pears—so that they turn out more fragrant, according to Florentinus <note>A Roman jurist and agricultural writer</note>: if there are any flowers or fruits that are pleasant in the beauty of their shape and color, but do not match that beauty in taste or scent, and if you wish them to please no less by the delight of their flavor or scent than by the allure of their form. For we have already said how they may be*

...as they can be when they are mature; but now, indeed, we discuss what is done before they are sown. Seeds or grains should be steeped for three days in honey-water, ewe’s milk, or water in which you have dissolved sugar (which the ancients believed to be effective), then placed in the sun to dry before planting; the fruits will then take on a greater sweetness. If you seek more fragrant fruits: soak the seeds a little in spikenard oil, juice, or distilled rose water, with musk and what is called civet <term>zibetum; a pungent, musky secretion from the civet cat, highly prized in historical perfumery</term> dissolved in the water. Once they are slightly moistened, sow them after they have dried, and sweet-smelling fruits will arise, imbued with the wonderful fragrance of the scent the seeds drank in. But if you break the tips of the seeds, let them stay in the liquid for a shorter time. 

But if you wish to make wine or grapes fragrant, or a perfume-like variety that smells strongly, we find that Paxamus <note>Paxamus was an ancient Greek author whose agricultural advice was frequently collected in the *Geoponica*</note> recorded this: we split the vine-shoot that is to be buried and cast in everything fragrant, or the ointment whose scent we want the grape to have; let it be steeped a little in water of that same scent, and it will produce a fragrant grape once the moisture is absorbed. And thus is produced the scented flower, commonly called "Indian," distinguished by its form and color, and all those which are modified by this method <note>original: "hac labe polluuntur" (literally "are stained by this mark"); here the author refers to the artificial infusion of scent as a kind of "tainting" or successful modification</note>. 

The same result will be achieved if musk or another scent is placed inside each graft-shoot; it will yield fruits smelling of that same odor—often "muscatel pears" <term>moschatula pyra; pears characterized by a spicy, musk-like aroma</term> are seen to be produced in this way. If you wish to make that white rose, which they call "centifolia" <term>centifolia; literally "hundred-petaled," a type of cabbage rose known for its dense layers</term> due to its multitude of petals, very fragrant, graft it onto the rose they call "musk" because of its wonderful flow of musky scent. By repeating the grafting often, it will delight no less in its form than in its fragrance. If we also want a more fragrant lettuce: let the seed be sown together with the seed of a citron. If (as Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a Roman scholar and author of *De Re Rustica*</note> says) we hollow out laurel berries, place an artichoke <term>cinara; a plant of the thistle family, likely an artichoke or cardoon</term> seed inside, and plant it, the resulting growth will smell strongly of laurel...

...we make artichokes <term>cinaras; the cardoon or globe artichoke</term> redolent with scent and crowned with laurel. We have already given instructions for sweet and fragrant fruits: now it remains 
->To increase the size of all fruits.<-
Wherefore, if you wish them, and especially pomegranates <term>punica; literally "Punic apples," the Roman name for pomegranates</term>, to become larger: bury an earthenware jar full of water near the roots. In this jar, place the blossom together with its trailing branch by bending it inward so it is submerged; let it be tied tightly so it is not moved. Bury the jar in the earth and pack it down around the branch so that no air from the outside may blow in. In this way, once the fruits have matured and time has passed, the tree will overflow with fruit of marvelous size—larger than any that ever hung upon a tree—and with an exceeding thickness of skin. For the jar preserves and constrains the moisture which the sun and air usually steal from the fruit, and the rising vapors thicken and enlarge the fruit. We can also increase the seeds of **POMEGRANATES** in such a way that they appear marvelous for their excessive growth. Plant the pomegranate next to a cornel tree <term>cornum; the cornelian cherry, prized in antiquity for its hard wood and tart red fruit</term>, drill into the trunk of the cornel, and (as we have said before) insert the pomegranate shoot into the opening. When it has completed its third year of growth <term>trimatum; a period of three years' maturity</term>, you shall separate it from its own roots; then cut back the cornel near the graft so that it does not take the sap away from the foreign tree or divert it, nor serve any other purpose than to nourish the graft. In this way, it will provide fruit whose seeds will rival the fruit of the cornel and be so distinguished in flavor that they could not appear more beautiful. If you desire larger **CITRONS** <term>Citria; the citron, a large citrus fruit with a thick, fragrant rind</term>, shake down and prune away the "forest" of branches; spare only a few, so that because fewer are left behind, the fruits may grow more ample. If you wish to make a gourd <term>cucurbitam; a variety of squash or pumpkin</term> thicker: gather the seeds from the belly of the fruit and plant them with the tip inverted. If you want them thin: take them from the neck of the fruit. If wide: take them from the bottom. This is what Columella <note>Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella was a celebrated Roman writer whose works on agriculture remained the standard authority through the Renaissance</note> teaches in these verses:

> For if it is to your heart's liking 
> To have one longer, take from the slender head that hangs from the top,

# NATURAL MAGIC

And choose the seed from a slender neck: or if it is from a round body and a belly that swells to a vast size. You should choose from the middle of the belly, for that will produce a capacious offspring.

According to the teachings of the Quintilii <note>The Quintilii were two brothers, Sextus Quintilius Condianus and Sextus Quintilius Valerius Maximus, Roman authors of a lost agricultural work often cited in the 10th-century Byzantine "Geoponica."</note>, you may produce waterless <note>original: "inaquosos"; in this context, it likely refers to cucumbers that are firm and not overly watery, which was often preferred for flavor.</note> cucumbers in this way: When you have dug the pit in which you intend to plant the cucumbers, fill half of its depth with chaff or brushwood, and plant them in the earth heaped on top, but do not water them. 

In this way, celery <term>apium; likely wild celery or smallage</term>, rocket <term>eruca; also known as arugula</term>, leeks, and similar plants will grow to an excessive height of stalk and breadth of leaves, according to the opinion of Sotion <note>Sotion was a Greek writer on agriculture whose work is preserved in fragments.</note>, if the seeds are sown enclosed in individual pellets of goat dung. Or, we may wrap as much as we can grasp with three fingers in papyrus, and bury them in a shallow pit and manured earth. 

It will be no unpleasant sight to see **LETTUCE** grow planted with many seeds, if we pull away the leaves of the lettuce near the roots and place a seed—namely of basil <term>ocymum</term>, rocket, cress <term>nasturtium</term>, and the like—at each level, and bury them mixed with dung: for a lettuce stalk will spring up crowned by all of them.

You shall achieve the **SAME** result in this way: place the seeds of lettuce, basil, and such together into a hollowed-out bean <note>original: "faba"; likely referring to a large broad bean used as a capsule, or a bean-shaped pellet of dung.</note> of goat or sheep dung; then sink that bean, smeared with rich dung, into a somewhat deep pit. You shall fertilize it by throwing manure on top and watering it frequently: thus the lettuce and all the others spring forth together, each preserving its own flavor. Take care that they grow out, says Didymus <note>Didymus of Alexandria, an agricultural writer from the 4th or 5th century.</note>. If individual seeds are also enclosed in individual beans and sown wrapped in papyrus or a small linen cloth, they will provide the same result. 

And we shall make it low-growing <term>sessilem; literally "sitting," referring to a squat, broad lettuce that does not bolt or grow a tall, bitter stalk</term> according to the doctrine of Florentinus, if the root is exposed <term>ablaqueata; a Roman gardening term for digging around the base of a tree or plant to expose the roots to air or water</term>, and when it has grown a little, we heap cow dung around it and water it; and when it raises a stalk, we cut it off, and immediately place a stone, a potsherd, or a weight on top...

If we seek a more delicate flavor for it, Aristoxenus of Cyrene <note>A philosopher and musician; he was famously called "the Voluptuary" because of his dedication to luxury and fine dining.</note>—a philosopher of pleasure according to Athenæus <note>Referring to the Greek author of *The Deipnosophists*, a massive work on dining and ancient customs.</note>, who surpassed all in his desire for delicacies—teaches that since he indulged his palate more than others, he would water the lettuces growing in his garden in the evening with mead <term>mulsum; a mixture of wine and honey</term>, and satisfied them with plenty of that drink. 

**CELERY** <term>apium; in ancient texts, this can refer to celery or parsley</term>: if you prefer it to have curly leaves, its seed should be pounded in a mortar with a willow pestle, and once smoothed, it should be tied in a small linen cloth and buried. **THE SAME** result can be achieved if, no matter how it is sown, once it has sprouted, its growth is restrained by rolling a cylinder <note>Likely a stone or wooden garden roller used to flatten and compress the plants.</note> over it. 

**LEEKS**: if you prick the head with a rod or a sharp reed, and place a cucumber or turnip seed in the middle, the inserted seed unites with the leek and makes it swell up. In this way, they will turn out as

->Artichokes, so that they do not bristle with thorns,<-

# NATURAL MAGIC

Varied experience of things, and labor, and practice as a teacher, show new arts to those in need <note>This is an adaptation of a famous passage from Virgil’s *Georgics* (1.133), emphasizing that human ingenuity and hard work uncover the secrets of nature.</note>. And since the cause of these things remains hidden to most people, they cause great wonder to those who observe them, so much so that they are thought to have occurred beyond the normal laws of Nature. For if you fit earthenware vessels to growing fruits, they will fill the molds as they grow vigorously and take on whatever shape you wish; and if any ground pigments are heaped in the proper places within the mold, they will stain the fruits as if they were natural colors. Hence one often sees a human head in quinces <note>original: "chrysomelis"; literally "golden apples," often referring to quinces or apricots</note>, with white teeth, rosy cheeks, and dark eyes, so that, with all greenness removed, it imitates a human head. But the method of the African <note>Referring to Africanus, an ancient writer on agriculture whose works are preserved in the *Geoponica*</note> for making these is clear:

->Method:<-

If you desire to counterfeit a human head, a horse's head, or those of other animals: you will form a mold the size of the ripened fruit using clay or soft plaster. As it dries, split it with a sharp blade so that you may remove the original model <note>the "archetype" or pattern used to make the mold</note> and so that the pieces can later be joined back together properly. If the mask is made of wood, let it be hollowed out inside; if made of clay, once you have fired it and the fruits have grown strong, they should be enclosed in these earthenware molds (with an opening left for the stem to be let in). They should be bound with strong willow-twigs so that they do not burst open as the fruit grows. This should be done from their very infancy <note>meaning when the fruit has just begun to form</note>. When you have achieved its growth into that shape and it has reached its proper size, it will yield the figures and likenesses you chose to fashion. This works best with gourds, pears, quinces <term>strutheis; a large variety of quince</term>—as Democritus favored—citrons, pomegranates, and eggplants <term>malis insanis; literally "mad apples," a common Renaissance name for the eggplant</term>. I see it written from the decrees of the Quintilii <note>Two brothers, Condianus and Maximus, who were famous Roman agronomists</note>: If we hollow out the segments of a reed split lengthwise, and the newly sprouted fruit of a cucumber or a long gourd is enclosed inside, it will fill the reed with its vigorous growth and take on a long, rounded shape...

### Imprint features onto fruits:

If you have described marks with a stylus upon the skins of a **quince** <note>original: "cidonij mali"</note> or a **pomegranate** using baked plaster mixed with thin water, once they have grown to their proper thickness and you pick them, the pressed traces of the writing will stand out and become visible to us when rubbed. According to the <term>Africans</term> <note>Referring to Cassius Dionysius or other North African agricultural writers preserved in the Geoponica.</note>: this same thing will happen in all other fruits. ### How flowers and fruits may deviate into various colors.
### CHAPTER VI.
In the promiscuous union and metamorphosis of flowers and colors, *variegated* plants delight us with such pleasant hues that nothing more delightful meets our eyes. Now, that which glows with a deep purple passes into blue; now, those which are milky-white fade into yellow, green, or crimson, and delight us with a mingling of diverse colors. In the contemplation of these, the sharp edge of the mind is soothed by pleasant fruit, and, overwhelmed by the magnitude of things, wonders how it is made so much inferior to the excellence it perceives. Therefore, to achieve this, although the effect is one, the way is manifold: such as graftings and waterings, some of which were handed down by the ancients, and many others we have added, which were not previously devised. And first, let us teach by grafting how

### Roses and Jasmine may turn yellow:
Because the flower of the broom <note>original: "genista"</note> shines most yellow among the others, we seek to make the rose and jasmine emulate it. And since the association is not joinable by a small branch or leaf-grafting, we therefore plant the rose next to the broom, transferring it with its native soil—for they grow more freely in the bosom of a parent than a stepmother—and we bore into the plant. When we have cleaned the wound, we insert the rose, scraped on all sides, smear it round with mud, and bind it.

A Rose to otherwise become green, yellow, and blue.

But when the sun has crept forth, bring them back into the open and let them bask in the sun. You shall color the water not with harmful things, but with beneficial ones: for instance, if you want blue, let the blackberries of the bramble that creeps through the thickets be gathered—those you believe to be sufficiently mature and which stain the hands with a dark color. Let them be dried in the shade, and with them, stain the water. If you want yellow, use immature blackberries, and do the same with others: thus the water we irrigate with, staining and penetrating, ensures the flower is born tinted. If we desire them to become variegated with different colors, irrigate with different waters at different times likewise. So it is with any other liquid you might use for irrigation: for it is natural that the plant reflects whatever it has been soaked in. We can achieve the* **SAME** *result as we taught regarding fruits: it is necessary to cut around the buds of different plants, divide them, and graft them into the bark of another by splitting it, and then fit a prepared plaster so that it matches the peeled part. # SECOND BOOK

According to the readings of Anatolius <note>Anatolius of Berytus, a 4th-century writer on agriculture</note> and the elders: in the month of July, when they have fully finished flowering, gather ten or twelve small stalks tied together into bundles; let them be hung in smoke, and thus the stalks will yield bare little knots resembling bulbs. In the month of February, when the time for planting arrives, soak these same stalks in the dregs of dark wine; when they have taken on a purple color, plant them in small trenches, pouring more of the same dregs over each one. When they open, they will bloom saturated with a deep purple. We have already taught the method for dyeing flowers; now we shall speak concerning fruits. And first, as Florentinus <note>A Roman jurist and author of agricultural texts</note> teaches:

**That apples may turn out red through grafting:**
If we graft a shoot of a citron or a pear onto a red mulberry tree, the fruits produced from it will be red. THUS it is established that peaches <note>original: "Rhodacena," likely referring to a variety of peach or nectarine</note> of a remarkable blood-red color have resulted from grafting onto a mulberry. If, however, you desire them to be only slightly more reddish, learn from Democritus <note>The Greek philosopher, to whom many agricultural secrets were attributed</note>, who plants roses around the plants. THE SAME man teaches another way: A buried peach stone is uncovered of its own accord after seven days; let cinnabar <note>A bright red mineral pigment</note> be poured in, and let it be buried again, and you will have red peaches. And if you seek to color them otherwise, you may pour in another color: thus other things are seen. And if you wish to make mulberries themselves white, let them be grafted or budded onto a white poplar, and they will yield whitening fruits, as Beritius <note>Anatolius of Berytus</note> relates. THUS we turn the muscat grape black if we graft it upon a black vine; nor will the color please but a little. THUS Appian apples can be turned black if you graft them upon that apple tree which we call a "pear" because of its dark brown color <note>original: "fuscedine," suggesting a dark or dusky hue</note>. AND the apples themselves will begin to turn purple, says Beritius, if they are continually watered with urine. And we shall make pomegranates redder if the tree is saturated daily with water and lye <note>A solution of wood ashes</note>, as Diophanes <note>An agricultural writer who abridged the works of Mago the Carthaginian</note> left written.
