### How fruit may turn out without stones or pits.

#### CHAPTER III.

There is an old saying of the <term>Philosophers</term> <note>referring to ancient natural scientists</note>: especially those who handed down selected precepts of <term>agriculture</term>, that if you remove the pith from the grafting shoots or living roots with an <term>ear-pick</term> <note>original: "auriscalpio" - a small, delicate scooping tool</note> or a bone knife, the fruit will come forth "boneless" and without the woody shells of the pits. This is because the pith itself is the nourisher and parent of the bony substance. The <term>Arcadians</term> <note>ancient Greeks from the central Peloponnese, often cited in pastoral and agricultural lore</note>, however, oppose this opinion: for they say that every tree lives if the pith is removed to some extent; but if the pith is withdrawn entirely, it is necessary not only that the fruit will not bulge out pit-less, but that the tree will be widowed of life and dry up completely. They are supported by this reason: since that part is the most moist and most vital. For the nourishment, which is supplied from the soil, runs through it until it reaches all parts: for by a natural spirit, all nourishment of a living thing—like a kind of soul—is drawn through the pith of the trunk as if through a siphon. This is demonstrated by the fact that wood deprived of its pith bends and is reduced into a circle until it is completely dried out; and the ancients stubbornly dreaded this. To us, however, whom the decrees of <term>Theophrastus</term> <note>the "Father of Botany," a student of Aristotle</note> remind of truth and experience, it will be of great use, and brings no small amount of convenience.

<summary>This chapter discusses the ancient horticultural theory that removing the pith from a graft can produce seedless or pitless fruit, while weighing the risks of killing the tree in the process.</summary>

### *How to make it so, as Democritus <note>Democritus of Abdera (c. 460–370 BCE), a Greek philosopher often cited in early modern texts for his natural observations.</note> teaches,*
### *That the grape is empty of seeds:*

Take a vine-shoot, or a young branch intended for planting, and split it evenly down the middle. You shall wedge it open at the top with a small stone, and from there, using a bone stylus, scrape out all the pith <note>The soft, spongy tissue in the center of the stem.</note> from that part which will be hidden underground; or, hollow it out as deeply as possible. Immediately thereafter, bind it tightly with a flexible osier <note>A type of willow branch used for binding.</note> or with moist papyrus. Dig a single trench in damp, very rich soil and lay it down, tying it to a fixed stake so that it cannot be twisted. In this way, the structure is joined back together as it was before; it will be even more successful if you submerge a squill bulb <note>original: "scyllæ bulbum"; the sea onion, often used in ancient grafting to provide moisture and prevent rot.</note> inside the hollowed-out shoot. For, as if with a kind of glue, the bulb joins the parts with its moisture and warms them with its heat, acting as a sort of graft. This same result will also occur if all the pith is withdrawn when inserting a graft-cutting.

You may also achieve this if you wish a cherry tree to produce fruit without stones: cut back a still-tender trunk and remove the pith; bind the separated parts together and smear them with clay and dung until they grow back together, and you shall have it. And if it wearies you to wait for this through the long cycles of time, once the year-old bud has appeared, you may graft it onto other young shoots, or onto its own branches elsewhere. We are also able to produce

### *A Peach without a Stone*

By a new method of grafting: we plant a peach seedling near a willow in a well-watered, damp, and overflowing place; if it is not naturally so, assist it with irrigation so that the wood swells and offers its overflowing sap both to itself and to the guest-shoots. Let the willow be the thickness of an arm; let it be bored through the middle. Leaving the head of the peach tree intact, we shall prune away all its branches and lead it through the hole of the willow trunk; the hole...

-># NATURAL MAGIC<-

<margin>Grafting</margin>
We diligently cover the branch with clay and bind it tightly with ties. After a year has passed, once it has bonded so that one tree is formed from two, we cut back whatever protrudes above the drill-hole and the joint. This prevents the nutrients from being carried away to the old growth; instead, the sap is diverted to the union of the graft, and the tree—now burdened by a new stock—bends under the weight of its adopted fruits. Alternatively, by sinking the head of a willow into the earth, let it be bent into the likeness of an arch; once it has taken root, a peach is grafted onto it, transplanted, and the earth is heaped up around it. Thus the peach, wedded and married to the willow, will produce fruit of wonderful sweetness without stones <note>The author suggests that grafting a stone-fruit onto a willow, which has no stone, will result in pitless fruit—a common but mistaken belief in early modern "natural magic."</note>. The same can be done with plums, jujubes, truffles <note>original: "tuberibus"; likely referring here to a type of apple or fleshy fruit rather than the fungus</note>, and other orchard fruits. WE HAVE ANOTHER WAY from Africanus <note>Cassianus Bassus, often called "The African," a 6th-century writer on agriculture</note>: if we pierce the trunk of the tree at the bottom, driving in a wedge or a willow peg, the pith thus cut away will dry out. In a somewhat different manner, the same result occurs regarding:

### Gourd without seed
This will happen, as is drawn from the sayings of the Quintilii <note>Two brothers, influential Roman writers on farming</note>: if the shoot of gourds, melons, or cucumbers, when it has grown and lengthened, is layered in the earth like a vine-shoot and buried so that only the head stands upright and projects above. When it has grown up, do this again a second and third time, sprinkling water over it. When you know by this coaxing that it has struck all its roots and succeeded, cut the curved shoots that stand above the ground through the middle. The final stem will then give fruit without internal seeds, wrapped only in cartilage. They will also grow without seeds if you soak them in sesame oil for three days or a little more before you sow them. From these authors it is also taught how there might be born:

### A nut that is tender and without a shell,
As Africanus says: When the nut is already fully formed, break it on both sides...

-># SECOND BOOK.<-

<note>The author is describing how to produce walnuts with thin shells or "naked" kernels.</note> ...let them be gathered from the shell <note>original: "carina," literally the keel or hull of the nut</note>, so that the whole kernel is divided into four parts, and the woody membrane running through it is pulled away. With the flesh unharmed, wrap it in wool, papyrus, or vine leaves so that it is not gnawed by ants while exposed, and it will produce tender fruits. OR, in the place where you have destined to plant the walnut, dig a trench and place powdered earth within it, and plant the seed of a giant fennel <note>term: ferula (giant fennel), often used in antiquity as a conduit for planting delicate seeds</note>. When it has sprouted and grown, open it, and hide the naked nut kernel within its pith; and in this way, you will have the long-desired fruits for a long time.

IN THIS WAY we can also produce the Tarentine nut, which others call the "Mollusca" <note>meaning "soft-shelled"</note>. For they call it Tarentine when it has a soft and fragile shell, such that it is easily broken when handled with the hands. Water this frequently throughout the year with lye <note>original: "lixiuio," a solution of alkaline salts leached from ashes</note> and cast ashes upon the roots, as Damegeron <note>an ancient writer on agriculture and stones</note> has taught. Also, by boring deep into the tree itself, you will make hard and masculine nuts crumbly.

THE SAME applies to the hazelnut and the almond: before it produces a flower, with the roots laid bare <note>term: ablaqueatis (the practice of digging around the roots to expose them to air or water)</note>, warm water should be poured on for several days, and they will give tender nuts, as Africanus <note>likely Cassius Dionysius of Utica, who translated Mago the Carthaginian</note> says. It is done in the former manner so that they bear a naked kernel and a crumbly shell, so that it is covered not by a husk, but more truly by a skin so tender that it is eaten at the same time; and finally, this applies to all fruits covered by a shell.

We can turn the same method to the Myrtle, so that it produces berries without little stones. You should dig two palms' width in a circle around it, and frequently irrigate it with a shower of lukewarm water. Theophrastus <note>the Greek "Father of Botany"</note> relates that this was discovered by chance: for when a neglected myrtle grew next to a bathhouse, it gave fruit without a stone, and people sought cuttings from this plant; thus a variety of this kind began to exist in Athens. He also reported that the same happens with the spring apple. NOR should the saying of Africanus be passed over...

## ON NATURAL MAGIC

*I believe that to produce a pomegranate without seeds, it happens if you remove the rubbed-away pithy part, as is done with a Grapevine, and plant the split wood; after a time, if you prune the part of the plant that rises above, it will yield the desired fruit.* <note>The author is describing a method of grafting or propagation intended to create seedless varieties by removing the central "medulla" or pith.</note>

#### How fruits may become sweeter, more fragrant, and larger.

### CHAPTER IV.

**T**here are some trees which, through the splitting of the trunk or some other mutilation, perish suddenly due to the intrusion of air and foreign heat; for as the corruption descends to the lowest parts, they waste away and dry up immediately. There are, moreover, many trees which not only endure a wound, but even the splitting and boring <note>original: "terebrationem"</note> of the trunk, and from being poor bearers, they are rendered prolific. This is seen in the pomegranate, the almond, and the apple, which we use most frequently. For when wounded, they bring forth better and sweeter fruit; they take in as much nourishment as is sufficient, and from there they sweat out the harmful moisture (as one may often observe in animals), and they more easily digest the remainder, rendering the fruits sweeter and more beautiful to the sight. Since they live with a delicate structure, they easily compress and join together the split and scattered parts. From this, many experiments have arisen. Thus, so that

#### Almonds and citrons may become sweeter,

Although the bitter Almond is believed to be more healthful, it is nevertheless despised and rejected. But if you wish to make that same bitter tree sweeter, according to the African <note>likely referring to Cassius Dionysius of Utica or another North African agricultural writer</note>: with the root dug around four fingers deep on all sides, bore into the lowest part of the tree; thus the phlegm <note>original: "pituita," referring to the excess sap or watery humor of the plant</note> or the undigested moisture with which it swells will continually flow out, and it renders the fruit sweeter; the citron likewise will then bear ripe fruit. Thus we shall have an edible citron, even if it cannot be eaten due to its internal acidity. The root obliquely...

According to Anatolius <note>Vindanius Anatolius, a 4th-century writer on farming</note>, apples produce sweeter fruit if you constantly water the roots of the plant with urine, goat dung, and the dregs of old wine. # NATURAL MAGIC

->On the Scent and Flavor of Fruits<-

Plants can be influenced when they are fully grown, but also before they are even sown: *Seeds or grains should be soaked for three days in mead <note>original: "mulso" - a mixture of wine and honey</note>, sheep's milk, or water in which you have dissolved sugar <note>original: "sacharon"</note>—which the ancients believed to be effective—and then placed in the sun before planting; they will then acquire a greater sweetness of fruit. If you seek more fragrant results: soak the seeds in spikenard oil, juice, or distilled rose water, or in water in which musk and what is called civet <note>zibeto - a musk-like perfume obtained from the civet cat</note> have been dissolved. Once you have moistened them slightly, sow them after they have dried, and sweet-smelling fruits will arise, imbued with a wonderful fragrance of that scent which the seeds absorbed. However, if you break the tips of the seeds, let them remain there for only a little time.*

*But if you desire to make wine or grapes fragrant, or like a perfume-maker's oil that smells strongly—something we found that Paxamus <note>A Greek author on agriculture, likely 4th century AD</note> handed down: we split the vine-shoot that is to be buried and cast in every sweet-smelling thing or ointment that we wish the grape to smell of; let it be soaked for a little while in water of that same scent, and it will produce a grape smelling of the absorbed moisture. And if there is a fragrant flower, commonly called the "Indian flower" <note>likely a reference to a tropical import like the Canna or Tagetes</note>, notable for its form and color, and all those which are imbued with this quality, it will perform the same. If musk or something else is placed within each little shoot during grafting, it will yield fruits smelling of those same scents; often "muscatel" pears are seen produced this way. If you wish to make that rose which they call "hundred-leaved" <note>the Rosa centifolia</note> because of its multitude of petals, or a white rose, extremely fragrant: graft it onto the rose which they call "musk-rose" because of its wonderful flow of musky scent. By repeating the graftings often, it will delight no less in its form than in its scent. If we also seek a more fragrant lettuce: let the seed be sown together with a citron seed. If we hollow out laurel berries (says Varro <note>Marcus Terentius Varro, a Roman scholar and satirist</note>), and we hide the seed of an artichoke <note>original: "cinarae"</note> inside and plant it, it will smell greatly of laurel...*

### Increase the size of all fruits.

Wherefore, if you wish them—and especially pomegranates <note>original: "punica"</note>—to become larger: let an earthenware pot <note>original: "fictilis olla"</note> filled with water be buried near the roots. In this, let the flower, with its following branch, be placed by bending it in a connected coil; let it not be moved, but bound tightly. You shall cast the pot into the earth and pack it down around the branch so that no air from the outside may blow in. Thus, when the fruits are completed and the time has passed, it will abound with apples <note>original: "pomis"; used here as a general term for orchard fruit</note> of wonderful magnitude, larger than any that ever hung upon a tree, and with an exceeding thickness of the rind. For the pot preserves and restrains the moisture which the sun and air usually steal from it, and the rising vapors thicken and enlarge the fruit. We can also increase the seeds <note>original: "acinos"; specifically the juicy grains or arils of the pomegranate</note> of the **POMEGRANATE** so that they appear marvelous in their excessive growth. Plant a pomegranate next to a cornelian cherry tree <note>original: "cornum"</note>, and drill a hole in the trunk of the cornelian cherry; through that opening (as we have said before) insert the pomegranate branch. When it has completed three years of growth, you shall separate it from its own roots; then, cut back the cornelian cherry near the junction, lest the host tree take away the sap from the newcomer and be able to summon it away, or lest it serve any other branch more than the graft. For in this way, it will provide fruit whose seeds will rival the fruit of the cornelian cherry, and they will be so distinguished in flavor that they could not appear more beautiful. If you wish **CITRONS** <note>original: "CITRIA"; likely the large, thick-skinned citrus fruit used in ancient medicine and perfumery</note> to be larger, shake down and cut back the forest of branches; spare only a few, for the fewer that are left, the more ample the "apples" will come forth. If, however, you wish to make a **gourd** <note>original: "cucurbitam"</note> thicker: you should gather the seeds from the belly of the fruit and plant them with the tip inverted. If you want them slender: take from the neck. If broad: from the bottom. Columella <note>Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella, a Roman writer on agriculture from the 1st century AD</note> teaches this in these verses:

->For if it is your heart's desire<-
->That it be longer, it hangs from the slender top of the head,<-

According to the decrees of the Quintilii <note>The Quintilii were two brothers, influential Roman landowners and authors of agricultural texts in the 2nd century AD.</note>, you shall make cucumbers waterless in this way: When you have dug the trench in which you intend to plant the cucumbers, fill half of its depth with straw or brushwood, and having heaped earth over it, plant them, and do not irrigate. In this way, celery, rocket, leeks, and similar plants grow to an excessive height of stalk and breadth of leaf, according to the opinion of Sestion, if the seeds are sown enclosed within individual berries of goat dung: Or, as much as we can grasp with three fingers, let us wrap in papyrus, and bury them in a shallow trench with manured earth. Nor will it be unpleasant to see

*Lettuce born, planted with many seeds*
It will be so if we pluck the leaves of the lettuce which are near the roots, and hide a seed in each step <note>likely referring to the leaf-axils or notches</note>, namely of basil, rocket, nasturtium, and the like, and bury them mixed with dung: for a lettuce stalk crowned by all of them will be born. YOU MAY EFFECT THE SAME in this way: Having emptied a bean of goat or sheep dung <note>likely referring to a pellet of dung</note>, hide the seeds of lettuce, basil, and such things together, and having smeared that bean with rich dung, sink it into a somewhat deep trench, and having cast manure over it, you shall make it fruitful with frequent irrigation: thus the lettuce and all the others leap forth at once, with the flavor of each preserved: take care that they grow tall, says Didymus <note>Didymus of Alexandria, a 4th-century agricultural writer</note>. If individual seeds are also enclosed in individual beans, and sown wrapped in papyrus or a small linen cloth, they will provide the same result. And we shall make it "squat" <note>sessilem: a variety of lettuce that grows low and broad rather than tall</note> according to the teaching of Florentinus <note>A 3rd-century Roman agricultural writer</note>, if, having laid bare the root when it has grown somewhat, we heap ox dung around it and water it, and when it raises its stalk, we cut it off, and soon fit a stone, a potsherd, or a weight over it—

## NATURAL MAGIC
*Diverse experience of things, and labor,*

<note>original: "Ipfa nouas artes, Varia experientia rerum, Et labor oftendis miferis, Vfusque magister." A poetic sentiment on how trial and error lead to discovery.</note>
And since the cause remains hidden to most people, these things strike observers as great miracles, so much so that they are thought to have occurred outside the laws of Nature. For if you fit earthenware vessels <note>original: "testacea Vasa"</note> over growing fruits, as they grow vigorously they fill the molds and take on whatever shape you desire; and if certain colors are painted and layered in the proper places, they will dye the fruits as if they were natural. Thus, one often sees a human head in quinces <note>original: "chrysomelis," literally 'gold-apples'</note>, with white teeth, reddening cheeks, darkening eyes, and with all greenness cast aside, it imitates a human head. But the method of Africanus <note>likely referring to Julius Africanus, a 3rd-century writer on agriculture and magic</note> for making these is revealed here:

### The Method:
If you desire to counterfeit a human head, a horse’s head, or those of other animals: you shall form a mold out of clay or soft, drying plaster <note>original: "gypso"</note> the size of the fully grown fruit, and split it with the edge of a blade so that you may remove the original model and the pieces can be joined back together properly. If the mask is made of wood, let it be hollowed out inside; if of clay, once you have fired it and the fruits are in a stage of vigorous growth, let them be enclosed in the earthenware molds (with an opening left where the stem may enter). Let them be bound with strong wicker so they are not forced open by the fruit’s growth. If you do this from their very infancy <note>meaning the earliest stage of the fruit's development</note> and allow them to grow until they have reached the proper size of the fruit, it will yield whatever figures and likenesses you chose to fashion. This works best with gourds, pears, and citrons—as was pleasing to Democritus <note>an ancient Greek philosopher often associated with early science and alchemy</note>—as well as pomegranates and eggplants <note>original: "malis insanis," literally 'mad apples'</note>. I see it written according to the opinions of the Quintilii <note>referring to the brothers who authored the 'Georgica,' a Roman work on farming</note>: If we take a reed split lengthwise and hollow out the space between the joints, and the newly born offspring of a cucumber or a long gourd are enclosed within it, it will fill the reed with its relentless growth, taking on a long and rounded fig-

### To imprint designs upon apples:

### How an almond may grow inscribed:
Soak an almond for two or three days, or the pit of a peach <note>Latin: *rhodaceni osiculum*</note>, as Democritus has committed to his records; then lightly break the shell so the kernel is not harmed, and write whatever you please somewhat deeply upon it. Then let it be closed with papyrus, or wrapped in a linen cloth, and smeared around with clay and fertilized with dung; when grown, it will yield inscribed fruits. How flowers and fruits may deviate into different colors.

### CHAPTER VI.
In the promiscuous mingling and metamorphosis of flowers and colors, they delight us with such pleasant, variegated hues that nothing more delightful meets our eyes. Now, that which glows with a deep purple <note>original: "purpura"</note> passes into blue; now, those which are milky-white fade into yellow. Green, Phoenician red <note>original: "phoeniceum," a deep scarlet or purple-red</note>, and the mixture of diverse colors delight us; in the contemplation of these, the sharp edge of the mind is softened by pleasant fruit, and, overwhelmed by the greatness of things, it marvels that it is rendered much inferior to the excellence it perceives. Therefore, to achieve this, although there is one effect, there are many ways: such as grafting <term>institiones: the process of joining a shoot or bud to a different plant</term> and irrigation, some of which were handed down by the ancients, and many others we have added that were not previously devised. And first, through grafting, let us teach how

Roses and Jasmine may turn yellow:
Because the flower of the broom <note>original: "genista"</note> shines most yellow among the others, we seek to make the rose and jasmine emulate it. Since the association is not joinable by a shoot or by leaf-grafting, we therefore plant the rose next to the broom, transferring it with its native soil—for they grow more willingly in the bosom of a parent than a stepmother—and we bore into the plant. When we have cleaned the wound, we insert the rose, which has been scraped all around, smear it with mud, and bind it.

As soon as the graft has been secured by the strength of the growing trunk, we shall separate it from the root and cut back the trunk above the graft. In this way, the rose will bloom with a charm that is not unpleasing; thus the jasmine shines among us with such an elegant and bright color that it dazzles the sight of the eyes. Thus it delights on all sides with whatever diverse colors it may be adopted into. In almost the same way,

### The flower of the Crowned Veronica may turn into blue.

Many call this the "clove-pink" <note>original: *garyophyllum*; likely referring to the carnation or dianthus</note> because of the pleasantness of its clove-like scent. Whether this so famous flower—which is not inferior to the rose in scent, color, or beauty—was known to the ancients or remained shrouded in silence, seems inconsistent to determine. And because, following the aforementioned example, the same thing might seem difficult to some, you will achieve your goal in this way: we cut the *Chicory* <note>original: *Intubum*</note>, or the *Cornflower* <note>original: *cyanum*</note>, but better the *wild chicory*, an old, thick one exceeding a thumb's width, down to the root. We split it through the middle, as if in an eccentric graft, and we insert the shoot of the flower plucked with its root. We bind it with a willow withe and heap very crumbly earth around it. Thus it will produce a flower that will entice with a cornflower-blue color, than which nothing more pleasing will be seen by the eyes.

In the same way, if you insert some white flower into the root of *Alkanet* <note>original: *Anchusa*; a plant known for its red dye</note>, you will have a *purple* flower, turning from there into *crimson*. The same flower, if it is held over various sulfur fumigations, will appear tinged with diverse colors. Now let us examine whether it can be done

### That a rose may otherwise become green, yellow, and blue.

We shall accomplish it thus: we split the stem of the flower, such as a rose, near the roots and all the branches, and we stuff them abundantly with color: if green with verdigris <note>original: *arugine*; a green pigment formed on copper</note>, if blue with indigo or the stone so named <note>likely lapis lazuli</note>, if yellow with saffron, first in a drink... <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "linem" suggests the next page continues with "liniment" or "smearing."</note>

reduced to a fine powder: however, take great care that you do not mix in any orpiment <note>arsenic trisulfide, a yellow pigment</note> or any other substance of that kind: for such a potency kills the plant; soon the fertilizers, both by binding and by color, will stain the flower, carrying the nourishment along with it. We can **OTHERWISE** achieve the same effect of making them multi-colored <note>original: "Versicolorem"</note> through watering: if you plant the flowers in pots or earthenware vessels with fertile, sifted earth, and water them twice a day with colored water—using the color you desire for the flower—keep them in sheltered places at night, safe from the cold: but when the Sun has begun to creep upward, bring them back into the open and allow them to bask in the sun. You shall color the water not with harmful substances, but with beneficial ones: for instance, if you want a blue color, let the berries of the Bramble <note>original: "Rubi mora," likely blackberries</note> which creep through the thickets be gathered—those you believe to be sufficiently ripe and which stain the hands with a dark color—let them be dried in the shade, and stain the water with them. If you want yellow, let the berries be unripe, and do the same with others: that which we water thus dyes and penetrates, so that the flower is born already tinted. If we desire them to become variegated with different colors, water them with different waters at different and equal intervals of time: likewise, water them with any other liquid you choose: for it is natural that the flower should reflect whatever it has been soaked in. **AND THE SAME** we can achieve, as we have taught regarding fruits: it is necessary to cut around the buds of different plants, divide them, and graft them into the bark of another by splitting it, and then fit a prepared plaster so that it matches the peeled part. # SECOND BOOK.
<page-num>42</page-num>

According to the readings of Anatolius <note>Anatolius of Beirut, a 4th-century writer on agriculture</note> and the elders: In the month of July, when they have fully finished flowering, gather ten or twelve small stalks together into bundles like brooms. Let them be hung in smoke; in this way, the stalks will release bare little knots resembling bulbs. In the month of February, when the time for planting arrives, soak these same stalks in the dregs of dark wine. When they have taken on a purple color, plant them in small pits, pouring a generous amount of those same dregs into each one. When they bloom, they will open saturated with purple flowers. We have already taught the method for staining flowers. Now we shall speak concerning fruits. And first, as Florentinus <note>A 3rd-century Roman agricultural author</note> teaches:

### How to make fruit turn red by grafting:
If we graft a shoot of a citron tree or a pear tree into a mulberry tree, the fruits produced from it will be red. **THUS** it is well known that blood-red peaches <note>original: "Rhodacena"</note> of remarkable color have resulted from grafting onto a mulberry. If you desire them to be a little more reddish than before, learn from Democritus <note>The Greek "laughing philosopher," often associated with early natural magic and alchemy</note>, who plants roses around the plants. **THE SAME AUTHOR** teaches another way: After seven days, the buried pit is uncovered of its own accord; let cinnabar <note>A bright red mineral (mercury sulfide) used as a pigment</note> be poured in, and let it be buried again, and you will have red peaches. And if you seek to color them otherwise, you may pour in another color: thus it is with other things that are seen.

And if you wish to make the mulberries themselves white, let them be grafted or budded onto a white poplar, and they will yield whitening fruit, as Beritius <note>likely referring to Anatolius of Beirut</note> relates. **THUS** we turn a muscat grape black if we graft it upon a black vine; the color will provide no small delight. **THUS** Appian apples can be turned black if you graft them upon that apple tree which we call a "pear-apple" because of its dark brown color. **AND** the apples themselves will begin to turn purple, says Beritius, if they are continually watered with urine. And we shall make pomegranates redder if the tree is saturated daily with water and lye <note>term: lye (an alkaline solution made from wood ashes)</note>, as Diophanes has left in his writings.

White seeds of the **Pomegranate** <note>original: "PUNICI"</note> are produced in this way: you shall mix clay <note>original: "argilla"</note> and chalk <note>original: "creta"</note> with a fourth part of gypsum, and apply this to the roots, maintaining it for three years. We can also produce the same result by another artifice, though it is quite troublesome; yet I shall explain it, as *perhaps it may be useful to have read*: they tie the branches laden with apples to a stake fixed near the roots, and place vessels full of water nearby, moving them here and there so that when the hot midday ray of the sun strikes the water, it is reflected, and leaping back onto the apples with a warm vapor, *it procures a purple color*.

Various fruits, and medicated wines.

## CHAPTER VII.

The ancients strove with the greatest zeal and diligence to find by what means they might preserve wine with various antidotes, and adapt it for use if necessary; nor was this done without reason, as nothing is more excellent for convenience. This is shown by the fact that the greater part of them recorded much for posterity—more curiously than was perhaps necessary—things wonderful to tell and easy to do. Theophrastus <note>A Greek philosopher and successor to Aristotle, known as the father of botany</note> mentions these: in Heraclea of Arcadia, a wine is made which, when drunk, drives men mad and renders women infertile. Athenaeus <note>A Greek rhetorician and grammarian</note> confirms a similar wine exists in the territory of Thrace. And at Thasos, a wine is made that induces sleep. Another is prepared by a certain art that makes the drinker more vigilant. There are various seasonings for wines which you will find among diligent writers on medicine and agriculture; these are not difficult to know, nor do I believe they will be arduous for those who know the powers of simples <note>Medicinal herbs or plants used as a single ingredient</note> and can reach conclusions by conjecture. Finally, they produce those effects which arise from the property of the location, and I think it will be most useful that they be applied—

### The Theriac Vine:
<note>Theriac refers to a "universal antidote" or a medicine used against poisons and venom.</note>

A vine cutting must be planted, as Florentinus <note>A 3rd-century author on agriculture</note> teaches in the first and second books of his *Georgics* <note>original: "Georgicorum"; a work on farming</note>. It should be split at the bottom of the root to a width of four fingers; once the pith is removed, Hellebore <note>original: "Veratrum"; a powerful herbal purgative</note> should be inserted in its place. It should then be bound tightly with a willow twig and committed to the earth. In this way, it will produce grapes whose eating softens and evacuates the bowels. OR, if you wish the power to evacuate to be more effective, the same cuttings may be saturated with an antidote by inserting them into a Squill bulb <note>original: "scyllæ bulbo"; a sea onion used in medicine</note> buried in the earth, while constantly pouring the medicine over it with frequent soaking, so that the power is absorbed by the sap and does not fade so quickly. IF, however, you wish for medicinal drinks to grow spontaneously, as Palladius <note>A 4th-century Roman agricultural writer</note> teaches, you should place the vine cuttings in a vessel half-filled with potions—either seasoned with wormwood, or roses, or violets. Then, dissolve the earth into a slurry like lye until the buds of the cuttings burst into new foliage; then plant those cuttings in any location like other vines, and they will yield such fruits. A Phborian wine <note>original: "VINVM Phborium"; likely a corruption of "Phthorium," meaning "destructive" or "abortive"</note>—that is, one which kills the fetus and causes abortion—is made thus: Scammony <note>a purgative resin</note> or Black Hellebore is grafted into the vine. Bore a hole into the vine with an auger, insert it into whichever branches you wish, and it will be done. OR, let wild cucumber be sown around the vine itself, from which it draws and drinks in the potency. Or if we macerate Hellebore in the wine, it will provide the same effect; and thus the vine adopts the powers of various mixtures. And

### Purgative and Sleep-Inducing Plums.
If you desire to bring about sleep and a clearing of the bowels, this method is most excellent: bore a hole into a branch or the entire plant, then fill it with scammony <note>a resin from the root of Convolvulus scammonia, used as a strong laxative</note> or opium. Leave it properly wrapped in papyrus or bark; when the fruits have ripened, they will cause the emptying of the belly and induce sleep. **OR**, if you desire the same effect more quickly, take dried figs—which the Greeks call *Ischiades* <note>original: "Ifobiadas" - likely a transcription error for Ischiades, a type of dried fig</note>—and raisins, and soak them for a day in wine or water until they swell. First, dissolve the scammony or opium in the liquid, and if they taste somewhat bitter, mix in something sweet and use them after they have been dried in the sun. But enough regarding medicated wines for now.

### The Preservation of Flowers and Fruits.

## CHAPTER VIII.
So great is the inconstant power of the stars' heat <note>original: "siderei caloris"</note> that all things surrounded by it and contained within the sublunary world <note>the realm below the moon, subject to change and decay</note> tend toward their end and never cease from motion. Yet the minds of men are not so darkened, nor their senses so failed, that many things cannot escape almost unharmed. And if we have beautified a fruit with a pleasing shape, a delightful flavor, and the allure of a distinguished color, lest their glory perish so quickly and the wicked motion of the shifting sky cause them to waste away and die, you may render them lasting against the opposing force of cold or heat. You shall not be without honor if, to the best of your ability, you protect them from the injuries of the surrounding and pervasive air, which by its warm affinity consumes what it finds in the fruit...

The location of the site also contributes greatly to this. Therefore, direct your open windows toward the North; for those which turn toward the South must be closed. Indeed, by the breath of the South Wind <note>The Latin 'Auster' refers to the hot, humid south wind, believed by ancient naturalists to promote decay.</note> fruits are disfigured with wrinkles—yet they should not be without small shutters, lest when they have lost their moisture, they become overcome by a stubborn wind, or rot with the stain of decay. ### Roses and Lilies may be kept fresh:

Let roses and other flowers be gathered, not when they are already tasteless <note>original: "fatui," referring to flowers that have lost their scent and vigor.</note> and enduring in a withered state; but when the flower is just opening, dip the stem that is about to fall into liquid pitch, and let them be stored in a split reed, properly sealed with pitch, and buried in a sloping place in the open air.

immerse it, showing it to the Sun. Now let us hand down the method for how

### Fruits may remain in their vigor for a long time.

Early-ripening fruits, pears, apples, quinces—*but must-apples <note>A variety of sweet, juicy apple that ripens early</note> are to be rejected*—figs, truffles, and jujubes <note>original: "ziziphacum"; a small reddish fruit similar to a date</note> should all be gathered with their leaves while immature, yet not entirely raw; those that have fallen must be set aside. *By inspecting them diligently on all sides, ensure they are whole and not bruised by hands; take care to sear their cut stems with hot pitch, touching them as little as possible, for they would easily show the beginning of rot. Then, by wrapping them in hemp or tow <note>coarse, broken fibers of flax or hemp</note>, smear them all over with boiling wax; once smeared, let them be submerged in honey. Ensure they are all submerged and keep them stored separately; do not mix them together, lest they touch one another, for one is corrupted by the other. With a lid placed upon it, seal the vessel with a skin <note>original: "Vas pellicolato"; likely a bladder or parchment cover</note>, and you will always have them green throughout the year; and in this way every fruit can be preserved in honey, which is stored for old age.*

*BUT Africanus <note>likely referring to Cassius Dionysius or a similar agricultural writer cited in the Geoponica</note> teaches how to preserve green figs: From green gourds, remove parts from the sides that look like surgical compresses <note>original: "splenijs"; linen strips used in medicine</note>, and hollow them out like little compartments or sheaths. Then, in each one, place individual figs with their stalks, so that they are brought forth whole and more durable—since they last longer when whole—then stop up the opening and hang them in a shaded place, lest fire or smoke breathe upon them.*

*OR, according to the opinion of Sotion <note>An ancient Greek writer on agriculture</note>, apples gathered in their vigor are preserved if you smear them with worked gypsum, or if you encrust each one with potters' clay. When they have hardened, you will keep them unharmed for a long time; and when you wish to use them, wash them thoroughly with sweet water.*

*You shall also have GRAPES that do not wither in this way: if you place individual small earthenware vessels hanging over them, closed up, but perforated at the bottom; and by placing a piece of turf on top, you will ward off the rain, protecting them so that neither...*

## For fruit to remain long on the tree:
Order the small branches to be twisted, so that the ripening moisture does not flow into the fruit itself, and they will be preserved unharmed against the charms of the summer heat; in this way especially are pomegranates preserved, lest they gape open and scatter. Otherwise, we preserve

### Service-berries and pears.
Store those that are a little more pleasantly ripe, but still firm and not fallen, in earthen vessels with lids, which you should have both pitched and plastered with gypsum; bury them in a trench dug two feet deep with the mouth turned downward, with earth drawn over them and trodden down firmly with the feet, in a sloping place through which water flows year-round. But

#### Grapes and pomegranates
if you wish to preserve them: Let the grapes be of the hard-skinned variety, and order them to be gathered unblemished, neither hard with sourness nor dropping off with over-ripeness, but soft to the touch with a pleasant toughness; and if there are any rotten parts, they ought to be cut away. Then, dip them into boiling water for a very short time so they are not cooked—for a certain moderation is needed—then, having been removed, let them be hung in the shade. Nor should this be overlooked, which Columella <note>Lucius Junius Moderatus Columella, a famous Roman writer on agriculture from the 1st century AD</note> observed, so that every grape can be preserved: When the Moon is waning, after the fourth hour, when it has already been warmed by the sun and is no longer dewy, let it be pulled from the vine and stored. Dig a hole to the depth of a standing man, and spread sand over the floor so that it may better preserve the contents through dryness; above this, fix reeds and poles in the ground. By loosening the twigs, carefully twist them so the clusters of grapes hang attached and unharmed from the stakes, ensuring they do not touch the floor. Cover the structure with a roof so that rain cannot penetrate, and they will be preserved until Spring, remaining enclosed for the entire year.
Bringing these matters to an end, there remains:

## To Kill Trees

If we wish, we may do so in this manner: let us strip the bark in a circle, for every tree stripped of its bark perishes, though some more slowly and others more quickly. For instance, the linden and the hard oak die more rapidly, while weaker trees perish more slowly. It also matters at what time of year this is done: in March and February they die immediately, but in winter, the death of robust trees takes longer. However, one ought to strip the bark from the cork tree <note>Latin: subero</note> lest it grow worse. Likewise, the sting of a stingray <term>Pastinaca marina; the venomous spine of a ray, believed in antiquity to have caustic properties</term> fixed into the trunk causes it to fall. Also, a cloth stained with menstrual blood applied to the roots—especially of the walnut tree—works, as Democritus <note>A Greek philosopher often cited in early scientific texts for his observations on nature</note> suggests. Furthermore, a plane tree that had been chipped all around, which occurred in Antrandus and Philippi, was regarded as a portent if it revived and the bark wove itself back together. For a soothsayer <term>Auruspex; a diviner who interprets omens</term> then persuaded them to offer sacrifices and guard the tree as a sign of good fortune. 

Now it remains to describe a certain property of the Mullein plant <note>Latin: verbasci</note>: in the morning when it opens its flowers, if the plant is lightly shaken, the flowers gradually wither and all fall to the ground. No one who sees this would fail to believe it happens through magical incantations, especially if the person shaking it murmurs some empty words. From all that we have gathered and told so far, we have known of one tree, often called the "Garden of Delights" tree for the sake of a joke; for it was thick...

<meta>This page continues the second book of the treatise, moving from general botanical observations to a description of a singular, grafted "wonder-tree" and the origins of fire.</meta>

The tree was of a thickness and height that was not unpleasing, planted in a suitable vessel with very rich, stiff, and over-fertile soil, so that it might supply nourishment both by the vigor of the plant and the abundance and fruitfulness of the earth to the grafted parts. It was three-forked: on one branch it bore a grape <note>original: "Vinaceis"</note> empty of seeds, variegated in color, and medicinal, of which one part induced sleep and the other a purging of the bowels. The second branch brought forth a peach, distinguished at equal intervals between the peach and the nectarine <note>original: "nuciperſico"</note>; it was stoneless, and the small branch protruded now a peach, now a nectarine. If any fruit did possess a pit, it gave one as sweet as an almond, and it would falsely take on the appearance now of humans, now of animals, showing diverse features. The other branch gave stoneless cherries, both sour and sweet. It also gave golden apples <note>likely oranges or lemons, often called "mala aurea" in Renaissance botany</note>, the rind of which was composed of flowers and roses; the fruits exceeded all due size, being sweeter and more fragrant, blossoming in the spring time and producing fruit beyond the expected season. They lingered longer on the branch, providing a perpetual circle of continuous fertility throughout the year; the fruits succeeded one another by certain steps, the bearing was intermittent, the branches lay prostrate, bent down by the weights, and finally the heavens were so favorable that I have never beheld anything more beautiful. Enough has now been said of this matter: for we have wandered further than was fitting. Some of these things we have excerpted from the monuments of our ancestors, adapting them to the time and region, and we have augmented those things known to many by experience with many ingenious and most useful additions.

### NATURAL MAGIC

*When branches rub together with strong friction, their parts colliding and becoming thinned by heat, they draw out warmth and stir up fire. From this, a great flame followed; the wild and rustic men of that time, terrified by it, turned their backs in flight. Yet, once the flame had subsided and they approached more closely, they weighed the great convenience that came from it for human bodies and began to preserve the fire. Thus, fire provided the reasons for urbanity <note>The author suggests that the communal hearth was the foundation of civilized society and social gathering</note>, social custom, and speech. From there, the necessity of shepherds in the wilderness and soldiers in camps explored how various fire-making tools might be prepared when the opportunity to strike flint for hidden fire is not always given. They taught which woods were suited for this use. And although it sometimes happens that both the drill <note>The rotating stick used to generate friction</note> and the receptacle <note>The base board or hearth-piece</note> are made from the same wood, they nevertheless judge that one should be made from a wood that acts and retains the power of effecting, and the other from one that is passive—one soft, the other strong. For example:*

*The woods which catch fire by friction are those that are especially "hot" in nature, such as laurel, buckthorn, holm-oak, and linden. Menestor <note>A Greek botanical writer from the 5th century BCE</note> truly adds the mulberry, and they conclude this because these woods immediately blunt and dull adzes <note>A type of cutting tool</note>. From all of these they make the drill, so that it may resist more sharply during friction and accomplish the work more stubbornly; the receptacle, however, is made from soft wood: such as ivy, giant fennel, wild vine, and similar plants when dried and entirely emptied of all moisture. To sum up, those woods that grow up in shady and covered places are worse for fire-making use and are rejected. And thus you will accomplish it more conveniently: nor do I think it matters if you rub laurel against laurel, or stripped ivy or fennel against fennel, and (what is more excellent) by moving a rope over it quickly and vehemently; and when the smoke rises, by adding—*

But since the human mind rarely rests content with the same discoveries, but investigates a path more deeply to find new things, by its industry there was discovered:

->**A stone that excites fire by any moisture**<-

The method for making such a thing is as follows: Cast a lodestone <note>original: *Magnetem lapidem*</note> into a pot or other similar vessel, immersed in quicklime <note>original: *viua calce*</note>; it will be better if you add colophony <note>a type of pine resin</note> in equal measure with the lime. When you have filled the vessel, smear the vent-hole with potter's clay and place it in a furnace until it is thoroughly cooked. Then let it be removed and cast into a new pot; smeared again with clay, let it be placed in the furnace, following the same steps alternately until it becomes intensely white and parched. When needed, water or saliva poured over it will raise a flame; when the flame is extinguished, store it in a warm place for future use. There is also:

### Another method of making it:

If an equal weight of "living" sulfur <note>native sulfur</note> and purified saltpeter <note>original: *salnitri*</note>, and double the amount of camphor <note>original: *caphuræ*</note> be added to new lime, and all are ground in a mortar until they are so fine that they might fly into the ears <note>an idiom for a very fine powder</note>, let them be wrapped together tightly bound in a linen cloth. Closed in an earthenware vessel and smeared over with clay, let it be dried in the boiling sun, then placed in a potter's furnace. When the earthenware vessel has been fired—in which the greatest care must be used—the contents unite and are reduced into the hardness of a stone. Once extracted, they serve for use. Thus:

# BOOK TWO

## BOOK TWO

The "fire-powder" <note>original: "pyrius," referring to gunpowder</note> is prepared (as they say) for engines of war, which are given the name "bombards" from their burning, booming sound <note>original: "bombo," an onomatopoeic term for the deep roar of a cannon</note>. This powder enters into all these mixtures as the base and foundation. To this should be added a third part of colophony <note>a brittle, yellow resin derived from pine trees</note>, a fourth part of common olive oil, and a sixth part of sulfur. Let them be mixed thoroughly according to the rite. Once all the ingredients have been dried, they perform a test: if it burns more violently than desired, add more colophony and sulfur; if it burns too weakly, suggest a little more of the aforementioned powder. 

This mixture should be wrapped in straw and linen cloths, or in small pouches made from them, as tightly as possible. Then, once wrapped with cords and bindings, they immerse it in boiling pitch and see that it is dried. Protecting it again with straw, they smear it with pitch so that it is shielded from the moisture of water and does not allow the force of the fire to burst it prematurely. When these have been properly dried in the sun, through a small hole

Once these are sealed in a glass vessel, bury it under manure for two or three months, always renewing the manure and remixing it every other day. From this composition, if you apply fire, it does not cease to burn until it is entirely consumed; for it is not extinguished by the sprinkling of water—on the contrary, it is ignited by it. However, it is smothered by mud, earth, dust, and generally by any dry materials. If you hurl this at a helmet, a shield, or at armed men, it causes them to glow white-hot with fire, so that they are forced to be burned or to strip off their armor. 

We hand down another mixture which is of <term>stronger</term> operation: equal portions of turpentine resin, liquid pitch, and varnish, then of pitch, frankincense, and camphor; one and one-third parts of live sulfur <note>native or unrefined sulfur</note>, double the amount of purified saltpeter, triple the amount of "burning water," and just as much naphtha oil; but you should add a little dust of willow charcoal. Let these be prepared together and formed into small balls, or fill small jars. It burns in such a way that it is useless to try to extinguish it. Another is found among these:

When the dung was already rotting, heated, and emitting a vapor in the middle of summer, and the sun fell upon it with full force, it ignited the resin and the window. From there, other doors also smeared with resin began to catch fire and sent the flames up to the roof. Once the flame had reached the roof, it quickly raged through the whole house, as it possesses the greatest power for setting things on fire.

This effect comes from sulfur: for it is extinguished with difficulty once it conceives a flame. For leading armies, however, or for other necessary matters, they use the following:
They boil the wick in saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> and water, and once dried, they moisten it with sulfur and "burning water" <note>aqua ardens, or high-proof alcohol</note>. Then they make candles from this mixture: it consists of sulfur, then camphor, and a half-portion of turpentine resin, double the amount of colophony <note>pine resin</note>, and triple the amount of wax. They make four candles and join them together, throwing quick-sulfur <note>native or "live" sulfur</note> into the middle; this resists all things most stubbornly. If you cover a candle with snow, as boys are accustomed to do, and light it, the flame will be seen to burn in the snow. But so that it may be easily ignited, use:

This burns wonderfully, nor will it cease to burn until it is entirely consumed or very little remains. **Gunpowder** <note>original: "Puluis pyri," literally "pear-dust," a common early term for explosive powder</note> performs wonderful works in instruments of war:
If one adds to common powder a twelfth part of **quicksilver** <term>mercury</term>, an equal amount of **marcasite** <term>iron pyrite or similar metallic mineral</term>, and **colophony** <term>pine resin</term>, and if he pounds and mixes them correctly—if he fills war machines with this powder, when he applies fire, the engine will shatter with a massive crash, so as to kill many of those standing nearby.
ON THE OTHER HAND, if burnt paper is mixed into this gunpowder, or—what fulfills the same purpose—if double the amount of common **hay seed** is mixed in after being very finely ground, it holds back that most powerful force; it will emit neither a flame nor so powerful a noise. With such a powder, an ingenious man will devise wonderful experiments.
How a liquid might be made that shines in the dark.

#### CHAPTER XI.

You who approach with foresight the reading of those things which Nature, both bountiful and lavish, generously lends for human use—those things hidden and lurking—and when she cares to make the darkness harmless (which is certainly not to be despised), seek ingeniously from those things which are able to provide it. For you have many things which strike and stir the senses in the night; **Aristotle** demonstrates many, and experience demonstrates many more everywhere:
Such as the little beast of the insect kind, which the Greeks call *nyglampidas* <note>original: "νυγλαμπιδας," likely a variation of lampyris</note> and the Latins call *nitedulae* or *cicindelae* <term>fireflies or glow-worms</term>. There are **mushrooms**, and the heads and scales of **fish**, such as the more recently discovered *trichia*, commonly called **herrings**. So too the **sea-swallow** <note>original: "miluus" or "miluago," referring here to a bioluminescent fish rather than the bird</note> is called a lantern because its eyes shine at night. The shells of **mussels** <note>original: "Petlinum vngues," likely referring to Pholas dactylus or piddocks</note> shine with fire in the dark, even in the mouths of those eating them. So also the eyes of **wolves** and **cats** <note>original: "alurorum," likely a corruption of ailurorum</note>. And there is...

In the forests of Germany, specifically in the Hercynian Forest <note>The ancient name for the vast, mountainous forest region of central Europe</note>, there appears a Bird whose feathers shine like fires; by their splendor, travelers are guided through the uncertain outcomes of their journeys across those countless solitudes. So it is with the *Spondylis* <note>A type of thorny oyster or bivalve</note>, and many kinds of shell-skinned creatures, or even the moss growing upon them. Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric</note> mentions the marine and terrestrial *Aglaophotis* <note>original: "Aglaophotidis"; a legendary herb believed to glow at night</note>, a name bestowed because of its brilliant light, as well as other creatures nurtured by the sea. We have often seen seawater, when stirred by the hands, flashing with fiery sparks. Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus, the Romano-Jewish historian</note> tells of a valley containing a place called Baaras <note>original: "Baruas"; likely referring to the root mentioned in 'The Jewish War'</note>, named after the root found there, which flashes with a fiery color. Thus also the *nyctigretum* <note>original: "nictigretum"; an unidentified night-shining plant</note>, which Democritus greatly marveled at. Even the trunks of very dry oaks, withered by decay, produce and strike the eye with a silvery splendor at night. The Carbuncle <note>A term used in antiquity for any red gemstone, like garnet or ruby, believed to emit its own light</note> glows red in the darkness, illuminating the surrounding air according to the size of its body: and many other things are also visible by night, as proven by the testimony of the most serious authors. But the sequence of our work reminds us to teach how to extract the moisture from these things, from which a light may proceed and emanate more broadly and diffusely, so that it may be seen at night.
As revealed below:

### An Example.
Fireflies <note>original: "Nitedula"</note>, flashing greatly with a fiery color, hold the first place among all such things. We cut their tails away from the body, taking care that no foreign matter is mixed with the shining parts; after grinding them on a porphyry stone, we bury them in glass under manure for fifteen days, or a few more. It would be better if they do not touch the sides of the vessel, but rather hang within it. Once the days have passed, with the vessel placed and fitted in an oven or a bath of hot water, a bright and dripping liquid will gradually be collected in the dish placed beneath.

lime <note>original: "calx"; likely quicklime or calcium oxide</note>, four times the amount of saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> frequently purified, an equal weight of camphor, sulfur of wine <note>likely a distilled sulfurous spirit</note> that has not yet felt the knot of fire, oil of turpentine resin, and the hardened dregs of wine—which we shall hereafter always call "tartar." Let all these be ground in a mill or with a pestle and sifted; those bits which remain below should be ground and sifted again. Furthermore, you should have ready a burning water <note>aqua ardens; high-proof distilled alcohol</note> made from harsh wine; pour this in so that it stands above the mixture. Store it in a glass vessel, sealed so that it does not breathe out its vapor, and place it under manure <note>a common alchemical method for providing gentle, steady heat via fermentation</note> for two or three months, renewing the manure every ten days, until it mimics the thickness of honey and shows no sign of settling. Then, let it be heated over a glowing coal until all moisture evaporates and it hardens entirely into a stone. If you have mastered this, break the vessel and grind the composition again; if water or any moisture is lightly poured over it, a flame will rise up. This substance claims for itself the first place among all such things. How fire can also be kindled by mirrors or by other means will be discussed below when we treat those powers.

### Various compositions of fires.

## CHAPTER X.

The artificial composition of fires is neither to be despised as unnecessary, nor is it unpleasant to behold; nor does it differ much from the subjects discussed above. We shall relate some experiments concerning this which, although handled by the ignorant and the unlearned <note>original: "idiotis"; referring to those without formal scientific training</note>, nevertheless possess a great deal of subtlety, and we shall not be loath to review them. And first:

->A mixture of fire which burns under water.<-

How this can be done will be widely evident, for their composition is manifold. We shall speak of those which, being easy to prepare, complete the work more quickly. First, the powder...

<summary>This page provides a detailed alchemical recipe for a "stone" that ignites upon contact with water and introduces a chapter on various combustible mixtures, including those that burn underwater.</summary>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>Continuity from page 47: The author continues his discussion of chemical mixtures and combustible substances used in warfare, specifically focusing on the preparation of incendiary devices.</meta>

<page-num>48</page-num>
-># SECOND BOOK<-

Gunpowder <note>original: "pulvis pyrius"</note> is prepared (as they say) for engines of war, which are given the name "bombards" from their burning, booming sound <note>original: "bombo"</note>. This powder enters into all these mixtures as the established base and foundation. To this is added a third part of colophony <note>a type of distilled pine resin</note>, a fourth part of common olive oil, and a sixth part of sulfur. Let them be mixed together properly. Once all the ingredients are dried, they perform a test: if it burns more violently <note>original: "Valentius Vritur"</note> than desired, more colophony and sulfur are added; if it burns too weakly, a little more of the aforementioned powder is supplied. 

This mixture is wrapped in straw and linen cloths, or in small bags made from them, as tightly as possible. Then, once wrapped with cords and bindings, they submerge it in boiling pitch and see that it is dried. Protecting it again with straw, they coat it with pitch so that it is shielded from the moisture of water and does not allow itself to be burst apart by the force of the fire. When these have been properly prepared, they make a small hole through which they introduce fire; when it begins to burn, they wait a short time and then cast it into the water. It does not allow itself to be extinguished by the water, seeking now the bottom, now the surface of the water, rolling about within it. 

It is also recommended that they add naphtha to these, which they call petroleum <note>literally "rock oil"</note>; for it is a most rapacious fire, and it forces things to leap away from a great distance when it is seen, just as a magnet draws iron, and it is consumed by fire. This bitumen burns greatly in water, a property which craftsmen have borrowed from nature. No other reason can be given for baths that are always boiling, except that they burn with bitumen within and are fed by water, which is the cause of the continuous boiling. 

Engines of war are often filled with compositions of this kind; from them, they discharge certain fireballs <note>original: "igneas pilas"</note> at a distance, and they burst apart. They prepare them thus: they wrap this powder in tow <note>coarse flax or hemp fibers</note> and the mixture we have described, coating and entangling them, and they fill the hollow engines with powder, balls, and so forth.

## NATURAL MAGIC

In turn, when these mixtures are scattered and fire is applied during encounters with the enemy, the ignited ball is hurled through the air. To ensure it fulfills its role and burns more fiercely, some add hog's fat, goose grease, and "unfired" sulfur—which the Greeks call *anthrax* <note>original: "ἀνθραξ"; here referring to a coal-like mineral sulfur</note>—sulfurous oil, naphtha, frequently purified saltpeter <term>salnitrum: potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder</term>, "burning water" <note>aqua ardens: an early term for distilled alcohol or ethanol</note>, turpentine resin, and liquid pitch, which everyone calls *Kitram* <note>likely a derivation of cedria, or cedar resin</note>, commonly known as liquid varnish. They also add the oil of egg yolks, and sometimes, to add bulk and thicken the liquids, they mix in laurel sawdust. Once these are sealed in a glass vessel, bury it under manure for two or three months, always renewing the manure every other day and mixing the contents. If you take a sample of this composition and apply fire, it does not stop burning until it is entirely consumed; for it is not extinguished by the sprinkling of water—on the contrary, it is further ignited. However, it can be smothered by mud, earth, dust, or any dry materials. If you hurl this at a helmet, a shield, or armored men, it causes the metal to glow white-hot with fire, forcing them to be burned or to strip off their armor.

### We hand down another method, which is of more powerful operation:
Take turpentine resin, liquid pitch, and varnish; then equal portions of pitch, frankincense, and camphor; one and one-third parts of live sulfur <note>native or unrefined sulfur</note>, double the amount of purified saltpeter, triple the amount of "burning water," and just as much naphtha oil. You should also add a little dust of willow charcoal. Let these be prepared together and shaped into small balls, or fill small jars with them. It burns in such a way that attempting to extinguish it is in vain.

### Another is found among these:
A fiery mixture which the Sun can ignite.
It is most effective at high heat at noon, and especially in those regions where the Sun blazes, or during the rising of the Dog Star <note>original: "canicula"; the "Dog Days" of summer, typically the hottest period of the year</note>. This occurs through nothing other than the composition of highly flammable materials.

preparation, you shall nevertheless diligently prepare it in this manner, consisting of these ingredients: Let Camphor <note>original: "Caphura"</note> be prepared, then *live sulfur* <note>native or elemental sulfur</note>, turpentine resin, juniper, and oil of egg yolks, liquid pitch, colophony <note>pine resin</note> reduced to powder, saltpeter, and double the amount of all these of burning arsenic water, and a little tartar; let all these be well crushed and mixed together, and store them in a glass vessel. All these things are ascribed to Marcus Gracchus <note>likely a reference to Marcus Graecus, the legendary author of the "Book of Fires for Burning Enemies"</note>. Dove dung retains the greatest power of burning. Galen <note>the famous Roman physician and philosopher</note> reports that in Mysia, which is a part of Asia, a house was burned down in this way: Dove dung had been thrown out and was touching a nearby window, reaching its wooden frame which had recently been smeared with resin. When the dung was already rotting and heated, emitting a vapor in midsummer when the sun was at its strongest, it ignited the resin and the window. From there, other doors also smeared with resin began to catch fire and sent the flames up to the roof; once the flame was ignited from the roof, it quickly raged through the whole house, as it possesses the greatest power for inflammation.

*The fire conceives a greater heat when water is poured upon it. These torches are made of such a composition that they are not extinguished by crossing rivers or in rainy places. Livy <note>Titus Livius, a Roman historian</note> relates that certain old women in their games, having lit torches made of these materials, crossed the Tiber <note>The main river in Rome</note> to show a miracle to those waiting and watching. Hence, for journeys, torches are made which are not extinguished by winds, which happens because of the sulfur: for it is difficult to extinguish once it catches flame. In Germany, within the Hercynian Forest <note>The ancient name for the vast, dense mountain forests of Central Europe</note>, there appears a certain Bird whose feathers shine like fires; by their brilliance, travelers are guided through the uncertain outcomes of their journeys across those countless wildernesses. So it is with *spindles* <note>original: "spondylis," likely referring to a type of bivalve or articulated shell</note>, and many kinds of *shell-skinned creatures* <note>original: "ostracodermorum," referring to shellfish or crustaceans</note>, or the moss that sits upon them. *Aelian* <note>Claudius Aelianus, a Roman author and teacher of rhetoric</note> mentions the marine and terrestrial *Aglaophotis* <note>A legendary herb, literally "bright-light," said to glow at night</note>, a name bestowed because of its splendid light, as well as other creatures nurtured by the sea. We have often seen seawater, when stirred by the hands, flashing with fiery sparks. *Josephus* <note>Titus Flavius Josephus, the Romano-Jewish historian</note> tells of a Valley in which there is a place called Baaras, named after a root of the same name, which flashes with a fiery color. So too the *nyctegreton* <note>literally "night-waker," a plant mentioned by Pliny that glows at night</note>, which Democritus greatly marveled at. Even the very dry trunks of oak trees, withered by decay, stir and strike the sight with a silvery brilliance at night. The *Carbuncle* <note>A term used for red gemstones, particularly garnets or rubies, believed in folklore to emit their own light</note> glows red in the darkness, illuminating the surrounding air according to the size of its body; and there are many things visible by night, proven by the testimony of the most serious authors. But the sequence of our work reminds us to teach how to extract a liquid from these things, from which a light may be produced and emanate more broadly and diffusely, so that one might be able to see at night. Now, what follows below reveals:

### An Example.
Glow-worms <note>original: "Nitedula"</note>, flashing greatly with a fiery color, hold the primary place among all such things. We sever their tails from the body, taking care that no foreign matter is mixed with the shining parts; after grinding them on a porphyry stone <note>A hard, purple-red igneous rock used for grinding pigments and chemicals</note>, we store them in glass under manure <note>A common alchemical method for providing steady, gentle heat through fermentation</note> for fifteen days, or a few more. It would be even better if they do not touch the sides of the vessel, but rather hang within it. Once the days have passed, with the vessel placed and fitted in an oven or a bath of hot water, a bright and dripping liquid will gradually fall into the receiver placed below...

## CHAPTER XII.

Regarding secret marks for letters, which the common people call Ciphers <note>original: "Zipheras"</note>, they establish a double standard: one for visible marks, which is a study worthy of treatment in itself, and another for those that are hidden. Since the occasion now demands it, we have decided to comment on some of these matters and those that follow, looking out for the affairs of Nobles and Princes while they manage matters from afar and write to one who is knowledgeable of the invention. We shall set a few examples before your eyes, not only by reviewing them but by showing what will result from them; and those who add, combine, and uncover things woven and veiled with skill from every side will understand them, lest they become cheapened by falling into the hands of the unlearned and the common soldier: for they are more precious when they are secrets and remain for a long time shut away in a faithful breast. ->*To read letters polished by light,*<-

# NATURAL MAGIC

<note>original: "Magiae Naturalis"</note>

When the skin has become moist, by gently waking the area and rubbing over it, our native dark color is wiped away and hidden, while the former invisible color, having been rubbed, will become conspicuous to us. But if you wish for

**Letters beneath the skin and indelible on any limb**

To appear: you will accomplish it quickly in this way. Soak Spanish flies <note>original: "Cantharides"; these beetles were used in medicine to blister the skin</note> for the course of a natural day in strong water <note>nitric acid</note>, or more commonly in the water used for the separation of gold. Then, with a stylus or a suitable pen, trace characters upon the surface of the skin of the arm or another limb. The flesh, damaged by the moisture, rises into swollen blisters and becomes ulcerated; thus, by the power of the sharp water, it permanently leaves white scars in its tracks, and the limb is marked with letters that never vanish. Or

**You may make letters emerge immediately in any place, black and colored:**

If you secretly write letters on the hands or elsewhere with vinegar or urine, once they have dried and no traces appear, and you wish the power of the writing to shine forth, rub it with soot or a dye provided in abundance by the dyers' shops, and they will turn very black. If you seek to make those same letters white, draw on paper with the milk of a fig tree; once dried, sprinkle it with charcoal dust, rub it, and wipe it away. But to render

**Letters visible by fire or water**

We are able to do so in this way, by drawing out letters in the intervals between verses, and in the joints and distances between syllables. Let the letter contain some trivial matter, so that the observer thinks they are placed confusedly and interruptedly, and they will perceive either nothing at all or only with great difficulty. If you write with the juice of a citron, a "Median apple" <note>another term for citron or lemon</note>, an onion, or almost any pungent substance, if it is heated by fire, their sharpness is immediately uncovered. There is a somewhat **GREATER** subtlety if you write with alum dissolved in water.

once you have written, and when you come to the reading, by immersing it in water, the letters will emerge broadly visible and elegant. If, however, you wish them to be white and milky, you shall make them thus: first, <term>litharge</term> <note>original: "Lithargyris"; lead monoxide, often used in alchemy and pigments</note> is ground and cast into an earthenware vessel containing water and vinegar; it is boiled down, and you must pass the decoction through a strainer and save it. Then, write the letters with the juice of a lemon of the citrus variety; for as they dry, they are hidden. If you immerse them in the saved liquid, you will see them become milky and conspicuous. If the breasts or hands of women are moistened with these, and the aforementioned liquid is sprinkled upon them, they will flow back with "milk" <note>This refers to a chemical reaction creating a white precipitate that mimics the appearance of milk</note>. Use this if you should ever be in need of such things. Also, if letters are inscribed upon a stone with goat's fat, they are entirely hidden to the stone; yet if that same stone is submerged in vinegar, they come forth immediately, and will stand out as if they were sculpted in relief. If, however, you command the letters to turn black using only water for the writing—so that we may prepare for journeys more skillfully and expeditiously—finely crush <term>oak galls</term> <note>original: "Gallas"; growths on oak trees used to make permanent ink</note> and <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "calcanthū"; copper or iron sulfate</note>, then sprinkle the powder upon the paper. Rub it with a cloth so they are well polished, and so that it matches the color of the paper and adheres more stubbornly, let crushed <term>juniper gum</term>—which is called <term>varnish</term> <note>original: "Vernigo"</note> by scribes—be added to the rest. When the time for use comes, write with water or saliva, and they turn black. Very similar tricks <note>original: "techna"</note> occur, which would be too long to recount here. But

## To imprint letters upon an egg

Africanus <note>Likely referring to Sextus Julius Africanus, a 3rd-century traveler and writer on natural wonders</note> favored this method: grind <term>alum</term> <note>original: "Alumen"</note> finely and extensively with vinegar, and you shall draw whatever you wish upon the shell. Once dried in the burning sun, place it in <term>brine</term> <note>original: "muria"</note> or sharp vinegar for three or four days. When dried again, boil it, and when it has been cooked, strip away the shell; you will find the letters written upon the hard egg white. FROM THIS another method occurs: smear the egg round with <term>wax</term>, carving the letters with a stylus; fill the gaping cracks with moisture <note>likely a staining agent or acid</note>, and let it soak in vinegar for a day. When the wax...
