If you lift the wax from the peeled shell, you will find the bark <note>referring here to the inner membrane of the egg</note> is permeable, and the shapes of the letters will be impressed upon the egg itself. Now read of a method which necessity, through wicked ingenuity, has devised: how 

*Letters* may be made to fall away and vanish on appointed days.

*How far* does human genius soar while it lays open the secrets of nature? Let a thin, polished piece of steel be submerged in "separating water" <note>Nitric acid, used to separate gold from silver</note> until its weight has tripled; to this, add the soot of liquid pitch or turpentine resin so that it becomes blacker, and cover the jar. Let it be ground thoroughly on a porphyry stone; write with it, and as the letters age, they will fall off. I thought this should not be passed over in silence, as it is the most important point and, as it were, the head of the matter: to make the attempt frequently. For if the ink remains too long on the paper, let a little more "strong water" <note>acid</note> be applied, and if you are diligent, no yellowish traces will remain; by this rule of indication, you may enjoy the secret. 

If you desire another similar kind, should it be better to deceive in this way: let <term>Chryscocolla</term> <note>likely borax or a copper carbonate</note>, <term>ammoniac salt</term> <note>sal ammoniac</note>, and <term>alum</term> contend with one another in equal weight. Once all are crushed, let them be stored in an earthenware vessel. Prepare a lye from strong lime, and having stretched a linen cloth over the mouth of the receiving vessel, strain it. Let it boil a little, mix it with ink, and while the letters will remain intact for a short time, they soon become faint and fall away; store this for your use. But if you seek 

## *To utterly wipe away letters*
or blots and stains: draw over the letters with a quill dipped in a water made of <term>Vitriol</term> <note>original: "Calcanthi"</note> and <term>saltpeter</term>. OR, rub the spot with small gathered spheres made of <term>alkali salt</term> and <term>sulfur</term>; this will erode the text so that not even a trace remains. As for announcing letters by the disk of the Moon <note>a reference to long-distance signaling or "moon-writing"</note>, we shall write of that once we have been made more certain; for now, we have written these things in haste.

# BOOK THE SECOND.
## Concerning banquets and wonderfully prepared feasts.
### CHAPTER XIII.

I had thought these matters should be passed over in silence, left to the gluttons and heavy drinkers, the cooks and the tavern-keepers, as things largely foreign and unsuitable to our purpose, and not to be sent into refined <note>literally "purified" or "strained"</note> ears. But for the sake of satisfying everyone, we have set them down, as these things may be more pleasing to some than the rest, and they may never repent having indulged their appetite and their nature. Thus, writing briefly so as not to detain the minds of readers with a longer narrative, let our discourse turn to those things that make frugal banquets and dinners appear more elegant in their preparation. And first:

Lest a guest become intoxicated by too great a draught of wine, or if he feels harm to his body from food taken too copiously, he shall dispel it as Cato <note>Marcus Porcius Cato, the Roman statesman known for his work on agriculture</note> teaches: At the beginning and end of dinner, let there be four or five sprouts of raw cabbage; it settles the hangover, extinguishes the harm of the wine, and makes it as if you had eaten or drunk nothing. So much do they <note>wine and cabbage</note> disagree with a deadly hatred, and by a discordant nature, one flees the fate of the other. Hence Androcydes, famous for his wisdom, indicated that it was effective against drunkenness and commanded it to be eaten against it. Nor shall I omit what Nestor said in his *Alexicepus* <note>original: "Alexicepo," likely referring to a "garden of remedies"</note>: for he calls the cabbage the "tear of Lycurgus." He says that when Bacchus <note>The god of wine</note>, fearing him, had plunged into the sea, Lycurgus, bound by a vine, shed a tear, and from that tear the cabbage was born; and for that reason, there is an opposition between wine and cabbage. Aristotle reports that this happens because it possesses a sweet and dispersive juice. Whence Plutarch in his *Symposiacs* <note>Table Talk</note> says that sweet things, if infused in wine, dispel drunkenness. It happens sometimes from a dripping of the head <note>a "distillation" or catarrh</note>...

# NATURAL MAGIC

<margin>The uvula</margin> When the uvula <note>original: "columellam," literally 'little column'</note> is loosened in the throat, then the juice of raw cabbage applied to the head draws the uvula back up into the high part of the mouth. There is also in cabbage <note>original: "crambe"</note> a power to resist wine: for if it is planted in a vineyard, the wine produced will be more diluted. For this reason, it was a solemn custom among the Egyptians and the Sybarites <note>inhabitants of Sybaris, famous for luxury</note> to eat cooked cabbage before all else. Some were accustomed to do this with an amethyst vessel <note>The name amethyst comes from the Greek 'a-methystos', meaning 'not drunken'</note> before a drinking party, so that they might indulge in wine with impunity, according to Athenaeus. But if you wish to inhibit the harm of wine in ANOTHER way—especially the more diluted kind, for people feel the hangover <note>original: "crapulam"</note> from that more quickly than those who drink it pure—Africanus teaches that if you are going to drink much, first eat three or four bitter almonds. These, being drying and consuming of humidities, will drive away drunkenness. Plutarch of Chaeronea relates that there was a physician in the service of Drusus, son of Tiberius Caesar, who, when he ate two or six bitter almonds during a meal, surpassed everyone in drinking; once he was caught and deprived of them, he was unwilling to compete even a little bit more. NOR is the powder of pumice stone used in vain, if a drinker takes it beforehand in a drinking contest. Theophrastus asserts this is risky unless one is filled with a great deal of drink. Thus Eudemus lasted through twenty-two rounds, and finally entering the bath, he threw nothing up, and dined as if he had not been drinking; the drying power consumed the liquid. Even if thrown into a vat of boiling new wine <note>original: "musti"</note>, the heat of the wine is immediately calmed. To ward off the deadly effects of pure wine, the ancients also wore floral crowns bound around their heads during drinking parties.

Hence Ovid says:
> The drunken guest with linden-bark binding his hair
> Is accustomed to wander, and unknowingly uses the art of wine.
And Valerius Martial:

*Let my moist hair grow rich with excessive balsam,*
*And let my temples be weary with fleeting roses.*
<note>original: "Pinguescat nimio madidus mihi Cannus amomo..." This is a poetic reference to the Roman custom of wearing perfumes and floral crowns at banquets.</note>

*And they say the reason is that the skull grows cold from excessive chill, so that it may extinguish the drunkenness and its power. Whence we read that Dionysus* <note>The Greek god of wine, also known as Bacchus.</note> *instituted the ivy crown for those reclining at table, which, being endowed with a cooling power, might blunt the potency of the wine; for it hedges against the onset of thickening drunkenness. Indeed, the heat of unmixed wine makes the pores of the head more open, while the cold so tempers them that it strikes back the rising vapors. There is also another care of the ancient fathers to extinguish a hangover, namely that lettuces were plucked at the end of dinner, since they possess the greatest natural coldness; but now we use them at the start of the meal to stir the appetite. Whence Martial* <note>A Roman poet known for his epigrams.</note> *says:*
> *The lettuce that used to close the dinners of our grandfathers,*
> *Tell me, why does it now begin our feasts?*
*Dioscorides* <note>An ancient Greek physician and pharmacologist.</note> *seems to call it a "hangover-preventer" because it prohibits drunkenness. Now that we have mentioned wine, let those things be related which have been observed, and how it is necessary*

### To make drunkards abstain.

*For there are many who drink wine so liberally that nothing becomes more ruinous to them; they fall into diseases and finally into death. But if you wish them to spurn and abhor it, and the Clitorian spring* <note>A legendary spring in Arcadia said to make those who drank from it lose their taste for wine.</note> *is far away: let three or four eels die in wine by being submerged for a day. Offer this wine to the drunkard, and he will grow weary of it and always hate it; nor will he drink further, or at least he will live more modestly. Athenaeus* <note>A Greek rhetorician and grammarian.</note> *also recorded in memory that a mullet suffocated in wine, if consumed immediately by a man, has the power to impede the works of Venus* <note>A euphemism for sexual desire or activity.</note>; *if by a woman, she shall never conceive.*

Perhaps it will please you in ANOTHER way, *so that* they may taste these things more fully, *and this* is what *Iarchas* <note>A legendary Indian sage and king mentioned in ancient Greek literature</note> teaches, *as* *Philostratus* demonstrates in the *Life of Apollonius*: *Observe where an owl builds its nest*, *snatch away its eggs*, *and after they are boiled, offer them to an infant to eat*; *from then on, he will always loathe wine*. *SO ALSO the water distilling from a pruned vine*, *if drunk in large quantities, makes one sober*, *as Democritus taught*. *Furthermore, to know*

Whether wine has water mixed into it,
*You shall learn from Democritus and Florentinus*: *Submerge apples or wild pears into the wine*; *if they float on the surface, it is a sign of pure wine*, *but if they sink, it is already diluted*. *FROM Sotion’s experiment*: *They cast a reed, or some brushwood smeared with oil, into the vat*, *then pull it out*; *if any drops cling to it, the wine contains water*. *Pour it onto lime*; *if the lime scatters, it has been cheated by the mixture of water*, *but if it binds together, it is pure wine*. *SO ALSO if it is poured onto a linen cloth*: *and many other methods exist*. *But if the mixture displeases you, behold*

A method for separating water from wine:

*Have a "Cissibium" ready*, *that is, a vessel turned from ivy wood*, *or otherwise fashioned*; *pour in the wine*, *and if any water has been mixed in, it will drip out in a short time*. *I see that all the ancients and moderns have felt the opposite regarding this*: *against which both reason and experience cry out*: *for since the wood is porous and open with many small cracks*, *water—which according to Aristotle is the thinnest of all liquids—will flow out*, *while the wine, being more full-bodied, is held back*. *THERE IS also another method of separation using threads or brushes made of cotton or linen immersed in the vat*, *so that they hang over the edge; by this, the water is secreted*. *Likewise, a sponge cast into wine and squeezed out will eject more water than wine*. *Thus, in a short process of time*

## Wine scented in various ways

You shall proceed thus: let the simple ingredients <note>botanicals or herbs</note> of whichever scent you desire the wine to possess be submerged in "burning water" <note>aqua ardens; a high-proof distilled spirit or alcohol</note>; for the nature of the water will immediately drink in the scent. Pass it through a strainer, and once it is clarified, let it settle. Mix this into the wine, for it is not unlike that flavor, and it will leave behind those things that smell too pungent. Now it is taught:

## How salt water may be rendered drinkable.

This is what Aristotle teaches: If we fashion a hollow vessel of wax and submerge it in the waters of the sea, that which glides inside through the pores of the wax will become drinkable. Likewise with a vessel of raw clay with its mouth stopped up; for that which permeates into the interior is filtered, because the substance which creates the saltiness through mixture is separated out. It may be done another way, both more truly and more quickly: cast river sand into the salt water and let it sit for a little while; then let it be strained through a linen cloth stretched over the mouth of a receiving vessel until it has lost all saltiness and becomes sweet. Many other common methods may remain unmentioned. But now we shall discuss whether it is possible to see:

## A goose cooked while still alive.

For such things are often served to great men at elegant and sumptuous tables, so much has the luxury of gluttony prevailed. Take this rule for the preparation: Let there be a duck, or a goose, or another long-lived animal, but for this purpose the goose is preferred above all. Excepting the head and the neck, let its whole body be plucked of feathers. Let it be walled in on all sides by fire and hedged around—not too closely, lest it be suffocated by smoke or scorched by the fire too quickly; nor too loosely, lest it escape unharmed. Inside, place little cups and jars full of water here and there, to which you should add salt and honey; and let there be dishes full of boiled apples, cut into small squares in the bowl. Let the bird itself be entirely coated with a savory liquid and larded with fat, so that it may be more edible and more easily cooked by the fire...

Apply the fire, but do not be in a hurry. When it begins to boil over from the heat, the fire, by moving around and escaping, hedges it in and blocks its path; thus the wicked spirit <note>The author likely refers to the "spirit" or steam of the cooking process</note> is restrained by absorbing the water. By cooling the heart and other members, the power of the medicine loosens the bowels, wipes them clean, and is emptied out. And when it has boiled within, it will cook the internal parts; continuously moisten the brain and heart with a sponge. But when you see it being driven by madness and then beginning to stop, the moisture in the heart is failing; remove it and set it before the guests. With its parts always being plucked away, it cries out so that it seems almost to have been eaten before it appears dead. This is not unpleasant to behold. <note>This describes a "living" cooked bird, a common spectacle in Renaissance banquets designed to shock and entertain.</note>

### CHAPTER XIV.

<insert>T</insert>here exist certain experiments of no small subtlety, free from any addition of mixtures, which we thought would not be unpleasant to the ingenious and to craftsmen, so that this entire book might be of use. There is also a device, which by some is called a

<term>Flying Dragon</term> <note>An early description of a kite, often used in the Renaissance for entertainment or to simulate omens.</note>

or a Comet Star, the construction of which is as follows: Let a quadrangle be fashioned from the finer slats of reeds, such that the length is in a sesquialter <note>original: "hemiolia" — a 1.5 to 1 ratio</note> proportion to the width. Let two diagonals be placed from opposite parts or angles, at the intersection of which a cord shall be tied; let this be joined with two others of the same length coming from the heads of the machine. Thus, let it be covered with paper or fine linen, so that there is nothing heavy in it. Then, from the summit of a tower, a mountain, or some higher place, it is to be entrusted to equal and uniform winds—not so strong that the machine is broken, nor so light that the breeze is silent everywhere, for that will not lift it, and the sluggishness of the winds would make the labor in vain. It does not move in a straight path, but obliquely, which is caused by the pull of the rope from one head, and from the other...

# SECOND BOOK.

The other has a long tail, which you shall fashion from equidistant ropes with papers tied here and there; thus, when launched with a light pull, it must be entrusted to the hands of a skilled operator who pushes it neither sluggishly nor carelessly, but with strength. And so the flying sail <note>original: "carbasus"; likely referring to a kite made of linen or canvas</note> seeks the air once it has been slightly elevated (for here the <term>Wind</term> <note>The author notes that wind is often disrupted or turbulent near the "windings of houses" or alleys</note> is broken by the turns of the buildings), so that it can scarcely be restrained or held back by the hands. Some place a lantern on top so that it may appear as a comet. Others attach a firecracker <note>original: "Sclopum"; literally a "pop" or "crack," here a paper tube filled with gunpowder</note> wrapped in paper and gunpowder; when the device is steady in the air, a climbing cord is sent up along the rope by means of a ring or some smooth sliding object. Reaching the sail immediately, it applies fire to the opening, and with a great thunder, the machine bursts into many pieces and falls to the ground. Some tie a cat or a small dog to it, and listen to the voices sent through the air. From this, an ingenious man might be able to foresee the principles of how a human might also be able to fly, by binding other huge wings to the elbows and chest; however, he should become accustomed to flapping them gradually from childhood, always starting from a higher place. 

If anyone should think this a marvel, let him look upon those things which <term>Archytas the Pythagorean</term> <note>A 4th-century BC Greek philosopher and mathematician credited with inventing the first self-propelled flying machine</note> is said to have devised and made. For many of the noble Greeks, and <term>Favorinus the Philosopher</term> <note>A Roman-era scholar and historian known for his vast knowledge of ancient lore</note>, a most diligent follower of ancient memories, wrote most affirmatively that a model of a <term>wooden dove</term> made by <term>Archytas</term> through a certain method and mechanical discipline actually flew; it was so suspended by counterweights and set in motion by an enclosed and hidden aura of spirit <note>original: "aura spiritus"; likely referring to compressed air or steam</note>. Thus we can also make it so that:

### Slips of paper may change their position without being touched

Because this is entirely misunderstood, it is impossible to say how marvelous it appears: *Let three rather long slips be made of paper or linen, and let them exceed each other by an equal length. For when they are all leveled at one end and rolled up equally, they are then unrolled and found in different positions. The longest may appear in the middle or in the first place, even if that same longer one was originally in the last place. They remain unmoved <note>The author implies they move relative to each other during the rolling process without manual intervention</note>, which there is no one who would not think was done by demons. However, it happens from no other cause than because at the end of the revolution, the taller one remains, and the ends from which the rolling began remain behind. It was the error of some who thought this happened by the power of words <note>original: "verborum Vi"</note>, so that they provided answers to all questions as if from an Oracle. For if the slips changed position, it was a favorable omen and the effects were propitious; if not, it was unfortunate. They did not wish to exchange their faith for reasons and experiences, since they had formed a habit in their believing. There is also another device, so that*

### A burning candle may be carried under water

And it is prepared by such an art: *Have a long Vessel <term>Vas: a container or jar</term> of convenient capacity; let a piece of wood be fitted to the opening, upon which a burning candle may sit immovably. With the Vessel inverted, let the light strike the bottom. In this way, you may plunge the whole thing entirely into the waters, and the water shall not enter since it is filled with air, and it will burn for a long time under the waters according to the capacity of the Vessel. Nor is it less a matter of wonder in experience when you wish that*

### How an inverted vessel may draw up water.
You shall accomplish it in this way: let a vessel with a very long neck be prepared—and the longer it is, the more marvelous the effect—made of glass and transparent so that you may observe the water ascending. Let this be filled with boiling water, and when it has all bubbled up, apply the bottom to a fire. Immediately, before it cools, touch the water with the inverted mouth, and it will suck the whole amount inside. In this way, those who explore the nature of things <note>The author refers to "physiologoi" or natural philosophers.</note> say that water is drawn up and absorbed by the rays of the sun from the concave places of the earth into the mountains, from which spring-fed flows are created. From this principle arise no small number of devices in pneumatic mechanics, as Hero <note>Hero of Alexandria, a Greek mathematician and engineer active in the 1st century AD.</note> relates; but as these are foreign to our current purpose, let them be set aside for elsewhere. A similar thing is asserted by Vitruvius <note>Marcus Vitruvius Pollio, the Roman architect and engineer.</note> regarding the origin of winds; but now this has moved into domestic use. For in this way:

### A vessel projecting wind.
It can be constructed if a bronze *aeolipile* <note>An aeolipile is a simple bladeless radial steam turbine which spins when heated; here used as a steam blower.</note> or one of another material is made. Let it be hollow and round, and in its belly let it have a very narrow puncture through which water is poured. If this should prove difficult, use the previous experiment. If, when placed near a fire, it grows hot, and since it has no other vent, it ejects from there a great spirit <note>original: "spiritum" - here meaning steam or pressurized air.</note>, though of a moist and steamy thickness. Now let us move to other things. If a ROPE is twisted with the hands so that it crosses over itself, it is broken with a slight pull of the arm, whereas without this it is broken with difficulty. SO, too, a brick or piece of marble set upright and struck slightly with the fist shatters into many parts; likewise, stones that are rounded and worn by the rolling of rivers are broken. AND, an apple is cut with a light blow if you strike the head of the knife while it is cutting only a little. IF you search in barley, you will find the black and twisted awn <note>The hair-like bristle on a grain of barley.</note> of wild oats, similar to the legs of a locust; if you bind it to a page with wax...

### Concerning women's cosmetics:

### CHAPTER XV.

Among other things, certain *experiments are widely circulated and greatly sought after which contribute to removing women's blemishes and enhancing their beauty; and because they say it matters much, we have decided to write them here so they need not be sought elsewhere.* First, before all else,

#### To dye the hair

We teach how to make it *blonde, black, golden, or whatever color may please you*: *For you will make it blonde with a great deal of oil of honey and egg yolks.* No less effective are lyes made from fermented ash, barley straw, licorice bark, boxwood shavings and leaves, saffron, and cumin; *if you wash the hair frequently with these, it will turn very yellow and imitate the color of gold.*

*Hair will turn very black with a lye made from fig bark, gall nuts <note>oak galls used for ink and dye</note>, fir cones, brambles, cypress, and similar things.* However, you may conveniently dye the *beard and hair in this way if someone is turning gray: mix litharge <note>silver dross or lead monoxide</note>, burnt copper, and four times the amount of strong lye; while it boils over light coals, wash the hair, and once dried, rinse it with warm water.*

In this way the eyebrows are blackened: *Let gall nuts be fried in oil, grind them with a little ammonia salt, then mix with vinegar in which bramble and mulberry bark have boiled; let the eyebrows be smeared with this and leave it on overnight, then wash it off with water.* *But because it often happens that a place is either disfigured by a multitude of hairs or becomes too smooth, you should take such*

# To wipe away the lividity of the cheeks

*You may do so in this manner, especially for women during the time of their monthly purgations <note>original: "menstrua patiuntur"; refers to the belief that the menstrual cycle caused skin discoloration</note>: Let the place be smeared with white lead <note>original: "cerussa"; a common but toxic pigment used in antiquity</note>, powder of beans, and mixed vinegar: Or with the whites of eggs mixed with honey.*

## Cleansing soaps for women,

*Which bring a brightness and smoothness to their faces. Such as breadcrumbs cast into goat-milk whey; you shall dab the face with the water distilled from it, which is quite wonderful for whitening the skin and bestowing a luster: No less does donkey milk contribute to whiteness, for it removes wrinkles from the skin, polishes it, and makes it soft. Therefore, it was not without reason that Poppaea Sabina <note>original: "Pompeia Sabina"; the second wife of Emperor Nero, famous for her beauty rituals</note>, the wife of Nero, always kept five hundred female donkeys with her, and soaked her entire body in a bath filled with that milk. ## To procure a rosy color for the face

If you boil nettle <note>original: "Urticam"</note> in water for a long time and frequently wash the body, it will render it colored and marked with a rosy hue, if you continue this for a long time. # BOOK SECOND.

## Waters for beautifying the face,

And for preparing those things which are sought after, if by chance it is a matter of the heart <note>original: "res cordi"; meaning a matter of great interest or desire</note>, so that it may shine with a smooth lift. For this, egg whites should be cooked until they harden; from these you shall distill a water, and it will be fit for use. Likewise, use the juice of rosemary, bean flowers, and lemons. However, this water is the most excellent of all, devised with skillful care: Let talc <note>a mineral known for its shimmer and softness</note> be reduced into a very fine powder and placed in a pot, and likewise you shall put in a great abundance of snails; cover it so they do not escape: for being deprived of other food, they devour the introduced talc and digest it. When you know they have devoured it all, you shall place them with their shells into a glass apparatus <note>an alembic or still</note>; you shall distill the water and keep it for the face. Set the dregs <note>original: "faces"</note> out in places open to the sky for three nights, and by putting them back into a vessel you will receive an oil, than which nothing shall be more excellent for the hair. But

## To wipe away scurf from the face

Women will be able to do so thus: Let cow, buck, and goat gall <note>bile used as a detergent or solvent</note> be mixed with glass powder, and let the soft face be smeared; it cleanses it exceedingly and makes the face lovely. Furthermore, the juice of Dragonwort <note>original: "Dracontea"; likely *Dracunculus vulgaris*</note> corrects all spots, especially if the mouth is disfigured by them. Now we shall set down some

## Tooth-powders,

Which were prepared best in ancient times from the burnt shells of purple-fish <note>murex</note> and trumpetshells; but now you can quickly use the crumbs of burnt bread, powdered pumice, red corals, cuttlefish bones, stag's horn, and similar things; each of which properly polishes and wipes clean. Likewise with grains of scarlet dye <note>original: "cocci granulis"; kermes insects used for red pigment</note>, if you rub them with purple. Yet the best of all is with oil of sulfur, for it polishes, smoothes, and drives away all spots. A distilled water of alum and salt is also made for this purpose.

# NATURAL MAGIC
<note>original: "MAGIÆ NATVRALIS"</note>

## NATURAL MAGIC

Truly, once a woman has conceived, she must beware of all these things, for they can destroy the conception. For instance, **mint** <note>original: "mentha"</note>, if applied to the entrance of the natural parts after intercourse, corrupts the genital seed. If added to milk, it prevents it from curdling or thickening even if rennet is added; indeed, if applied to the breasts, it does not allow the milk to thicken. **Saffron** <note>original: "Crocum"</note> is most powerful at driving away the ability to conceive. A decoction of **willow** <note>original: "Salicis"</note>, if drunk on an empty stomach, renders one sterile, since the willow tree loses its seed so quickly that it turns into a cobweb before it reaches maturity. For this reason, **Homer** calls it "fruit-destroying":

->The willows that lose their fruit.<-
<note>original Greek: "Κλήτοισ οἰαγοικαί ἴτεα ἁλωσόικαί" - likely a corruption of Odyssey 10.510: "willows that shed their fruit before it is ripe."</note>

Likewise, the smoke from a **mule's hoof**, or its urine, sweat, or the water in which blacksmiths extinguish iron—all these [cause sterility] if used after the menstrual flow. But among these, **dancing** prevails as the most harmful to them when they move themselves excessively after union with a man; for when the body cannot retain the infused genital seed, it slips out in vain and leaps back, even that which perhaps had already begun to cohere. This is like that **female lutist** <note>original: "psaltria"</note> of whom **Hippocrates** tells, who, not wishing to retain the seed because she did not want to conceive (lest a pregnancy make her less noble), leaped down to the ground, and by the forceful movement, the seed flowed out of her. Others, being superstitious and perhaps following the **Pythagoreans** <note>original: "pythagorizantes"</note>, ascribed this to the power of the number seven and to occult properties, since Hippocrates had said she leaped seven times. However, this is proven false, as the seed may flow out sooner or later; know that the more she dances, the less likely she is to conceive. Other dangers include drinking the juice of **savine**, or applying **rue** or **scammony** to the womb like a pessary, or the fumes of **opoponax**, **galbanum**, and **living sulfur**. Among these, a **hen's feather** smeared with what they call **black soap** and moved toward the womb immediately causes an abortion. All these things are to be strictly avoided, for they are accustomed to harming pregnant women.

### Concerning lamp-wicks, and their illusions, and how men may appear to have the heads of horses or other animals.
#### CHAPTER XVII.

I have long and deeply pondered whether these secrets were unknown to the ancients, or whether the things said of them and promised by impostors correspond to the Truth. I was not a little rejoiced when I discovered many among the ancients who took care of these matters, especially Anaxilaus <note>Anaxilaus of Larissa, a Pythagorean philosopher and magician active in the time of Augustus</note>, giving credit to the assertions of Pliny. We have sweated not a little in discovering these things to complete our history, nor would it be out of place to remember them and bring them into the open, adapting them through experience. Let the operator first become accustomed to these, gradually arriving at other matters. First, they are taught how everything can be seen, and

### A Colored Chamber,

But I consider this most important to weigh: remove all other lights, lest the color emanating from the lamp be hindered or overcome by that light, and the illusion be frustrated. If it be day, let the windows be closed, lest any breaking light destroy the trick. You shall see **GREEN**, for example: Let there be a green lamp, made of glass and transparent, so that the rays passing through the middle are stained with the color. And (what contributes most to this) let verdigris <term>verdigris: a green pigment prepared from weathered copper</term> be mixed with the oil, or whatever liquid feeds the flame; let it be ground together properly so the liquid becomes green. Let the wick <term>original: elichnium/lichnus</term> be prepared from linen of the same color, or from cotton. When it is lit in that lamp, the light striking the opposite side shows everything green, including the faces of those looking on.

If you desire to see everything **BLACK**: mix ink into the liquid, or soot or something similar; but it is better if the ink from cuttlefish <note>original: sepiys</note> is added, for in lamps...

Fill it with oil and its own fat so that it overflows; cover the mouth and fortify it with tenacious clay. Apply a slow fire so that the oil is kept boiling plainly for three days, and the boiled flesh runs into the oil until the bones are seen bare. Pound these with a mortar and let the powder be mixed with the oil, with which the heads of those standing by may be anointed. Similarly, let hempen cords be placed in the middle of lamps, neither too near nor too far as the matter requires, and you will be seen with a monstrous face. # NATURAL MAGIC

*I believe I have said enough, if the observation has been diligent. Oil extracted from a human head recently severed* <note>This refers to the "oil of the skull," a common though macabre ingredient in early modern "natural magic" experiments believed to hold vital spirits.</note> *induces the appearance of a human face upon animals; likewise, with the heads of various animals, you will render bodies more monstrous if the house is illuminated by wicks soaked in these substances. Keep this hidden in a faithful breast, for just as secrets were concealed by the ancients, the truth is not easily extracted from their sayings. However, Anaxilaus* <note>Anaxilaus of Larissa, a first-century BC physician and Pythagorean philosopher often cited by Pliny the Elder for his magical experiments.</note> *teaches otherwise, and not in vain: The "fluid of horses"* <note>original: "Equorum Virus"; likely referring to hippomanes, a substance gathered from mares in heat, frequently mentioned in classical magic.</note> *is taken from their mating, and when burned in new lamps with wicks, it monstrously represents the heads of horses to the sight of men; the same is also reported concerning donkeys. SO ALSO that which they call "boar-fluid" when they are in heat: for when taken and burned, it performs the same. SO ALSO with the ear-wax of other animals burned. IF you thoroughly burn that which we call "seed," and smear the faces of the spectators with it, you will behold the heads of those same animals; keep this secret. Nor in a dissimilar way thus:*

*You will be able to see a house full of Grapes, and all will be deceived by their own illusion: When the grape begins to finish flowering, let a vessel full of pure oil be placed beneath it, into which you should submerge the cluster with its leaves; let it be secured so that the wind does not shake it here and there, and let the sun strike it. Seal the lid with gypsum and skin, leaving only a small hole where the stem may be inserted, and let it remain so. When the grape has reached perfect maturity, it is pressed through a linen cloth, and the pressed liquid is preserved in oil. After a few days in the sun, when finally lit in lamps, you will see everything full of grapes; you will seem to be surrounded by leaves and trees, even where the trees perhaps entirely lack foliage. Indeed, this may be used with other fruits, for I judge the same result can be provided.*

### Concerning many experiments with lamps.

### CHAPTER XVIII.

I see that some experiments with lamps still remain, which, although they may not be as pleasant to the sight or as wonderful as the subject might require, we have decided to review so that we do not seem to have omitted anything. It will be neither tedious nor useless to add these to the first series. For instance:

#### How to make someone shrink back when lighting a lamp:
Let a wick be made of linen, in the middle of which an old snake skin <note>original: "anguis senecta" - literally the sloughed-off skin of a snake</note> and lightly crushed salt are placed; offer this to someone to be lit with oil. For when the salt feels the flame, it will crackle and leap apart, and the snake skin is distorted as it is cooked, so that it strikes fear into children. In the same way, if you light the hide of a dog and a wolf, or a wolf and a sheep, sewn together with oil, they are agitated, still displaying their innate enmity even as they flee <note>The author refers to the ancient belief in "antipathy," where the natural hatred between animals persisted in their skins or remains</note>; this is handed down by Albert <note>referring to Albertus Magnus, the 13th-century polymath</note>. If you wish that:

#### Frogs may be silenced at night by a lamp:
Albert relates that if a wick is prepared from dolphin fat and wax bleached white by the sun, and lit on the banks of lakes, all the frogs immediately fall silent. However, Africanus <note>Sextus Julius Africanus, a 3rd-century writer</note> says more naturally and with greater certainty in the Greek *Geoponica* <note>A 10th-century collection of agricultural lore</note> that this happens with any lamp: for if you place a lit lamp right against the bank, all the clamoring frogs immediately become still. Impostors are also accustomed to insert a frog through a hole in a wall, then cover it with paper upon which they paint a raven; when a torch is lit outside or fire is moved near it, the warming frog croaks so that its voice is mingled with that of a raven, and thus they show the painted raven as if it were cawing.

#### There is also made:
#### A wick which is burned when the hand is held tight, but is extinguished when it is loosened:
Draw out the water from camphor <note>original: "caphura"</note> using the glass vessels of the chemists...

# ON NATURAL MAGIC
<note>original: "MAGIÆ NATVRALIS"</note>

And if the vents are properly sealed with clay so that the spirit <note>The "spirit" here refers to volatile vapors or flammable gases</note> does not escape, smear your hands with such water, and by clenching your fist over a flame, it burns **strongly**; yet if the hand is opened, the light is weakened. *It is also done in this way:*

### A light by which the stars may seem to wander
There are those who burn the tortoise herb <note>likely *Velari* or a species of spurge</note> or centaury, and by looking at the stars through the smoke of that flame, the stars appear **wandering** thus, and **seem** to be moved from their place everywhere through the sky. This happens not only from that specific plant, but from the smoke of any substance; for since the smoke is of a thicker transparency and the form of the image is mobile, it **will seem to vary** from its actual place. Therefore the **sight** is deceived and will think they move. But if this is expected to **appear** among wonders, hide the fire so it is concealed from the viewer, and let the passing rays be refracted; from this, an ingenious person can devise many things that deceive the eyes, which he may take from the books of the opticians. *Take another similar example:*

### A light by which men may be seen in the form of giants
*Some* take a species of nightshade, which the *Arabs* call *alachachegni* <note>The Winter Cherry or Physalis alkekengi</note>, and prepare it with dolphin fat; they see that it is soaked in the ointment and gathered into small cakes, and they burn them with a weak flame of *cow* dung. Those standing opposite **are seen in the form of a larger man**, which happens most of all if the viewers are positioned lower and those being viewed stay in a higher place, so that the ray reaching the man's head strikes the highest panels of the roof, and they are viewed at the same angle so that he **appears of the same height** <note>i.e., as tall as the room itself</note>, which is aided by the thickness of the medium <note>The "medium" refers to the smoky or hazy air through which light travels</note>. *Thus it often happens at night in the twilight, under the uncertain light of the moon or when it is hidden under a cloud*, in the woods, where wolves and other animals of the mountains and forests **are seen** exceeding their height, so that people think they are phantasms. And men walking before **travelers**, in a moderate light—

## On the art by which poisons can be prevented.
### CHAPTER XIX.
To follow our established purpose, it would now be opportune to hand down those remedies which heal fatal poisons, since they are commonly prepared by the wicked and sometimes bring about inevitable destruction. From these, anyone will be able to take precautions, for they always render their venom useless and do not allow the effects to manifest; for we have known these truths through long experience. We shall treat some of them which, although not ineffective, will nevertheless accomplish nothing unless handled by the learned hand of craftsmen, and they are accustomed to bring no small harm to those working with them. Nature has established particular poisons just as she has universal ones. Leopard-strangler <term>Pardalianches: a type of aconite or monkshood</term> kills leopards and panthers, and Theophrastus calls the same plant "female-slayer" <note>original: "Thelyphonum"</note> because it renders scorpions stupid and makes them grow numb when the root is moved over them. Dog-killer <term>cynoctonon</term> and wolf-killer <term>lycoctonon</term> are for dogs and wolves...

### To induce a wound by a brief touch

A most terrible one, they do it thus: they take that <term>Rubeta</term> <note>original: "Rubetam"; a species of venomous land-toad</note> which lives in bramble thickets, and whose back is roughened as if with little tumors, which some call a <term>toad</term> <note>original: "bufonem"</note>; this is the more harmful kind, and it will be all the more lethal the more it has spent its life in shady and cold places, such as forests and reed beds, for it is infamous for those <term>poisons</term>. They put it into a long little bag, along with enough salt to fill the size of an Indian nut <note>likely a coconut or large nutmeg</note>; grasping it on both sides with the hands, one shall shake it violently and much, until it dies: then, retaining the deadly salt, they preserve the toad's venom. OR they bury the toad in salt, and keep it in the lowest depths for half a month; they dissolve the preserved salt into foodstuffs, and whoever eats it, as it runs through the internal parts, it will cause harm, infect the blood, and he will die within a short interval. Or they deliver it to a damp place to dissolve, and a linen cloth soaked in such water, or anything touching the bare flesh, induces a most savage wound. ### Antidote.

Keep the pressed liquid in the same vessel, and devote your labor to it; and when it has brought forth flowers and produced seed, mix these three: and when it has boiled over a fire, throw in one hundred <term>scorpions</term>, a <term>viper</term>, and a toad...

If you consume a few drops in a cup of Malmsey wine <note>a sweet, high-alcohol wine</note> for three days, it liberates you; if you provide help immediately, a single dose will suffice.

It is tested thus: it is moved toward a Toad; the toad rises up against it, and if it can, reaches for it and strives to snatch it away with a leap; thus it is proven by the toad.

### On sleep-inducing medicines.
#### CHAPTER XX.

No less among the secrets are revolved sleep-inducing experiments, and they are of the greatest esteem among those who devote their labor to testing and experiencing the virtues of things, by soothing the pains of some through sleep. Nowadays almost everyone abuses them, and being burdened with food and wine, they are lulled into a deeper sleep. From these we shall hand down some to the ingenious, which have seemed closer to the Truth to us, so that they may learn to know and compose them. ## To induce sleep
If one wishes, it can be done thus: Take water drop by drop from fresh opium and naked heads <note>likely referring to poppy heads with the husks removed</note> in glass vessels; let them be mixed with others, and let there be given to the one about to sleep as much as a nutshell will hold. For once the medicine has been drunk, the head is immediately filled with vapors so that he falls into a slumber. No less effective will be mandrake water, extracted with boiling water in a bath, provided the drinker is not offended by the smell. ANOTHER more violent <note>meaning more potent or "stronger"</note> means for sleep is made from these: let choice opium be prepared with an equal weight of *methella* nut <note>likely *Datura metel*, or thorn-apple</note> and the seed of black henbane; let it be dissolved in lettuce juice, or more excellently in water; it ought to be left for a little while buried under dung <note>a common alchemical method for maintaining steady, gentle heat</note>. Then, fit it into suitable distillation vessels. When it begins to boil, draw off the water, save the dregs, and dry them in hot ashes so that you may turn them into a fine powder by shaking through a sieve. Then prepare a sweet lye <note>alkaline solution</note> from the water, and a sharp one; then let all the vapor fly off by fire, and let the residue be mixed with the previously reserved water. Let it be prepared for eating or drinking, not in the same quantity, but in a smaller amount than what was laid down; to have taken this is plenty, and it should not be shown to anyone unless great necessity urges it. OR mandrake water, opium, and poppy seed may be mixed with garlic or other things that strike the head: of which a portion equal to the size of a bean is enough to have taken. It is also made thus from all these:

->Concerning certain marvelous experiments, the true causes of which cannot be known, nor do they always respond to testing.<-

### CHAPTER XXI.

I have judged that I would be doing something worthwhile if I described many remaining experiments, wonderful to hear and see, which are all the more marvelous because the knowledge of their cause can by no means be grasped. In this, I know for certain that I shall fall into the disgrace of the ignorant, since these things seem almost impossible to happen, nor do they respond so easily to experience. As we have received them from the Ancients, so shall we set them forth: for we add many things so that they may have some small measure of Truth. But lest anyone should perhaps think our words are not to be believed at all, I would wish indeed (which would be better for them) that they first undertake this task of labor, testing for so long until they obtain experience: let them pluck the sayings scattered from all sides, adapt them to Use, and give diligent ears to the words of those who have explored them: for they would find greater things and harder to believe, and would recognize that I have reported the Truth. Authors write that there is nothing in the sea more cursed than this <note>The author likely refers to the sea-hare (Aplysia), often cited in classical texts like Pliny as a source of potent and dangerous magic</note>, which works many wonders. Among these, it possesses this quality: if you apply your mind to it, wherever there is soft earth or a garden, and an old woman urines there, immediately fix a sting or thorn there so that you hide it completely. You will prevent her from urinating for as long as it takes for you to dig a trench and remove it; for you will delay her for a few days, and she will urinate by example <note>original: "exemplò mejet" - likely meaning she will only be able to trickle or mimic the action without relief</note>. In this way, young men often restrain the urine of old women if they have forbidden them the use of something.

### If you wish that guests at a banquet do not eat while reclining,

it is done thus: and although it may have less truth to it, I shall not omit it. Take that needle with which the dead are often sewn up when wrapped in linens, and which has often pierced them; secretly fix it under the table at the start of the dinner. This prevents the guests from eating, so that those reclining are weary of the dinner rather than having any will to eat or sit. Remove it once you have mocked them a little.

Nor should I pass over what Florentinus <note>A 3rd-century Greek writer on agriculture whose work 'Georgica' contributed to the later Byzantine 'Geoponica'</note> handed down in his *Agricultural Works*, nor has experience proven it entirely false; therefore, I have not scorned to write it down. 

### If you wish that women do not eat:

Take Basil <term>Ocymum: the herb Basil, often associated with strange properties in ancient botany</term>, since we have often noticed it to work, and to be so opposed to women that if someone, without her knowing, places the whole plant with its roots under a dish of food, the woman does not dare to touch the food, or does so with difficulty, unless the basil is removed.

and you may try it if you wish: Have the noose of a hanged thief; when you have it, tie it to that part of the tool <note>The "pala" or baker's peel</note> which enters the oven. If the baker seeks to put the bread into the oven, running to and fro, he will never find the way; and if he does put it in, it will be cast out by the shovel, which will seem very wonderful, though less than true. # SECOND BOOK

Philostratus says: If a mirror is shown to me while I am leaning over, it indicates that hail is about to pass by. <note>Philostratus was a Greek sophist; this refers to a folk belief in the sympathetic power of reflective surfaces.</note> Palladius, however, claims that if someone carries the skin of a hyena, a crocodile, or a seal <note>original: "Vituli marini" or sea-calf</note> through the boundaries of their property, or hangs it in the farmhouse or courtyards when you see evil threatening, the hail will not fall. Also, if you carry a marsh turtle <note>original: "palmistrem testudinem"</note> on its back in your right hand while walking through a vineyard, and having returned, you place it on the ground in the same way and throw clods of earth over the curve of its back so that it cannot turn over but remains on its back, it will confront the cloud and restrain the threats of the hail.

We have excerpted these things from the monuments of the ancients: however, how unseemly (if I may say so with their kind permission), impossible, and worthy of laughter they may be, we leave to be considered by the ingenious. More naturally, however, the loud ringing of bells or the noise of war machines <note>artillery or cannons</note> might be able to hinder [the hail] to some extent: for by beating and cutting through the air, they might perhaps dissipate and drive away that consistency of the clouds. Many think this should also be done during plagues, so that the density of the clouds does not linger any longer.

Democritus, moreover, says that rains and thunders happen suddenly if the head and neck of a chameleon are burned with the wood called oak <note>original: "robur"</note>, and that the same thing happens if the liver of the same animal is used on the highest roof tiles. However, Gellius thinks that Pliny branded him with the mark of such ridiculous vanity, rather than it being written by Democritus, that most noble philosopher.

But if you wish for men to be tormented by a long dancing fit, or by laughter, weeping, singing, and other affections, this can rightly happen, and it has more natural causes. ## NATURAL MAGIC

*In the regions of Apulia <note>A region in Southern Italy</note> there is a creature called the tarantula; indeed, the entire province suffers so greatly from them that very few people escape. Their bite is more painful than the sting of a wasp, and the men who are struck by them are afflicted with various symptoms: for many sing perpetually, others weep, or fall into a delirium; but almost all of them dance. For the reapers, while they are bent over their work and unaware of the danger, are often sharply bitten. They are soothed by the application of musical melodies that soften the spirit, and by these means they are restored to health. These creatures live in small burrows which they build among the crops. You may catch them easily in this way: fashion a whistle with your mouth using the leaves of a reed so that you imitate the sound of a fly. When the animal hears this, it emerges immediately, for it often feeds upon them, just as the spiders do who weave vast webs in our houses and spread them for flies. Once captured, you should properly turn it into a powder. A tiny amount of this—as much as one can grasp with two fingers—should be mixed with other powders so that the smell does not offend, for it is a poison. When someone has swallowed this, they will be stirred to dancing and frolicking, especially if they are enticed by sounds. But if you wish that*

### A person with a hernia should have crackling testicles

These will give off such a crackling sound that he will be forced to withdraw from that place. Does this happen because the air he belches out is similar to that wind which is also accustomed to harm them? But if you are seeking to discover whether*

# BOOK TWO

<page-num>74</page-num>

We know clearly from the writings of the ancients that they did not put forth these things rashly, such as certain experiments regarding stones which we have often admired and often laughed at. This magnet stone <note>The lodestone or natural magnet.</note>, if placed under the head of a sleeping wife, will cause her to hold her husband in sweet embraces if she is chaste; but if she is not, she is cast out of the bed as if pushed by a hand. 

But since we have happened upon the mention of this stone, and even if its fame is very well known, we have thought it right to bring into the open the pleasant experiments we know concerning it; nor would it be incongruous if I connect them to this series. Lucretius <note>Titus Lucretius Carus, Roman poet and philosopher.</note> thinks the magnet is named after Magnesia; others call it the "Heraclian" stone after the city of Heraclea, or after Hercules; but it is called *sideritis* <note>original: "sideritin"</note> because it draws iron, for among the Greeks *sideros* <note>original: "σιδηρος"</note> means iron. Indeed, it attracts iron with such greed that it often happens that onlookers are amazed, and for this reason it was said by Anaxagoras <note>A Pre-Socratic Greek philosopher.</note> to be animated <note>Possessing a soul or life-force.</note>. 

If you arrange it above and at the sides, the iron will stand hanging in the air, even though it is supported by nothing from below, nor bound by any visible connection from above. For this reason, Dinocrates the Architect had begun to vault the temple of Arsinoë at Alexandria so that her statue, made of iron, would appear to hang in the air within it. The Greeks also say that in the arches of the temple of Serapis at Alexandria, a magnet was fixed which held a hanging bronze statue, because it had iron in its head. 

Nor does the magnet only attract iron itself, but it pours its power into it, so that you may see the drawn object drawing another, and then another, and thus that virtue is propagated so that often ten rings touching each other are drawn, appearing at times like a chain of rings, and they cling more stubbornly than they can be torn away. Such is the power of attraction within it, that it draws not only through contact, but from a great distance: for if the iron stands in equilibrium—

# ON NATURAL MAGIC

If there should be an obstruction in the rotations, and the stone is moved beneath a solid table the thickness of a palm, you will see the iron move, following the stone. From this, no small deceptions arise; for women often see a little figure made of fennel-stalk or wax moved here and there in a vial by impostors, believing it to be a demon providing answers to their doubts by pointing its finger at a written alphabet. Or they may see a waxen boat in a large bowl full of water, moving with oars, and they falsely claim it to be a form of <term>hydromancy</term><note>Divination by means of water.</note>. How many wonders lie hidden in the prodigious bosom of Nature! It possesses an even more excellent power: for if an iron needle is rubbed with it and you release it to balance freely, as if it were sentient, it directs itself toward the <term>meridian</term><note>The great circle passing through the celestial poles and the zenith of a given location.</note> passing through the pole of the world. By its convenience and use, such vast seas are plowed, with it showing the way. Indeed, our ancestors sailed by observing the Sun by day and the stars by night; for otherwise, wandering in the middle of the sea, they could not perceive the regions of the world. Many attempt to give a reason for this kind of power, and they say that the iron is drawn because the magnet is much superior to iron in its alignment with the celestial Bear <note>The constellation Ursa Major or Minor, associated with the North Pole.</note>. Or, because it cannot descend to the earth due to its weight, and this is denied by some other impediment, then, fleeing the violence of circular motion since it can achieve it in no other way than by becoming the axis of the world—for in such a way it is not twisted by any motion of the heavens—the iron, having been touched by that part of the stone, directs itself toward the North. Likewise, just as there are opposite parts in the position of the stone, so the iron touched by them is directed toward the South. For this reason, one must be most diligent: for if you do not know the true line from South to North by experiment, the further it is removed from it, the more it will be bent toward the West or the East. We have also seen...

We have found by a not unpleasant chance an experiment whereby it separates white sand from black, or sand notable by some other distinction; and perhaps this was an experiment of the ancients, that the magnet and iron would draw sand, oil, and everything. Finally, by its convenience, people speak to one another across long distances and deliver messages simultaneously.

### An Experiment
Let the shavings of a **jet stone** <note>original: "Gagatis lapidis"; jet is a type of lignite coal often used in jewelry and mourning beads</note> (which is common among us, and from which we fashion small beads for prayer rosaries to count or complete prayers) be pounded with a mortar, or the stone itself, and passed through a hair sieve to be reduced to a very fine powder. ## To Produce Variegated Cattle or Horses

Seeking to bring forth such offspring, he achieved what I bid all who desire the same to imitate: He took rods and branches of poplar and almond, and those which can easily be stripped of their bark; he carved them, stripping the bark in sections, encircled and curved with rings, appearing like snakes distinguished by white or black color. These he fixed in the channels near the waters, in the pastures, and in the stalls where the flock of sheep was stabled, so that during the act of mating and while looking around, they would behold nothing but the rods. Thus, the offspring they produced were diversely colored, and here and there the white fleeces were darkened with dusky spots, and so the wool-bearing animals were varied to the delight of the sight. This is also very effective in horses, and those who have such care observe it most closely; when they admit the mares to mating, they adorn the stables where they complete the union with tapestries and cloths depicted with various colors. From this, they produce horses of different colors: dappled, chestnut <term>spadices: a term for a bright bay or chestnut color, derived from the Greek word for a palm branch</term>, and diversely colored. ### How monsters are produced, and concerning the wonderful power
### of putrefaction. CHAPTER 24.

THERE is no easier way to produce monsters than from the things mentioned above. However, if anyone should greatly desire it, we shall reveal many ways of producing such things. In the beginning, Democritus <note>A Greek philosopher (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) known for his atomic theory.</note> thought that from the mixing of many seeds—when this one is cast in first and that one later into the parts of the womb, and they are confused together—animals with conflicting limbs are born, such as a two-headed man or multi-limbed creatures. <margin>Africa always brings something new</margin>
It is said that Africa always brings forth something new <note>original: "Aliquid Africam afferre," a reference to the proverb "Ex Africa semper aliquid novi," meaning Africa is always producing something strange.</note> because, due to the lack and scarcity of water in those parched regions, animals are forced to gather together from afar in one place for the sake of drinking. Thus, either driven by the pleasure of mating or by the various mixing of seeds in that place, diverse forms of animals are born from time to time. Such offspring are not considered so miraculous in the regions of Africa, since their birth is familiar to the inhabitants; therefore, use these as examples for procreating new forms. **ALSO**, I read in Aelian <note>Claudius Aelianus, a 2nd-century Roman author who wrote on natural history.</note> that there was a shepherd at Sybaris named Crathis, who, having fallen desperately in love with the most beautiful of all she-goats and being sharply tormented by the sting of lust, would couple with her in a strange manner and embrace her like a girlfriend, not without frequent kissing. This same goat-loving shepherd would offer her the most joyful and pleasant fodder so that her breath might become sweeter to him. They say that from this brutish intercourse an infant was born, who resembled the mother in its legs, but the father in its face. **A CHICKEN**, however, mixed from different species will result in this way: you shall see to it that a male wild pigeon mates with a hen, and a chick will emerge that is not unpleasant to look upon. This will also happen with partridges, hens, pheasants, and various hawks and peacocks. The hen will give a mixed offspring, very much like herself and like the one from whom she received the fertile seed. But if a mother bird is lacking, it shall be provided for in this way:

Let nests be built with feathers spread beneath them and placed all around. Let the more pointed part of the eggs face upward, and every twenty-four hours, with the dung being renewed by turns...

I am not speaking of those which have their birth in their own waters through the legitimate order of nature—that is, from mating—but those which are born of themselves and are called "temporary." These are generated only by summer rain in the dusty sands of shores and paths, and their life is short. Or often, by the wrath of the winds raging upon the high ridges of mountains, dust is lifted and, mixed with waters, is condensed not only into frogs but into stones. Phylarchus <note>A Greek historian of the 3rd century BCE</note> is the authority that it has sometimes rained frogs; and Heraclides Lembus reports that the same happened around Dardania and Paeonia, and so copiously that houses and roads were filled. And Aelian <note>A Roman author and teacher of rhetoric</note>, when he was traveling from Naples in Italy to Puteoli, saw frogs of which the part pertaining to the head was crawling and moving on two feet, while the other part, not yet formed, was dragged along like a mass of muddy moisture; from the same body, one part was alive and the other part was still earth. Macrobius relates that in Egypt, mice are born from the earth and rain, and in other places frogs, serpents, and similar creatures, which is why their procreation is easy. For when a certain man had spat, a frog was immediately created from the spit; and Daumatus the Spaniard, as often as he pleased, produced a very great abundance of frogs on the spot. Thus, the slime <note>original: "virus"; here meaning a potent biological secretion or discharge</note> taken from the mating of boars and sows—bloody in the manner already described—at the beginning of the Sun entering Capricorn, so that they may cast it out at the departure of the Fish <note>referring to the astrological transition from Pisces</note>, while they are being sated with plenty of milk and crumbs during their heat; and having gathered that which they call *apriam* <note>a term for boar-heat secretion</note>, which is the same as the *hippomanes* <note>a substance famously used in ancient philters and magic, usually associated with mares</note> in horses, it is stored in a glass vessel under dung and covered so that the rising heat does not escape, and it lies hidden for some days, and once gathered...

## Concerning the Lyre, and certain of its wonderful properties.
### CHAPTER XXV.

The Lyre <note>The author uses "Lyra" to refer generally to stringed instruments and the mathematical harmony they represent</note> retains within itself several powers, properties, and observations which we have deemed worthwhile to bring into the open; although it is not hidden from me that the common herds and the "know-it-alls" <note>original: "sciolos"; those with superficial knowledge</note> will not give them credit. Even if some of them think these writings of ours are "lyre-ravings" <note>original: "delyria"; a pun on the word "deliria" (madness) and "lyra" (the instrument)</note>, I know nevertheless that I shall greatly please the devotees of secret letters <term>arcanarum literarum: the study of esoteric or occult texts</term>, and those who have their minds deeply devoted to investigating the wonders of nature. I do not write of unknown things. It is certain that rhythms and melodies <note>original: "modulos"</note> have power over men; there is no heart so harsh or wild that it is not softened by fitting melodies and songs that soothe the soul, or conversely, is not distressed and constricted by those that are indecent and discordant. ->Musaeus<- <note>An 11th-century BCE legendary Greek poet and polymath.</note> declares that music is the sweetest thing to mortals; and it is noted by the followers of Plato that everything which lives is soothed by music, and many effects of it are seen. In war, the drums bellow to serve as encouragement and to rouse those who are sluggish, and similar practices are found to have been frequently done by the ancients. Whenever the musician Timotheus <note>A famous Greek musician and poet known for his innovations in the cithara.</note> pleased, he would play a Phrygian melody <note>The Phrygian mode was considered by the ancients to be exciting, warlike, and emotionally intense.</note> and so inflame the mind of Alexander the Great that, driven wild, he would rush to arms; and when Timotheus wished otherwise, he would change the tune, draining away that boldness and making his mind sluggish, drawing him back, softened, from weapons to feasts and banquets. And Plutarch says: when he himself had heard Antigenidas <note>A celebrated flute player from Thebes.</note> playing the flute in the modes called "Chariot-songs," he was so inflamed that he rose up with his weapons and laid hands on those sitting nearest to him.

Cicero reports that Pythagoras <note>The famous Greek philosopher and mathematician who studied the harmony of the spheres.</note> once encountered a young man from Tauromenium who was soaked with wine and ensnared by a harlot's love. When this youth, incited by a Phrygian melody, sought to burn down the house of his rival where the girl was being kept, Pythagoras rendered him peaceful and milder by having a spondaic rhythm <note>A slow, solemn musical meter consisting of long syllables.</note> played. He likewise said: if young men, driven by the sound of flutes, attempt some crime, they are distracted if the flute-player plays a spondee, so that their raging petulance is settled by the gravity of the rhythms. When a guest attacked Empedocles <note>A Pre-Socratic philosopher who believed in the four elements.</note> after being provoked by quarrels and insults, Empedocles turned back the mode of singing and tempered the man's raging anger. Theophrastus <note>The successor to Aristotle in the Peripatetic school.</note> is said to have employed musical sounds to restrain the disturbances of the soul. And Agamemnon, when departing from his homeland to set out for Troy, doubting the chastity of Clytemnestra, left behind a harper who so incited her to continence and modesty with his sound that Aegisthus <note>The lover of Clytemnestra who eventually murdered Agamemnon.</note> could not enjoy her until after the harper had been killed.

could have done. The Thracian Orpheus, through the playing of his lyre, moved savage nations who were as unyielding as stone and devoid of reason. Arion the lyre-player won over the dolphins—creatures lacking human reason—so that they carried him safely to the shore after he had been cast into the sea. Indeed—what is much more wonderful—wise antiquity consoled wounds and diseases with melodies, as is gathered from the histories. Terpander and Arion of Methymna cured the Lesbians and Ionians of grievous illnesses. Asclepiades the physician used music to heal the deaf among the crowds and quelled the seditions of the people with song. Hermenias the Theban wiped away the pains of the hips <note>likely sciatica or gout</note> for many of the Boeotians with melodies. Xenocrates cured the "lymphatic" <note>those suffering from madness or water-borne ailments</note> with instrumental sounds. Thales of Crete put a plague to flight with his lyre, and Herophilus weighed the pulses of the sick <note>original: "venas... pensiculabat," referring to the ancient practice of measuring the pulse rhythmically</note> according to musical numbers. 

Thus, the ancients applied specific melodies to specific conditions: just as the **Dorian** mode bestows wisdom, chastity, and learning; the **Phrygian** excites battles and inflames fury (which the flute also does). Therefore, Aristoxenus <note>A Greek philosopher and music theorist</note> in his accounts turned to the Phrygian harmony for things the Dorian was unable to achieve, as it was more suited to them. The **Lydian** mode sharpens the intellect of the dull, and for those weighed down by earthly desire, it induces a longing for the heavens, as handed down by Aristotle in his *Politics*. ### A Lyre for Inducing Sleep

*It is reported, and has been experienced by many, that this may be achieved through sweet and pleasant harmony. Let it be done in this way: prepare the material from softer woods so that it produces a sweeter and more relaxed melody—such as fir and ivy, where the lower part is joined from one and the upper from the other. The strings are to be made from "tyri" <note>likely referring to a specific type of gut or fiber</note> and serpents, specifically from the intestines or the membrane touching the spinal marrow. When this instrument is struck by the fingers and begins to sound, it produces a charming, soft, and not unpleasant tone, winning over the listeners with a pleasing slumber, so that they will close their eyes against their will in a gentle sleep. This should not seem marvelous, if it is also said that the Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, who believed in the healing power of musical mathematics</note> achieved this: when they wished to dissolve various cares in sleep, they used certain songs so that a light and quiet sleep would creep in. Upon waking and rising immediately from bed, they would use other specific melodies to purge the stupor and the confusion of sleep, preparing themselves to undertake some duty. It is handed down that this occurs with the Aeolian mode <note>A musical scale or "mood" in ancient Greek music theory</note>, because it calms the tempests of the soul and grants sleep to those who are pacified. There is also another truly marvelous thing: its sound is said to be a present "atocium" <note>original: "atocium"; likely a Greek-derived term for a remedy against difficult labor or perhaps a contraceptive, though here used in a broader medicinal or soothing sense</note>. For while it glides through the ears to the soul—the reason why things are expelled by its sound may be left to the judgment of those who believe it. Lest these things be a stumbling block to some, it would be more prudent to omit them. You shall receive these experiments, however, concerning the common lyre.*

### On the Lyre, which when struck moves another of the same tone lying nearby:

*Let the strings be stretched together, and let each one reach the same and perfect melody; if you strike one of the deep strings with your fingers, the other vibrates <note>original: "roboat," likely a variant of "reboare," to echo or resound</note> and the deep string in that one is moved, and likewise for the high strings: provided there is a proper proximity. If this should not be particularly conspicuous to the eye, place a piece of straw upon it, and you will see it move. Furthermore, Suetonius Tranquillus <note>A Roman historian (c. 69 – c. 122 AD) known for "The Twelve Caesars"; the "Book of Games" or "Ludicra Historia" mentioned here is a lost work</note> says in his book of the history of games, that on a wintry day, when some strings on an instrument are struck by the fingers, others sound. From this, even someone ignorant of sound could tune a lyre: if one string remains at rest, equally stretched and brought to harmony, by tightening, loosening, and striking the other with the hands until the resting string moves—a sign of the same tone—and so on for the rest. If, however, you wish that*

-> A deaf person might hear the sound of the Lyre <-

*You should cover your ears with your hands so that you do not hear the sound; then grip the neck of the lyre or cithara <note>A professional version of the lyre used in Ancient Greece</note> with your teeth. Let another person strike it, and it will produce a harmonious sound within the brain, and perhaps a sweeter one. Not only by catching the neck with the teeth, but even a very long spear that touches the lyre allows the sound to be heard clearly through it; and it could be said that the sense of hearing does not perceive it, but rather the sense of taste. There remains this, which I think is by no means unpleasant, that*

### Lyres, citharas, and other instruments may be played by the wind,

*You shall accomplish it thus: when there is a vast storm of winds, you shall arrange the instruments facing the opposing region, such as citharas, pipes <term>tibia: an ancient flute-like reed instrument</term>, harps <term>sambuca: a high-pitched stringed instrument of Oriental origin</term>, or pan-pipes <term>fistula: a simple pipe or shepherd's flute</term>. For the incoming wind rushes with force, strikes lightly, and runs through the gaping reeds: from which, through the sounding of them all, you will perceive a most sweet harmony in nearby ears, and you will be delighted.*

# NATURAL MAGIC

### How to induce clear and pleasant, or dark and fearful dreams. CHAPTER XXVI.

Food, through the process of digestion (which must be considered a proven fact), is dissolved and melted into a vapor. It is only natural that this vapor, joined with heat, should turn into a light substance. Since its nature is to be carried upward, it rises to some extent and overflows through the veins into the brain. There, because the nature of the brain is perpetually stiff with cold—much like the generation of rain often seen in the greater world <note>The "greater world" refers to the Macrocosm or the universe, comparing the human body (the microcosm) to the Earth's atmosphere.</note>—it becomes moist and turns into a mist. Thus, by an internal cycle, it is taken back again and carried down to the heart, the home of the primary sense. Meanwhile, it fills the head and makes it heavy, so that one is submerged in a deep sleep. 

If it happens that one is more heavily drugged with sleep in the dead of night, the images are interrupted by the meeting of others as they descend, so that they appear distorted and monstrous. But if it is in the morning—when the excremental and foul blood has been separated from the pure and useful, and has itself cooled and ceased to boil—the visions shown are pure, complete, and pleasing. 

Therefore, we do not consider it irrational that when the sleeping faculty languishes, swollen by immoderate drink, and the vapors of food are raised by nature, those things which abound in the body should leap up and be tossed about immoderately in sleep. For example, one might see various fires, darkness, hail, or rot, arising from yellow or black bile, or from cold and putrid humors. Likewise, to dream of slaying someone or being impiously covered in blood indicates an abundance of the blood humor. Indeed, both Hippocrates and Galen <note>Famous ancient Greek physicians whose theories of the "four humors" dominated medicine for centuries.</note> agree that a prediction of one's physical temperament can be drawn from these dreams. Hence, those eating flatulent foods <note>Foods that cause gas or "wind" in the body.</note> experience monstrous images due to the turbulent force of the vapors which are similarly raised; but if the exhalations are thin and moderate, they gladden the soul with graceful and healthy visions.

It suggests that the timing of sleep and the quality of food directly influence whether dreams are monstrous or pleasant.</summary>
