*So that by gathering many of these ingredients together they may compose*
->**The Ointments of the Witches,**<-
<note>original: "Lamiarum vnguenta" - Lamiae were mythical female demons, often used interchangeably with witches in the Renaissance.</note>
Although these women mix a great deal of superstition into their craft, it is clear to an observer that the effects occur through natural power. I shall relate what I have learned from them. They obtain the fat of boys by boiling it in a bronze vessel with water, thickening that which remains as the final residue of the boiling; this they store and keep for constant use. With this fat they mix wild celery, monkshood <note>original: "aconitum" - a highly toxic flowering plant</note>, poplar leaves, and soot. **Or otherwise in this manner:** Water-parsnip, common sweet flag, cinquefoil, the blood of a bat, sleep-inducing nightshade <note>original: "solanum somniferum"</note>, and oil. Even if they mix different ingredients, they will not differ much from these. They prepare them all together and rub all their limbs, having first rubbed them vigorously so that they grow red and heat is restored, and that which was hardened by cold becomes loosened. So that the flesh is relaxed and the pores opened, they add the fat (or oil acting in its place) so that the power of the juices may descend within and become stronger and more vigorous; I have no doubt that this is the cause of the effect. Thus, on that night, they seem to be carried through the air to banquets, sounds, dances, and the company of beautiful youths, which they desire most of all. So great is the force of the imagination and the habit of these impressions, that the part of the brain called the memory is filled with such things. Since they are themselves naturally prone to belief, they take in these impressions so deeply that their very spirits are altered, thinking of nothing else day and night. They are aided in this by their diet, as they eat nothing but beets, roots, chestnuts, and legumes. While I was devoting my efforts to investigating these things more carefully—for I was lingering in a state of doubtful judgment—I happened upon a certain little old woman, of the kind they call "striges" <note>original: "striges" - a term for witches derived from the strix, a mythical nocturnal screech-owl said to feed on human flesh and blood.</note> from their resemblance to the nocturnal bird...

They call them *Striges* <note>original: "Striges"; a term for owl-like spirits or witches believed to suck the blood of infants</note>, those who by night suck the blood of little children from their cradles. She promised of her own accord that she would bring me answers within a short space of time. She ordered everyone who had been summoned with me as witnesses to go outside; then, stripped of all her clothing, she rubbed herself thoroughly with a certain ointment, while we watched through the chinks of the door. Thus, by the power of these sleep-inducing juices, she fell and sank into a profound sleep. We then opened the doors from the outside and gave her a sound beating, yet the power of the sleep was so great that it took away all sensation. We returned to our place outside. Now, as the strength of the remedy began to fade and weaken, she was called back from sleep and began to utter many delusions—claiming she had crossed seas and mountains—and producing false answers. When we denied her claims, she insisted; even when we showed her the bruises from the beating, the witch stubbornly resisted the truth. But what do I think of such women? A place for telling that will be granted elsewhere; let our discourse return to the intended purpose, for we have been quite long-winded enough. Furthermore, I think this warning should be given, lest those experimenting be easily misled: these things will not happen equally to everyone, but especially to those of a melancholic temperament <note>In the theory of the four humors, "melancholy" was associated with coldness, dryness, and a tendency toward vivid imagination or depression</note>, since they are by nature very cold and chilly, and their evaporation of spirits is not great; for they correctly perceive what they see and are able to report it.

### How love may be won, and concerning things that retain the power of a love potion.

### CHAPTER XXVII.

It was our resolve from the beginning of this work to perform all things only by natural means, and especially those things which occur through the works of impious mages, in order to utterly destroy their "science." For they ensnare the minds of mortals in these traps and coverings of error, since a great part of humanity persists in this, and by deceitful—

# SECOND BOOK

I see many people being tormented by its deceptive allure, their minds captured by wonder, and their very intellects—those that are elevated and most eager for learning—slipping toward ruin. It will not trouble us to recount the enticements of love as best we can, without departing at all from the boundaries of nature; I ask that my readers take this in good part. To begin, then, it is worth knowing that the human mind is directed toward nothing else but the effort to kindle the small sparks of love in the souls of men or women, to make them gentler and ready to obey our will. When we deal with things in which a hidden power of action lies, we make use of them: some were handed down by our ancestors and proven to us by experience, and many others have been acquired through the industry of more recent men. 

First among these is <term>Hippomanes</term> <note>A substance legendary in antiquity for its use in love philters; the name literally translates from Greek as "horse-madness."</note>, which was praised to the stars in ancient times, although there have been those who claimed these were merely the fictions of common women and crazy fables. These skeptics were perhaps devoted to logical demonstrations and refused to give credit to the prodigious miracles of nature and their hidden causes; however, evident experience contradicts them. 

Authorities establish two types of this substance. One is a fluid dripping from the private parts of mares when they are driven by an excessive fury of lust. Our poet <note>Referring to Virgil.</note> sang of it thus in his <term>Georgics</term> <note>A poem by Virgil concerning agriculture and the natural world.</note>:

> From here at last (that which the shepherds truly call
> Hippomanes by name) a viscous fluid drips from the groin,
> Hippomanes, which wicked stepmothers have often gathered,
> Mixing in herbs and harmful incantations.

->As found in the works of <term>Tibullus</term> <note>A Roman elegiac poet.</note>:<-

> Hippomanes drips from the groin of the lustful mare.

Its application is not ineffective, and we have described its use and its powers elsewhere when the occasion demanded. The second type, however, is the size of a dried fig, circular in appearance, somewhat broad, and in color—

# NATURAL MAGIC

As soon as the foal is born, a blackish piece of flesh clings to its forehead. The mother, having given birth, immediately devours the afterbirth; then, forgetting her labors, she licks and wipes the foal, tearing away that piece of flesh called the *hippomanes* <note>original: "hippomanes"; a legendary substance believed to be a powerful aphrodisiac found on the heads of newborn foals</note>. If someone should snatch this away beforehand, the mother will not admit the foal to her udders; she hates it and drives it away as a stranger, never to love it. The Poet <note>referring to Virgil</note> rightly perceived this same thing in the *Aeneid*:

> It is sought for, torn from the forehead of the newborn horse,
> A love snatched away from the mother. <note>original: "Quæritur, &amp; nascentis equi de fronte reuulsus, / Et matri præreptus amor." - Virgil, Aeneid 4.515-516</note>

Therefore, they thought it not absurd that love is created from that flesh, that it is a snare for the heart, and that a love-poison is born within it. And as Pausanias relates, nor does Aelian omit the fact, Phormis the Arcadian knew such power in this substance at Olympia that he cast a bronze mare mixed with it. He depicted the mare with its tail cut off, where he had enclosed the *hippomanes*. The craftsmanship was not so accurate that horses should have been deceived by it to such an extent, yet at the sight of it, they were driven by such furies: for it so enticed them, and they were led by such madness, that breaking their reins, they would leap upon the bronze statue more violently than upon a beautiful and living mare. Nor do they despair of mating because their hooves slip upon the bronze base; rather, with gaping mouths and much more ardently, they neigh over it, and cannot be driven from the bronze image until they are dragged away by whips and the great strength of their handlers. From this, the name *hippomanes* <note>original: "ἱππομάνες"; from the Greek 'hippos' (horse) and 'mania' (madness)</note> was given to the poison, because by its likeness to equine desire, it induces love in men and makes them rage unbridled for Venus <note>the goddess of love; here meaning sexual desire</note>. Men of no small authority report that shepherds have deep knowledge of this, and if they wish to prepare a love-snare for someone, so that he may burn with the flame of love and be twisted by the wasting disease of desire for a beloved woman,

### On Fascination, and how one can be ensnared by it, and amulets against fascination.

#### CHAPTER XXVIII.

Now we must discuss fascination <note>The "Evil Eye" or the power to harm others through a look or speech</note>, and who the bewitchers were must not be passed over in silence. For if we care to run through the monuments of the ancients, we find many such things have already been handed down to posterity, while new events of a more recent age support the ancient reputation as not entirely vain. Nor did I think it necessary to detract from the faith of histories if we cannot fit the true causes of the matter to valid reasons; especially since there are many things that utterly defy investigation. However, what I myself have perceived from other operations, I have led forth to be explained in public. You will find many things in the poets Theocritus and Virgil, whence:

> I know not what eye fascinates my tender lambs.
<note>original: "Nescio quis teneros oculus mihi fascinat agnos." A quote from Virgil's Eclogues.</note>

By their gaze and mere presence, these victims waste away into diseases which kill them and dissolve them into consumption. *These spirits arise from the heat of the heart and from the purest blood; because they are the lightest, they reach the highest part of the body, and they fall forth and are darted out through the eyes, which are the most porous and full of small veins, and the brightest of all parts. Together with the spirit itself, a certain fiery force is emitted in rays, so that those who look upon red and bleary eyes are forced to suffer from a similar disease. This very thing once caused me harm. For it infects the air, and the infected air infects another; thus, the air closest to the eyes carries with it the vapor of corrupted blood, by whose contagion the eyes of the beholder are affected with a similar redness. In this way the wolf takes away the voice, and the basilisk <note>original: "regulus," literally "little king," a mythical serpent said to kill with a glance</note> takes away life, for it stirs up poison with its gaze and strikes virulent blows with its rays—though if a mirror is used, these are thrown back upon their author by a reciprocal darting. In this same way, a polished mirror dreads the gaze of an unclean woman <note>a reference to the medieval belief that a menstruating woman's gaze could stain a mirror</note>, as Aristotle claims, and by her look it becomes filthy and its brightness is dulled. This happens because the bloody vapor gathers into a unity upon the surface of the mirror due to its smoothness, so that it is covered with a kind of small film and is clearly shown. Indeed, if the mirror is new, you will wash it off with great difficulty; this does not happen on cloth or stone because on the former the vapor creeps along and descends into the depths, while on the latter it is dissipated by the unevenness of the parts. But because the mirror stands as a stubborn obstacle, and because it is polished it preserves what is reflected, and because it is cold it produces droplets from the condensed air. In much the same way, if you breathe upon clear glass, the surface is moistened by a dewy spray of saliva, and the thin part flying out falls back into saliva. Thus the flow of rays from the eyes, which are the vehicles of the spirits, strikes through to the eyes of the person encountered and affects the inner parts of the person struck, seeking its own proper region, since they arise from the heart: thus they themselves, at the edges of the heart,*

They are forced into the blood; this foreign blood, discordant with the nature of the fascinated man, consumes the rest with a wasting disease <note>Latin: tabe; a slow, consuming sickness often associated with melancholy or infection</note>, from which he falls ill. The contagion persists as long as the power of the tainted blood thrives in the limbs. Since this is an affection of the blood, it is observed as a fever that never lets up; if it were rooted in bile or phlegm <note>The author refers to the four humors; bile and phlegm were thought to cause intermittent fevers, whereas blood-based issues were constant</note>, it might perhaps subside at intervals.

However, so that all things may be more clearly revealed, this must first be known: two types of fascination <term>fascinum: the act of enchanting or harming through a look or word</term> are found among the authors. One is of love; the other of envy or ill-will. If indeed one is ensnared by the desire for beauty and the harmony of form, even if the shot is fired from a distance, the poison is drunk in through the eyes. The beautiful specter of that form settles in the lover's heart and kindles little fires, from which he is accustomed to be always afflicted. And because the very soft blood of the beloved wanders there, it represents the face and shines back in his own blood; he finds no rest in himself and is drawn to the other, just as the blood of a wounded man flows toward the one who struck the blow. Lucretius rightly suggests this as follows:

> And the body seeks that source from which the mind is wounded by love,
> For almost all fall toward their wound, and the blood
> Spurts out toward that part from where we are struck by the blow;
> And if the foe is near, the red fluid covers the face.
> <note>Original: "Idque petit corpus..." from Lucretius, De Rerum Natura, Book 4. The poet describes the physical attraction of lovers as a literal physical wound.</note>

Nor can anyone deny that when the soul is ill-affected, the body suffers, and an affected soul strengthens the powers of the body and renders them more potent. It does not only change its own body, but renders another's body changed as well, and it does so in proportion to the interior fires of vengeance or desire stirred in the heart. Do not greed, sorrow, or love change our colors and habits? Does not envy [stain] the body with paleness...

but it also recoils upon the perpetrators themselves, and they draw the very enchantment they cast back onto themselves. It is recorded in ancient writings concerning Eutelidas, who brought the destruction of fascination upon himself, as the author relates, by means of water reflecting back—whether from a mirror or a springing fountain—which twisted the image back upon the viewer.

> Seeing himself in the eddying river, Eutelidas perished from a terrible destruction; immediately a disease of injustice struck him. <note>original: "Εὐτελίδ’α αὑαλ’ βεκαίρειν ἰδὼν ὀλόφησης αἰνης / Δινήεντι ποταμῷ ἄδ’ ἄντικα νῦσος ἀδίκης." This Greek verse describes the myth of Eutelidas, who, like Narcissus, was fascinated by his own reflection.</note>

He appeared so beautiful to himself that he fell by the same enchantment as others do; thus he lost his former physical condition and paid the penalty of a peculiar disease. In this way, boys corrupt themselves by their own fascination, yet parents truly ascribe the cause to sorcerers and enchanters. Now, however, take hold of the disease's

### Protective remedies, <note>term: Alexiteria remedia. From the Greek 'alexiterios', meaning to ward off or protect against evil or poison.</note>

For there are many things which wise antiquity established. If you endeavor to cast off the fascination of love, you shall avert it thus: withdraw your sight, lest she drive the sharpness of her gaze into your eyes, nor let your lights <note>eyes</note> be joined with hers; for you must remove the cause from the place where the affection usually begins. Interrupt the habit of seeing one another, repel idleness, apply the mind to serious cares, and expel blood, sweat, and all excrements <note>bodily fluids</note> in abundance, so that the harmful vapor <note>term: aura. In this context, a subtle, invisible breath or influence believed to carry the contagion of fascination.</note> may be cast out together with them; and the opposites of the first symptoms will also emerge. If it should be the witchcraft of the envious, you will recognize it thus: the person loses color, raises the eyes with difficulty, always looks downward, heaves frequent sighs, the heart is constricted by anguish, and yet no sign of any physical illness is seen; he sheds false and bitter tears. To disenchant him, because the air is contagious and contaminated, scented fumigations should be substituted to restore the air, and no less by sprinkling with waters containing cinnamon, cloves, galingale, aloeswood, musk, and ambergris. Therefore, an ancient custom has propagated itself down to our own age, and women retain this, if

*When they see that children have contracted harm, they fumigate them with incense to purify them and move them in a circular motion. Let them be kept in clear air, and let these stones hang from the neck: carbuncle <note>likely a deep red garnet or ruby</note>, hyacinth <note>a yellow or orange zircon</note>, and sapphire. Dioscorides <note>a Greek physician and botanist of the 1st century AD</note> considers madwort <note>original: "alissum"</note> hung in the house, buckthorn, and valerian <note>original: "phu"</note> to be effective amulets. Scent the area with hyssop and lily. Let a ring be worn made from the hoof of a wild ass or a domestic donkey, as well as satyrion, which is called orchis and "the female." Aristotle approves of rue for its own natural gift. All these things blunt the powers of sorcery. However, those experiments of an uncertain kind which have been proven by experience and seemed closer to the truth, we have written in this book.*

# OF GIOVANNI BATTISTA
## PORTA OF NAPLES
### NATURAL MAGIC
### BOOK III.

### PREFACE.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'I' featuring floral and vine motifs within a square border.</image-desc>

I NOW seem to have arrived at those experiments (while we are carried along to examine the various effects of things) which the common people call chemical. These contain no small amount of profit, a matter upon which not only a great part of mortals is fixed, but for which the entire world burns with an inextinguishable thirst. Therefore, we are compelled to approach them so that we may review some of these matters. It is surely a thing not to be undervalued, but especially to be sought after by those who devote their efforts to the study of Philosophy and search into the secrets of nature; for they shall find many things which they may admire,

<summary>This page concludes the second book on protective amulets and herbal remedies against sorcery before introducing the third book, which focuses on the "chemical" arts and the pursuit of natural secrets for profit and knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>amulets, gemstones, Dioscorides, Aristotle, alchemy, chemistry, Giovanni Battista della Porta, natural philosophy, protection against witchcraft</keywords>

--- Page 230 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's philosophical defense of alchemy while distancing the practice from fraudulent "puffers" or ignorant pretenders.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

These things exist, and are wonderfully necessary for our use, as they will perceive many transformations <note>original: "transmutationes"</note>, and they will rejoice not a little to have seen them: not of those metals, however, which are separated by the greatest interval, but those which are related and neighboring, and differ only by certain accidents. This is something that philosophers of the greatest authority have not been ashamed to admit, as we also see in other natural things which we have mentioned. Now, however, drawn by the vain hope of profit, and under the bait and lure of its continuous pleasure, such unrefined and ignorant men <note>original: "idiotae," referring to those untrained in the philosophical arts</note> approach the work that they handle it to the great reproach of this age, and they render the practitioners of this art hated by all. For while they labor in preparing sophisticated <note>artificial or counterfeit</note> gold, being utterly ignorant of the principles of these things, when they have boiled away their goods they rush into ruin, and are deluded by the vain hope of gold. As Demetrius Phalereus <note>A Greek orator and statesman of the 4th century BCE</note> rightly says: "That which they ought to have taken they did not take, but that which they possessed they have lost." And the metamorphosis <note>transformation</note> which they expect in the metals, they experience in themselves; then (since they find this their only solace) they strive to deceive others with fabricated frauds and to make companions for themselves. Thus they convert the faith of a good thing into a worse use, as we see often happens in higher matters. And so greatly has the desire for the art or for profit increased the number of books and lies, that almost nothing else is circulated; it was for the best that by the order of Diocletian <note>Roman Emperor who famously ordered the burning of alchemical texts in Egypt c. 296 CE to prevent the funding of rebellions</note> they were all burned and turned to ash. But you, who desire to see something, know this: that nature cannot be imitated in all things, and the origins of metals must be rightly understood...

<summary>The author distinguishes between true philosophical transmutation of related metals and the fraudulent practices of ignorant "gold-makers" whose failures lead them to deceive others. He cites the historical burning of alchemical books by Diocletian as a justified response to the proliferation of lies in the field.</summary>

<keywords>transmutation, alchemy, fraud, Demetrius Phalereus, Diocletian, metamorphosis, philosophy of nature, metallic transformation</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page introduces the practical applications of natural magic, specifically focusing on the extraction of essences. It follows a general introduction to the principles of transformation and the limits of alchemy.</meta>

# NATURAL MAGIC

The means must be thoroughly understood; and if these things are sought to be transformed or deeply tinted, they must first be dissolved into their primary elements <note>The four basic building blocks of matter: earth, air, fire, and water.</note>. Do not attempt to achieve those things which are known to be impossible. We shall set down here certain things which may be observed in these matters, and we have spared no small labor in testing and experimenting with them: learn these examples well. We do not promise mountains of gold here, nor that most famous "Philosopher's Stone" <note>A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note> which men imagine, discussed for many centuries and perhaps discovered by a few. Nor do we promise "potable gold" <note>original: "poculentum aurum" — a medicinal liquid gold believed to be a universal cure.</note> by which men might be protected and made immune from death; for it is necessary that in this changeable and alterable world, all things remain subject to transformation. To promise such things would be utterly reckless and without consideration. However, we do not deny that things useful for protecting the health of the human body and prolonging life can indeed be made. These are the things we intend to hand down:

### Concerning the extractions of water and oil, and certain operations which are encountered throughout these works.
### CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut initial 'O' featuring a bird (possibly a phoenix or pelican) perched amidst scrolling foliage and floral patterns.</image-desc>

IT HAS SEEMED FIT to first set forth certain operations which are read about incidentally in this work, so that the things taught may proceed in an orderly fashion, and so they need not be sought elsewhere. And although some may perform these differently, or judge them impossible to do, they nevertheless enter into our work, and we are accustomed to achieve them by these means; if these are unknown, you will not easily attain your goal. Distillat- <note>The text cuts off mid-word; the catchword indicates the next page begins with "distillatio" or distillation.</note>

<summary>The author establishes a realistic foundation for natural magic, rejecting the pursuit of impossible alchemical myths like the Philosopher's Stone in favor of practical medicinal extractions of waters and oils.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, elements, philosopher's stone, potable gold, medicine, distillation, extraction, alchemy</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of chemical processes from the previous section, specifically focusing on the physical apparatus required for distillation.</meta>

# BOOK THREE

Since we have mentioned the work of distillation, or the proper vessel for distillations, this seems the opportune place to speak of it, lest it remain unknown. It is a discovery of certain more recent authors to draw water out of all things, so that we may have pure water without earthiness (as far as can be achieved). For you see: distilled water is not overflowing with any dregs, and many things are more excellently freed from putrefaction <note>In this context, "putrefaction" refers to the chemical breakdown or decay of organic matter to extract its essence.</note>, or if we have need of something to be absorbed. 

First, they prepare a vessel of earthenware, though one of hollow glass is better, swelling like a ball or rather shaped like a top or a pear. It should have a long neck, to which a glass cap is fitted, so that when coals are placed beneath it, the matter is dissolved into thin vapors, fills all the empty spaces, and is carried upward. When this steamy thickness touches the coldness of the cap and encounters the glass, it is forced into dew upon the edges; from there, gliding down the arches of the cap, it falls back into water, and through a certain open channel belonging to it, it flows down in generous streams. A receptacle placed beneath receives it drop by drop. This the chemists <term>chymistæ: early modern practitioners of alchemy and chemistry</term> call a "distillatory" or an **alembic**. 

Many people, lest the stench of the smoke offend those drinking the result, place the apparatus in a vessel of boiling water, and a thinner water is drawn out; they call this a **bath** <note>original: "balneum"; specifically the *balneum Mariae* or water bath, used for gentle heating.</note>. You will also draw off an extremely thin water if you fit these glass vessels into some hollow earthenware pot so that the necks project outward. Then they place a bronze cauldron full of hot water beneath it, so that by the force of the rising steam—which cannot escape elsewhere—the water is ingeniously drawn out of the substances; this will be the superior kind among drinkable waters. Some tie this glass vessel into an empty pot, not touching the sides anywhere, and stop up the mouth, leaving only a hole through which the neck projects outward. Thus the glowing pot, heating the air, dissolves the contents of the vessel into vapors. Choose from these the method that is most convenient.

...and let it suffice to have described this once. There are, furthermore, many vessels which they use, almost infinite in number. However, if the material is stubborn and resistant to distillation, they are accustomed to rot <note>original: "putrefacere"; a process of breaking down organic matter through gentle heat</note> and soak it, sometimes in horse dung—which is always of a consistent heat—renewing it every few days. Sometimes they expose it to the sun by means of a concave mirror, and in this way we ourselves have often extracted water. At other times, they bury it in grape skins, or in ashes and lit juniper coals; for since that wood is dense and very thick, it remains lit for a long time. Now, however, we must proceed to the operations, and first let us show how to extract:

### Oil of Talc,

Which many judge to be difficult, or almost impossible to make; nevertheless, you shall prepare it in this way when there is a need: Put the talc into a small bag with coastal pebbles, or those commonly seen in rivers, and let them be shaken vigorously until it is reduced to a very fine powder. Or, you may accomplish this by other industrious means, for this is how it is customarily done by everyone. Then, place it in a raw <note>unfired</note> clay pot of excessive thickness and strength. Once the lid is placed on the work and secured with iron hoops, and after you have smeared it with potter's clay, leave it in the sun to dry. Afterward, place it in a stone furnace where the flame breaks out, or elsewhere, provided the fire is most violent. When the furnace has ceased to burn, take it out and break the vessel; if you find it has been reduced to lime <note>a powdery mineral residue</note>, you have done well. If not, do not be loath to do it again and expend the same effort. When the lime has become perfectly white, grind it on porphyry marble and place it in another small bag, or on the marble in a very humid place—such as a very deep well or cistern—and let it remain there for a long time. You will see it flow out drop by drop from the excessive humidity. Save this, and in a glass...

### Oil of Sulfur,

Or its water, you shall do it thus: Have a glass vessel with a wide mouth and concave, in the shape of a bell; where you have smeared it with <term>lute</term> <note>a clay sealant used to airtight joints</note>, let it be suspended by a wire from an iron stand. Below, however, place a wide receptacle so that it may receive the oil dripping from the edges of the bell. In the middle of this, let an earthen or iron vessel filled with sulfur be fitted. Then kindle the fire, and while it is being burned, let more fresh sulfur be put on top, for it is consumed while it burns. The rising smoke, striking the bottom of the vessel, becomes bodily through moist exhalations, and turning back, it coalesces into oil and flows down from there. We show this to be excellent for whitening and awakening <note>brightening</note> the teeth. But in fiery mixtures, there is ANOTHER way: for it admits fire and is able to retain it. Take <term>live sulfur</term> <note>native or "virgin" sulfur that has not been previously processed by fire</note>, and mix it in equal portions with oil of juniper, and draw out the oil with fire in glass <term>cucurbits</term> <note>gourd-shaped chemical flasks</note>; use it in necessary matters. ### Oil of Eggs

You shall prepare thus: Place a large pan or another vessel over the fire; into this, throw the <term>yolks</term> <note>original: "boleta"; referring here to the round yolks of eggs</note>, mixing them always with a spatula and with frequent stirring so they do not burn. When they have been turned into a powder, take care that they are squeezed in a press, and keep the expressed oil in a <term>small box</term> <note>original: "pyxide"</note>. Or, you may harden them by boiling, and thus the liquid may be squeezed out. However, in incendiary mixtures, there is ANOTHER way...

*You shall prepare it thus: Mix many egg yolks with half as much live sulfur <note>original: "sulphuris viui," meaning native or raw sulfur</note>, and place them in a pan over the fire. Now, however, let us undertake a more laborious operation, namely, how one may extract*

### Water of Quicksilver <note>Mercury</note>.

*You shall accomplish this diligently in the following way: Prepare an earthen vessel with a rounded, thick belly, but with a neck slightly tapered so that a glass cap <note>original: "vitreum pileum," an alembic head used for distillation</note> can be fitted to the head. Then, smear the joints with potters' clay <note>a technique called "luting" to create an airtight seal</note> so that the quicksilver, vanishing into thin air, cannot escape. Place a vessel beneath the open channel to receive the liquid. Let the main vessel have an opening on the side through which the quicksilver can be introduced. Let the whole vessel be heated until it is glowing red; and when it is glowing, pour in the quicksilver using a funnel or another instrument, and immediately apply chalk <note>to seal the opening</note> so that it fits the part perfectly. For when it has thundered loudly and made a crackling sound, it is forced to dissolve into vapor, and gradually thickening, it glides down into the jar placed beneath.*

### On sublimation, reduction into lime, and other necessary matters in this art. CHAPTER II.

It remains to teach how one ought to sublimate and reduce substances into lime <note>original: "in calcem," referring to calcination or reducing a mineral to a powder through heat</note>, which processes are seen everywhere to be necessary for our operations and follow in a way from what has been said before. These we shall describe very briefly: And first,

# BOOK THREE

*Orpiment <note>original: "Auripigmentum," a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used as a pigment and in alchemy</note>, so that by its example you may learn the process for others. Because we sometimes see substances utterly corrupted, blackened, and fouled—which happens according to their diverse natures—it occurs sometimes because of their earthy parts. We shall purify them thus: the mastery of this work cannot be achieved through any means other than sublimation alone. When the subtle parts fly upward, the substance will appear more transparent and clear, and by this process it is stripped of its tendency to burn. First, let it be ground as finely as possible; cast it into an earthenware pot glazed on the inside, and pour in oil so that it stands no less than nine inches <note>original: "dodrante," a Roman measurement of three-quarters of a foot</note> above the material. Stir constantly with a wooden rod so it does not stick to the bottom. Once dried, grind it again, and perform the same labor using vinegar and sharp lye. Finally, the powder—mixed with tartar <note>potassium bitartrate</note>, quicklime, or copper filings—should be enclosed in a long, vaulted glass vessel. Do not fill it to the very brim, but only halfway. Let the "belly" of the vessel be properly protected on the outside with lute <note>a clay sealant used to fireproof glass</note> and place it in the sun; leave it in the sun until it is dried out and able to resist the fire. Then place it in the furnace. Do not close the mouth, lest the spirit be strangled by being shut in; let it breathe the constrained air. Let a light fire strike the bottom of the vessel; then, gradually increasing over six hours, let it finally glow with fire. Its "fugitive" <note>volatile</note> part will seek the vaulted chambers of the vessel, and there it will settle, gathered into a white silver. Break the vessel, draw it out, and store it for use. The same result occurs by "descent" <note>distillation downwards</note>, for it is carried downward more easily. If, however, the bodies are heavy, let them be joined to other lighter ones so they may ascend more easily. We have provided the method of sublimating, which you shall use for the rest; for they do not vary much from one another. If, however, you wish*

Prepare a <term>malagma</term> <note>A soft, dough-like mass or ointment used in alchemy</note> by tripling the amount; then, you shall smooth it extensively with common salt upon a porphyry marble slab. When you know it has reached a perfect union, place it in a twisted glass vessel—which allows for an easier ascent of the vapors—and set it over a fire. By the strength of the heat, the <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "vivum argentum"; mercury</note> shall glide down through the necks of the tubes into a receiving vessel. Rub that which remains at the very bottom of the vessel with fresh water, repeatedly pouring on new water until no trace of saltiness is perceived and it has lost all its sharpness. Once the water becomes sweet, you will then have your <term>calx</term> <note>A powder produced by heating a metal; an oxide</note>. There is also ANOTHER method of preparation: Let silver be dissolved in <term>aqua fortis</term> <note>original: "forti aqua"; nitric acid, literally "strong water"</note> as <term>goldsmiths</term> <note>original: "aurifabri"</note> are accustomed to do, and mix in spring water that has consumed common salt. In this way, you will see the silver sink to the lowest parts of the vessel. Suck away the water with brushes and place the calx in an earthenware vessel over glowing coals; remove it, and wash away the saltiness with fresh water. Repeat this process continually until you see it has all departed; the method of washing should be the same as we described in the first instance. Thus you shall turn silver into calx and wax: Let its shavings be mixed with <term>sublimated mercury</term> and stored in a suitable glass vessel. Expose it to glowing coals so the force of the fire expels the quicksilver, and in the bottom part of the vessel, you will find the silver fixed like wax, suitable for gems; let it be kept in a small box. But

### Lead and Tin

If we wish to turn these into calx: Let the tin be melted in some vessel and cast into very fine salt, stirring it with a hazelwood stick so that the adhering parts are separated and turned into granules very much like millet. Or, pass the melted metal through the narrow holes of a sieve into cold water, and you will form it into something like little worms. The work must be repeated again until you make it very fine; then, place the powder into boiling water

Or in **another** way, so that it may take the form of granules, as Geber <note>Jabir ibn Hayyan, a foundational Persian alchemist</note> teaches: *Let it melt in an open, wide-mouthed vessel, scraping the surface with an iron hook to draw off the superficial skin, continually stripping it away until you have it all turned to ash or powder; it is then placed in a jar and put into the furnace, and viewed by loosening the stopper until it turns into a white lime.* Or **otherwise**: *Let the lead be melted in a large earthenware vessel fortified with clay, stirring it with an iron spatula for a quarter of a day without interruption until it turns to powder; cast this into a jar and subject it to a strong reverberatory fire <note>a furnace that reflects heat onto the material</note> for a day, and you will find it glowing white; keep it after passing it through a silk sieve.* It remains for us to teach also how to

### Cook Copper,

Which is described by many, yet the use of antimony <note>original: "stibium"</note> is, as far as I know, nowhere recorded; you shall dispatch it in such a manner: Melt the copper in a casting vessel with equal portions of antimony; once melted together, add just as much more antimony and pour it out onto a flat marble slab so that it may cool on the surface, the better to be drawn into thin plates. Then, hollow out two bricks so that the plates can be conveniently fitted inside; when you have fitted them, let it be covered with another brick, bound with iron chains, and packed with clay. Once dried, it is placed in a glassmaker's furnace; let it remain there for a week so that it may be perfectly burned; remove it and set it aside for use. We are also able to

### Extract Quicksilver from Lead,

# NATURAL MAGIC

And in this manner: let the thinnest scrapings of lead be cast into "burning water" <note>original: "ardente aqua"; likely high-percentage distilled alcohol or a strong acid</note> from which no watery residue remains, adding a small portion of tartar and salt—or an amount equal to half the weight—so that the water stands a finger's breadth above the lead. Seal the mouth with a lid and bury it under dung <note>A common alchemical method for providing steady, gentle heat through fermentation</note> for a month. Afterward, remove it and place it in a glass vessel with a twisted neck <note>A retort or alembic</note> so that the substance does not struggle too much by ascending too high. Apply fire beneath it, and as the water distills, you will see the "living silver" <note>original: "vivum argentum"; mercury</note> ascend, turned into small droplets. When these accompanying signs appear, increase the fire and collect the result.

**Salt of Tartar** <note>Potassium carbonate</note> is made thus: Choose the dregs from old wine; once these are diligently dried, let them be burned in a new earthen vessel over a large fire until they are thoroughly ignited. The test of a proper burning is that it turns white with an airy brightness and seems to burn the tongue when touched. Dissolve this in hot water, strain it with a brush <note>or fine cloth filter</note>, and let it all evaporate over a slow fire in a new pot. What remains at the bottom is the salt which we use in our works.

Or **OTHERWISE**, you may extract living silver from lead more abundantly and by an easier way: let the bottom of an earthen pot be pierced with many small holes, and let it fit into another pot. Pack the joints with tenacious clay <note>Lute of the philosophers, used to create airtight seals</note>. Bury it in a small pit of the same capacity dug for it, with the earth trodden down by feet all around. Fill the upper empty pot halfway with quicklime <note>original: "viva calce"; calcium oxide</note> that has not touched water. Then, scatter lead that has been filed and finely ground; fill the rest with quicklime again so that the lead is placed in the middle. Pour "urine of boys" <note>A common source of ammonia and phosphorus in early chemistry</note> over the top to awaken it. Then, having placed the lid and smeared shut every vent, apply a vehement fire and heap it all around; let it remain for a day. For by the force of a very strong fire, the living silver falls through the open holes of the bottom into the vessel placed beneath, yielding a sixth part of the lead's weight. Nor is it much different—

### BOOK THREE

### To elicit the spirit from tin

We are able to do this whenever we wish: for the filings of tin <note>original: "stanni elimata scobs"</note>, mixed with an equal weight of saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note>, are placed into a pot. Above this, you shall fit seven—or many—perforated pots, and you must close and fortify the vents with luting clay. Above these, you will fit a glass vessel with its mouth inverted, or an open channel with a small dish placed beneath it. Then apply the fire, and you will hear the roar of the glowing heat; the spirit flies out in smoke, and you will find it compacted within the chambers of the pots and at the bottom of the glass vessel. So that you do not waste too much time in filing the tin, let a half-portion of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> be submerged into molten tin and pounded with a mortar; you will have it instantly, the spirit flies out more quickly, and you possess a fixed quicksilver. If you perforate the earthenware apparatus from the side, you may more conveniently cast the material in little by little and then stop it up. In this same way

### From Antimony

We are able to extract it: Stibium, which is called antimony by the apothecaries <note>original: "seplasiarijs"</note>, is ground and rubbed very finely with hand-mills. Then, let a new pot, set among glowing coals, become entirely red-hot. Into this you shall cast, little by little, the antimony, a double portion of tartar <note>potassium bitartrate</note>, and a quadruple portion of saltpeter, all very well pounded. When the smoke rises, close it with a lid so that the rising smoke does not escape; then remove the lid and cast in more, until all the powder is consumed. Afterward, let it remain in the fire for a little while longer. Once extracted, allow it to cool and remove the dross <note>original: "faeces"</note> remaining on top; at the bottom, you will find a settled "living silver" <note>metallic antimony</note> which the chemists call the *regulus*. It displays the properties of lead and is easily transformed into it; for if (as Dioscorides says) <note>Pedanius Dioscorides, a 1st-century Greek physician and pharmacologist</note> it is burned a little more intensely, it turns into lead.

<keywords>tin, antimony, saltpeter, mercury, distillation, regulus, Dioscorides, alchemy, metallurgy</keywords>

# NATURAL MAGIC

### How brittleness is removed, how metal is restored to a solid body, and how color is drawn into the surface.

### CHAPTER III.

It seemed necessary to add certain things essential to what is being discussed; for these issues often arise in the coloring and adulterating of metals. These instructions are provided so that the operator may approach these tasks more skillfully, for in this way, the results will be seen as more beautiful and perfect. First, however, we shall teach how to:

->Remove brittleness<-

As far as possible, reduce the metal into a lime <note>original: "calcem"; refers to a powdered oxide produced by heating</note>, and bury it under manure. After this, when it has been made white-hot by fire, cause it to be quenched, or cast it while molten into a place where there is "burning water" <note>original: "ardens aqua"; likely a reference to distilled alcohol or a strong acid</note> that has been frequently purified, turpentine resin, oil of the same, wax, tallow, euphorbium <note>a caustic resin from the spurge plant</note>, myrrh, or man-made chrysocolla <note>original: "chrysocolla"; a soldering agent or "gold-glue" used by goldsmiths</note> which goldsmiths use to give speed to the melting and for the soldering of gold. For if a metal is unworkable by hammering, it is because oily substances often tend to soften bodies. If we work the metal with all or some of these liquids and divide it into small cakes <note>original: "pastillos"</note>, we then cast them inside where the metal falls under the fire of the bellows. Alternatively, let these substances thicken like mud until they become like clay; then place the metal in the fire so that it glows among the kindled coals, and once removed, let it be quenched, and allow it half an hour to soak it up. Or, let the casting channels be greased and the metals poured through them; this will greatly suppress the fragility, and by this aid, they endure the hammer and are extended when cast, whereas previously, when struck, they would shatter into many pieces. But if you wish to:

->Restore metals into a solid body<-

Because metals turned into lime are not easily reunited by light skill, we have considered it appropriate to teach this, as it frequently occurs in our operations. These are of this sort:

# BOOK THREE

From all of these, or certain ones among them, we fashion small balls and throw them into the melting vessel where the <term>calces</term> <note>metallic powders or oxides produced by heating</note> are liquefied by fire, and they are returned to their original state. When you know this has been done properly, draw it out. It is, however, worthy of notice that if it be a <term>calx of gold</term>, it should be treated with egg yolks and similar substances; silver with egg whites and tartar. Indeed, the <term>dregs of oil</term> <note>original: "amurca," the watery residue of pressed olives</note> are especially powerful in this, so that the bodies are not deprived of their brilliant color during reduction, but rather present a more elegant appearance. Now indeed, I see I must speak of what remains: in what manner one can, regarding a noble metal,

->Draw it to the skin,<-

As the ignorant <term>chemists</term> <note>original: "chymistæ," here used somewhat derisively for those practicing deceptive alchemy</note> say; for they think that by their deceptions they entice and draw out the parts positioned in the center to the surface, and that the internal parts consist only of viler materials. But they depart from the truth, because the process only eats away the weaker parts on the surface, and a little <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: "vivum argentum," mercury</note> is drawn outward. For all the things that enter into the medicine corrode; the harder parts are left behind; they polish and whiten. They are perhaps led by the evidence of ancient <term>coins</term>, seeing pure copper within but simple silver on the outside; but these were forged together, beaten with hammers, and only then struck with dies. However, I judge it to be utterly difficult, if not impossible, for such a thing to be done by a similar artifice. Nevertheless, these are the things that polish: common salt, alum, <term>vitriol</term> <note>original: "calcanthum," likely copper sulfate or iron sulfate</note>, <term>native sulfur</term>, and for gold only, <term>verdigris</term> and <term>sal ammoniac</term>. When the work is to be performed, they reduce a portion of these to powder, place them with the metal in some vessel, and smear it round with potter's clay.

into a vessel, and having covered it with a lid, they place it over a slow fire with a small breathing hole left open, and allow it to be thoroughly burnt. Take care not to disturb the fire with bellows, lest the metal should melt. When the burnt powders settle—which they recognize by the smoke—they inspect it by loosening the stopper; the metal is heated in the fire until it glows, and while glowing, they immerse it in the mixtures. **Or OTHERWISE:** They prepare it with vinegar until it becomes drossy, and when you have wrapped the smeared work in linen cloths, it shall be boiled for a long time after being cast into an earthenware vessel containing vinegar. Once extracted, you shall cast it into urine; let it finish boiling with salt and vinegar until absolutely no filth emerges and the foul stains of the medicaments are abolished. If you do not find it to be of the brightest white, the same task must be undertaken again until it is perfected. **Or proceed OTHERWISE** in this order: Let the work boil in a pot with water, salt, alum <note>a mineral salt used as a fixative or astringent</note>, and tartar <note>potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used here as a cleaning agent</note>. Take it out and rub it thoroughly with fine sand until the stench of the sulfur has vanished. Finally, boil it again as before, and thus it becomes so white that it protects itself from the fire; it will not be rejected as faulty, but you will find it useful if you perform it rightly, and you will rejoice, unless you intend to abuse these secrets to your own ruin.

### In what manner every metal may be made heavier. CHAPTER IV.

It is often asked among chemists <note>original: "chymistas"</note>, and those who devote themselves to this kind of study, in what way it might be possible for silver to increase to the weight of gold, and for every metal to exceed its own due weight. We, who have undertaken the duty to teach here those things which are usually achieved only by their great efforts and expenses, shall easily

Some keep silver, drawn into thin foils, in the fire with salt and ancient potsherds, so that they may liberate it from moisture and the parts that provide weight may be thickened. But you may achieve the **SAME** more expeditiously: they sprinkle silver, drawn into fine plates, alternately with cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note> and sublimated quicksilver <note>mercury</note> in a vessel resistant to fire, fortified above with luting-clay and bindings so that it does not breathe out its strength. Then, take out the brittle plates and cast them into a cupellation-dish <note>a shallow, porous bowl used in assaying</note> glowing with fire, and into melted lead. By the great force of the bellows' blast, they expel the lead—which sinks down—to the edges of the receptacle, so that it drags the impurities away with it and leaves the silver purged. This is the process usually called by everyone the "inventive" or "cupellation" proof, because every falsehood is purged away. But if you do not find it to be of much weight, perform the operation again until it fulfills the weight of gold. We can also augment silver **OTHERWISE**: distilled and strong vinegar is poured into a small basin; crushed antimony and lead sought from filings are pounded and sifted. Whatever does not pass through is returned again to the mortar and ground so that it may be sifted finer. Cast this into the distilled vinegar and let it be buried under dung <note>a common method for providing steady, gentle heat</note> until you see everything dissolved. ### Gold to increase

*Greatly, and to be augmented: If it has been shaped into a vessel or something else, and the size does not correspond to the weight: rub the gold with fluid silver <note>original: "argento vivo," meaning mercury or quicksilver</note> using your hands or fingers until it drinks it in, continuing until it fulfills the desired weight by adhering to the surface. Then, prepare a strong lye from sulfur and quicklime, and cast it into a wide-mouthed pot with the gold. With gentle coals placed beneath, let it boil incessantly until you perceive it has contracted its proper color; then take it out, and you shall have it. Alternatively, extract a water from the yolks of eggs and golden litharge <note>lead monoxide</note> over a strong fire, and extinguish the glowing-hot gold in it. ### Another excellent method.

*If you perform this correctly, you will render the gold sufficiently heavy, and it will not be an impediment to the engraving or the form, provided you are diligent. Let a powder be prepared from the reddest ancient bricks, salt (which is for sale everywhere among us), and the most fiery vitriol <note>term: calcanthum; a sulfate mineral, likely copper or iron sulfate</note>, and sprinkle this into a suitable vessel. You shall pulverize silver, either with strong water <note>nitric acid</note>, or with lime, or by some other light artifice. Then, you will arrange the gold in turns, sprinkling it with the powder, filling the vessel in reverse order and sealing it. Subject it to a suitable fire for a third part of a day, but do not vex the fire with the breath of bellows. Remove it, and you shall renew and complete the process with the powders alone, without the lime of silver. If the gold loses its color, you shall restore it thus: make a paste with saltpeter, sal ammoniac, vitriol, and the powder of small bricks, mixed with urine; cover the gold and hide it in a slow fire. There is also another kind of washing as is customarily done: you shall take care to boil it in vinegar.*

### Concerning Air, and its medicines of the first order.

#### CHAPTER V.

Lest we disturb the order of our writing, we are about to record certain tinctures of metals, now that we seem to have said enough regarding their preparations. We have decided to hand these down according to their ranks, both because the order demands it, and because I find this was observed by the ancient philosophers. Others followed this as the practice grew strong, so that each might retain his own experiments; these, however small they may be, shall not be omitted by us. Let the eager reader approach these studies by whatever path he is drawn. Here, those things are first taught which color metallic bodies, dye them, and adulterate them—not permanently, but vanishing little by little. These do not withstand all tests <note>original: "examinibus"; likely referring to the "cupel" or fire-test used by assayers to prove purity</note>, and while they are not to be craved as true gold or silver, they are not to be entirely disregarded. Because there are not a few books overflowing with such matters, we shall record only those which are easy to prepare and beautiful to look upon, so that by their brilliance and seductive form they may occasionally deceive the eyes of even the best critics, who cannot discern whether they are true or false; nevertheless, let them be held at the price they are worth. But these require the hands of a most skilled craftsman; those who lack such experience should at least consult the best masters, lest they be frustrated. Copper is whitened <note>This refers to "dealbatio," the alchemical process of making copper look like silver</note> chiefly by arsenic, quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, and the dross of silver—which the Greeks call *lithargyron* <note>litharge or lead monoxide</note>—pyrites, magnetite, sublimated quicksilver, tartar, sal ammoniac, common salt—which the Arabs call *alkali*—saltpeter, and alum. If of all, or some of these...

# BOOK THREE

*...whether the bronze, having been melted into a liquid, is extinguished while inflamed by the fire, or is submerged once fully liquefied; or, having been drawn out into thin plates and reduced to powder, it is placed back into a casting vessel and held in the fire for a longer duration so that it is rendered fluid. *For the path to understanding is easier through practice; therefore, what the discourse has described, let the operation demonstrate. Let a clay pot grow white-hot, heaped with glowing coals; cast lead <note>original: "plumbum"</note> inside, and when you see it spread by the fire, sprinkle in a third part of arsenic <note>original: "arsenicum"</note>—the kind that shines like crystal—which has been reduced to a powder. Let it be added gradually while it burns and flows like oil across the surface. Some burnt parts will fly off as smoke; let them rest while some remains of the extinguished coals are still present. Then, break the vessel and scrape off the "congealed oil" <note>This likely refers to a glassy slag or surface crust formed during the reaction</note> and the ash-colored material sitting on the surface. Otherwise, it vanishes, which is a point worthy of notice in these matters. For if they remain in the fire longer than necessary, they breathe out their color and, their strength having been exhaled, they grow faint, returning immediately to their original state. Therefore, let them be held in the fire for as short a time as you are able; in this way, it will yield a white and "counterfeit" silver. OTHERWISE, if from scrapings of bronze...*

### Another Method

With orpiment <note>original: "Auripigmentum"; a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral</note>, it is not ineffective in working; and if in time marks of leaden-blue stain it, we shall endeavor to wipe them away. Let the orpiment be of the best quality, gaping into scales, easily split, and gleaming with a golden color. You shall sublime it three or four times with filed copper shavings, sprinkled with tartar <note>original: "tartaro"; potassium bitartrate</note>, and with an equal weight of it; or you shall make it descend so that it may shine perpetually and endure most splendidly. Let silver be dissolved in <term>aqua fortis</term><note>Nitric acid</note> which is not diluted by any watery residue, and let it be dried in a bubbling vessel until you have completed this seven times, or fewer. Mix it together and smooth it on a porphyry stone, sprinkling it with a light rain or drop by drop with oil of tartar or oil of fixed sal ammoniac. But when the glowing Sun has shone, expose it to it and let it be dried; then let more oil be added and let it dry for a while until it fulfills the weight of the silver. Thus, stored in a glass vessel and placed under dung <note>a common alchemical method for providing gentle, steady heat</note> until it is dissolved, let the solution be coagulated, and let ten or eight parts of copper be thrown upon it, and it whitens it remarkably. There is another way: if you throw the quicksilver extracted from <term>stimmi</term><note>Antimony</note>—which is the color of lead and which everyone calls the <term>regulus</term><note>The metallic form of a mineral after reduction</note>—upon copper, it whitens the copper greatly. *...having soaked it in distilled vinegar: expose it to the heat of the sun, and once dried, let it be moistened again, and dried again. Then, spread it in the bottom of a vessel, and let the force of the fire sublime it <note>Sublimation is the process of heating a solid until it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, used here to purify the substance</note>, so that it clings to the neck and the chambers of the vessel, which will occur within twelve hours. Afterward, let the artificial vitriol <note>original: "calcanthum"; a sulfate mineral often used for its corrosive or coloring properties</note> be melted by fire—for it does not resist liquefaction—into which a half-portion of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> should be immersed. When a third part remains, let it be kept in a damp place overnight; add to this an equal amount of "burning water" <note>distilled alcohol or "aqua ardens"</note> and let it be dissolved under manure <note>a common method for maintaining a gentle, steady heat for digestion</note>. Then, grind the substances you have saved and add three times the amount of oil of tartar, and let it dry under the sun or in a small furnace by the light of a lamp. Renew this many times with burning water, and mix it with an equal weight of the silver-water. Once the mixture has been dried under the sun, store it in a warm place so it does not liquefy. One part of this, cast over twenty parts of purified copper—or thirty parts if reduced to lime <note>a metallic powder or ash</note>—will yield the finest "false-silver" <note>original: "pseudogyron"; a high-quality silver-like alloy</note>, which yields to the bite of the teeth without roughness and can be beaten under a hammer. Thus, however, is another way to make the best...*

### The Same

*We shall make it from brass <note>original: "aurichalco"</note>, which is similar to gold yet hostile to it; for it is not able to imitate gold's efforts, as the unrefined common folk believe. However, you may easily turn it white in this way: boil plates of brass in vinegar for a day, and with an equal portion of quicksilver, salt, and tartar infused in water, let it boil in a new earthen pot. Always mix it with a wooden rod, or one made of fired clay, but never one of iron or any other metal, which must be especially guarded against; for it would then infect the mixture, so that it contracts too much of that metal's nature...*

### Concerning iron, and its medicines of the first order.

#### CHAPTER VI.

The order of our work now advises us to comment on the medicines of iron. The wise Indians <note>Likely a reference to practitioners of "Indian" or Eastern alchemy, often cited in Renaissance texts for their metallurgical skill.</note> held this metal in high esteem, since it retains much good within itself and easily transforms into a more noble metal. However, some reject it as useless because it melts with difficulty in the fire due to its fixed sulfur and large amount of earthy parts, making it hard to manage. We, however, shall speak on how to labor toward its liquefaction and coloring, as these differ not a little from the medicines of copper. First, we shall cleanse it of the venom of dross and rust; for it is more earthy than other metals, and therefore, as often as it is smelted, it produces excrementitious filth from itself. You shall frequently quench very thin plates of it in a strong lye and vinegar in which common salt and alum have boiled, until they glow white-hot. You shall grind its filings with salt in a mortar, changing the salt repeatedly until it shows no sign of blackness and the refuse of the iron is washed away. You shall whiten its plates in this manner: make an amalgam <term>malagma: a soft mass or paste, here referring to a metallic amalgam</term> from quicksilver <note>original: "argento viuo" (mercury)</note> and lead; grind it, and place its powder in a clay vessel interspersed with the plates. Let it be closed and secured with lute <note>A clay or cement used to seal alchemical vessels airtight.</note> so that no vent-hole is left. Let it remain in the fire for a day, and finally let it melt with a vehement heat. For the amalgam will bring about both whiteness and easy melting. You shall beat it into small plates again and repeat what was begun until you see it has acquired enough whiteness. This also occurs if, while melting, it descends through the bottom of the vessel with lead, pyrite, arsenic, and everything we mentioned regarding copper; if of silver...

### Concerning lead, and its medicines of the first order.
### CHAPTER VII.

It would be an arduous task to change lead into a more noble metal: yet it has this advantage, that it is easily tinted into red, or gold; but to be transmuted into true gold would be utterly difficult, since it falls far short of gold in nobility <note>In alchemy, "nobility" refers to a metal's purity and resistance to corrosion; gold was considered the most noble, while lead was the most "base."</note>. Therefore, so that we may approach the method of tinting, let this be instructed regarding lead: that before you attempt anything with it, let it be washed thoroughly. For it possesses a great abundance of earthy impurities; when you have washed it well, then easily

->To tint lead into gold<-

**You will be able:** *Grind burnt copper <note>original: "combustum æs," likely copper oxide or chemically treated copper</note> finely in a stone mortar, and sift it thinly; likewise, take crystalline glass. Then, let a clay smelting vessel be filled with thin sheets of lead, sprinkling these substances in alternating layers so that the burnt copper touches both sides. Apply fire gradually: then let it melt by the blast of bellows. When it has cooled, you shall separate the crusts, and repeat the same work three or four times, and it will be colored. Then, take calamine earth <note>original: "cadmiam terram," a zinc-rich ore used in making brass</note> finely pounded and red, and spread it in the bottom of a vessel mixed with raisins, dried figs, and dates. Then add galingale root, or that called turmeric: and sprinkle the semi-colored sheets in turns, and close it with a lid—leaving, however, a small open hole—applying a slow fire for as long as it takes to exhale the moisture. Then, by provoking the fire with the force of bellows, it will fuse together, and let it be cast into a rod: yet it does not endure the company of gold <note>This suggests the resulting alloy looks like gold but fails the tests of true gold, such as the "trial by fire."</note>.* **CALAMINE** *earth, moreover, is made to turn purple in this way: sprinkle sal ammoniac <note>A crystalline salt used as a flux in metalworking.</note> upon iron filings when they are glowing hot in a cauldron,*

*It breaks and renders materials brittle, except for lead; and for you who are skilled in mixing them by some artifice, no small work is achieved. ### To remove the shriek and softness of Tin.

*There are those who hand down that this may be achieved with ashes, lime, oils, and distilled waters, if the melted metal is extinguished in them, and no less by boiling. But you will accomplish it more perfectly and conveniently in this way: After the tin has been melted by fire, cast in quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, remove it, and place it in a glass cucurbit <note>a gourd-shaped chemical vessel used in distillation</note> whose belly swells into a sphere and whose neck is stretched out long and curved. Let it boil over the fire so that its force may sublime the quicksilver, which should hang in the neck of the vessel and descend drop by drop until it is entirely consumed. The tin remains at the bottom. Do this three or four times until it is inwardly like ice and yields no shriek at all. But you may accomplish it* OTHERWISE *more correctly: Reduce it to a lime <note>original: "calcem"; a powder produced by heating a metal, now known as an oxide</note>, as we have taught, so that it destroys that fleeting and unfixed substance of the quicksilver, or rather the cause of that sulfur. And if it does not happen after the first or second time, follow through a third time in turn. By reducing it back into a body with the proper methods, you will obtain your wish; it becomes so hard that it appears glowing white in the fire before it melts. For a vehement fire consumes the moisture of the quicksilver, which otherwise allows for easy melting.*

### To remove the deafness from tin:

Because it is soft by nature, it is "deaf" <note>original: "surdum"; refers to a metal that does not ring when struck</note>: for it yields to the striker, but when associated with the remaining metals, it becomes harder and more resonant. But "this is the task, this is the hard labor" <note>A reference to Virgil’s Aeneid: "hoc opus, hic labor est"</note>: for it stubbornly rejects the company of all others except lead, and it renders everything brittle. But you shall accomplish it by such a craft: Let it be broken down by being dissolved in strong water <note>term: "aqua fortis" (nitric acid)</note>; thus, let silver, properly purified from lead or another source, be mixed together with the water, and let the vessel boil over a light fire. By the force of the redundant heat, the water, dissolved into vapor and raised to the top, flies away. Draw out the dried material and let it be transferred into another vessel, with strong water poured in again for so long until they appear joined and embraced in their smallest parts. Or, let both be reduced in lime water <note>original: "aqua ex calce"</note> and mixed thoroughly: for thus occurs the mixing of spirits (as they say) and of bodies. If it should happen that its luster is dimmed: let it be submerged in sowbread <note>term: "cyclamino"; the juice or extract of the Cyclamen plant, used in alchemy for cleaning metals</note> once melted by fire: thus you will have a sweet-sounding, shining, and excellent metal, which they call "white." Tin can also be mixed with silver and others by means of quicksilver <note>term: "vivum argentum" (mercury)</note>, which I see some have perceived, and they prepare an excellent "false-silver" <note>term: "pseudargyron"; an alloy resembling silver, often a type of brass or tin-amalgam</note>: into the tin melted with quicksilver, they mix silver and keep it under the fire for a longer time; they draw it out, and thus wrap the brittle material into a ball of potter's clay, and keep it under the fire for a day, or in hot ashes. # ON NATURAL MAGIC

### On gold and silver, and their medicines of the first order. CHAPTER IX.

There is no one who works with gold itself, since it is the most noble metal, but everyone attempts to imitate it, as even in ancient times it was rarely mixed with other things. Therefore, if I were to set down any medicinal recipes concerning it, I would be doing nothing but repeating what has already been said; nevertheless, we shall attempt to imitate it using silver in this manner. And

->To tint silver into gold<-

The means shall be granted thus: first, you shall prepare a sharp lye <note>A strong alkaline solution made from wood ashes</note> in this way: let lime be placed in a pot, the bottom of which is pierced with many tiny holes. Above this, you shall fit a piece of wood or a tile. Then, pour in powder and warm water little by little, so that it flows through the narrow holes in the bottom into a clean vessel placed beneath. You shall repeat this until it becomes extremely sharp. Into this, place ground antimony <note>original: "stibium"</note> so that it may be able to vanish into thin air; let it simmer over a slow fire. For when it has boiled, the water will turn purple. Then, strain it through a linen cloth into a clean vessel. Pour more lye onto the remaining powders and boil it over the coals until the water no longer appears blood-red. Keep the colored lye over the fire until the water has evaporated. Take the remaining dried powder and mix it with dried and dissolved oil of tartar <note>Potassium carbonate</note>. Sprinkle this over equal weights of gold and silver plates, layered alternately in an earthen melting crucible. Hide it among the coals for a time and repeat the work until, by the privilege of its perfect color, it is seen to rival gold. You may also fashion a golden color from burnt copper <note>original: "aere combusto"</note> in this way: if you make a strong water <note>An acid, likely a form of aqua fortis</note> from vitriol, saltpeter, alum, cinnabar, and verdigris, and let the burnt copper dissolve and break down within it,

### Concerning quicksilver, and its medicines of the first order.
### CHAPTER X.

Now it seems a timely moment to approach the operations of Quicksilver <note>original: "Hydrargyri"; mercury</note>, and its minor congealings—which I once thought impossible to freeze—but the true methods shall be revealed later. Now, we will disclose to those who desire it the things which are customarily done by some, not without profit; and first:

->The congealing of quicksilver with odor<-
*Of metals, and especially of lead, it is now pleasing to work with it: Let the lead be purged and separated from its dross. Cast the melted metal into a pit; when it begins to cool, having fixed and then withdrawn a wooden point, pour in the fluid silver <note>mercury</note>, and it is congealed. Strike it with a pestle, and you shall repeat this many times. When you have it hard, melt it frequently and immerse it in clear water; you shall repeat the work until you have it hard and ductile under the hammer, yet do not judge it to be useless. There is also another*

->Congealing of quicksilver with an iron helmet,<-
*Or a pan, by casting with it the water in which blacksmiths extinguish glowing iron; and let there be put into it double the amount of salt ammoniac <term>sal ammoniacum: ammonium chloride used as a flux</term>, vitriol <term>calcanthum: likely blue or green vitriol/sulfate</term>, and verdigris <term>ærugo: the green pigment/patina formed on copper</term>. Let it boil with a vehement fire, always stirring with an iron spatula. If the water is consumed by the boiling, let other warm water be prepared and cast in, so that it does not stop boiling. Thus in a quarter of a day you have it congealed. Then, with a linen bag or leather, squeeze the congealed mass tightly with the hands so that it sweats out whatever little liquid it has. Once pressed, let it be congealed again until the whole is perfected. Then let it be placed in a washed earthen vessel with spring water.*

Once the impurities that had sunk to the bottom are removed, mix them into the same earthen jar and stir until you have a clean and clear substance. Place it under the open sky for three nights, and it will thicken to a hard consistency in the clay vessel. Now, **if you wish to tint this congealed quicksilver <note>original: "Argentum viuum"; mercury</note> into gold, and beautifully so:** Break it into small pieces and fill a ceramic melting crucible with alternating layers of powdered calamine earth <note>original: "cadmiæ terræ"; a zinc ore used in making brass</note>. The mixture should be mediated by a preparation of dried vine-shavings, raisins, and that Indian galingale root which the apothecaries call turmeric <note>original: "curcumam"</note>, all crushed and wrapped together. Once the vessel is full, coat it with sealing clay and dry it in the sun—or by a fire to compensate for the sun—then place it in the fire so that it boils for six hours and becomes white-hot. Then, let it be blown upon with the force of bellows and agitated greatly to make it flow. When it has flowed, let it cool while buried in coals; thus you will find a colored and most splendid gold. And another way:

### Quicksilver with copper filings

We can render it fixed <note>term: "Fixum"; in alchemy, making a volatile substance resistant to fire so it does not evaporate</note> and congeal it: Let us fashion two copper hemispheres so that they fit into one another perfectly, lest any vapor escape. Inside, let quicksilver be placed with an equal portion of crystalline arsenic and tartar <note>original: "tartari"; potassium bitartrate deposit from wine barrels</note>, both properly crushed and passed through a sieve. Let the joints be smeared on the outside with clay so they do not breathe. Let them dry thus, and you shall heap coals over them and bury them for a quarter of a day; finally, let the whole thing become white-hot. Then you shall draw it out and open it, and you will see the whole mass has grown together, adhering to the hollows of the copper vessel. When struck with a hammer, it will fall off. Melt it and cast it, and it will give the finest silver color, and it is difficult to distinguish from true silver. If you please to mix it with copper, mix it with three times the amount of molten copper, and even without silver, it will show an excellent white, sweet, and malleable metal. OR OTHERWISE: In a clay pot, a copper...

*Let a bronze lid be placed upon it, and once the fire is lit, you will find the quicksilver <note>original: "argentum vivum"; mercury</note> gathered in the lid in a wonderful way, and it will be frozen <note>congealed or solidified</note> even more miraculously.
