It is also done thus:*

### The solidification of quicksilver with oil

*Another method, and I find it frequently practiced by many, yet it retains something unique and is very ingenious: Let a vessel be formed in the shape of a goblet from silver, red arsenic <note>likely realgar</note>, and brass <note>original: "aurichalcum"</note>. Cover this properly with a lid so that it does not breathe <note>leak vapor</note>, fill it with quicksilver, and let the joints be protected with some lute <note>sealing paste</note>, made of egg white or pine resin, as is customary. Suspend it in a pot full of linseed oil and let it boil for half a natural day <note>twelve hours</note>. Draw it out, and once removed, squeeze it through leather or straw; if anything remains unsolidified, repeat the work and compel it to coagulate. If the vessel should solidify it too slowly, compensate with as much weight of brass and arsenic as you see has been lost, for we can judge by the weight, and then use it. Now the order of operations requires us to add certain fixations, since they always follow after solidifications, and they retain more or less of the parts. Whence:*

### The fixation of solidified quicksilver

*This proceeds as follows, and not without reason: Prepare an earthenware vessel that can resist fire, at the bottom of which you should spread a layer of shavings from elderberry roots <note>original: "sambucus"</note>, treading and arranging them with your hands. Then spread another floor of crystalline glass, crushed with a pestle and passed through a sieve, being very fine. Then add another layer of pepper, ginger, and cinnamon. Place that mixture <note>amalgam</note> or solidified quicksilver upon it, then fill the vessel with the same powders in reverse order. Put on the lid, secure it with sealing clay, and leave it to the sun—*

Of all the methods one might happen to see or try, this one succeeded most happily. You may use all these methods, or fix it into that body by which it was frozen. There is also ANOTHER fixation of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>, not inferior in usefulness, which you may use if it pleases you: let saltpeter <note>potassium nitrate</note> and tartar <note>potassium bitartrate</note> be ground and reduced to a fine powder. When fire is applied within, it burns, sending up a flame; let what remains be turned into water, and let the moisture evaporate over a light flame. Let the remaining salt be mixed with a triple amount of burnt artificial chrysocolla <note>a flux, likely borax in this context; it burns like alum</note> and a double amount of alkali salt. Wrap this frozen mass in a hard vessel; let fire be applied above, then around it, and finally below for six hours each, and it retains some parts of the frozen substance.

### On medicines of the second order. CHAPTER XI.

We shall now speak of medicines of the middle order; for those are called so by us which, when cast upon certain diminished bodies, alter the body so that they are but little away from full perfection, and are distinguished by the smallest differences. They do not fail when exposed to the final tests <note>the "examinibus" refers to the cupellation or assaying processes used to test purity</note>, surpassing medicines of the first order just as they are surpassed by medicines of the higher order; they lack very little to pass over into them. But because they are most difficult and found with hardship, we have tested and set down only a few. And first:

### To tinge silver into gold
#### It is taught, and the dye is so complete, that it is truly believed to be so.

# BOOK THREE

*Gold: From the filed shavings of silver, create an amalgam <note>original: "malagma," a soft mass or paste</note> with three times as much quicksilver <note>mercury</note> over a fire. Let it boil in a glass vessel over coals until the quicksilver evaporates, by which the silver is overcome. Then, let sal ammoniac <note>ammonium chloride</note> and native sulfur be ground together in a weight equal to the silver. Let this remain in a glass vessel for two hours over glowing coals until its force sublimes the sal ammoniac, the sulfur, and then the quicksilver, so that they adhere to the neck of the vessel. Break the vessel, and you will find the silver marked with a golden color, having preserved its own weight or even increased it. Immediately prepare such a water as this: take Roman vitriol <note>original: "chalcãthum," likely copper sulfate</note> with double the amount of red, distilled Cyprian vitriol—which they call copperas <note>original: "coparosam"</note>, and let it be of the best quality, for everything depends on this. Add three times as much saltpeter, a third part of verdigris <note>original: "æruginis," copper acetate</note>, and a sixth part of cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide</note>. Extract the water drop by drop using glass instruments. Let two parts of this water boil with the reserved silver over a slow fire for a natural day <note>a 24-hour period</note>. Then, increasing the heat of distillation, let all the water evaporate. That which remains at the bottom should be placed in a melting crucible with chrysocolla <note>gold-solder or borax</note> and powdered lime. With the mouth covered and blocked with clay, apply the fire of melting, and you shall have it: for the silver turns yellow, and most importantly, it will never lose that color or change it, so that when exposed to all the tests for gold, it loses little or nothing of its hue. Thus, IN ANOTHER WAY, it will perfectly and singularly emulate the color of gold: You must now remember the burnt copper made from antimony <note>original: "stibio"</note>; when that is melted with half as much silver, it will give a perfect color of gold, as can be seen. If you mix it with gold, it will make it better, so that it may endure the test of some. It is also done IN ANOTHER WAY most excellently: if you mix the congealing of quicksilver, which we spoke of regarding tin <note>original: "casside"</note>, with a third part of silver, you will find the silver marked with a golden color, when joined with an equal portion of gold.*

### Concerning medicines of the third order.

#### CHAPTER XII.

It is now timely to approach the medicines of the third order. Indeed, I know that many minds have been held back from this kind of reading, since from the beginning of the book they have desired nothing else—so does the lust for money creep in. Now the deeper mysteries of nature are revealed, at least to those who long for coins (if indeed there is anyone who does not perish for want of them). And although we have elsewhere handed down medicines of the third order from the ancients, since we do not have an abundance of them at hand, we decided perhaps to defer them to another time. There are many who have risked many experiments; of these, we shall hand down those which are more profitable and more expedient. And from these, first:

->To render Cinnabar fixed.<-
<note>To "fix" a substance in alchemy means to make a volatile material, like mercury-based cinnabar, resistant to heat so it does not evaporate.</note>

For whoever seeks to do this, I judge it should be done thus: Take pieces of broken cinnabar the size of walnuts and place them in a glass vessel of the same capacity. Bury them in three times the weight of silver (or more), scattered and sprinkled in turns. Fortify the vessel with lute <note>a clay sealant used to make vessels airtight</note> and allow it to dry; if not in the sun, you must repeat the process. Then, immersed in ashes, cook it over a slow fire for as long as it takes until, when broken, it turns a leaden blue color. If it does not, you must apply more effort until it reaches that state. Then wash it with twice the amount of lead, and once washed and exposed to all tests, it will more stubbornly—

### BOOK THREE

It will stand in the way, being of greater weight and power; and the lighter the fire you use, the more successfully the work will be carried out. In this way, indeed, silver <note>original: "argentum"; likely referring to mercury or "living silver" in this context</note> is animated, and what was lost shall be restored. Once the quicksilver <note>original: "viuo argento"; mercury</note> has been sublimated from distilled vinegar, let it boil; then you shall remix the quicksilver, and in a curved glass vessel, the quicksilver flies off when heated in the fire, slipping down into the receiver; let it be preserved. If you are wise, you will find it has lost little weight.

You may do it another way, more quickly and profitably: arrange broken pieces of cinnabar <note>mercury sulfide, a common red ore</note> the size of dice into an oblong linen bag, keeping it away from the sides of the pot on all sides. Then pour in a very sharp lye <note>original: "lixiuium"</note> made from alum, twice as much tartar, four times as much quicklime, and an equal portion of oak ash, prepared as is customary or by some other method. Let it boil for a day; take it out, and let it boil with oil. Devote constant labor to it, and let a full day and night pass while it remains there. Once the pieces of cinnabar are removed from the oil, smear them thoroughly with beaten egg white, and having rolled them in a third part of filed silver dross <note>original: "argenti scobis"; metal filings</note>, spread them at the bottom of a suitable vessel. Once it is properly protected with potter's clay <note>original: "figularia creta"</note>, as we have said, apply fire for three days, and at the very end let the heat increase so that it yields to the fire and almost melts. Withdraw it; wash it from the dregs in a final examination of the silver, and lead it to its true and natural state.

In this way, and in another, we can render it fixed: if in a round earthen vessel closed on all sides, where you have placed the cinnabar through a small vent, you smear it with a glue prepared from egg white so that the force of the fire does not char it. Once placed in the fire, gradually strengthening it—perhaps with sawdust—until you see it has contracted the same color. Do not hurry, for haste ruins the whole work, as you will know is often taught by the philosophers; but this service requires a greater master.

### To extract gold from silver.

This is the rule for its preparation: Place thin filings of iron into a melting vessel that is hardy against fire, until it glows white-hot and melts. ### Concerning the Battle of Phoebus and Python.

#### CHAPTER XIII.

Let Python <note>In Greek mythology, Python is the serpent slain by Apollo (Phoebus); here, it represents the "crude" or "poisonous" state of the base metal or mineral matter.</note> be removed from his place—a creature of vast body, of a horrid countenance, with reddish crests, threatening with the greatest poison, and crowded with a broad heap of stings. He is the fiercest of all things that Mother Earth produces, and the site takes up much space, so that everything may depend upon this. Here, by the force of the wasting air, more virulent than a viper, he inflicts hard blows from a distance. When he has swallowed his enemy, let him be submerged in a dark cave where he first becomes silent, lest by the force of the rising vapor, belching forth a strangling breath, he should kill those standing by. For this reason, it is useful that they cover their heads with bladders, if that should be safe; but it will be safer if the soldiers are left alone. <note>The "soldiers" likely refer to the chemical agents or the heat itself; the "bladders" are protective masks against toxic fumes.</note>

Thus Phoebus, piercing him little by little with his arrows <note>The "arrows of Phoebus" represent the rays of the sun or the application of heat.</note>, shall lay low the huge Python, and shall strike him in the end so that the quiver is exhausted and the poison poured out. Nor should the mercy of heaven be lacking, but a long storm of rain should restrain the raging one, and he himself should drink up the desired moisture, and hold back the dying creature with much humidity. Thus let the war of Phoebus and Python linger for a quarter of a day, or a little more, which I think matters not at all, provided he brings back the victory. 

Thus, with the vitals ravaged, the enemy prostrate, and the poison exhausted and emptied, the wrath of Phoebus shall cease. And if he revives further, let the path for life to sprout again be cut off, and let him fight only until he falls in the fighting. Then let Phoebus, his head wreathed with leaves and garlands, bear the prize of distinguished Victory; nor should he provoke the playful boy <note>Likely referring to Cupid/Eros, a nod to the myth where Apollo's pride after killing Python led to his conflict with Cupid.</note> with insults, and he shall know he has done enough. Yet it is not hidden from me that there are very few whom impartial Jupiter will love, and to them [he will be] favor-

### How to arrange coral into shapes, and to form one piece from many fragments. CHAPTER 14.

It often happens in practice that many ornaments are fashioned from coral, and sometimes it is rendered into various shapes from small chips, so that vessels and other composite objects may be formed, which then pass into the hardness of a shell. These are of no small value; therefore, let us provide the proper method of preparation, since such compositions are demanded by many. If you take the greatest care, know that it will differ little from sea coral. Take the shavings or chips of the best coral, which are exceptionally red, and pound them in a mortar or grind them in a mill. Sift them through a fine sieve, and those pieces that do not pass through should be returned to the mortar until they are reduced to the finest powder, so fine it can scarcely be felt and might fly away into the breeze. We shall teach this as diligently as possible. To wipe away all filth, let <term>alkali salt</term> <note>likely potash or soda ash used as a cleaning agent</note> be submerged in boiling water so that it dissolves and turns into liquid. Pour the water into a large bowl, and when you have thrown in the powders, stir them diligently with your hands and turn them well. When they have finished settling, strain it; pour off the first water and pour more over it. Stir again with an open hand until all impurities have departed. Then, treat them with a large amount of plain water for as long as it takes for the salt to completely leave and the water no longer tastes of brine. When you have ascertained this, transfer it from the vessel to substances that are especially good for dyeing, so that they are made more ready for coloring. Such substances include <term>cinnabar</term> <note>mercury sulfide, a bright red pigment</note>, <term>dragon's blood</term> <note>a bright red resin from the Dracaena tree</note>, <term>minium</term> <note>red lead</note>, <term>hematite</term> <note>iron oxide stone</note>, <term>Armenian bole</term> <note>an earthy red clay used as a base for gilding and pigments</note>, <term>red ochre</term>, <term>scarlet dye</term> <note>original: "coccus baphica", referring to kermes or similar insect-based dyes</note>, sandalwood, brazilwood, and the roots of <term>madder</term> <note>original: "eritrodani radi-", referring to Rubia tinctorum</note>

Using either small brushes or the glass instruments of the chemists <note>original: "chymistarum organis"</note>, cook them together as long as they retain any moisture, turning them often with a spatula or a spoon so that they are thoroughly mixed. Then transfer the mixture into a glass vessel with the remaining juice so that its sharpness may be dissolved. Use a vessel with a long neck and a wide belly, so that an opening stands out in the middle which almost reaches the mixture. After mixing it correctly, bury the vessel in dung <note>A common method in early chemistry to provide steady, gentle heat through fermentation</note>, renewing the dung until the materials are dissolved. You can recognize this by the following evidence: when you see a very red oil <note>original: "oleum rubicundissimum"</note> emerge drop by drop day by day. When it has become abundant, the oil is poured off by tilting the vessel and saved. Take the mixture, which should now be soft and workable by hand—though you must first protect your hands with lard or some other fat, for if it sticks stubbornly, it can hardly be removed. Then shape a vessel, fashion images, or create a many-branched object. First, expose the rough-formed shape to the hot sun; take care that they are not troubled by dust or wind, which might mar the surface. Or, keep it buried in the dung in a vial, always anointing it with the saved oil, for that imparts a scarlet color. Gradually turning to stone, it returns to its original hardness and sound. You will bring out a shine by polishing or lightly smoothing it; in this way, we easily restore it to its special and native constitution.

### Likewise, to gather broken pearls into one mass.
->CHAPTER XV.<-

No less diligence must be applied to pearls <note>original: "Margaritas"</note>, since they are greatly desired by everyone, and especially by women, as incentives for luxury or pleasure. Large, half-ounce spheres of these little stones are worn on the neck, and they are carried as ear-pendants <note>original: "crotala"; literally "rattles," a Roman term for pearls that tinkled against one another</note>, and so...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Natural Magic, specifically focusing on the artificial creation and refinement of pearls to mimic those found in nature.</meta>

# NATURAL MAGIC

They are so skillfully adulterated to mimic a neat and elegant form that the Indian Ocean sends out none better, nor are those born in nearby shells any finer. To proceed with the work as planned, first, so they are not defiled by any blemish or dirt, you shall render them purified with alum in this way: Let them be placed in a small bag with powders of emery <note>original: "smiri"; a stone used for polishing</note>, pumice, and cuttlefish bone, and worked with the hands in water until you recognize them to be washed and polished. Once dried, reduce them to a fine powder as we have said. Then, let them be dissolved either with lemon juice or by the rising vapor of the same, hanging from the neck of a glass vessel for a day; or they may be made workable with "strong water" <note>original: "aqua forti"; likely a reference to an acid or potent solvent</note>, though not the common kind. It is better, therefore, to bury them in dung, renewing it every five days until you find them dissolved. You will see a certain fat or oil swimming on the surface of the liquid; let this be carefully gathered, either through an opening in the vessel or with a silver spoon. Then, you shall repeatedly take up the softened powder with your hands, and by kneading it, shape it into spheres or like tapered pears. If your skill does not suffice, prepare molds made of silver, or of another metal gilded with gold. If you wish them to be pierced, let them be perforated with a hog's bristle or a silver needle. Always preserving the "fatness" <note>the oily residue from the dissolution process</note> by greasing them, hang them from a thread and enclose them in a smooth glass vessel. Expose this to the sun for a few days so they may be hardened, ensuring they do not touch the sides, so that we may protect them from the injuries of wind, dust, and other things, lest they become clouded and render the pearl liable to various spots. When you see they have acquired hardness, let them be smeared and preserved in a dough made of ground millet or barley flour, as in the baker's craft, and finished by cooking in an oven. Alternatively, offer the pearls to be swallowed by young pigeons whose innards have been purged; let their stomachs be empty or their bowels moved. When they have swallowed them, wait a little while, then extract them from the belly, either suspended by a thread or by killing the bird.

THERE IS a craft by which spots are removed from pearls, and among many methods, I have found this to be easily the most excellent: In the month of May, take the dew scattered upon lettuces, in which you shall submerge the pearls for a day and smear them; the pearl will emerge shining. I do not think this was discovered without reason: for pearls are born from dew. For at a certain time of year, in the luxuriance of conception, they thirst for dew as if for a husband, for whom they gape in desire. When the lunar sprinklings are most liquid, they draw in the desired moisture with a kind of yawning; thus they conceive and become pregnant, and they render the condition of the pearls according to the quality of their "food." For if the dew was pure, the little stones are white; if it was turbid, they languish with paleness or turn red. OTHERS are those who counterfeit false and deceptive pearls thus: They keep the cleaned eyes of fish in sharp vinegar until they soften; they shape them and allow them to harden, but these languish with a pale color.

### Operations of crystal and glass, which we use in the counterfeiting of gems. CHAPTER XVI.

Now we must approach the compositions of precious stones, for which the ancient age is not a little praised; nor is there any fraud more lucrative to life, as *Pliny* says. <note>Pliny the Elder, Natural History, Book 37. He frequently commented on the Roman obsession with luxury and the skill of counterfeiters.</note> And so the desire for profit has kindled the torch of luxury, to such an extent that even the best judges of these things have often not been safe from deception. Some prepare them from glass, others from crystal, and from other materials, with such a fitting excellence that they appear natural. Let us review the means by which they may be imitated. Now we shall set forth some preliminary things which are necessary for them, and first:

->How crystal may be made meltable<-

We shall teach this, for the delay in melting will be no small hindrance to some, nor would we be able to shape that which was desired; however, you shall undertake it thus: Grind the crystal and sift it through a fine sieve into a very thin powder. With half as much salt of tartar <term>Salt of tartar: Potassium carbonate, often used as a flux to lower the melting point of minerals.</term> and water, form little cakes. Place these in raw clay in the strongest fire of a furnace so that it glows white all night, yet does not liquefy. It is then collected in a suitable vessel, but far removed from all filth; for if it should happen to be foul, the trick is revealed and the deceit uncovered, but it should shine through with unoffended transparency. If a little more salt is added, it melts more quickly. The method for the salt is already clear. Many are accustomed to prepare crystal IN ANOTHER WAY, so that it melts more easily and is more suitable for the work: they take a large iron spoon, protect it with clay, and commit broken pieces of crystal to the fire until they glow white; then they quench them in oil of tartar. They repeat this many times and grind it in a bronze mortar so that the powder may be liquefied more easily. It is also made—

### Amber

### Counterfeit Gems

Are made: First, they polish crystal, beryl, or other less noble stones on wheels, making the sides equally square or into whatever shape they desire. If they are too deeply red, add water; if too pale, add color.

### Deep Blue Glass
<note>original: "Cyanus"</note>

If you wish to create this: cast a small amount of that earth into liquefied crystal <note>likely a high-quality lead or potash glass</note>, which we previously mentioned in the section on sapphire. ### Smalt,
<note>A deep blue glass colored with cobalt</note>

And also white glass, let it be done thus: mix lead ash <note>lead oxide</note> with double the amount of crystalline powder. Form these into small balls and place them over a slow fire for a night. Take care, however, that the material does not stick to the vessel; stir it with an iron spatula and increase the heat for melting, and it will be done. If you wish for it to shine in some parts and appear white in others, take the aforementioned earth with double the amount of glass, form small globes using water, and let them melt in a suitable vessel over a reciprocating fire <note>a fire with alternating intensity</note> for a night, stirring occasionally with an iron rod. In this way, both translucent and opaque parts may be found within a small disc of the material. If, however, you desire a green color, such as is used to plaster walls: once you have produced the white glass, mix it with a little of that blue earth and let it melt, stirring with an iron rod throughout the night, and it will be finished. By following these examples, you will be able to fashion the rest by your own skill <note>original: "tuo marte," literally 'by your own Mars'</note>; for instance, if you want jasper, use the powder of an ancient brick; if a milky color, use lime and gypsum. However, if any of these gems shine too faintly or are clouded by some mist, let them be ground with many facets <note>original: "angulis," literally 'angles'</note>, so that the cloudy color may be awakened by the reflection of the angles and kindled to a richer luster. These observations on chemical matters and the imitation of gems are sufficient; there remains the subject of mirrors and the engraving of gems, which must be addressed in the next Book.

# JOHN BAPTIST
## PORTA OF NAPLES
### NATURAL MAGIC
### BOOK IV.

->PREFACE<-

I believe I shall have completed this entire undertaking once I have described certain remaining experiments in <term>Catoptrics</term> <note>The science of reflected light and mirrors</note>. For I have, in my estimation, touched sufficiently upon <term>Chemical</term> matters so that some of their principles might become known to the uninitiated; the present occasion now requires me to teach these optical matters. There is a certain part of Geometry called <term>optics</term> <note>original: "ὀπτική"</note>, that is, the science of perspective, which pertains to the eyes. Indeed, it produces many such marvelous experiments: sometimes it projects an image outward into the air, sometimes it reflects nothing at all, and sometimes it creates images shifted to another place. Likewise, by looking into a mirror more directly, the feet appear upward and the head downward, along with other effects that will be seen more extensively in the following pages. Know this, however: if you intend to produce any variation, you must move away from the flat plane, and you will always see something different from the truth. Yet, I think it inappropriate to provide the mathematical causes for these things here, since that discipline <note>Geometry</note> satisfies them abundantly, and many authors have already provided them. Whoever prefers to seek those causes should consult Archimedes of Syracuse, the *Optics* and *Catoptrics* of Euclid, Ptolemy, Witelo, and the rest, from whom we shall take many things, while also adding many newly devised ideas.

### How it is possible to observe the stars during the day.
### CHAPTER I.

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'O' featuring a bird (possibly a phoenix or eagle) perched amidst foliage within a square frame.</image-desc>

**O**ne and all now find it plain that a small light vanishes when it is near a stronger and more vivid one. If you place a torch or any flame near the bright sun, its light is scattered and fades away. During the day, the stars hide because of the excessive brightness of the sun, even though they shine equally by day and by night. Therefore, those who wish to behold them will not be left without a remedy. During a great eclipse of the sun, when the earth is deprived of light and covered in deep darkness, and the eyes are no longer struck by the glare, the sky is seen scattered with stars. Thucydides <note>Greek historian, c. 460 – c. 400 BC</note> committed to memory that this happened in his own times, and it has often been evident in our own age. Not only do the eyes grow dim from such vehement light, but they are even harmed, as one reads concerning the soldiers of Xenophon <note>Greek philosopher and general; this likely refers to snow-blindness described in his 'Anabasis'</note>, and...

<summary>The author outlines future topics including mirror-making and amulets before explaining why stars are invisible during the day due to solar glare, noting they can be seen during eclipses.</summary>
<keywords>natural magic, optics, mirrors, astronomy, solar eclipse, Thucydides, Xenophon, starlight, amulets</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of optics and the behavior of light, specifically focusing on how the eye perceives faint light sources in the presence of overwhelming brightness.</meta>

# BOOK FOUR

Regarding Dionysius, the tyrant of Sicily <note>Dionysius I (c. 432–367 BCE), known for his cruelty and psychological torture</note>, who used to blind those confined in foul dungeons by exposing them to a most brilliant light. For since the sharp focus of the eyes cannot resist the Sun, nor endure it, it is immediately dulled. Those who are about to look at something bright stretch their hands over their eyelashes or use some other defense. From this we begin our observation, as taught by Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher (129–c. 216 CE)</note> and Philoponus <note>John Philoponus (c. 490–570 CE), an Alexandrian philologist and commentator on Aristotle's physics</note>, for whoever desires to see such things: let him descend into a very deep well, or something similar, so that through the darkness and the great distance he may gaze upon the clear sky—provided it is not covered by clouds—and with unblinking eyes and without twitching, he may behold the bright and shining stars. 

For in that place, the great darkness (acting like night) concentrates the vision and does not allow it to be scattered by the light poured over it from above. However, you will not risk this while the Sun occupies the meridian <note>the highest point in the sky at noon</note>, for you will be hindered by the great light. If you descend deeper, you will see more clearly and more quickly; if less deep, then more obscurely and more slowly. Thus, someone submerged in a deep and dark cavern will see a candle burning in the sun, which does not happen when there is a sudden encounter with very brilliant light, because it is prevented by the greater light of the Sun. 

In this way, I have found many men of no small authority to be mistaken—as I remember often reading and hearing—who, if they are unable to observe the stars for a long time, attempt to contemplate them by this device: they believe and demonstrate that they can see the fixed stars in the sky at midday by using a mirror submerged in water. For the perpendicular rays of the Sun falling onto the surface of the water strike the mirror obliquely, and from that very surface they are twisted back toward the eyes of a well-positioned observer, representing the shape of the Sun. But the rays striking the water obliquely are refracted from there, hitting the mirror and the sight; the shape of the same object will be seen, though smaller, because of the refraction of the denser medium, and the star...

<keywords>Dionysius of Sicily, Galen, Philoponus, optics, refraction, astronomy, solar observation, mirrors, light concentration</keywords>

*and you will think you see it, following the body of the sun, as is evident in the study of Optics <note>original: "Opticis"; the science of light and vision</note>. For this reason, many think that object is Stilbon <note>original: "Stilbon"; the Greek name for the planet Mercury, meaning "The Gleaming One"</note>, since it recedes very little from the body of the Sun, and they observe it always following him. Others think it is the Dog Star <note>original: "Canicula"; Sirius</note>, and therefore hasten to see it during the summer days. But if you place less faith in demonstration and trust more in experience and the senses: seek the true interval of that star during the equinox, and you will not always find the same distance, so that the stars appear now further away, now indeed closer. A mirror always shows the same distance if you place it evenly; however, I do not deny that with such help we can more freely behold not only the rising or setting Sun, but even the Sun occupying the Meridian, as well as eclipses of the luminaries <note>original: "luminarium defectus"; solar or lunar eclipses</note>. For since weak eyes cannot endure looking at the sun at close range due to the excessive brilliance of the light, you may discern its likeness in this way in clear light without any strain, no less than by using a black cloth or paper with a small hole pierced through it.*

### How you may see those things in the dark which are illuminated by the Sun outside, and with their own colors.

### CHAPTER II.

If anyone should desire to see this, he must close all the windows; it will also be beneficial if any vents are stopped up, lest any light breaking in should destroy the whole effect. Bore only one hole, and let the hole have the shape of a round pyramid, whose base faces the sun and whose point faces into the room. Opposite this, you shall place white walls, or surfaces covered with linens and paper. Thus you will see everything illuminated by the Sun, and those walking through the streets (appearing like the antipodes <note>people on the opposite side of the earth who appear upside down</note>), and things on the right will appear on the left, and everything will be changed; and the further they are from the hole, the larger the form they will take on; and if the paper, or

# BOOK FOUR

As you approach the tablet, the objects appear smaller. However, by lingering there for a little while—for the images <note>original: "simulachra," referring to the visual representations or "idols" of objects</note> will not appear immediately—you will see them. This is because a strong stimulus sometimes produces a sensation of great intensity within the sense, and introduces such an impression that these sensations not only act upon the sense and reside within the sensory organs <note>original: "sensorijs"</note> while provoking them, but they even remain for a longer time after the objects themselves have departed. This can be clearly perceived: for when walking in the Sun, if we turn toward the darkness, that impression accompanies us so that we see nothing, or only with great difficulty, since the impression made by the light is still preserved in the eyes; then, as it gradually fades, we see clearly in the dark. Now, however, I shall announce what I have always kept silent until now, and thought should be kept silent. As

### All things with their own colors

If you seek to see: place a mirror directly opposite—not one that scatters by dispersing light, but one that unites by collecting it—moving it both toward and away until you recognize the true size of the image by the proper approach to the center. And if you, the observer, examine it more attentively, you will recognize the faces, gestures, movements, and clothing of men, the sky scattered with clouds in a deep blue <term>cyaneo: a dark blue or sea-blue color</term> color, and birds flying. If you arrive at the truth of this, you will rejoice not a little, and you will recognize a marvel: that all things are inverted because they are near the center of the mirror. For if you move them further away beyond the center, you will see them larger and upright, just as they are. To make this appear more clearly: let the Sun strike the faces; if not, let the mirror be directed so that it casts a reflection of the sun to illuminate them with a remarkable brightness, yet at a proper distance, varying the position until you recognize that you have achieved the true effect. From this, it is clear to philosophers and physicians in what place vision occurs in the eyes, and the much-debated question of "intromission" <note>The theory that vision is caused by light or "species" entering the eye, rather than the eye emitting rays.</note> is resolved; nor is there any more excellent way.

### How the Rainbow may be seen.

#### CHAPTER III.

This can happen in many ways, yet it is more conveniently prepared from crystal, or also from the Iris gemstone <note>likely a reference to quartz or a similar prismatic mineral</note>, similar to a hexagonal crystal, which the ancients named so because, when subjected to the rays of the sun, it strikes the high ceilings of the roof and the floor below with a trembling shadow, and displays colors similar to the rainbow. For it is born hexagonal, but if not, let a triangular one be prepared by art, a span <term>dodratali: a Roman measurement of about nine inches</term> in length and two fingers in breadth; then let the whole be polished and prepared. # BOOK FOUR

You may seek to see this: take a crystalline or glass <term>prism<note>A solid geometric shape with flat sides that refracts light; here, likely a triangular glass rod.</note></term> in your hands and hold it lengthwise to your eyes. If you look through the lower surface, everything appears diversely colored in crimson, green, yellow <note>original: "xantho"</note>, and blue. Likewise, if you turn your eyes toward the upper surface, the arrangement of the colors is swapped, since the perpendicular lines are changed. This is seen more clearly in the light of the sun. It will be no small sight to behold if you look upon gardens spread everywhere like carpets, adorned with flowers and wreaths; even people walking about appear like angels, the hems of their garments wreathed in these same colors. If the prism is held widthwise to the eyes, you will see the colors spread wide. If you turn it above or below the eyes, you see things inverted or upright without colors. The observer will seem to see with four eyes, yet everything appears bent due to the convexity of the eyes. By darkening some part of the surface with wax and frequently resolving these things through the eyes, one sees things that are more tedious to describe than they are pleasant to narrate.

WE CAN also see the same thing in this way: if a mirror is submerged in a basin full of water, a diligent observer will find the colors of the <term>rainbow<note>original: "iridis"</note></term> generated upon the edges of the walls, and they are quite beautiful. OTHERWISE, place a glass vessel or some round transparent object, moistened on the outside with water, against the sun. For when struck by the rays of the sun, it will imitate the appearance of the heavenly bow <note>The rainbow.</note> upon the flat surface below by the red reflection of the air, through the various bendings of the sunlight. SO ALSO by throwing water drop by drop against the sun, a rainbow will be seen leaping upon the surface against a dark background, as often happens to rowers by the movement of the water. The same is also observed to happen around lamps when the South wind blows, and especially to those who have watery eyes.

# NATURAL MAGIC

### How an object may be seen multiplied.
### CHAPTER IV.

Among the diversions that are passed around, there is no small delight in the <term>specillum</term> <note>original: "specillum"; here referring to a faceted lens or "multiplying glass" rather than a simple telescope</note>, that glass instrument which we place to our eyes in order to observe something more conveniently. Indeed, among those things which deceive the eyes, no better way can be found than through a medium; for when the medium is changed, all things are changed. Let it therefore be prepared from a more solid glass of greater thickness, so that it may be more easily ground into the faces of a wheel. Let it be shaped into many faces and angles, according to how many of an object we wish to count. In the center of these, however, the angles should reach a point <note>literally a "goal" or "turning post"</note> and align with the sharp focus of the eyes, so that the vision is dispersed and cannot perceive the truth. Once these many surfaces are prepared and placed to the eyes, if we look closely at someone's face, we shall see an <term>Argus</term> <note>A giant from Greek mythology with a hundred eyes</note> or a creature entirely made of eyes; if we look at a nose, we see nothing but noses; likewise with the fingers of the hand and the arms, so that one sees not a man, but the <term>Briareus</term> <note>A hundred-armed giant of Greek myth</note> imagined by the poets. If you happen to look at a coin this way, you will perceive many instead of one, such that it cannot be grasped by the hands, but often mocks the one reaching for it; it would be better to give money with such a device than to receive it! If you look from afar at a galley, it will appear as an armed fleet. If you look at a walking soldier, he will seem an army marching in a formed column. Thus it happens that a thing appears doubled, and you see the faces of men and their bodies as twofold. From this, diverse ways of seeing are created so that one thing appears as another, all of which are revealed to those who seek and explore.

->Q<-

If anyone wishes to see themselves in plane mirrors <term>plane mirrors: flat mirrors, as opposed to curved ones</term> with their feet upward and their head downward (although representing things this way is usually the property of concave mirrors), we shall nevertheless attempt to achieve it with plane mirrors in this manner: you shall arrange two plane mirrors along their length so that they stick together and cannot be easily moved apart, forming a right angle. When this has been properly prepared, let it be placed opposite the face along the line where they join, so that half the face is seen in one and the rest in the other. Then, by tilting the mirror to the left or right side for a more direct inspection, the head will appear to be twisted; when they bisect the face along their width, the image will be formed such that the head appears located below and the feet above. If the mirror is large enough, the entire man will be seen inverted. This happens, moreover, by mutual and multiple reflection: for the image leaps back from one into the other so that it appears turned upside down. <note>The author is describing a "corner reflector" effect where two mirrors at 90 degrees flip the geometry of the reflection.</note>

### A mirror made of plane surfaces in which several images of one thing appear. CHAPTER VI.

Wise antiquity invented a mirror composed of plane surfaces which, if one thing is shown to it, gives back several images of it at once, as is understood from the writings of Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, a 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian mathematician and astronomer who wrote a famous treatise on Optics.</note>. Its construction is as follows: Let a semicircle be laid out on a flat board or in the place where you seek to erect such a thing. Divide this semicircle equally with points according to the number of images desired; draw chords <term>chords: straight lines connecting two points on a curve</term> between these points, and let the segments be cut away. Then, erect plane mirrors of equal width and height in the shape of parallelograms; let them be firmly glued and fitted so they cannot be pulled apart, joined along their length and standing upright upon the flat surface. Finally, let the eye of the viewer be placed at the center of the circle, so that it has a uniform view of everything, seeing individual images in each individual mirror...

<margin>Theatrical Mirror</margin>
...it will look upon the faces, arranged in a circle, as is often seen in dances or theatrical spectacles. For this reason, it is called the "Theatrical" or "Theater-like" mirror by them. Indeed, all perpendicular lines originating from the center fall upon their surface and are therefore reflected back upon themselves; thus, they bring images to the eye, each one bringing forth its own. By revolving and varying the position in different ways, it shows various and diverse placements of the images <note>original: "simulachrorum" - often used to mean ghosts or phantom images in this period</note>.

### To construct a many-viewed mirror.

#### CHAPTER VII.

A mirror is constructed which is called a *polythaton* <note>Greek: "many-visible"</note>, that is, a mirror of many visible things. For by opening and closing it, it demonstrates twenty or more images of a single finger. You shall prepare it thus: let two rectangular mirrors of bronze or crystal be erected upon the same base, and let them be in a *sesquialter* proportion <note>original: "hemiola proportione" - a 3:2 ratio</note> or some other, and let them be joined together along one side of their length so that they can be aptly closed and opened like a book, and the angles varied, such as they are accustomed to making in Venice. For by presenting one face, you will perceive many faces in both; and the more tightly you close it and the smaller the angle, the more images there will be; but by opening it, they will be diminished, and at a more obtuse angle, fewer will be seen in number. Thus, showing a finger, you will see nothing but fingers; moreover, the right side is seen as the right, and the left as the left, which is contrary to ordinary mirrors <note>The author is describing how double reflection corrects the lateral inversion of a single mirror</note>. This happens by mutual reflection and striking back, from which arises the alternation of images.

### To compose a mirror from flat surfaces, in which one image is seen coming forward and another receding.

#### CHAPTER VIII.

But you will be able to easily construct a mirror from flat surfaces in which one image is seen approaching while another is seen receding: You shall take two flat mirrors whose length is double or one-and-a-half times the width, and this is for the sake of convenience; for their proportion does not truly matter. Let them be of the same length and equal, and let them be joined together inclined upon a rod <note>original: "stylus," likely a hinge or central axis</note>, and let them be erected perpendicularly upon some flat surface. When the mirrors are moved while fixed upon that mobile side, there is no doubt that you will see an image approaching in one and receding in the other; and as much as the one is brought near, the other will be moved away, so that at the same time an image is seen coming forward in this one and departing in the other.

### Using flat mirrors to see things that are happening far away and in other places.

#### CHAPTER IX.

In this way, one can truly look secretly and without suspicion at things that are happening safely far away and in other places, which could not be done otherwise. However, be diligent in the placement of the mirrors: Let a place be established in a home, or elsewhere, from which you wish to observe something, and let a mirror be adjusted in the area of a window or an opening so that it faces you. Let it be well-erected, and if necessary, fixed to the walls, moving and inclining it in every direction until it reflects the desired place. You will achieve this by moving your eyes and approaching the mirror. And if it should be difficult, you will not fail if you use a Dioptra <note>A classical surveying instrument used for leveling and measuring angles</note> or another instrument. Let the mirror be erected perpendicularly above a line bisecting the angle of the lines of reflection and incidence <note>The law of reflection: the angle at which light hits a mirror equals the angle at which it bounces off</note>, and you will look upon the things happening in that place clearly. This will also work for various different locations. From this it follows that if one mirror is not sufficient, you will be able to observe the same thing using several mirrors.

you will be able to: whether the visible object is lost due to excessive distance, or separated by intervening walls and mountains. But if **OTHERWISE** you seek to look at something peaked or upright which the sight can in no way perceive: you shall join two mirrors according to their length, as we have said. As it creates now an obtuse, now an acute angle as use requires, the line of the seen object is refracted from the middle of the second mirror to the sight. Let the angles of incidence and reflection be equal <note>This is the fundamental law of optics: the angle at which light hits a mirror equals the angle at which it bounces off</note>. If you seek to see high things, raise it; if low things, lower it, until it is refracted to the sight. Then at last it will be seen; if you hold the second mirror in your hands, it will happen more easily by looking into that one.

### How a mirror may be constructed from plane surfaces, by which you may see an image flying in the air.

#### CHAPTER XI.

Nor will that mirror composed of plane surfaces be of less construction or pleasure, which, lying on the ground, shows men flying, nor will you behold it without wonder. If anyone wishes to achieve this, the construction will be easy in this way: Let two pieces of wood be joined so that they imitate the shape of a <term>gnomon</term> <note>A carpenter's square or an L-shape</note>, and being fixed on all sides, let them form an angle so that they have the shape of a <term>right-angled</term> <note>original: "orthogony"</note> and <term>isosceles</term> triangle, and let it be made firm. Then, on each foot, let one large mirror be laid down, facing each other equally from the opposite side and equally distant from the angle; let one of them lie flat, and let the spectator be placed in the middle of them, raised somewhat from the ground, so that he may more easily see the shape of his heel by approaching and receding. For you will see it immediately if you place yourself in a straight line bisecting that angle, and let it be parallel to the horizon. Thus, the person imagining that mirror strikes across into the other; let the viewer look, let him move and toss his hands and feet as feathered creatures are accustomed to do when flying; thus he will see his own image flying in the other mirror, so that it is always moved, provided he does not depart from the place of reflection, for then it would be prevented.

### How one looking into a convex cylindrical mirror may see the hanging image of another object in the air.
#### CHAPTER XII.

A convex columnar mirror, or its half-cylinder (which I judge matters little), when placed in a house or another location, has such a position that it clearly projects the likeness of an object hanging in the air, not without a sense of wonder. If you desire to see this, you shall prepare it thus: let a cylindrical segment be fixed upright in the middle of a room upon a table or a tripod, so that it stands perpendicularly to the floor. Then, let the eye be placed at some hole or small chink at a little distance from the mirror, and let it be fixed so that it cannot move from side to side. Next, let the wall in the area facing the mirror be broken open and fitted like a window; let it have the form of a pyramid, with the point <note>original: "conus"</note> inside and the base outside, as is usually done. There, let a picture or some image be placed so that it can be viewed by the eye, but only as reflected from the surface of the columnar mirror. Thus, that picture placed outside, which cannot be seen directly through the eye-hole, will appear hanging in the air, which will be viewed with great admiration. A convex pyramidal mirror also achieves this, if you arrange it so that it reflects the same image. It is also done in the manner which we shall investigate further below.

The reflected image of the same object can be seen in the air even more easily than with the convex cylinder, and it will happen more clearly with a concave spherical mirror <term>sphærali speculo: a mirror shaped like a portion of a sphere's interior</term>. It is even more marvelous in a segment of such a mirror, for the image will be seen far from the mirror, appearing at the center of the sphere's curvature. You shall set it in some dark place, and when you stand at a little distance from it, inverted

You will discern the head, provided you look at the center with fixed and unblinking eyes, until the reflected image reaches your sight in the air beyond the mirror, entirely removed from the surface. So long as the visual rays are driven through the center of the mirror, you will see the specter and likeness within the mirror as well; from there, as the vision approaches closer, it becomes larger, until it seems it could be touched by your hands. If the portion of the mirror is large, there is no one who would not be amazed: for if one approaches nearer, he is terrified by the image, and it will seem as if nose meets nose, and as if they are breaking apart. If someone should attack it with a drawn blade <note>original: "stricto mucrone"</note>, he will seem to be attacked by another, and his hand pierced, so that he pulls his hand back; and if someone shows a fist while another looks in, the viewer will seem to be struck by a blow, so that he fears, twists his face away, and ducks. To finally know the center more easily, use this rule so that you do not have to turn to complex Mathematics: take the arc of the mirror's section with a page or wax, so that you may draw a chord on both sides, and cut it perpendicularly in two; in the intersecting lines or radii, you will necessarily find the center, which can be recognized in all concave objects. There are many experiments with concave mirrors, which we shall hand down in the following sections.

### The appearances and operations of the concave mirror.

#### CHAPTER XIV.

Once the center of the concave semi-circle <note>original: "hemicycli"</note> has been found, it will be easy to recognize all the variations, since all things are regulated and known from that point. If you wish, therefore, to see a head inverted, hold your head outside the center of the mirror, and you will immediately see it turned upside down, with the feet upward. If it is not a perfect hemisphere, but a segment of one, you will be able to fit your head inside more easily, and you will see the huge faces of a monstrous Bacchus <note>The Roman god of wine, often depicted with a wide, distorted, or theatrical face</note>, and a finger the size of an arm.

# NATURAL MAGIC

<margin>On the thickness of the image</margin>
It will render the image with a certain thickness. Hostius <note>Hostius Quadra, a Roman profligate mentioned by Seneca the Younger</note> made such concave mirrors, as Seneca reports, so that they would return an image larger than life. He was a man of great lust who arranged his mirrors in such a way that while he submitted to his partner, he could see all the movements of his stallion <note>original: "admissarii" - a term for a breeding horse, used here as a metaphor for a male lover</note> while turned away, and by deceiving himself with such a false thickness of the member, he took delight. 

But leaving those things aside, right and left will be seen reversed. By gradually moving the head further away, the face is made larger; when it is near the center, one will perceive two faces and four eyes, which will be seen by moving the mirror or the head to the side; for due to the smallness of the mirror, they cannot be seen all at once. When the eye is at the center, it will see nothing but itself; once past that point, it will see both doubled faces and two inverted heads, provided that the line drawn through the center strikes the space between the eyes. However, everything will appear to move in the opposite direction. Let the observer look with a firm gaze through the two ocular axes so that he sees everything doubled, just as it often happens by reason <note>i.e., through optical principles</note> that one thing appears to be doubled in many ways. 

So also, if the mirror lies on the ground or on some table, and people stand at an equal distance from it and look at each other, the mouth will appear narrow and long, and the face will appear greatly compressed and deformed. 

<margin>Burning mirrors</margin>
However, this mirror retains this best quality among others: that it hurls fire from a distance and causes no small combustion. Whoever desires to make a trial of this should place the mirror opposite the rays of the shining sun and place tinder or some ignitable thing in the location of the center—which you will find by moving it toward and away, as the cone of light reveals—and it immediately induces a flame. If it persists for a long time, it can melt lead or tin; although I remember reading that others have melted gold and silver. If, however, it is a segment of a larger sphere, it ignites across a greater distance.

### BOOK FOUR

### On the Parabolic Segment, and Other Burning Mirrors. CHAPTER XV.

We have frequently reported that mirrors can burn intensely, but now it is appropriate—having mentioned these things—to construct a mirror that is most powerful and can hurl fire from a great distance, such as the one we read that Archimedes <note>Archimedes of Syracuse (c. 287–212 BCE), the Greek mathematician famous for his war machines.</note> constructed according to the testimony of Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire.</note> and many others, and with which he set fire to the enemy's triremes <note>Ancient warships with three banks of oars.</note>. Know that the parabolic section burns most intensely among all others, for it gathers the rays more violently and forces them together into a single point. This section is called "rectangular" or a "parabola."

So that the method of making such a mirror may be clear to those who truly desire it, take this rule: Let there be a known distance at which you seek to emit rays and burn; double that distance, and let a right-angled round pyramid <note>original: "pyramis"; here referring to a cone.</note> be erected with such a diameter. For the sake of convenience, let it be made of wax or clay. Then, let a portion of the pyramid be cut off by a flat surface equidistant to the axis; this will be called "parabolic." If you cut toward the summit in an obtuse-angled cone <note>original: "amblygonio"</note>, you will have a hyperbola; toward the base in an acute-angled cone <note>original: "oxigonio"</note>, you will have an ellipse; but we seek the parabola.

However, let the portion be small, so that the mirror may be hollowed out more easily and the result may seem more miraculous. You shall imitate the shape of the surface of the cut-off portion using a flat board or an iron plate, as will be taught below. Then, introduce a fixed axis through the summit and the middle of the base, and you shall hollow out some material of steel or iron, or (if you prefer) some alloy, with which you will prepare the archetype. A mirror formed in this way is called a parabola, and when placed forcefully toward the Sun according to the given distance—so that its axis is directly against the Sun—it will burn. For the rays falling upon the mirror and striking it perpendicularly are reflected and coincide together into one, so that they all perform the office of a single, intensely burning ray. Therefore, there is no [distance] between...

among the sections, there is no other that projects fire further or more powerfully than the parabola <note>A parabolic curve focuses all incoming parallel light rays to a single point, making it the most efficient shape for a burning mirror.</note>. **OTHERWISE**, many mirrors are prepared which ignite, and not without a strong burning effect, such as those made from many flat mirrors arranged together; for this could not be done from a single flat mirror, as is established by the examination of geometrical reasoning. However, you shall construct a burning mirror from flat surfaces in this way: let a concave spherical base be prepared from any material, and let the mirrors be arranged together on the hollow surface so that they touch one another and leave no empty space. Let the segments of the mirror be hexagonal, quadrangular, or triangular; it would be more excellent if the rays were numbered by the multitude of surfaces. Thus, when placed opposite the Sun, it ignites a fire around the center. Anthemius <note>original: "Antenius"; likely referring to Anthemius of Tralles, the 6th-century mathematician and architect of the Hagia Sophia who wrote on burning mirrors.</note> reported that fire could be ignited from seven hexagonal mirrors bound together into one, yet the experiment does not succeed unless they are slightly inclined, so that the fire is kindled by the reflection of them all. **NOR OTHERWISE** do I deny that fire can be kindled from several convex spherical mirrors, so that by the number of many mirrors a meeting of rays occurs in each one; however, it is impossible from a single one, and the composition of such things would not be without great labor. The **SAME** can also be done by the composition and intersection of many concave mirrors, not without the laborious industry of the operator, but the work would be more effective; and no less so by the intersection of many pyramidal mirrors. # BOOK FOUR

### Fire is also kindled by round crystal
Fire may be ignited by a round crystal, either a perfectly round sphere or a flat glass prepared like a <term>specillum</term><note>A small lens or magnifying glass; the author refers here to a convex lens</note>. When placed somewhat opposite the sun, it unites the rays toward the opposite side and scorches. You must move the material intended to catch fire back and forth until you find the <term>cone</term><note>The focal point where the light rays converge</note> of the reflected rays; then, by pausing there for a little while, the fire breaks forth. We should especially marvel if only a small portion of a large sphere is used. Physicians report that when parts of the body must be cauterized, it cannot be done more effectively than with a crystal ball held against the rays of the sun.

### To construct a mirror in which many diversities of images may be seen. CHAPTER XVI.

Now we shall attempt to construct a mirror in which many diversities of images will appear; and if such a thing should be difficult to build, it will nevertheless repay the effort through the variety and occurrence of the images. To proceed with its fabrication: take a circle of either narrow or great capacity, depending on the size you wish the mirror to be. From this, cut away two portions of an arc: one based on the proportions of a pentagon, the other from a hexagon, in the manner revealed in mathematical principles. Then, let a certain board or iron tool be hollowed out according to the arc of the pentagon, so that it receives it perfectly in its lap, as if it were cut from it. The side of the hexagon, however, shall be of the opposite arrangement: for its convex quantity should be received by a board so that it stands out like that arc. In the meantime, let a plate of wax or lead of suitable thickness be taken, exceeding the arc of the hexagon in width and both arcs in length. Then, let the small plate be curved so that it sits properly in the hollowed wood, leaving no gap or small crack, and let the convex surface be preserved, standing out—

If it is protruding, then let it be applied from the inside according to its width, so that the shape of the concavity does not oppose the convexity; rather, let the same metal plate receive both portions without hindrance. Once the archetype <note>The "archetype" here refers to the master mold or original form used to shape the mirror.</note> is prepared in this way, let a mirror be constructed from a mixture of steel <note>original: "chalybis"</note> or another material, as we shall teach, and once polished, it will show many diversities of images. 

First, right sides will be seen as right, and left as left; whereas the property of all flat mirrors is that the eye which was right becomes left, and from the left it mutually becomes right. If, however, you move backward, the phantom <note>original: "idolum" - used here in the classical sense of an optical reflection or image rather than a religious idol.</note> will appear proportionate, and the image will project forward. If you approach closer to the convex surface, the image will become ugly, and the more you cling to it, the more deformed it will become; you will seem to more closely resemble a horse's head. But if you tilt the mirror, the image itself will tilt, and by varying the position and the mirror, you will see diverse variations—now the head downward and the feet upward—and you will see many things which I did not think necessary to recount at present. For if it is placed upon a revolving base so that it can show both faces, the observer will perceive everything for himself, both in front and behind. 

We also compose a MIRROR from all the aforementioned types, so that in it alone all images may be seen which appear in all others: many faces, now larger, now smaller, now right, now left, some closer, others further away, and some equal. If it is placed sinuous in one spot, concave in another, and flat in the middle, a great diversity of images will be observed. With a cylindrical convex MIRROR placed opposite the face, the more it is deformed in length, the more it will appear ugly in its thinness; if its length cuts across the face transversely, it shows a low and compressed countenance like a frog, so that nothing but the teeth are seen. It is in almost the same manner as one who is seen in a sword, or in iron...

With another polished and long piece of iron <note>The author refers to a metal mirror, common before glass mirrors were perfected</note>, if you tilt it forward, the forehead appears very large while the chin is small and slender, like the likeness of a horse. Conversely, if you look inside the hollow back, many versions of a single thing are seen, acting as the image <note>original: "idoli," referring to the reflected image or "phantom"</note> of the aforementioned mirror. If you place your eye at the center, you will see the whole image within the width of the mirror—the forehead, mouth, and the rest. If you invert such a mirror so that it cuts across the face broadly, you will immediately see the head upside down, along with the other effects we reported regarding the concave surface. If you look into a convex pyramidal mirror, the forehead appears sharp and the chin wide; if reversed, the forehead is wide and the nose becomes very long. Furthermore, you will see many faces in a concave mirror if you arrange several portions of flat mirrors according to that hollow; for someone looking in will count the faces according to the number of mirrors, all moving with the same motion. Finally, whatever the mirror may be, provided it is not flat, the reflection will always be seen as different from the actual object.

### How an image may appear to project outward from a hollowed mirror.
### CHAPTER XVII.

Through the industriousness of more recent scholars, it has also been achieved that many faces, or likenesses of a single thing, may be seen in the same mirror without the first image being obstructed. For they hollow out the mirror from behind and fashion a small concave space; then, by applying a thin foil <note>A metallic backing, usually tin or lead, used to create the reflective surface</note> as we shall teach, and arranging it properly, it will project the likeness of another thing outward. From this comes a most excellent invention: a person looking into a mirror sees the upright image of another object, so that to the viewer's admiration, when they try to seize it with their hands, they touch nothing but air. I remember seeing this often, and this is how the matter stands. From crystal prepared into a mirror—but better from an iris gem <note>likely a type of iridescent quartz or labradorite</note>, as I have always seen—they hollow out the image or likeness from behind; with whatever diligence they have placed it there, they then fit the foil over it, and

### How the metal foil is applied to mirrors, and how glass ones are finished. CHAPTER XVIII.

I have now, it seems, recounted all the apparitions in mirrors that we know of; now, however, it remains for me to say a few things that must not be overlooked, so that I may hand down a perfect knowledge of them. First, I shall discuss the finishing of mirrors prepared from crystal and glass, and then the mixtures and polishes of others, so that the skilled craftsman may know both how to recognize and how to compose them. For although among the many things that reflect an image—such as water, certain gems, and polished metal—nothing renders likenesses as clearly as lead spread under glass. Transparent mirrors are prepared either from crystal or glass; on flat crystal ones, certain foils are applied to the back, but for hollow <note>concave</note> and convex glass ones, a certain mixture is introduced with which the whole is smeared. When the crystal mirrors are flat and leveled, the craftsman makes a flat, thin tin foil <term>bracteam: a thin leaf or foil of metal</term> of the same size with as much diligence as possible. For if the crystal or glass were not lined with lead <note>or a lead-based alloy</note>, because of its own strength and the density of its nature, it would not hold the impressed image but would allow it to slip away; because glass is porous and transparent, it would not contain the image due to its translucency, and thus the likeness would vanish in it like light in the sun. Then, you shall spread quicksilver <term>viuum argentum: mercury</term> over this foil with your fingers so that the foil drinks it all in; and when you see it adhering to the surface and it appears silvery, you shall take this in your hands,

# BOOK FOUR

and when you begin to apply the foil to any part of the mirror, occupy and embrace the whole bit by bit with that thin plate, using no small amount of care, lest by trapping air inside, it renders the labor useless, as the air cannot be expelled afterward. When you have fitted this, place it upon some flat surface, apply a weight on top, and let it rest thus for a day. From this, Nature—the parent of all things—composed the eye in the likeness of a mirror; for behind the transparent parts, she placed a certain blackness, and if this were removed, the faculty of seeing would likewise be taken away. <note>The author draws a parallel between the "silvering" of a mirror and the choroid or retinal pigment of the human eye, which prevents light scattering.</note> **Otherwise**, we can finish convex mirrors in this way: fashion a huge round vessel of glass like a ball, as is usually done in the furnaces of glassmakers, and while it is glowing with fire, let it be pierced in the belly, either with a suitable instrument or by another method; let the molten glass touch the vessel, and let the blower perform his duty so that the blown part bursts into that spot. Through that hole, the mixture which you prepared beforehand is poured in—which you should transfer, already melted, into another convenient vessel, consisting of equal weights of antimony <term>stibium: a metallic element used here in an alloy</term> and crushed and filed tin. Let the operator revolve it for a short time in every direction, moving the work about; when the entire hollow has been coated, let the surplus flow out through the same hole and let it cool. Then you shall cut it into two or more mirrors, which you will successfully accomplish with emery stone <term>smiris: a hard mineral used for cutting and polishing glass</term>; for it possesses such a nature that by its touch it cuts glass and crystal. Thus you will have bright and finished mirrors.

### How mirrors are cast, and the mixture and polishing of mirrors. CHAPTER XIX.

# NATURAL MAGIC

The wax is now brought back to its form, and it will be more suitable for the work. Once you have the wax archetype <note>original: "archetipum," the master model used for casting</note> in the desired shape of the mirror, you should smear it with a thin brush using the following earth, properly prepared. Continue until it has reached a certain thickness; then, you shall apply another layer of more coarsely ground material by hand, of such solidity that it can support the molten metal and not break or fly apart into many pieces from the force of the fire. A small hole must be left, however, through which the wax may be drawn out and the metal poured in. Commit this to the sun to be dried out; for when heated by fire, the wax melts and flows. Thus, pour the liquefied metal into that hot cavity, let it cool, and you will have the desired form of the mirror.

For the **EARTHS** <note>The "earths" refer to various mineral and organic powders used for molding and polishing</note> which we are able to use are many: such as emery <note>original: "smiri"</note>, the substance called tripoli <note>a fine-grained sedimentary rock used as a polishing abrasive</note>, pumice, small stones, cuttlefish bones, the powder of ancient bricks (when rubbed against one another), the burnt bones of a goat, iron rust, and many others. These should be properly pounded and sifted through a fine sieve, then returned to the mortar and ground again. Afterward, they allow them to glow in a vessel over the fire, and they smooth them on a porphyry marble slab, working them until they are turned into the finest powder—so fine that it almost escapes the touch and could fly away into the air. 

Then, they make it even finer with water or leather; they moisten all or some of these with a specific water which they call a "menstruum" <note>In alchemy and early chemistry, a solvent used to dissolve or prepare solids</note>. They heap up a pot full of salt over live coals: when the salt ceases to crackle, they dissolve it in water. If you moisten it with "burning water" <note>original: "ardenti aqua," likely referring to distilled alcohol or a strong acid</note>, you will be able to cast the metal into the earth while it is still cold. For this is of the greatest importance: that the earth receives the liquefied metal, just as the substance called tripoli supports gold, small stones support lead and silver, and bronze is supported by the powder of millstones. You may use these not only for mirrors, but for other things as well.

The **MIXTURE** which we use in the forming of mirrors is generally as follows...

...is commonly achieved by everyone in this manner: *Bronze with three parts tin, a little tartar <note>potassium bitartrate, a byproduct of winemaking used as a flux</note>, and arsenic, so that it may flow and be incorporated together. Others add three parts of bronze to tin, with a tiny bit of antimony <note>original: "stibij"</note>, silver, or white pyrite <note>likely arsenopyrite or marcasite</note>. By others, the metal is made from lead with double the amount of silver. In this and other ways mirrors are tempered, but let it suffice to have mentioned these.* NOW that we have reported the constructions of mirrors, I see that the polishing remains to be discussed; so that after they have been cast and constructed, they may be smoothed and pre-polished. Thus, the ray of light leaping back from it may reflect an image, flow, and make the mirror represent a likeness; the smoothness and the symmetry of the parts matter greatly. If, however, it is not smooth, the image is split, and the figure may appear larger or smaller here and there, or distorted. When the mirror is in its rough state, it should be fitted to a wheel where tools are polished, and it should be brought to a state of smoothness and equality in all its parts. If you are polishing a concave or convex mirror, lest the motion of the wheel break it, you shall hew a piece of wood, shape it to the form of the mirror, and glue it with pitch so that it does not move. Then, you shall rub it vigorously with finely crushed emery <note>original: "smiri"</note> using a cloth or leather. Next, you shall properly polish it with pumice reduced to a fine powder (for it is easily pulverized) so that it is made fit and you induce a smoothness. Or, where it remains fixed to a board, let oxide of tin <note>original: "stanni calx"</note> and tripoli <note>a fine-grained sedimentary rock used for polishing</note> be applied and rubbed out. In the final polishing, however, you may use tartar, soot, or the ashes of willow or juniper, and it will shine most brilliantly. Emery is prepared thus: seek out the best kind, grind it, sift it through a cloth, and let it be moistened with water. Let these things said concerning mirrors and their workings be sufficient.

# ON NATURAL MAGIC

### Concerning Physical Bindings.
### CHAPTER XX.

Among natural phenomena are also found the experiments of physical "bindings," or as some prefer to call them, suspensions from the neck. These objects must either be hung from the neck or carried on other parts of the body so that they may bestow their inherent power upon those wearing them. I have chosen to translate these accounts from the writings of the Indians and Greeks—especially from Hermes <note>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with alchemy and magic</note>, Costa ben Luca <note>A Syrian Christian physician and scholar known for his translations of Greek science into Arabic</note>, and others. For these things work naturally; the power and operation they retain are derived either from the power that Zeno <note>The founder of Stoicism</note> calls "universal," or from the heavens themselves. No one will be granted the ability to witness these operations unless, as Plato and Socrates say, they are bound to and carried upon the appropriate limbs and locations. 

They say—and all agree with one voice—that the thought of the soul and a certain faith in the effect can be of great benefit. For Plato says that if the human mind holds for a certainty that something unhelpful will benefit it, it can indeed benefit from the mere intention of the mind. For through fear and joy, the body is not only changed while remaining healthy, but it can fall into a loosening of the bowels or into the most long-lasting sufferings. If these bindings help, they help through a natural cause alone: some contribute to the safety of life, while others produce strength, wisdom, or skill; still others make men sad, unhappy, unfortunate, lazy, or fearful. 

For example, if someone hangs a viper around their neck and strangles it with a linen thread—specifically one dyed with sea-purple—until it dies, that thread is effective against suffocation of the neck and abscesses <term>apostematibus: inflammatory swellings or tumors</term> of the throat, if you bind it to someone's neck. Likewise, Galen <note>The prominent Greek physician and philosopher in the Roman Empire</note> has recorded that if you hang a necklace of green jasper around the neck so that it touches the pit of the stomach, it strengthens the opening of the ventricle in no small measure. The teeth of a dog, when they [have bitten] a man...
